->**Decorative Marbled Endpaper**<-

<note>This page is purely decorative. Hand-marbled papers like this were frequently used by bookbinders from the 17th century onward to line the inner covers and flyleaves of manuscripts and printed books. The process involved floating pigments on a liquid surface to create unique patterns before dipping the paper.</note>

This page features a complex, swirling organic pattern. The marbling includes a vibrant array of colors—pink, green, orange, red, black, and white—accentuated by fine white speckles. 

A decorative border frames the design on the top, bottom, and left edges. This border consists of a repeating "U"-shaped motif rendered in a metallic gold or bronze pigment, set against a grey background.

<meta>This page serves as the title page for the 1618 edition of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens." It follows the initial library cataloging marks on the preceding flyleaf.</meta>

<margin>Aegle. Arethusa.</margin> <margin>Hespertusa.</margin> <note>These are the names of the Hesperides, the nymphs who tended the garden of the golden apples in Greek mythology.</note>

# ATALANTA FLEEING,
->that is,<-
## NEW ALCHEMICAL EMBLEMS ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE,

<margin>Hercules</margin> <note>Hercules is often associated with the eleventh labor: stealing the golden apples of the Hesperides.</note>
<margin>Venus</margin> <note>The goddess of love, who provided the golden apples used to distract Atalanta.</note>
<margin>Hippomenes</margin> <note>The suitor who defeated Atalanta in a footrace by dropping the golden apples.</note>

Arranged partly for the eyes and the intellect, with figures engraved in copper, and with added mottos, epigrams, and notes; and partly for the ears and the recreation of the soul with approximately 50 musical Fugues <note>A fugue is a musical composition where a theme is introduced by one part and taken up by others. Here, they represent the "chase" between the characters.</note> in three voices, of which two correspond to one simple melody perfectly suited for singing in couplets; to be seen, read, meditated upon, understood, judged, sung, and heard with unique pleasure:

<insert>Red circular institutional library stamp</insert>

->*By the Author*<-

## MICHAEL MAIER, Count of the Imperial Consistory, Doctor of Medicine, Knight Exemplary, etc.

<margin>Hippomenes</margin>
<margin>Atalanta</margin> <note>The heroine of the myth, known for her speed and her refusal to marry unless defeated in a race.</note>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page contains the author's introductory epigram, which sets the allegorical stage for the entire work. It continues from the title page, where the mythological figures of Atalanta and Hippomenes were first introduced. The catchword "Amplissimo" on the previous page's footer indicates the beginning of the dedication which likely follows this poem.</meta>

# EPIGRAM OF THE AUTHOR.

*The tireless youth <note>Hippomenes</note> carried off the prize of the Hesperian garden,*
*When the goddess Venus <note>original: "Cypride," an epithet for Venus referencing her birth on Cyprus</note> gave him the triple apple:*
*Following her, he threw it from the ground before the face of the fleeing*
*Maiden <note>Atalanta</note>, and by this means she was drawn into slow delays:*
*Soon he flashes, and soon she flashes, swifter than the East winds,*
*As he scatters the golden gifts upon the soil,*
*He delayed the slow footsteps of the girl*
*Once more, yet she once more turns her back in flight,*
*Until the lover threw the third weight, and then*
*ATALANTA yielded, the prize for her victor.*
*Hippomenes is the virtue of Sulfur; she is the virtue of fleeting*
*Mercury <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the "fixed" or masculine principle, while Mercury represents the "volatile" or feminine principle. Their "race" is the process of chemical transformation.</note>; in the race, the woman is conquered by the man.*
*After they embrace each other with longing love*
*In the temple of Cybele <note>The Phrygian Mother Goddess. In the myth, the couple's passion leads them to desecrate her temple, resulting in their transformation.</note>, anger corrects the Goddess;*
*The avenger clothed them both in a lion’s skin,*
*Thereafter they blush in their bodies, and are wild.*
*So that my Muse might express the very likenesses of this race*
*For you with a triple voice, she provides the fugues:*
*One voice remains simple and represents the delaying apple,*
*Another is fleeing, and the third is duly following.*
*Let these Emblems <note>term: Emblem (a symbolic picture accompanied by a motto and a poem, used to convey moral or natural secrets)</note> be set before your ears and eyes,*
*But let reason seek out the secret marks within:*
*I have brought these objects to the senses, so that the intellect*
*Might seize those precious things which lie hidden by those allurements.*
*Whatever wealth the world has, or whatever medicine of health,*
*The Twin-Lion <note>original: "Leogeminus." This refers to the two lions—the transformed couple—symbolizing the union of fixed and volatile principles in the Philosophers' Stone.</note> is able to supply it all.*

<summary>In this introductory poem, Michael Maier explains the alchemical allegory of the myth of Atalanta and Hippomenes, identifying them as Mercury and Sulfur. He also explains that the book's musical fugues are designed to mimic the movements of the mythic race to engage the reader's senses and intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Atalanta, Hippomenes, Sulfur, Mercury, Alchemy, Cybele, Fugue, Emblems, Philosophers' Stone, Twin-Lion</keywords>

->*To the Most Distinguished, Most Renowned, and Most Excellent*<-

# MÜHLHAUSEN
## IN THURINGIA, THE IMPERIAL
### SENATORIAL ORDER OF THE REPUBLIC,

->*To the men most outstanding in Virtue, Learning, and true Nobility of mind;*<-

# CHRISTOPHER
## REINART, Doctor of both Laws <note>original: "utriusque Juris Doctori." This refers to a doctorate in both Civil and Canon (church) law.</note>

->Most learned Privy Councilor to the Emperor<-
->*most agreeable, etc.*<-

->*To all and each of his Lords, who are always to be honorably revered, Michael Maier dedicates and offers this small token <note>original: "tecmerion," from the Greek "tekmērion," meaning a sign, mark, or proof.</note> of his benevolence and gratitude most dutifully.*<-

### MICHAEL MAIER, Doctor of Medicine, Count of the Imperial Consistory, Knight, etc. <note>original: "Med. D. Imper. Consist. Com. E. E. P. C." This refers to Maier’s titles as a Physician and a Count Palatine of the Imperial court.</note>

---

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'V' containing a central figure or mask surrounded by a dense frame of scrolled foliage and floral decorations.</image-desc>

Most distinguished and most learned men, they tell of that Tripod <note>The "Tripod of Pelops" was a legendary three-legged stand. In alchemy, the tripod often represents the furnace or the three principles of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note> given as a gift by Vulcan to Pelops the groom, when he took Hippodamia, daughter of Oenomaus, King of Elis, as his wife; that, since it was of exceptional craftsmanship, it was in turn offered by Pelops to Pythian Apollo at Delphi, from which then, through a virgin <note>The Pythia, the priestess and oracle of Apollo at Delphi.</note>, the oracles of that God were given forth <note>The text ends mid-word: "e-dita" (given forth/uttered).</note>

<summary>Michael Maier dedicates his work to the government of the Free Imperial City of Mühlhausen and specifically to the legal scholar Christopher Reinart. He begins the prose introduction by citing the mythological Tripod of Pelops, connecting the work's alchemical themes to ancient Greek oracular traditions.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a formal dedication to the Republic of Mühlhausen and its leaders. This page continues the introductory epistle, with the author, Michael Maier, explaining his motivations for dedicating this specific alchemical work to them, referencing his previous service to Emperor Rudolf II.</meta>

# E P I S T L E

...dedicated: Thus, since this **T R I P O D** crafted by Vulcan <note>In mythology, Vulcan (Hephaestus) was the god of fire and smithing. In alchemy, the "Tripod" often refers to the three-legged stand for the furnace or the chemical work itself.</note> has come into my hands, moved by the example of Pelops <note>According to legend, the hero Pelops dedicated a golden tripod to Apollo. Maier is framing his book as a similarly sacred or valuable offering.</note>, I have decided to consecrate and offer it to a most worthy place and order—and indeed before all others, to your Most Distinguished and Most Excellent [Lordships]. This is not with the intention that Oracles might be drawn from it (though neither those, nor chemical <note>original: "chymica." In this period, "chemical" and "alchemical" were used interchangeably to describe the study of matter and its transformations.</note> secrets, can be found wanting here), but so that I might publicly testify in some way my readiness of mind and benevolent will toward you. 

A few years ago, during my travels—while I was then serving as a medical councilor to His Imperial Majesty **RUDOLF II** <note>Rudolf II (1552–1612), Holy Roman Emperor, was a famous patron of the occult sciences, alchemy, and the arts.</note> (of divine memory)—you wished to declare through a servant with what spirit you regarded my Master: namely, a most excellent spirit and one most worthy of your status. From that time, I have always not only praised your virtues among foreigners with words as much as I could, but I have also applied myself to this: to reveal my mind to you more clearly through a physical object and to pour it out more abundantly. 

Since I perceived that this could not be done except through some small literary gift <note>original: "Xeniolo," from the Greek "xenion," a gift given by a host to a guest or vice versa.</note>, and having spent no little effort on this ***Atalanta Fleeing***, I wished to dedicate all of it, whatever it may be, to your Most Distinguished and Most Excellent [Lordships]. In this, I have imitated the writers of our own and ancient times, who never wished to go out among the common people, or to "walk upon the lips of men" <note>A classical idiom meaning to be famous or talked about.</note>, without a Scipio <note>Publius Cornelius Scipio Africanus; Maier uses the Roman general's name here as a synonym for a powerful, protective patron.</note> as a leader or companion. For if they had fallen, who would have come to their aid? I ask that I may be permitted to call you the Patrons of this little work—not because you have applied your hand or mind to this study (which is truly a doubtful one), as far as I know,

<meta>catchword: (nor</meta>

A

<summary>Michael Maier explains his decision to dedicate "Atalanta Fugiens" to the leaders of Mühlhausen, citing their past kindness during his service to Emperor Rudolf II. He compares his book to a "Tripod of Vulcan" and emphasizes the need for noble patrons to protect a writer's reputation.</summary>

# D E D I C A T O R Y

<sig>A 3</sig>

->(for indeed, so much leisure did not remain from my most pressing affairs)<-
but because you appeared to me sufficient to protect any branch of learning, and no other subject, whether more worthy or more honorable (setting aside its misuse <note>original: "excluso abusu." Maier is likely defending the reputation of alchemy, suggesting that while many "abuse" or fake the art, the core science remains honorable.</note>), occurred to me at the time. Whatever it may be, you will judge my efforts not by the cheapness of the paper, but by the sincerity of my mind (as I well know), and you will hereafter hold and count me among the most devoted of Your Honors <note>original: "Amplituddd." This is an abbreviation for *Amplitudines*, a formal Latin title of address for high-ranking civic leaders or magistrates.</note>. Farewell. Given at Frankfurt am Main <note>original: "Francofurti ad Moenum." Frankfurt was the heart of the European book trade and the site of the famous Book Fair where Maier’s works were often sold.</note>, in the year 1617, in the month of August.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the "Preface to the Reader" in Michael Maier's work. It follows the formal dedication to the officials of Mühlhausen. Here, Maier transitions from civic matters to the philosophical and anthropological justifications for studying alchemy, employing the common Renaissance theme of man as a microcosm.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<meta>catchword: secre-</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular decorative headpiece featuring symmetrical woodcut scrollwork and acanthus leaves, with two winged cherubs (putti) flanking a central floral element.</image-desc>

# Preface to the Reader.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'H' decorated with scrolling foliage and vines.</image-desc>

*It is confessed by all, honest reader, that man reflects a summary of the universe <note>original: "universi compendium." This refers to the concept of the Microcosm, the idea that the human body and soul contain the same structures and elements as the entire universe (the Macrocosm).</note> in his own composition, and is destined to live through three kinds of life: namely, the **vegetable** in the mother's womb, where he grows and increases just like a plant; the **sensible**, truly in this world, where for the most part he is led by his senses like the rest of the animals, from which he differs because he begins to use his intellect, though imperfectly; and finally the **intelligible**, in the other world with God and the intelligences or good Angels who assist Him. In this present life, the more one approaches the divine nature, the more he joys and delights in things to be investigated by the intellect—things that are subtle, wonderful, and rare. And conversely, the more one sinks toward the bestial race <note>original: "pecuinum genus," literally "the cattle-like kind."</note>, the less he is captured by these things, and the more he is bound to the baser corporeal senses. We see examples of both: some, as more learned men, are dedicated to those arts and sciences polished by the mind, while many more are given to these [vices], namely pleasures of the body, lust, gluttony, external pomp, and similar things. Moreover, for the cultivation of the intellect, God has hidden infinite secrets <note>original: "arcana."</note> in nature, which are struck out like fire from flint by innumerable sciences and arts and are brought into use. Among these are the **Chemical** matters...* <note>original: "Chymica." In this period, "Chymistry" encompassed both what we now call chemistry and the spiritual/transmutational pursuit of alchemy.</note>

<summary>Maier begins his preface by describing the human being as a microcosm destined for three stages of existence—vegetative, sensory, and intellectual. He argues that the study of nature's "secrets," specifically alchemy, is a divine pursuit that separates the learned from the base.</summary>

<keywords>microcosm, three lives, intellect, secrets of nature, alchemy, Michael Maier, philosophy of science</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Preface to the Reader" from page 6. Having previously discussed the nature of man as a microcosm, Maier now transitions into the necessity of intellectual study over blind experimentation and introduces the importance of the arts—specifically optics, painting, and music—as companions to philosophical inquiry. The catchword "secre-" from the previous page leads into "Secreta" (Secrets) at the start of this page.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>

# TO THE READER.

*These **secrets** are not of the least importance; rather, after the pursuit of divine things, they are the first and most precious of all. They are intended not for the wandering charlatans and "pseudo-chemical" deceivers who haunt the marketplaces (who, in these matters, are like **asses at the lyre** <note>original: "asini ad lyram." A classical proverb describing someone who is utterly unsuited to the art or subject they are attempting to engage with.</note>, being entirely estranged from all good doctrine and right intention), but for higher intellects—those who have been liberally educated and born for the investigation of greater things. Because these secrets are so subtle, majestic, sacred, rare, and hidden, they must be grasped by the **intellect** before they are perceived by the **senses**. They are mastered more through profound contemplation born from the reading of authors—and the comparison of those authors both with one another and with the works of nature—than through sensory operation or manual experimentation, which is blind without a preceding **Theory**.*

*Next in rank after these intellectual sciences are those which deal with visible and audible objects. These include **Optics** (or perspective) and **Painting**—which is called "mute poetry" by certain poets, just as poetry is conversely called "speaking painting"—as well as **Music**, whether vocal or instrumental. The ancient Philosophers practiced music so diligently that any man who refused the lyre at banquets was considered unlearned and was forced to **sing to the myrtle** <note>original: "ad myrtum canere." Referring to a Greek custom where a guest who could not play the lyre would instead hold a branch of myrtle while singing a "skolion" or banquet song.</note>, as is recorded of the statesman Themistocles. **Socrates** was instructed in Music, as was **Plato** himself, who declared that a man is not harmoniously composed if he does not take joy in musical harmony. **Pythagoras** was likewise celebrated in this art; it is said he used musical symphony every morning and evening to settle the souls of his disciples.*

*For Music possesses this peculiar power: to stir or soothe the emotions according to the different **musical modes** <note>In ancient Greek music theory, "modes" were scales thought to possess specific emotional or moral characters.</note>. For example, the **Phrygian Mode** was called "warlike" among the Greeks because it was played when entering war and battle, having a unique power to rouse the spirits of soldiers. In its place, the **Ionian Mode** is now used for the same purpose, which formerly*

<meta>catchword: ama- (likely referring to "amatory" or "loving")</meta>

<summary>Maier argues that alchemical secrets require intellectual contemplation and "Theory" rather than blind experimentation. He elevates Music, Painting, and Optics as essential sciences, citing how ancient philosophers like Pythagoras and Plato used music to harmonize the soul.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy vs. pseudo-chemistry, theory and practice, liberal arts, music theory, Pythagoras, Socrates, Plato, Phrygian mode, Ionian mode, philosophy of art</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Preface to the Reader" from page 7. The previous page ended mid-sentence while discussing how the ancient Philosophers practiced music so diligently that anyone who refused to play the lyre was judged poorly. This page concludes that thought by discussing specific musical modes, the legends of Timotheus and Terpander, and then introduces the core concept of the book: the synthesis of sight (images), hearing (music), and intellect (alchemy).</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

->PREFACE<-

*...was judged to be **amorous** <note>original: "amatorius"</note> (just as the Phrygian mode is regarded today), from which we infer that their musical modes have been exchanged for one another. They say that Timotheus of Miletus <note>A famous Greek musician known for his innovations</note> used the **Phrygian mode** to make Alexander the Great more eager and spirited in matters of war; Cicero mentions this in the second book of *On the Laws*. Terpander of Lesbos, on the other hand, used the **Ionian mode**; having been summoned by the Spartans while they were embroiled in civil unrest, he so soothed their spirits with the sweetness of his song that they returned to friendship and ceased their rebellion. Hence, in the judgment of the Spartans, Lesbian singers always earned the first prize. Regarding Music, Fabius <note>The Roman rhetorician Quintilian</note> says thus: "Music," he says, "is an honorable and pleasant pastime, most worthy of free-born minds."*

<margin>x</margin>
*Therefore, so that we might have these three objects of the more spiritual senses—namely of **sight** and **hearing**, and even of the **intellect** itself—as if in a single view and embrace, and so that we might instill them into our minds for better understanding all at once, behold! We have joined **Optics** <note>The visual art of the emblems</note> with **Music**, and sense with intellect. That is to say: things rare to see and hear joined with chemical emblems, which are proper to this science. For while other arts might publish emblems concerning morals or other matters rather than the secrets of nature, this seems foreign to their goal and purpose, since they wish to and should be understood by everyone. Not so with **Chemistry** <note>Alchemical science</note>, which, like a chaste virgin behind a lattice, or like Diana <note>The goddess of the hunt and the moon, often representing the pure but hidden matter of the Alchemical Work</note>, is not to be seen without a robe of many colors, for reasons expressed elsewhere.*

*Receive, I say, all four of these at once in this single book and apply them to your use: those things composed, poetic and allegorical; those painted and emblematic, engraved on **Venus** (that is, on copper) <note>original: "Veneri sive cupro." In alchemy, the planet Venus represents the metal copper</note>, and not without "Venus" (that is, grace); those most secret chemical matters to be investigated by the intellect; and finally, the rarer musical compositions. If your use of them be intellectual rather than merely sensual, it will be all the more [useful]...*

<summary>Maier explains the power of music to influence the soul, citing classical examples like Timotheus and Alexander the Great. He then introduces his "multimedia" approach, combining alchemical emblems, poetry, and music to engage the reader's sight, hearing, and intellect simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>Phrygian mode, Timotheus of Miletus, Alexander the Great, Terpander, Spartans, Quintilian, Optics, Music, Chemistry, Alchemy, Diana, Venus, Copper, Emblems</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the "To the Reader" preface from page 8. The author is explaining how sensory input (sight and sound) serves as the necessary gateway to intellectual understanding, specifically justifying the use of music and emblems to teach alchemical philosophy. He is about to explain the title "Atalanta Fugiens" (Atalanta Fleeing) through the myth of Atalanta and Hippomenes.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>->TO THE READER<-</header>

...one day be better and more pleasant: but if indeed it is first surrendered to the senses, there is no doubt that a transition will be made from sense to the intellect, as if through a door. For it is said that **nothing is in the intellect which did not first enter through some sense**, since the intellect of a newborn man is held to be like a **blank slate** <note>original: "tabula rasa"</note>, upon which nothing is yet written, but whatever one wishes can be written by the mediation of the senses, acting as a stylus. And it is commonly said: *There is no desire for what is unknown*, because it is necessary that the senses—as investigators and messengers—first bring every knowable thing and make it known to the intellect, acting as dictator and arbiter, just like **sentinels** <note>original: "excubitores"</note> watching over the gates of some city (at their respective organs). Concerning the reasoning behind the title of these emblems, I shall add a few words, lest this also seem strange and discordant to you. **Atalanta** is celebrated by the poets for her flight, by which she outran all her suitors in a race; because of this, death befell those who were defeated, while she was the prize of victory, until she was overcome and won by **Hippomenes**—a youth more daring and far-sighted—by the throwing of three golden apples at intervals during the race. While she stopped to pick these up, she was overtaken by him, who was already reaching the goal. Just as this Atalanta flees, so one **musical voice** <note>original: "vox Musicalis"</note> always flees before another, and the second follows it, like Hippomenes. Yet they are stabilized and held firm by a third voice, which is simple and of a single value, acting as a golden apple. This same maiden is purely chemical—namely, the **Philosophical Mercury** <term>The elusive, volatile spirit of matter</term> fixed and held back in its flight by the **golden sulfur** <term>The active, "fixing" principle that solidifies the fluid mercury</term>. If anyone knows how to stop this mercury, he will have the bride he seeks; if not, he will face the ruin of his affairs and his own destruction. From Hippomenes and Atalanta coming together in the temple of the Mother of the Gods—that is, in the **vessel** <note>The alchemical glass or retort</note>—they become lions, or rather, they acquire a **red color** <note>The "Rubedo" or Red Work, the final and most perfect stage of alchemical transformation</note>. He also obtained...

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...also (original: hac)</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that knowledge must enter the mind through the senses, justifying his use of music and images. He then decodes the book's title by linking the myth of Atalanta's race to both musical fugues and the alchemical process of "fixing" mercury with sulfur.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "To the Reader" preface. Having previously discussed how the senses act as a gateway to the intellect, the author now justifies the title of the work by connecting the various myths of Atalanta to the book’s allegorical and musical structure.</meta>

# PREFACE

...this maiden <note>Atalanta</note> achieved victory over men by killing a boar of remarkable size <note>the Calydonian Boar</note>, for which she was rewarded with a prize by Meleager. Near the Stethaeum, at the temple of Aesculapius <note>original: "Æsculapifanum"</note>, she drew forth water from a rock she had struck, which she drank when she was thirsty. Since these things are truly allegorical and <term>emblematic</term> <note>symbolic images paired with text to convey a deeper moral or philosophical meaning</note> and in no way historical, I wished to designate this intellectual treatise as "Emblematic" in commemoration of this heroine—especially since the apples thrown to her from the Hesperian gardens by Venus, the goddess of sweetness, were supplied to Hippomenes.

In those measures or musical <term>fugues</term> <note>original: "fugis"; a musical form where voices follow one another, mirroring the "flight" of Atalanta</note>, you see this observed: that the individual <term>distichs</term> <note>original: "disticha"; two-line verses or couplets</note> adapted to those three voices can be expressed appropriately by singing. In these, since so many varieties of fugues are accommodated to one simple voice, every wise and intelligent person will appreciate this emblematic figuration—both in these melodies and in the adaptation of the verses to each little note—and will consider it of no small value. 

For if some merchants value and purchase a work of painting for a great sum of money where only the eyes are deceived—judging it to be most near to nature—why should the learned not hold in high esteem and value these figures? They serve both the intellect and multiple senses in such a way that great utility, beyond mere delight, is to be hoped for from them. Farewell.

<summary>Maier explains that the myths of Atalanta are allegories for alchemical and intellectual processes, justifying his use of "fugues" and emblems. He argues that combining music, art, and text provides a more useful and profound experience for the learned than mere painting does for the eyes.</summary>

<keywords>Atalanta, Meleager, Hippomenes, Venus, Golden Apples, allegory, musical fugues, emblems, distichs, Aesculapius, intellect and senses</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues from the first emblem's image and epigram, moving into the first "Discourse." In Michael Maier’s *Atalanta Fugiens*, each emblem is followed by a scholarly commentary (Discourse) that explains the alchemical, philosophical, and scientific meaning behind the imagery.</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE I</header>

<margin>Lullius in the same place: the wind carries it in its belly, that is, sulfur is carried in quicksilver.</margin>
<margin>And Chapter 47. The Stone is fire carried in the belly of the air.</margin>

Hermes, that most diligent investigator of every natural secret, in his **Emerald Tablet** <note>original: "tabula sua smaragdina." The *Emerald Tablet* is a foundational text of Hermeticism and alchemy, legendary for containing the secret of the "Prima Materia" or first matter.</note> graphically, though succinctly, describes the work of nature, where among other things he says: *The wind has carried him in his belly*, as if he were to say: He, whose father is the **Sun** and whose mother is the **Moon**, before he is brought forth into the light, will be carried by windy vapors, just as a bird is carried by the air while it flies. From these vapors or winds (which are nothing other than air in motion), coagulated water is made; and from this water, when mixed with earth, all minerals and metals arise. Indeed, these very things are determined to consist of and be immediately coagulated from vapors. Whether, therefore, it is placed in water or in vapor, the matter comes to the same result, because both are the material of the wind.

The same must be said of minerals and metals, though more remotely. It is asked, however: who is he who must be carried by the wind? I answer, *chemically*, it is **sulfur**, which is carried in **quicksilver** <note>original: "argento vivo," literally "living silver" or mercury.</note>, as Lullius <note>Refers to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many alchemical works were later attributed.</note> witnesses in *The Codicil*, chapter 32, and all others as well. *Physically*, it is the fetus that must soon be born into the light. I say also *Arithmetically*, it is the **cube root**; *Musically*, it is the **double-octave** <note>original: "Disdiapason." A musical interval spanning two octaves, representing a perfect harmonic system.</note>; *Geometrically*, it is the **point**, the beginning of a flowing line; *Astronomically*, the **center** of the planets Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars. Though these are diverse subjects, yet if they are well compared with one another, they will easily demonstrate the "fetus of the wind," which must be left to the greater or lesser industry of each reader.

But let me point out the matter more clearly: Every **Mercury** <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of volatility, fluidity, and transformation.</note> is composed of vapors; that is, from water lifting the earth with it into an aerial rarity, and from earth forcing the air to return into a "watery earth" or "earthy water." Since the **Elements** are in it through all things, both mixed and crushed together into a certain viscous nature, they do not easily recede from one another. Instead, either the **volatiles** <note>Substances that easily vaporize or evaporate.</note> follow upwards, or they remain below with the **fixed** <note>Substances that remain stable and do not change state when heated.</note> parts. The first of these appears in common Mercury, the other in the Philosophical [Mercury] and fixed metals. In the latter, the fixed elements predominate over the volatiles; in the former, the volatiles over the fixed.

Truly, it is not without cause that **Mercury** is said and held to be the messenger, interpreter, and, as it were, the intermediate running servant of the other gods, with wings adapted to his head and feet. For he is windy and flies through the air like the wind itself, as is proven <note>original: "convincitur," completed from the catchword on the following page.</note> in common experience, to the loss of many...

<summary>Maier provides a multidisciplinary commentary on the first line of the Emerald Tablet, explaining that the "Philosophical Child" is a product of the elements (air/wind) and can be understood through chemistry, mathematics, music, and astronomy.</summary>

<keywords>Emerald Tablet, Hermes Trismegistus, Sulfur and Mercury, Four Elements, Ramon Llull, Volatile vs Fixed, Alchemical Fetus, Disdiapason</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse I, specifically expanding on the mythological and alchemical identity of the "wind" and the "fetus" mentioned on the previous page. It connects the Greek god Hermes (Mercury) and the North Wind (Boreas) to the chemical processes of maturation and purification.</meta>

->is bound: But because he carries the caduceus <note>A staff entwined by two serpents, the traditional symbol of Hermes/Mercury.</note> encircled by two serpents placed crosswise, with which he leads souls out of bodies and brings them back again, and performs many contrary acts of this kind, he expresses most perfectly the symbol of the **Philosophical Mercury**.<-

**Mercury**, therefore, is the wind, which receives the sulfur—or **Dionysus**, or if you prefer, **Aesculapius**, as a still-imperfect fetus from the maternal womb, or even from the ashes of the mother's burned body—and carries it to that place where it can be matured. And the Embryo is **Sulfur**, which was infused by the celestial **Sun** into the wind of **Boreas** <note>The North Wind.</note>, so that the wind might bring it forth when matured. When the time of its gestation is completed, the wind gives birth to twins: one with white hair, called **Calais**, and the other with red hair, **Zetes**. These were the sons of Boreas (as the *chemical* poet **Orpheus** writes) who accompanied **Jason** among the **Argonauts** to take the Golden Fleece from the Colchians.

Indeed, **Phineus**, the blind prophet infested by the **Harpies**, could not be liberated except by these aforementioned sons of Boreas. For this favor obtained from them, he, being grateful, declared to the Argonauts the entire plan of their journey. Yet the **Harpies** are nothing other than the corrupting sulfur, which is driven away by the sons of Boreas when they have reached the proper age; and from that which was imperfect or troubled by noxious flying things, it becomes perfect, no longer subject to that evil. This then indicates the way for **Jason** the physician to obtain the **Golden Fleece**.

Our own **Basil** <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist, often cited as a master of the "Great Work."</note> also mentions these ancient matters among others, where he clearly says in his sixth book: *For a double wind must come, called Vulturnus <note>The East-Southeast wind.</note>, and then a single wind called Notus <note>The South wind.</note>, which will blow impetuously from the east and south; when their motion ceases, so that water is made from the air, you may confidently trust that the spiritual shall become corporeal.*

And **Ripley** <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist and author of *The Compound of Alchymy*.</note> in his eighth "gate" says that our infant must be reborn in the air, that is, in the belly of the wind. To the same sense, the ***Ladder of the Philosophers*** <note>original: "scala philos." Refers to the *Scala Philosophorum*, an anonymous alchemical treatise.</note> says in its sixth step: *And it must be known that the child of the wise is born in the air.* And in the eighth step: *The aerial spirits climbing together into the air love one another, for as Hermes says, the wind carried him in its belly: because the generation of our offspring takes place in the air, and the one being born in the air is born wisely. For he ascends from the earth to heaven, and descends again to the earth, acquiring the power of the things above and the things below.*

<summary>This page explains that Mercury (as wind) acts as a protective carrier for the alchemical "embryo" (Sulfur), using the myths of the Argonauts and Harpies to symbolize the purification of matter. It concludes with citations from alchemists like Basil Valentine and George Ripley to emphasize that the transformation from spirit to body occurs through the medium of air.</summary>

<keywords>Mercury, Sulfur, Boreas, Calais and Zetes, Harpies, Argonauts, Jason, Basil Valentine, George Ripley, Emerald Tablet, distillation, purification</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 17, which introduced the Second Emblem and its epigram. While the previous page focused on the poetic imagery of the Earth as a nurse (drawing parallels to Romulus and Remus), this "Discourse" provides the philosophical and scientific justification for that metaphor, grounding the alchemical process in Aristotelian physics and medical theory regarding nutrition and gestation.</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE II.</header>

Among the **Peripatetics** <term>Peripatetics: Followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy, who often discussed logic and nature while walking through the Lyceum in Athens.</term> and rightly judging Philosophers, it is established that the **Nutrient** is turned into the substance of the **nourished**, and is assimilated to it—not indeed before, but after its alteration—and this is held as a most true **Axiom**. For how would a nutrient, which was already similar and the same as the nourished beforehand, need a change of its essence? If that happened, it would not remain the same or similar. Or how could those things which cannot be assimilated to the nourished be taken as nourishment, such as wood, stones, and other things of that kind? Therefore, as the first scenario is useless, so the second is contrary to nature. 

However, for a newborn human to be nourished by the milk of animals is not foreign to nature: because its assimilation can occur, though far more easily from the mother’s milk than from another's. Whence physicians conclude that it contributes to the health, the likeness of substance and character, and the strength of the infant, if it is always fostered and raised on its own mother’s milk; conversely, if on another's. This is the harmony of all nature: that **like rejoices in its like**, and follows in its footsteps as much as possible in all things, by a certain silent consensus and conspiracy. 

The same occurs in the natural **work of the Philosophers** <note>original: "naturali Philosophorum opere." This refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, which is just as much ruled by nature in its formation as an infant in the mother’s womb. And although a father and mother, and the nurse herself, are attributed to it by way of likeness, it is nonetheless no more artificial than the generation of any animal. Two seeds are joined by a certain delightful artifice by animals and by both sexes of man, from which, once united through successive alteration, an **Embryo** is made, which grows and increases, acquires life and motion, and is then nourished by milk. 

Truly, at the time of conception and pregnancy, it is necessary for a woman to conduct herself temperately in heat, food and drink, motion and rest, and other things; otherwise, a miscarriage will follow and the destruction of the conceived fetus. This observation of the **six non-natural things** <term>6 non-naturals: In Galenic medicine, these are external factors—air, food/drink, sleep/wake, motion/rest, excretion/retention, and passions—that must be balanced to maintain health.</term>, because it is prescribed by physicians according to their art, is also artificial. In the same way, if the seeds are not joined in the Philosophical work, they must be joined. But if they were found joined somewhere—as the seed of the rooster and the substance of the hen are held together in one container in an egg—then the work of the Philosophers would be even more natural than the generation of animals. But let us grant, as the philosophers test[ify]—

<meta>catchword: test[ify]— (original: testan-)</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical process through the lens of Aristotelian biology, arguing that the "Philosophical work" follows the same natural laws of nutrition, gestation, and assimilation as a human embryo.</summary>
<keywords>Peripatetic philosophy, nutrition, assimilation, maternal milk, Great Work, embryo, six non-naturals, alchemy as nature</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Discourse II" from page 17. While the previous page established the Aristotelian axiom that "like rejoices in like" and discussed how a child is best nourished by its own mother's milk, this page applies those biological principles to the alchemical process. It justifies the metaphor of the Earth as a "nurse" for the "Philosophical Son" (the Stone) by comparing alchemical heat to the natural warmth used in hatching eggs or raising silkworms.</meta>

### DISCOURSE II.

They testify that one comes from the East and the other from the West, and they become one; what more is provided to these but mixing in their own vessel, tempered heat, and nourishment? The vessel is indeed artificial, but in this, no distinction is made, whether the nest is made by the hen herself, or is arranged by a peasant in some random place (as is usual); the generation of the eggs and the hatching of the chicks from them will be the same. Heat is a natural thing, whether it comes from the tempered fire of ovens and the decay of manure, or from the sun and air, or from the mother’s vitals, or from elsewhere. Thus Egypt provides a natural heat for hatching eggs by art through its ovens. We read that the seeds <note>original: "semina"; here referring to eggs</note> of silkworms <term>Bombycum: Silkworms, whose cultivation was a frequent marvel in early modern natural history.</term>, and even the eggs of hens, have been hatched by the warmth of virginal breasts. Art and nature thus lend each other a helping hand, so that the former becomes the substitute for the latter, and vice versa: Nevertheless, Nature remains the Mistress and art the handmaid. **BUT WHY EARTH** should be called the nurse of the Philosophical Son might cause doubt for some, since earth is the most parched and arid among the elements, to which dryness occurs as its proper quality? But the answer is that "elementated earth" <term>elementated earth: In alchemy, this refers to the physical, tangible earth we touch, as opposed to the pure, abstract "Element" of Earth used in theoretical physics.</term> is understood here, not the Element, of which we have made abundant mention and explained the nature on the first day of the *Philosophical Week* <note>original: "Septimanæ philosophicæ"; likely a reference to a specific alchemical text or a division of the Great Work.</note>. This nurse is of the Heaven, not because she dissolves, washes, or moistens the offspring, but because she coagulates, fixes, and colors it, and converts it into pure sap and blood. For nutrition encompasses growth in length, breadth, and depth—that is, extending through all dimensions of the body—and since this is present here and is provided to the philosophical offspring by the earth alone, the earth should not undeservedly be called by the name of "nurse." Yet something contrary to other kinds of milk (which are converted and do not convert) occurs in this wonderful terrestrial juice: because of its most powerful force, it greatly alters the nature of the one nourished, just as the milk of the she-wolf is believed to have disposed the body of Remulus <note>Remulus: A variation of Romulus, the legendary founder of Rome who, along with his brother Remus, was suckled by a wolf.</note> toward a spirited nature ready for war.

C ij
<meta>Catchword: Sehe [Behold/See]</meta>

<summary>This page explains that the Earth acts as a "nurse" to the alchemical Stone by providing the necessary "terrestrial juice" that fixes and coagulates it. It argues that alchemical "art" simply mimics natural heat—like Egyptian egg-ovens or the warmth of the human body—to facilitate growth.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse following Emblem III of Michael Maier’s "Atalanta Fugiens." It expands on the metaphor of the laundry-woman, bridging the gap between common domestic chores and the high art of alchemical purification.</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE III.</header>

If certain filth should come upon linen cloths, by which they are stained and blackened, such as earthly dregs, they are washed away by the nearest element—namely, water—and the cloths are exposed to the air, so that by the heat of the sun (serving as fire, the fourth element), the humidity is drawn out together with the dregs. If this happens repeatedly, those cloths which were previously filthy and foul-smelling become pure and cleansed of stains. This is the "art of women," which they have learned from nature itself. For we see that the bones of animals exposed to the air, which are at first black and squalid, if they are often moistened by rain and dried again many times by the succeeding heat of the sun, are reduced to a state of highest whiteness, as <term>Isaac</term> <note>Likely a reference to Isaac Hollandus, a famous alchemical author often cited for practical laboratory instructions.</note> notes. In the same way, in the philosophical subject <note>The "subject" refers to the raw material or "Prime Matter" used in the alchemical work.</note>, whatever crudities and dregs are present are purged and erased by their own waters poured over them, and the body is reduced to a great clarity and perfection.

For the whole sum of <term>chemical</term> operations—such as <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to a high temperature to remove volatile components or reduce it to powder.</note>, <term>sublimation</term> <note>Turning a solid into a vapor and back into a solid to purify it.</note>, <term>solution</term> <note>Dissolving a solid into a liquid.</note>, <term>distillation</term> <note>Purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor and cooling it back into liquid.</note>, <term>descension</term> <note>A method of distillation where the vapors or oils are forced to move downward into a receiver.</note>, <term>coagulation</term> <note>Turning a liquid into a solid or semi-solid state.</note>, <term>fixation</term> <note>Making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates or changes when heated.</note>, and the rest—are completed by this one washing. For he who washes an impure thing with waters performs the same feat that is achieved by so many different forms of operating. For by fire, as the <term>Rosary of the Philosophers</term> <note>Original: *Rosarius Philosophorum*, a foundational 13th-century alchemical text.</note> says, the linens stained by the sweat of Prince <term>Duenech</term> <note>An alchemical code-name often used for lead or antimony; his "sweat" refers to the impurities or "blackness" that must be washed away.</note> are to be washed and burned with waters. Whence fire and water seem to have exchanged mutual qualities with one another, or it must be thought that the kind of Philosophical fire is not the same as the common kind, and the same must be understood regarding the water.

We have proof regarding quicklime and <term>Greek fire</term> <note>A famous incendiary weapon of the medieval world that was notoriously difficult to extinguish with water.</note> that they are ignited by waters and are in no way extinguished, contrary to the nature of other flammable things. Thus they affirm that camphor burns in water once ignited. And the <term>Jet</term> stone <note>Original: *Gagates*. A type of lignite (coal) often used in jewelry and folk medicine.</note> (as <term>Anselm de Boodt</term> <note>Anselmus de Boodt was a renowned 17th-century mineralogist and physician.</note> testifies), once ignited, is more easily extinguished by oil than by water. For oil mixes with it and suffocates the ignited body; water, since it cannot be mixed with the oily fatness, gives way to the ignited body unless it entirely covers and overwhelms the whole thing. Truly, that cannot easily happen because it is a stone, and like all oil, it seeks the top of the water. Thus <term>naphtha</term>, <term>petroleum</term>, and similar things do not greatly fear water once they are set on fire. Regarding the underground coals of the territory of Liege <note>Located in modern-day Belgium, famous for its coal mines.</note>, some write that once ignited underground, they are not extinguished by waters, but only by throwing earth and dust over them, in the same...

<meta>catchword: manner</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that the complex stages of alchemy can be understood through the simple metaphor of washing laundry, where water and heat work together to remove "earthly dregs." He cites various natural substances like Greek fire and naphtha to show that alchemical water and fire behave differently than their common counterparts.</summary>
<keywords>purification, calcination, sublimation, distillation, Greek fire, petroleum, naphtha, Duenech, Rosary of the Philosophers, alchemy as laundry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the purification of the "Philosophical body," comparing the alchemical process to various methods of cleaning and the unique properties of different fires and liquids. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on how bones and cloths are whitened through repeated exposure to elements.</meta>

### DISCOURSE III.

...the manner of the body. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, completing the thought from the previous page: "ad modum corporis."</note> <term>Tacitus</term> <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56 – 120 AD), a Roman historian.</note> tells of a similar kind of fire that could not be suppressed by water, but only by sticks and clothes stripped from the body. There is, therefore, a great diversity of fires, both in how they are kindled and how they are extinguished. Nor is there any less variety among liquids; for milk, vinegar, spirit of wine <note>Ethanol or high-proof alcohol.</note>, <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>Nitric acid, literally "strong water."</note>, <term>Aqua Regia</term> <note>A mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold.</note>, and common water differ greatly from one another when compared in their relationship to fire. Not only this, but sometimes the matter itself withstands the fire, such as those <term>asbestos</term> <note>original: "byssini." While this often refers to fine linen, in the context of fire-washing, Maier refers to "linum vivum" or asbestos.</note> cloths which were held in high price and use by the ancients. These were washed with fire, not water; they were restored to their original cleanliness by burning away the dregs. Whatever others may babble about the hair of the <term>Salamander</term> <note>A mythical reptile believed to live in or endure fire.</note>—as if incombustible <term>wicks</term> <note>original: "Elychnia."</note> could be made from it—should not be listened to. There are those who truly affirm that a fabric was once prepared from talc, <term>plumose alum</term> <note>A fibrous form of aluminum sulfate or asbestos.</note>, and other such materials, which was accustomed to be purged by fire. However, they say the craftsmanship perished when the woman who knew it (a woman from <term>Antwerp</term>) died out of professional jealousy, and that specific preparation could never be found again.

We are not speaking here of common combustible materials. According to all these differences, the **Philosophical subject** must be considered whenever it is being prepared. For here, the fire, the water, and the matter itself are not the common kind. To the **Philosophers**, fire is water, and water is fire. Their water washes and <term>calcinates</term> <note>Reduces to a powder through heat.</note> at the same time, and so does their fire. And the "cloths" to be washed have the nature of asbestos or prepared talc, the temperament and preparation of which is not obvious to everyone. To wash these, they make a <term>lye</term> <note>original: "lixivium." An alkaline solution used for cleaning.</note> not from oak ashes or their salt, but a metallic one, which is more durable than others. Nor do they make it from common water, but from 그 water which has been congealed into ice and snow under the sign of <term>Aquarius</term> <note>The zodiac sign associated with the peak of winter (January/February); also a metaphor for a specific stage of purification.</note>. This is because such water consists of finer parts than the stagnant water of ponds or marshes; thus, it can better penetrate into the interior of the black and impure **Philosophical body** in order to wash and purge it.

<meta>catchword: Suck</meta>

<summary>Maier distinguishes between common elements and the "philosophical" fire and water used in alchemy, using the historical example of fire-resistant asbestos cloth to illustrate how the alchemical "subject" is purified without being consumed.</summary>
<keywords>asbestos, Tacitus, alchemy, purification, Aqua Regia, aqua fortis, lye, Aquarius, Salamander, Antwerp, philosophical fire</keywords>

### IV. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The human race in this world would not be so well increased,
Had not the sister turned toward her brother.
Therefore, join together two siblings of one blood <note>original: "eines Geblütes." In alchemy, this refers to substances of the same root or nature that must be combined.</note>,
That they may live in marriage, man and wife of one mind.
Pour for them the cup of love <note>original: "Becher der Liebe." This allegorical cup represents the "solvent" or "spirit" that facilitates the union of the metallic principles.</note> with sweet juice of the vine;
This gives them a great and strong power in the hope of offspring <note>original: "Frucht," literally "fruit." This refers to the successful creation of the "Philosopher's Child" or the Stone.</note>.

## EPIGRAM IV.

The human race would not be so numerous in the world today,
If the first sister had not been given as a wife to her brother.

So that they may be female and male in the marriage-bed.

And love will generate the hope of offspring.

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse IV, following the epigram on the previous page which introduced the allegorical "incestuous" marriage of the alchemical brother and sister. Maier is now providing the philosophical and natural justification for this union.</meta>

### DISCOURSE IV.

Divine and political Law <note>original: "Lex Divina & politica"</note> forbids those joined by close blood through nature to be coupled in marriage—such as those directly ascending and descending in the genealogical tree <note>original: "arbore genealogica"</note> and those meeting at the sides—and this for the most certain of reasons. Yet when the philosophers <note>Maier uses "Philosophers" as a standard term for alchemists.</note> speak of joining a mother with a son, a father with a daughter, or a brother with a sister in marriage, they do not speak or act against the aforementioned law. This is because the subjects, the attributes, and the causes of the effects are distinct. For the "Persons of the Philosophers" <note>The chemical substances involved in the work.</note> are free from these distinctions just as the sons and daughters of Adam were, who took each other as spouses without the imputation of any crime.

The most powerful reason [for the human law] seems to be so that the human race may coalesce and be associated more firmly through affinity and friendship, and not be divided into hostile and hereditary family factions. Because this same cause remained intact for the first Adamic brothers and sisters, nothing prohibited them from being joined in marriage. For the human race then existed in them alone; no others lived besides themselves and their parents, and therefore, as they were connected by blood, they were necessarily to be connected by affinity.

But as the multitude of men grew, distributed into innumerable families, this true and equitable reason was found: that brothers should not marry sisters. Among the philosophers, there is another reason why sisters marry brothers: namely, the **similarity of substance**, so that like may be joined to like. In this genus, there are at least two things similar to each other in species, but different in sex; one of which is hailed by the name of **Brother**, the other of **Sister**. These, therefore, with the same liberty, condition, and even inevitable necessity as the first humans of the same blood, must be legitimately committed to a single marriage.

The Brother is burning and dry, and therefore highly **choleric**; the Sister is cold and moist, having much **phlegmatic** <term>phlegmaticæ: In the ancient theory of the four humors, phlegm was the cold and moist fluid; here it represents the receptive, cooling element of the alchemical matter.</term> matter within her. These natures, so different in their temperament, are accustomed to agree best in love, fruitfulness, and the propagation of offspring. For just as fire is not easily struck from steel and steel (the hardest body), nor from flint and flint (a fragile body), but from the hard and the fragile—that is, from steel and flint—so too, fire is not produced from a burning male and a fiery female, nor from...

<meta>catchword: both (original: utrifque)</meta>

<summary>Maier justifies the alchemical "incest" of the Brother and Sister by comparing the chemical reagents to the children of Adam, arguing that "like must join with like" to achieve a perfect temperament.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Law, genealogy, Adam and Eve, alchemical marriage, choleric, phlegmatic, four humors, steel and flint, similarity of substance</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the "alchemical marriage" between the Brother and Sister (often representing Sulfur and Mercury or Sol and Luna). Maier is justifying this union by appealing to the laws of nature, animal husbandry, and medical necessity.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE IV.</header>

if both were cold—for the coldness of the sea signifies infertility—a lively offspring could not be received; but rather, the offspring comes from a hot male and a colder female. For a woman, even the warmest within the range of human temperament, is considered colder than the coldest (yet healthy) man of his sex, as Levinus Lemnius <note>Levinus Lemnius (1505–1568) was a famous Dutch physician who wrote on the "miracles of nature" and the secrets of the human body.</note> proves in his book *On the Secret Miracles of Nature* <note>original: "de occ. nat. mirac."</note>. Therefore, the Sister and Brother are rightly joined together by the Philosophers <note>Maier uses "Philosophers" to refer to alchemists, the seekers of wisdom through nature.</note>. 

If anyone should desire an offspring to be born from a hen, a dog, a goat, a sheep, or other beasts, he joins to it a rooster, a dog, a buck, or a ram—whichever animal of its own species it most closely resembles—and thus he is not frustrated in his wish. Nor does he truly consider the "blood relationship" <note>original: "consanguinitatem"</note> of the beasts, but looks only to the nobility of each and its natural suitability. Should the same not be said of the trunk of a tree and the graft to be inserted into it? Indeed, should it not be said even more of the nature of metals? For metallic nature possesses the greatest similarity and homogeneity of substance; thus, when it requires something to be joined to itself, it seeks out its own kind. 

Yet, when this Brother and Sister are joined, they do not become fertile, nor do they persist long in love, unless a "Philothesium" <note>A Greek-derived term meaning a "cup of friendship" or a "toast."</note> or a cup of love—much like a magic potion <note>original: "philtri"</note>—is offered to them. By this, their minds are so calmed and reconciled that, as if intoxicated (in the manner of Lot <note>A reference to the biblical story of Lot’s daughters, who used wine to seduce their father to preserve their lineage; Maier uses this to illustrate how substances must be "tricked" or prepared into combining.</note>), they come together with all shame cast aside and generate an offspring—one that is not a bastard, but legitimate. 

For who is unaware that the human race owes a great deal to Medicine? Because of its benefit and labor, many thousands of people now exist in the world who would not have been here had their parents or ancestors not been rescued from the vice of sterility. This was achieved by removing the cause and lifting the immediate or distant impediments, or by preserving mothers from miscarriage. This same "cup of love" is therefore given to these "newlyweds" <note>The chemical substances being combined.</note> for these very reasons—which anyone can see are three-fold: for constancy of love, for the removal of sterility, and for the prevention of miscarriage.

<meta>catchword: So</meta>

<summary>Maier defends the allegorical incest of the alchemical "Brother and Sister" by comparing it to animal breeding and the medical treatment of infertility, arguing that "like must be joined with like" to produce the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

That she might nourish it with milk, just like a child,

# EMBLEM V. On the Secrets of Nature.

## Place the toad upon the woman’s breasts, so that she may nurse it; and let the woman die, and let the toad grow fat with the milk.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting an allegorical or alchemical scene. A man dressed in a blue tunic with gold trim, a brown cloak, and yellow buskins stands in a city square, holding a large toad to the exposed breasts of a woman. The woman wears a long red gown with a flowing yellow sash and holds her hands up in a gesture of surprise. The background features a paved courtyard surrounded by classical buildings and a distant cathedral with a tall spire. A red circular ink stamp with the initials "B.R." is visible at the bottom left, just below the illustration's border.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM V.

> **L**et a cold toad be placed upon a woman’s breast,
> *That like an infant it may drink from milky cups.*
> Let it grow into a massive swelling <note>The Latin "tuber" refers to a growth or hump, suggesting the toad distends as it feeds.</note> through the emptied breasts,
> *As the woman, failing in health, leaves her life behind.*
> From this you shall create a noble medicine for yourself:
> *A venom <note>original: "virus." In alchemy, "virus" often refers to a potent, raw substance or poison that, when processed, becomes a curative "arcana."</note> to drive out poison from the human heart and lift the plague.*

D 3 <note>This is a signature mark, used by early modern printers to keep the pages of a book in the correct order during binding.</note>

<meta>Catchword: THE WHOLE <note>original: "TOTA." The catchword at the bottom of the page matches the first word of the next page to ensure correct sequence.</note>; Institutional stamp "B.R." <note>Likely the stamp of the Bibliothèque Royale/Nationale.</note> below the image.</meta>

<summary>This page presents the fifth emblem of the collection, depicting a man applying a toad to a woman's breast. The allegory describes the production of a "noble medicine" by allowing a lower, poisonous form (the toad) to consume the life-force of a feminine principle (the milk/woman), illustrating the alchemical paradox of finding a cure within poison.</summary>

<keywords>toad, nursing woman, noble medicine, poison and cure, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, alchemical allegory, pestilence, heart medicine</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous translation of Emblem V, moving from the visual image and short poem into the prose discourse. It explains the alchemical theory behind the "male and female" principles and the biological metaphors used to describe the creation of the Stone.</meta>

### DISCOURSE V.

The entire assembly of **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists, the "philosophers by fire."</note> agrees on this: that their work is nothing other than **male & female**. It is the nature of the male to generate and to dominate the female; it is the nature of the female to conceive, to be impregnated, to give birth, to suckle, and to raise the offspring, and to be subject to the male’s command. For just as she nourishes the conceived fetus with blood before it is brought into the light, so she cherishes and nourishes it with milk once it is born.

Hence nature has prepared for the tender little one in the woman's breasts a digestible and proportionate food, which awaits its arrival like the first provision and *viaticum* <note>original: "viaticum," referring to the supplies or money given for a journey; here, it means the infant's first sustenance for the journey of life.</note> in this world's course. Thus it is fed with milk, grows, and increases until it possesses the instruments for grinding bread—that is, the teeth—at which point it is rightly weaned, because nature has provided it with other, more solid nourishment.

But here the Philosophers say that a **toad** must be applied to the woman’s breasts so that it may nourish itself with her milk like an infant. This is a wretched and horrendous spectacle, even an impious one: that milk destined for an infant should be given to a toad, a poisonous beast that is the very adversary of human nature. We have heard and read of **serpents and dragons** sucking the udders of cows dry; perhaps toads would desire the same if the opportunity were offered among cattle. 

There is a well-known story about a toad that occupied the mouth and inner lips of a certain sleeping peasant so firmly that it could not be driven away by any trick, only by violence. Such violence would have been joined with the danger of death from the spitting of its poison—which it is said to use for both defensive and offensive weapons—and therefore it was not to be attempted. 

Consequently, a remedy for the wretched man was found through **antipathy** <note>The Renaissance scientific concept that certain things in nature have an innate, lethal hostility toward one another.</note>: namely, the antipathy between a very large **spider** and the toad, who pursue each other with lethal hatred. The man was carried, along with the toad, directly to the place where a bloated spider had laid out its webs. When the spider saw the toad, it immediately lowered itself onto the toad's back and pricked it with its sting. When the toad received no damage from this first strike, the spider descended a second time and fixed him again even more strongly. From this, the toad soon swelled up and fell dead from the man's mouth, without any harm coming to the man.

But here, the opposite occurs: because the toad does not occupy the mouth, but the breast of the woman, on whose milk it grows so much that it emerges with immense size and strength. The woman, however, her strength consumed, wastes away and dies. For the poison easily [spreads] through...

<meta>catchword: the veins</meta>

<summary>This discourse explores the biological metaphors of alchemy, comparing the chemical process to the relationship between male and female, and uses a gruesome story about a toad and a spider to illustrate "antipathy" in nature.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Michael Maier, toad, spider, antipathy, male and female principles, nursing, poison, philosophical medicine</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose discourse of Emblem V. On the previous page, Maier established the biological metaphor of the "Philosophical Toad" suckling from a woman’s breast, comparing it to an infant's nourishment. This page explores the deadly nature of this process, its justification within alchemical theory, and the legendary "Toad-stone."</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE V.</header>

<margin>Theophilus <note>Likely referring to Theophilus Presbyter or a similar alchemical authority.</note> mentions a dragon joined to a woman in the womb.</margin>
...the veins of the breast are communicated to the heart, and it infects and extinguishes it, just as is clear from the case of **Cleopatra** <note>The last Pharaoh of Egypt (69–30 BCE), who famously committed suicide by the bite of an asp.</note>, who applied vipers to her breasts when she wished to anticipate a voluntary death, lest she be dragged alive into the hands and triumphs of her conquerors. But lest anyone think the **Philosophers** so cruel as to order a poisonous reptile to be applied to a woman, it must be known that this **toad** is the fetus or son of that same woman, brought forth by a monstrous birth; therefore, by natural right, it ought to enjoy and be nourished by maternal milk. That the mother dies is not the son's intent: for he who was formed in her bowels and increased by her blood until birth could not intend to infect her.

It is indeed an ominous thing for a **toad** to be born from a woman, which we know has happened elsewhere. **William of Newburgh** <note>An English historian of the 12th century known for recording various "prodigies" or strange events.</note>, an English writer, writes in his commentaries (how faithfully, let others judge) that in a certain quarry belonging to the **Bishop of Winchester**, when a certain large stone was split open, a living toad was found inside with a **gold chain**; by the Bishop's order, it was hidden away in the same place and immersed in perpetual darkness, lest it carry some evil omen with it. Such is also this toad: for it is distinguished by gold, if not externally by the artificial work of a chain, then internally by nature—namely, by the stone which others call <term>Borax</term> <note>original: "Boracem"; not modern borax, but a mythical stone.</note>, <term>Chelonitis</term> <note>original: "Chelonitin"; "turtle-stone."</note>, <term>Batrachitis</term> <note>original: "Batrachiten"; "frog-stone."</note>, <term>Crapaudina</term> <note>original: "Crapaudinam"; the "Toad-stone," famously believed to be found in the heads of old toads.</note>, or <term>Garatronium</term>.

For this stone far surpasses gold in its power against any animal poisons, and it is usually set in gold as if in a case or cover, so that it might not be damaged or lost. But it must be legitimate—taken from the animal. If, however, a stone taken from underground ditches (as is common) is fashioned into its shape and used in its place, it should be chosen from the best minerals that aid the heart. For in these the **Philosophical Toad** is truly found, not in a stone quarry (as that storyteller asserts), and it has the gold within itself, not outside for show. For what reason would a toad adorn itself while hiding in darkness and enclosures? Perhaps so it might be magnificently saluted by a beetle if it met one in the twilight? What subterranean goldsmith would have fashioned that gold chain for it? Perhaps the father of the **Green Children** <note>A reference to the legend of the Green Children of Woolpit, who allegedly appeared in England claiming to come from a subterranean "Land of St. Martin."</note>, who came out of the **Land of St. Martin**—indeed, out of the earth itself—as did the two dogs from the stone quarry, according to the same author?

<summary>Maier explains that the toad represents a "monstrous birth" from the female matter and discusses the "Toad-stone" (Crapaudina), a mythical medicinal gem found inside toads, while mocking literal interpretations of medieval legends.</summary>
<keywords>Cleopatra, Toad-stone, Crapaudina, William of Newburgh, Green Children of Woolpit, medicinal stones, Philosophical Toad, alchemy</keywords>

### VI. English version of the Latin Epigram.

> Farmers sow their seeds into the fertile earth,
> When it has been well crushed with iron teeth; <note>referring to the tines of a rake or harrow.</note>
> But the Sages teach to cast their gold into the earth
> That is entirely white and like leaves, <note>original: "weiße geblätterte Erden" – In alchemy, "foliated earth" (terra foliata) refers to a specific state of matter that has been purified until it appears flaky or layered like the pages of a book.</note> so that gold may come from it.
> That you may do this rightly, observe the work of the peasants closely,
> For gold grows like wheat, and has its own life.

### EPIGRAM VI.
