<meta>catchword: PLATO</meta>

<summary>This page presents an alchemical allegory comparing the creation of the Philosopher's Stone to the sowing of wheat. It instructs the seeker to "sow gold" into a specific "white foliated earth," using the natural germination of grain as a visible model for the hidden growth of metallic perfection.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, agriculture, gold, white foliated earth, germination, wheat, nature's secrets, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page begins the prose commentary (Discursus) for Emblem VI. It follows the preceding page's visual and poetic instruction to "sow gold" by providing a philosophical and practical justification for comparing the "Great Work" of alchemy to the common labors of a farmer and a physician.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE VI.</header>

Plato says that a city consists not of a physician and a physician, but of a physician and a farmer—that is, men of different roles. Indeed, he makes special mention of the farmer and the physician because their works are clearly visible in the imitation, correction, and perfection of nature. For each takes up a natural subject, to which he adds necessary things that are lacking according to his art, or removes those that are superfluous. Hence, the art of both can be defined by the addition of what is lacking and the subtraction of what is superfluous (just as Hippocrates <note>Hippocrates (c. 460 – c. 370 BCE) was an ancient Greek physician, often called the "Father of Medicine."</note> defines medicine). 

For what more does the farmer do than take a field left by nature and add plowing, harrowing, hoeing, fertilizing or manuring, and sowing, and the rest? He commits the growth and the harvest to nature, which provides the sun’s heat and the rain, and by these, she multiplies the seeds and brings them forth into crops soon to be harvested. Meanwhile, as the plant sprouts, the farmer removes thorns and all obstacles; he reaps the mature crops, and once harvested, he removes the waste, namely the stalks, husks, and other such things. 

In the same way, the physician (who is also a chemist <term>chemist: In this 17th-century context, "chymicus" refers to an alchemist or practitioner of chemical medicine (spagyrics).</term> in a different respect) strives to preserve existing health in the human body and to recover it when lost through various remedies; he removes the cause of disease, cures the illness, soothes symptoms, reduces excessive blood by bloodletting, restores what is lacking through an ordered diet, and evacuates harmful humors <note>humors: In ancient and medieval medicine, the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance determined health.</note> through purging. Thus, in a thousand ways, he imitates nature, supplementing and correcting it through the works of the intellect and art. Since these matters are well known, let us instead turn our consideration to Chemistry:

<margin>i</margin> For Chemistry <note>original: "Chemia"</note> especially bears witness to the operations of agriculture through its own secret terms and methods of working. Farmers have the earth in which they sow seeds; so too do the Chemists. They have dung with which they enrich the fields; so too do these chemists, without which nothing would be achieved, nor any fruit hoped for. The farmers have seeds, the multiplication of which they desire. If the chemists did not have these [seeds], they would be like a painter (as Lullius <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232 – 1315) was a Majorcan philosopher and polymath; many alchemical texts were attributed to him in this period.</note> says) who tries to depict the face of a certain man whom he has never seen, nor even seen an image of him. Farmers wait for the rain and the heat of the sun; so also, in truth, do the chemists provide a heat and rain suitable for their work. Why say more? Chemistry is entirely parallel to agriculture and acts as its substitute, expressing its functions in every way, but under a most perfect allegory. Hence the ancients brought forth Ceres, Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysus <note>Ceres, Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysus: These deities of grain, agriculture, and wine were interpreted by alchemists as symbols for the stages of the alchemical process.</note>—golden gods, as it were, or those pertaining to chemistry

<meta>catchword: as if</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains how chemistry (alchemy) mirrors the arts of medicine and agriculture by imitating and perfecting nature's processes. It argues that the alchemist's work follows the same principles as a farmer sowing seeds or a doctor balancing the body's humors.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Hippocrates, Ramon Lull, agriculture, medicine, chemistry, alchemy, allegory, Ceres, Osiris, Dionysus</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose commentary for Discourse VI. It moves from the general analogy of the farmer to specific alchemical interpretations of "white foliated earth" and introduces mythological allegories involving Achilles and the harmony of music.</meta>

# DISCOURSE VI.

...as if they were teaching mortals to cast the seeds of fruits into their own earth, demonstrating the cultivation of fields, the propagation of the vine, and the use of wine. But the ignorant have falsely twisted all these things toward rustic labor. For these are the most secret mysteries of nature, which are hidden from the common people under these veils of agriculture and revealed only to the wise. Hence the Philosophers say that their gold must be sown in **white foliated earth**, as if they wished the sowing of wheat to be seen as an example and an imitation. This is what the author of the *Treatise on Wheat* and Jodocus Greverus <note>Jodocus Greverus (16th century) was a priest and alchemist whose work "Secretum Nobilissimum" described the alchemical process using the metaphor of a farmer’s year.</note> have done most excellently in their descriptions. For both have very elegantly adapted the individual operations of agriculture in producing wheat to the sowing of gold or the generation of the tincture.

White earth, being sandy, provides little fruit for common farmers, who prefer black and fertile earth; but to the Philosophers, that white earth is especially useful if it be **foliated**—that is, well prepared—because the wise know how to fertilize it with their own dung <term>dung: In alchemy, "stercus" (dung or manure) often refers to the stage of putrefaction or the specific "sulfur" of the philosophers that facilitates growth.</term>, while the common farmers do not. Now, sowing is the propagation of the world, by which that which cannot endure as an individual is preserved through the species. This occurs in humans, animals, and plants—in the latter hermaphroditically <note>Maier refers to the botanical understanding of his time where plants were often seen as containing both reproductive principles within themselves.</note>, in the former under two distinct sexes. In metals, however, it occurs in a far different way. 

For in metals, from the flow of a point comes a line, from a line a surface, and from a surface a body. The stars produced that point before the line, surface, and body, because the point is the principle of them all. Nature added the flow after a long interval of time; that is, the celestial Phoebus <note>Phoebus is another name for Apollo, the sun god, representing the alchemical "Sun" or gold.</note> generated a little son under the earth, whom Mercury offered to Vulcan <note>The god of fire, representing the controlled heat of the alchemical furnace.</note> and to Chiron—that is, the manual craftsman—for his education. This is just as they write of Achilles, who was held under the flames and hardened by his mother Thetis.

<margin>Hence Ovid says: "The son of Phillyra [Chiron] placed the boy Achilles in charge of the lyre." <note>Original Latin: *Phyllirides puerum Cytharae praefecit Achillem.* From Ovid's *Art of Love*.</note></margin> From Chiron, Achilles learned, among other things, music and the art of handling the lyre. Since Achilles is nothing other than the **Philosophical Subject** (whose son is Pyrrhus of the red hair <note>Pyrrhus, also known as Neoptolemus, was the son of Achilles. His "red hair" symbolizes the *Rubedo* or red stage, the final perfection of the alchemical work.</note>, without which two Troy could not be captured, as we have demonstrated at length in Book 6 of our *Hieroglyphics*), we therefore treat music in this very work of ours—where we describe Achilles and his heroic virtues and deeds—not without cause, though in passing. For if music so adorned such a Hero, why should it not also make this little work of ours more varied and acceptable? For the angels sing (as the Holy Scriptures testify), the heavens sing (as Pythagoras <note>Pythagoras proposed the "Music of the Spheres," the idea that the movement of celestial bodies creates a divine harmony.</note> established) and they "declare the glory of God" (as the Psalmist says); the Muses and Apollo sing (according to the poets); even infant humans sing, birds sing, and sheep and geese sing in musical instruments <note>A witty reference to materials used in instruments: gut strings from sheep and quill plectrums or pens from geese.</note>—if we also sing, we do so with good reason.

❦ E 2
<meta>catchword: Therefore (Dee)</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that the agricultural metaphors of "white earth" and "sowing gold" are veils for secret alchemical processes and uses the myth of Achilles as an allegory for the preparation of the philosophical subject.</summary>
<keywords>white foliated earth, Jodocus Greverus, Phoebus, Vulcan, Achilles, Chiron, Pyrrhus, alchemical music, music of the spheres, Troy</keywords>

## The young bird flies from the nest and falls back into the nest.

### VII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

IN a hollow stone the Eagle had made his nest,
Wherein he secretly did nourish his young;
One of these could raise itself easily with feathers,
But the other, featherless, would not yield to such a thing.
Therefore the flying one falls back into the nest from which it came;
Join the head to the tail, and so you shall attain it. <note>This "joining of head to tail" refers to the *Ouroboros* or the circular nature of the alchemical process, where the "volatile" (feathered) part of the matter is fixed by the "non-volatile" (unfeathered) part.</note>

<meta>catchword: EMBLEMA</meta>

Here, the text shifts focus from the mineral growth in the earth to the celestial and volatile nature of the "bird of Jove," introducing the dynamic relationship between substances that want to evaporate (fly) and those that remain solid (fixed in the nest).</meta>

# EMBLEM VII. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.
<meta>Red circular library stamp centered below the illustration.</meta>

->A young bird flies from the nest and falls back into the nest again.<-

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting an emblem. In the center, a high, craggy mountain peak is topped with a bird's nest. ### EPIGRAM VII.

*In a hollow rock, the Bird of Jove* <note>original: "Jovis Ales." The eagle, which is the traditional symbol of the god Jupiter (Jove). In alchemy, the eagle often represents "volatility"—the tendency of a substance to vaporize or ascend when heated.</note> *had built a nest,*
*In which it lay hidden, and nourished its young:*
*One of these wished to raise itself on light feathers,*
*But was held back by its featherless brother-bird.* <note>The "featherless bird" represents the "fixed" or solid part of the alchemical matter that prevents the "volatile" part from escaping.</note>
*Therefore, the flying one returns to the nest it had left;*
*Join the head to the tail, and then you will not be empty-handed.* <note>original: "nec inanis eris." A reference to successful results in the "Great Work." The instruction to join the head to the tail refers to the Ouroboros—the serpent eating its own tail—signifying a circular process of distillation and condensation where the volatile and fixed are unified.</note>

<summary>Emblem VII uses the allegory of eagles in a nest to describe the alchemical tension between volatile and fixed substances. It instructs the practitioner to unify these opposing forces, symbolized by the "head and tail," to achieve success in the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>Bird of Jove, Eagle, volatility, fixity, Ouroboros, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, distillation</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page provides the "Discourse" (the explanatory prose) for the emblem shown on the previous page. It expands on the symbolic imagery of the two birds—one feathered and one featherless—by grounding the concept in classical medicine, physics, and natural history.</meta>

# DISCOURSE VII.

What Hippocrates, the leader of physicians, asserts regarding the humors—that they are diverse and multiple in man, and not just one, for otherwise various diseases could not arise—we observe to be equally true regarding the Elements of the world. For if there were but one element, no change of it into another, no generation or corruption would occur; indeed, all things would be one immutable thing, and neither meteors <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to all atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and lightning.</note>, minerals, plants, nor animals would be naturally produced from it. Hence the Supreme Creator fashioned this entire system of the world from diverse and contrary natures, namely from the light and the heavy, the hot and the cold, the moist and the dry, so that one might pass into another through affinity, and thus a composition of bodies might be made which differ far from one another in essence, qualities, powers, and effects.

For in perfectly mixed bodies, there are light elements such as fire and air, as well as heavy ones such as earth and water, which are so tempered among themselves by balances <note>original: "æquilibriis." Referring to the Alchemical concept of the "weight of the wise," or the perfect proportion of elements.</note> that one does not flee from the other, but each allows itself to be easily caught and held by the others, neighbor by neighbor. Earth and air are opposed to each other, as are fire and water; yet fire fosters friendship with air through the heat common to both, and with earth through dryness. So too air with water, and water with earth. Whence all things are connected by bonds of affinity, or rather of kinship, and remain together in one composition; if this composition abounds in light elements, it raises the heavy with it; if it abounds in heavy elements, it presses the light down with it.

This is illustrated by two Eagles, one feathered and the other featherless, of which the one attempting to fly is held back by the other. An evident example of this is found in the battle between the Falcon and the heron: for the former, having become superior in the air through swift flight and nimble wings, seizes and tears at the latter with its talons, and by the heron’s weight, both fall to the ground. The opposite appeared in the artificial dove or Automaton of Archytas <note>Archytas of Tarentum (4th century BCE) was a Greek philosopher reputed to have built the first self-propelled flying machine, a wooden bird.</note>, where heavy things were lifted by light ones—that is, its wooden body was carried into the air by the spirit <note>original: "spiritu." In this context, likely referring to compressed air or steam used to power the mechanical bird.</note> enclosed within. 

In the philosophical subject <note>The "subject" refers to the raw material used in the alchemical process, the *Materia Prima*.</note>, at first the light elements predominate over the heavy in terms of quantity, yet they are conquered by the power of the heavy. In the course of time, however, the eagle's wings are clipped, and from the two, one very large bird is made (namely, the Ostrich <note>original: "Strutio camelus." In alchemical lore, the ostrich was famed for its ability to "eat and digest iron," symbolizing a substance that has become so powerful and "fixed" that it can transform the hardest metals.</note>) which can consume iron, and which, being more hindered by its weight, runs 

<meta>The text cuts off here at the catchword "rat" to be continued on the next page as "currat" (runs).</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical principle of "fixation" and "volatilization" through the balance of the four elements, using the metaphor of a feathered bird (volatile) tied to a featherless one (fixed).</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, elements, humors, Archytas, automaton, ostrich, falcon and heron, alchemy, fixation, volatility</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the seventh emblem, explaining the necessity of combining contrary elements—the volatile (winged) and the fixed (wingless)—to achieve alchemical stability. It draws on authorities like Hermes Trismegistus and various medieval alchemists to explain how substances interact in the laboratory.</meta>

# DISCOURSE VII.

...[it would fall] rather than fly through the air, even though it has beautiful feathers. Concerning this bird, or one similar to it, Hermes (as the author of the *Aurora* <note>Referring to the *Aurora Consurgens*, a famous 15th-century alchemical manuscript often attributed to Thomas Aquinas.</note> testifies in chapter 5) writes: "I have considered the bird venerable to the wise, which flies while it is in Aries, Cancer, Libra, or Capricorn <note>These are the four "cardinal" zodiac signs, representing the beginning of the seasons and the four corners of the heavens.</note>; and you shall acquire it for yourself as an everlasting thing from pure minerals and rocky mountains." Senior <note>Senior Zadith, the Latinized name of the 10th-century Arabic alchemist Ibn Umail.</note> says the same in the *Table*, where a pair is seen, one winged and one without wings, of which one holds the tail of the other in its beak so that they cannot easily be separated. For this is the mechanism of the entire Universe: that it always lifts heavy things by means of light things, and in turn weighs down light things by means of heavy ones, as the author of the *Perfect Mastery* declares. He states that the seven mineral spirits, like the Wandering Stars <note>The seven classical planets: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, the Sun, and the Moon.</note>, and as many metallic bodies, like the fixed stars, must be married to one another.

Hence also the *Chemical Aristotle* <note>Pseudo-Aristotle, the name given to authors of various medieval alchemical texts falsely attributed to the Greek philosopher.</note> says: "When the Spirit has dissolved the body and the soul so that they exist in their own form, it does not remain fixed unless you seize it. The seizure, indeed, consists in joining it with the body from which you prepared it in the beginning; for in that body, the spirit is hindered from flight by the persistence of the body." In camphor, as Bonus <note>Petrus Bonus, a 14th-century Italian alchemist and author of the *The Precious New Pearl*.</note> mentions, the light elements—namely air and fire—prevail over the heavy ones; therefore it is said to exhale entirely and pass away into the air. 

In quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, flowers of sulfur, antimony, salt of hartshorn <note>original: "sale sanguinis cervini," literally salt of deer's blood, but chemically referring to ammonium carbonate derived from antlers.</note>, sal ammoniac, and others of this kind, the earth flies with the air into the alembic <note>A distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</note>, and is not separated from it. In gold, glass, diamond, emery stone <note>original: "lapide Smiri," a very hard rock used for grinding.</note>, garnets, and similar things, the elements remain joined unharmed for a very long time, even under the pressure of fire, and the earth retains the rest within itself. In other combustible things, a division and separation of one from another occurs, so that ashes are left at the bottom, while water, air, and fire seek the heights. Therefore, one should not look to the unequal composition of these last items, which is not so strongly mixed, nor to the mixture of the first ones—which, though more durable, is nevertheless volatile—but rather to the solidity, constancy, and fixity of the middle ones. For in this way, the featherless bird will detain the feathered one, and the fixed substance will fix the volatile one, which is what must be done.

It categorizes various substances—from camphor to gold—by how their constituent elements react to fire and distillation.</summary>

## Take the egg and strike it with a fiery sword. <note>original: "Accipe ovum & igneo percute gladio." — The "egg" refers to the Philosophical Egg, a glass vessel containing the "prime matter" of the alchemist’s work. The "fiery sword" represents the application of regulated heat or a sharp chemical "fire" used to initiate the transformation.</note>

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting a man in classical Roman military attire, featuring a blue-grey breastplate, red tunic, and golden buskins. ### EPIGRAM VIII.

There is a bird in the world loftier than all others; <note>The "sublime bird" often refers to the Phoenix or the Hermetic Bird, representing the spiritualized matter of the Alchemical Work.</note>
Let it be your single concern to seek out its egg.
The clear white surrounds the soft yellow yolk;
As is the custom, seek it cautiously with a glowing sword. <note>original: "Ignito... ense." The sword must be "ignited" or "fiery," emphasizing that the "breaking" of the egg is a thermal or energetic process rather than a literal physical blow.</note>
Let Mars lend his aid to Vulcan: <note>In alchemical symbolism, Mars represents iron or strength, while Vulcan represents the transformative fire of the furnace. This suggests a union of heat and a specific metallic agent.</note> and from there a young cockerel, <note>original: "pullaster." This "chick" or young bird represents the first appearance of the new, transformed substance arising from the decay of the old.</note>
Having emerged, will be the victor over both iron and fire.

<meta>This page contains the "Discourse" (commentary) for Emblem VIII. It expands upon the symbolic image of the egg being struck by a sword, transitioning from a literal discussion of biology to the metaphorical transformation of alchemical matter.</meta>

# DISCOURSE VIII.

There are many and various kinds of birds which, just as they are indefinite in number, remain unknown to us by name. Regarding a certain very large bird called the **Roc** <note>original: "Ruc" — A legendary bird of prey from Middle Eastern mythology, famously described by Marco Polo, said to be large enough to carry off elephants.</note> that appears at a certain time of year on a small island in the ocean, they tell that it can lift an elephant with it into the air. India and America provide **parrots**, **ravens**, and similar birds of various colors. However, to seek out the eggs of these birds is not the intention of Philosophy; the Egyptians annually attack the eggs of **crocodiles** with iron and pursue them as if in a publicly declared war; but the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to Alchemists, who considered their work "Natural Philosophy."</note> strike their egg with fire, not so that it might be destroyed and perish, but so that it might receive life and grow. For while the chick is produced from it, animated and living, this is not to be called its corruption, but its generation. To be sure, it ceases to be an egg through the loss of the oval form, and begins to be a two-footed, winged animal through the introduction of a nobler form.

Now, there are in the egg the seeds of the male and female joined together under one roof or shell. The **yolk** <note>original: "Vitellus"</note> constitutes the chick and its fundamental members and viscera, with the seed of the male being the internal former and agent. The **egg white** <note>original: "Albumen"</note> gives the matter or fabric and growth to the rudiment or thread of the chick. External heat is the first mover, which, through a certain circulation of the **Elements** and the changing of one into another, introduces a new form by the instinct or guidance of nature. For water passes into air, air into fire, and fire into earth; when all these are coupled together, and a specific form is sent down from the stars, an individual of a certain species of bird is made—namely, that bird whose egg and infused seed it was. 

Indeed, it is said to be struck by a **fiery sword** when **Vulcan** <note>The Roman god of fire; in alchemy, he represents the literal fire of the furnace or the "secret fire" of the process.</note> provides an exit for the chick with his axe (as he did for **Pallas** from the brain of **Jupiter** <note>A reference to the myth of Athena (Pallas) being born fully grown from the head of Zeus (Jupiter) after it was split open—a metaphor for the liberation of the spirit from matter.</note>), performing the duty of a midwife. This is what **Basil Valentine** <note>original: "Basilius Valentinus" — A legendary, likely pseudonymous alchemist of the 15th century whose writings were highly influential in the 17th century.</note> asserts: that **Mercury** was shut up in a prison by Vulcan at the command of **Mars**, and was not released until he had been totally putrefied and had died. 

Truly, this death exists for him as the beginning of a new life, just as the corruption or death of the egg brings about the new generation and life of the chick: so when the human fetus has died to that vegetable life (which alone it enjoyed in the mother's womb), another more perfect life comes to it through the passage into this worldly light, or through birth. Indeed, even when we are deprived of this present life which we lead, another most perfect and eternal life awaits us,

<meta>catchword: so</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains that the "Philosophical Egg" must be "struck" with fire to trigger a transformation from "death" (corruption) to a higher form of "life" (generation). It compares this alchemical process to the hatching of a chick, the birth of Athena, and the transition of the human soul to the afterlife.</summary>

<keywords>Philosophical Egg, Roc, alchemy, generation vs corruption, Vulcan, Basil Valentine, four elements, transformation, Mercury, Mars, birth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Discourse for Emblem VIII, moving from the biological description of an egg's components to the symbolic and chemical "striking" of the egg. It connects the "fiery sword" from the emblem's imagery to the theories of Ramon Llull and the assembly of philosophers in the Turba Philosophorum.</meta>

# DISCOURSE VIII.

Thus **Lullius** <note>original: "Lullius" — Referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many influential alchemical treatises were later attributed.</note> calls this **fiery sword** <note>original: "gladium igneum"</note> a **sharp lance** in many places; because fire, like a lance or a sharp sword, pierces bodies and renders them porous and passable, so that water can penetrate to dissolve them and reduce them from hardness to softness and tractability. In the stomach of the **sea-raven** <note>original: "corvi marini" — Likely the cormorant. In medieval and Renaissance natural history, it was famously voracious.</note>, which is the most ravenous of all birds, there are found long and smooth living worms, which serve as its "instrument of heat." While the bird captures eels and fish, these worms immediately attack them and pierce them like the sharpest needles (as we ourselves have observed at times) and thus consume them in a very short time by a wonderful operation of nature. Therefore, just as heat pricks, so that which pricks sometimes performs the functions of heat.

Hence, it is not without merit that it can be called a **fiery sword** with which the **Philosophers' Egg** <note>original: "ovum philosophorum" — A central alchemical metaphor representing the "matter" of the Great Work contained within a sealed vessel.</note> must be struck or disciplined. But the **Philosophers** <note>term: "Philosophers" — In alchemical literature, this almost always refers to the "True Masters" or Alchemists who understand the secrets of nature.</note> would rather have it understood as a **temperate heat**, by which the egg is fostered, as **Morsoleus** <note>A fictionalized version of a pre-Socratic philosopher appearing as a speaker in the *Turba Philosophorum*.</note> declares in the **Assembly** <note>original: "turba" — Referring to the *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the earliest and most famous Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a debate among ancient sages.</note> when he says: *It is necessary, wise men, that the humidity be burned first by a slow fire, as is shown to us by the example of the generation of a chick; and when the fire is increased, let the vessel be sealed on all sides, lest the body of the air and its fleeing spirit be drawn out.*

But of what bird is the egg? **Moscus** says in the same place: *I, however, say that no instruments are made except from our **white powder**, starry and splendid, and from the **white stone**; from which powder suitable instruments are made for the egg; yet they have not named the egg, nor specified what bird’s egg it might be.*

F iij
<meta>catchword: Schließ (German: "Close" or "Lock")</meta>

## *IX. English version of the Latin Epigram.* <note>original: "IX. Epigrammatis Latini versio Germanica." — The OCR title identifies this as a German translation of the Latin, here rendered into English.</note>

In the garden of Wisdom <note>term: "Wisdom" — Referring to Sophia, the personification of divine or philosophical knowledge sought by the alchemist.</note> a tree of golden fruit can be found,
Which you should join to our old man; <note>term: "Old Man" — In alchemical symbolism, the 'senex' or old man often represents the "prime matter" or a base metal like lead (Saturn) that must undergo death and rebirth to be transformed.</note>
And shut them both in a house of glass surrounded by dew,
And let him live there thus enclosed for many days.
So will the man (wonderfully) eat the fruits of the tree very soon,
And become young, who was previously very old in years.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical explanation of Emblem IX. The previous page introduced the visual allegory of the old man and the tree; this page provides the theoretical basis for rejuvenation, comparing the human life cycle to a lamp and discussing alchemical and medicinal means of life extension.</meta>

# DISCOURSE IX.

Whatever grows in length, breadth, and depth—that is, whatever arises, is nourished, increased, reaches its peak <note>original: "acmẽ"</note>, and is propagated—those same things also decrease; that is, they are diminished in strength, they die, and they fall away entirely, as is evident in all vegetables and animals. Hence, for man also, when he has come to his greatest growth, a decrease—that is, old age—occurs, in which he is gradually diminished in strength until death arrives. Indeed, the cause of old age is the same as that of a lamp fading and shining dimly when its oil is almost consumed. For just as in a lamp there are three things: the wick <note>original: "Elychnium"</note>, the oil <note>original: "pinguedo," literally fat or grease</note>, and the flame; so in man, the "wick" represents the vital members, organs, and limbs; the "oil" is the radical moisture <term>radical moisture: a medieval medical concept of the innate fluid or "oil" that sustains the body's life-force</term>; and the "flame" is the innate heat. The only difference is that the flame of the lamp shines, while the innate heat does not at all, since it is not fire but only heat; and that the oil of a lamp is greasy, while the radical moisture is viscous, being of a seminal principle. Just as a lamp is extinguished from a lack of oil, so man, from old age and without any other disease, falls into a wasting away <note>original: "marasmũ"</note>, senile decay, and finally death.

They say of the eagle that in its old age, hindered by an overgrown, curved beak, it would die of hunger, unless it cast off its beak through nature and were restored as if to youth. Thus stags by shedding antlers, snakes by shedding their skins or sloughs, and crabs by shedding their shells seem to grow young again; not indeed in reality, because their consumed radical moisture is not restored to them, but only in appearance. There is nothing that can make a man young again except death itself and the beginning of the following eternal life. 

However, regarding the restoration of external form and strength in some way, and the removal of wrinkles and gray hairs, there are those who say a remedy can be found, as Lullius <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan polymath and mystic to whom many alchemical works were attributed</note> affirms in his *On the Fifth Essence*, and Arnold <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous medieval physician and alchemist</note> in his *On Prepared Gold*. But here the Philosophers say that an old man, to become young, must be shut up with a certain tree in a dewy house, and then he shall eat of the fruit of the tree, and thus recover his youth. That such trees exist now in the nature of things is hardly believed by the common people. 

Physicians write marvelous things about myrobalans <note>a type of astringent fruit from India, highly prized in medieval pharmacy</note>, which are fruits of trees, as if they could provide such a thing: clearing away gray hairs, purifying the blood, and prolonging life. But these claims are called into doubt by many, unless they are said to achieve it "by accident," as do other things which purge the mass of blood from the dregs mixed in it, and dye gray hairs with blackness—for which purpose myrobalans are said to color the hair and the pupils of the eyes. Marsilio Ficino <note>1433–1499, an influential Italian scholar and philosopher</note> writes in his book *On Preserving the Life of Scholars* that to reach a long age, it is useful for one to daily suckle milk from the breasts of a beautiful and young woman; in place of which others [recommend] viper's flesh...

<meta>catchword: co-</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the biological process of aging using the analogy of a lamp's oil (radical moisture) and explores various legendary and alchemical methods for rejuvenation, including the fruit of a philosophical tree, medicinal myrobalans, and the advice of Marsilio Ficino.</summary>
<keywords>rejuvenation, radical moisture, innate heat, old age, alchemy, Ramon Lull, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Marsilio Ficino, myrobalans, life extension</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical explanation of Emblem IX, moving from the general analogy of human life as a flickering lamp to the specific alchemical and medical methods of rejuvenation. It transitions from discussing the eagle's renewal to the power of imagination and the symbolic transformation of the old man into a youth through the consumption of the "red fruit."</meta>

# DISCOURSE IX.

...praises what has been eaten: But truly these means are more rigid than old age itself, and should scarcely be used by one in a thousand, even if they did not lack a most certain effect. It is written by **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note> in his book *On Long Life* that a sick man through mere imagination can draw to himself the health of another, or an old man the youth of another; but in this, the author seems to have used his imagination alone, not experience. 

Concerning the **Psylli** <note>A legendary Libyan tribe said to possess a "double pupil" and the power to charm serpents or fascinate with a glance</note> with double pupils, and witches who fascinate children and cattle with their gaze alone—whence that line of **Virgil**: *I know not what eye fascinates my tender lambs* <note>original: "Nescio quis teneros oculus mihi fascinet agnos," from Virgil's Eclogues III.103</note>—this is beyond doubt. But these things occur without contact, whereas the tree restores the old man to youth by a different means. For this tree has sweet, ripe, and red fruits, which easily pass into the best blood, since they are of easy digestion and excellent nourishment, so that they leave nothing superfluous or dross-like in the body. 

An old man, however, abounds in white phlegm <note>According to the theory of the Four Humors, old age was considered "cold and moist," dominated by phlegm, which was associated with the color white</note>, and is of a white color and hair; which humor, color, and hair are changed by the eating of these fruits, and they become red, which is the color of youth. Hence the **Philosophers** <note>A common term for Alchemists</note> say that the Stone is first an old man—that is, white—and then a youth—that is, red—because this color belongs to youth, just as the other belongs to old age. 

It is added that the old man ought to be enclosed with the tree, not in the open air <note>original: "sub dio"</note>, but in a house—not a dry one, but one moist with dew. It is considered a miracle for trees to spring up or grow in an enclosed place; yet if it is moist, there is no doubt that they will endure for a long time. For the nourishment of a tree is moisture and "aerial earth"—that is, a fatty substance which can ascend into the trunk and branches, and there produce leaves, flowers, and fruit. In this natural work, all the elements concur: while fire gives the first motion as the efficient cause, air gives thinness and penetrability, water gives slipperiness <note>original: "lubricitatem"</note>, and earth gives coagulation. For air returns into water, and water into earth, if any of their superfluity should ascend. 

By fire, I understand the native heat which, propagated with the seed, fashions and shapes fruits similar to those from which the seed came—like a craftsman—by the power of the stars. Indeed, the dewy evaporation is profitable not only for moistening the tree so that it may bear fruit, but also for the old man, so that he may more easily be rejuvenated by those fruits; for it softens, refills, and restores his wrinkled and dry skin with temperate heat and moisture. For physicians, in cases of wasting away <note>original: "marasmo"</note> and senile decay, rationally command and prescribe lukewarm baths with great benefit. Truly, if the matter is well considered, that tree is the daughter of the old man, who, like **Daphne** <note>In Greek mythology, a nymph who was transformed into a laurel tree to escape the pursuit of Apollo</note>, was changed into a plant of this kind; whence the old man, not without reason, hopes for youth from her, of whose existence he himself was the cause.

Sib <note>The signature mark for the book's quire or gathering</note>

# EMBLEM X. On the Secrets of Nature.

## Give fire to fire, Mercury to Mercury, and it is enough for you.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting an alchemical scene with mythological figures. ### EPIGRAM X.

The entire machine of the world hangs connected by this chain,
WHOLLY, BECAUSE EVERY LIKE REJOICES IN ITS EQUAL:
*Thus Mercury is joined to Mercury, and so fire is joined to*
*Fire; and let this be the goal set for your art.*
*Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire; in alchemy, this represents the external heat of the furnace</note> drives Hermes <note>The Greek name for Mercury; here representing the volatile substance or "spirit" of the work</note>, but winged Hermes*
*Releases you, Cynthia <note>A name for the Moon, representing silver or the feminine principle</note>, while your sister <note>The Moon</note> releases you, Apollo <note>The Sun god, representing gold or the masculine principle</note>.*

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem X, moving from the poetic epigram to a detailed philosophical and physical explanation of why "like must be joined to like." The text transitions from general medical principles to specific alchemical distinctions between internal and external elements.</meta>

# DISCOURSE X.

This statement, if taken literally, simply increases the quantity of fire and Mercury without introducing any new quality into the subject. For everything similar added to its like becomes more of that same thing. Hence, physicians claim that "contraries are cured by contraries," and that one is removed by the other; thus we see fire extinguished by water, yet nourished by the addition of fire. The Poet <note>The Roman poet Ovid in his *Art of Love* (Ars Amatoria).</note> feels the same when he says: *And Venus in wine, and fire in fire, rages.* <note>original: "Et Venus in vinis, ignis in igne furit." This suggests that adding a stimulant (wine) to a passion (love/Venus) is as dangerous as adding fuel to a fire.</note>

Truly, it must be answered that fire and fire, or Mercury and Mercury, differ greatly from one another. For there are many types of fires according to the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>A term used by alchemists to refer to their predecessors and authorities in the "Great Work."</note>, and many types of Mercury. Moreover, the same heat or cold, differing at least by location or position, differs from another of its kind in such a way that it may call forth that which is similar to itself. We notice this when the heat of fire impressed upon a limb is drawn out and summoned by that same heat; likewise, limbs numbed by winter cold and almost dead are restored by cold water—if they are plunged into it—and not by the immediate application of external heat.

Just as a greater light obscures a lesser one, so does a greater heat obscure a lesser, and a greater cold a lesser. Nevertheless, it is necessary that the external heat and cold be less than that which was previously impressed upon the limbs; otherwise, the same impression would be made as before, and like would be increased by like much more than it would be drawn out. For the summoning of cold by cold water, and of fiery heat by heat, is in harmony with nature; because every sudden change into a contrary state is dangerous and less welcome to her, whereas that which happens gradually and, as it were, by degrees, is tolerable.

Thus we say that one fire is internal, already fixed or existing essentially within the subject of the <term>Philosophers</term>, and another is external; the same must be understood regarding Mercury. That internal fire is called "fire" <term>equivocally</term> <note>In logic, an "equivocal" term is one that uses the same name for things that are fundamentally different in nature—like the "bark" of a tree and the "bark" of a dog.</note> because of its fiery qualities, powers, and operations; the external is "fire" <term>univocally</term> <note>In its literal, direct, and standard sense.</note>. Therefore, external fire must be given to the internal, and a similar Mercury to Mercury, so that the intention of the art may be fulfilled.

To soften or ripen anything hard or raw through <term>decoction</term> <note>The process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence or change its state.</note>, we use fire and water. For water dissolves hardness and penetrates into compact parts, with heat adding strength and motion to it. This appears, for example, in the cooking of peas, which are whole and hard on their own, but they swell up from the water, are broken down, and reduced to a pulp, as the heat of the fire thins the water through boiling and renders it of a thinner or almost airy substance. In the same way, the heat of fire dissolves the raw parts of fruits or meats into water, and causes them to vanish into the air with it. In this same manner, the fire and Mercury here are fire and water, and these same fire and Mercury are the "ripe" and the "raw" parts...

<meta>catchword: dæ</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical principle of "like attracting like" by distinguishing between internal (essential) and external (applied) elements. It uses everyday examples, such as treating frostbite with cold water or boiling peas, to demonstrate how external heat and moisture act upon internal substances to achieve maturation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the alchemical process from Page 50. # DISCOURSE X.

[...substances], from which crude things must be matured through cooking, or mature things must be purged of superfluities by the ministry of fire and water. We shall briefly demonstrate here that these two fires and two Mercuries are primarily and uniquely necessary for the Art. *Empedocles* <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who proposed that the universe is composed of four elements joined or separated by the forces of Love (Friendship) and Strife.</note> established two principles for all things: strife and friendship. Through strife, corruptions occur; through friendship, generations are born. A strife of this kind is clearly seen between fire and water: while fire causes water to evaporate, if it can, water on the other hand extinguishes fire, if it is applied to it. Nevertheless, it is evident that from these same things, a certain friendship is formed through generation. For by heat, a new generation of air is made from water, and by that same heat, the hardening of water into stone [occurs]; and thus from these two elements, as if they were the primary ones, the remaining two are also made, and consequently, the production of all things. Water was the matter of heaven and of all corporal things. Fire, as the form, moves and informs this matter; so water, or this Mercury, provides the matter, and fire, or <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemical theory, Sulphur is the active, masculine "soul" or combustible principle, while Mercury is the passive, feminine "spirit" or fluid principle.</note>, provides the form. In order that these two may reach operation and move each other by dissolving, coagulating, altering, coloring, and perfecting, there was a need for external aids, as if instruments, without which no effect would follow. For just as a blacksmith effects nothing without a hammer and fire, so neither does the *Philosopher* <note>Alchemists often referred to themselves simply as "Philosophers" or seekers of natural wisdom.</note> without his instruments, which are water and fire. And this water is called by some "water of the clouds," just as this fire is called "occasional." It is called "water of the clouds" without a doubt because it is distilled, like the *dew of May*, consisting of the finest parts. For just as the dew of the said month, when enclosed in an eggshell, is said to raise the egg or its contents on high by the heat of the sun, so also this water of the clouds or dew makes the *Philosophical Egg* <note>The "Egg" is the sealed glass vessel or "aludel" used to contain the matter during the long process of transmutation.</note> ascend—that is, it sublimates, exalts, and perfects it. This same water is also the sharpest vinegar, which makes the body into pure spirit. For as vinegar is of diverse qualities—namely, that it may penetrate into the depths and bind—so this water dissolves and coagulates, but it is not itself coagulated, because it is not of its own subject. Moreover, this water is sought from the fountain of *Parnassus* <note>The Hippocrene spring on Mount Helicon (associated here with Parnassus), said to have been struck from the ground by the hoof of the winged horse Pegasus; alchemists used this as a metaphor for their "living water" or solvent.</note>, which, contrary to the nature of other springs, exists at the summit of the mountain, having been made by the hoof of *Pegasus*, the winged horse. Furthermore, it is necessary that an actual fire be present, which nevertheless must be tempered by its own degrees, as if by bridles. For just as the *Sun*, proceeding from *Aries* into *Leo* <note>This refers to the movement of the sun from the spring equinox (March) toward the height of summer (July/August), symbolizing a gradual increase in temperature required for the work.</note>, gradually increases its heat for growing things by always approaching closer, so it is necessary for it to be done here. For the *Philosophical Infant* must be nourished by fire as if by milk; the more it grows, the more abundant the fire must be.

It emphasizes a gradual increase in heat, likening the process to the sun's path through the zodiac and the nursing of an infant.</summary>

## XI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Everyone well knows the twin offspring of Latona /
(As rumor testifies) who are said to be born of Jove /
Others call these the **Sun** and **Moon** <note>original: "Sonn vnd Mohn". In alchemy, these refer to the celestial symbols for gold and silver, or the active and passive principles of the Work.</note> joined in every way /
In which many black spots <note>These "black spots" refer to the <term>nigredo</term> or the stage of putrefaction where the material is dark and impure before being "whitened."</note> can be perceived /
Therefore, make Latona’s face quite white in color /
And so that the books do not harm you / tear them into pieces. <note>The instruction to "tear up the books" is a common alchemical trope suggesting that once the practitioner understands the true secret of nature, textual study is no longer necessary and may even lead to confusion.</note>

## XI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Everyone well knows the twin offspring of Latona /
(As rumor testifies) who are said to be born of Jove /
Others call these the **Sun** and **Moon** <note>original: "Sonn vnd Mohn". In alchemy, these refer to the celestial symbols for gold and silver, or the active and passive principles of the Work.</note> joined in every way, /

<summary>This emblem uses the myth of Latona and her twins (Apollo and Diana) as an allegory for the whitening (albedo) of the prima materia to produce philosophical Gold and Silver. It emphasizes practical laboratory work and the removal of "black spots" or impurities over the study of confusing theoretical texts.</summary>
<keywords>Latona, Apollo, Diana, Albedo, Whitening, Nigredo, Sun and Moon, Alchemical Allegory, Purification, Practical Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of whitening Latona, moving into a philosophical discourse on the difficulty of interpreting alchemical texts and the necessary balance between study and practical work.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XI.

So great is the diversity among authors in their writing that seekers of the truth almost despair of finding the goal of the **art**. <note>original: "artis fine". In alchemy, the "Art" refers to the Great Work of creating the Philosopher’s Stone.</note> For not only are allegorical discourses difficult to grasp in themselves and causes of many errors, but this is especially true when the same words are applied to different things, and different words to the same things. If anyone wishes to extricate himself from these difficulties, he must either possess a divine genius to perceive the truth lurking beneath such great shadows, or possess inexhaustible labor and wealth to discover through experience what is true and what is not. 

But the philosophers say that one is not sufficient without the other—neither a sharp mind without manual labor, nor labor without a sharp mind—just as <term>theory</term> <note>From "theoria," meaning contemplation or study.</note> is nothing without <term>practice</term> <note>From "praxi," meaning the actual laboratory work.</note> and vice versa. For no one possesses an intellect so discerning that he can avoid a hundred thousand twists, errors, literal falsehoods, detours, and ambiguities to remain on the true path of **Nature**. Hence, the Philosophers say: *“He who has not yet erred, has not yet begun,”* <note>original: "Qui nondum erravit, nondum incepit"</note> and *“errors are the teachers of those things which ought to be done or not.”* 

Likewise, they emphasize that a person could consume their entire life distilling and re-distilling—even if they lived a thousand years—before they would arrive at the truth through **Experimentation** alone. The *Corrector of Fools* <note>original: "Corrector fatuorum". A reference to the "Correctorium Fatuorum," a famous medieval alchemical treatise often attributed to Richardus Anglicus.</note> implies that little progress is made without study and the reading of authors when it says: *“Study removes ignorance and leads the human spirit back to true knowledge and to the understanding of any matter.”* 

Therefore, it is primarily necessary to acquire knowledge of this sweet work through study and to sharpen the mind through physical sayings, since the knowledge of truth is found within them. If, therefore, those who labor do not despise study, they will sweetly taste the fruit resulting from it. However, those who shrink from study and yet wish to labor should see whether the art is an imitation of nature itself, which the art desires to improve. For it is impossible for such a person to prepare the secrets of the Philosophers to a perfect end. Of these people, the wise say *“that they approach practice just as a donkey approaches hay, not knowing toward what they stretch their snout, except insofar as the external senses, without intellect, lead them to the fodder through sight and taste.”* So says the author. 

Truly, lest anyone should exhaust themselves with excessive study (which is an inexhaustible sea of immense depth) or attempt to put into action every single letter, even those that agree with many others—

<meta>catchword: -tur (the start of "conetur," to attempt)</meta>

<summary>This discourse explores the challenge of interpreting contradictory alchemical texts, arguing that success requires a balance of intellectual study and physical laboratory practice. It warns that blind experimentation is as aimless as a beast eating hay, while excessive literal reading can be an "inexhaustible sea" that drowns the student.</summary>

<keywords>Theory and Practice, Alchemical Allegory, Corrector of Fools, Nature, Experimentation, Education, Error as Teacher</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the difficulties faced by alchemists, transitioning from the physical and mental toll of the work to the specific symbolic requirement of "whitening Latona."</meta>

# DISCOURSE XI.

...they waste [their strength] (though in vain), the age of their body, reputation, wealth, and resources in the meantime by consuming or diminishing them. The Philosophers use this **Emblematic** speech: that **LATONA** must be whitened, and those books must be torn up, lest their hearts be broken. For most books are written so obscurely that they are understood only by their own authors. Not a few were left out of envy to lead others astray, or rather to delay them in their course so they do not reach the goal without difficulty, or to obscure what they themselves had written before. 

What it means, however, to "whiten Latona"—this is the work, this is the labor <note>original: "hoc opus hic labor est," a famous quotation from Virgil's *Aeneid* regarding the difficulty of returning from the underworld.</note> to investigate. *The Sound of the Trumpet* <note>original: *Clangor buccinæ*, a well-known 14th-century alchemical treatise.</note> asserts that Latona is an imperfect body composed of the sun and moon. The poets and the most ancient writers establish Latona as the mother of the Sun and Moon, or Apollo and Diana; others call her the nurse. They say Diana was born first (for the Moon and the state of <term>whiteness</term> <note>original: "albedo," the second major stage of the Great Work.</note> appear first), and she then performed the duty of a midwife on the same day during the birth of her brother Apollo. 

Now, Latona is one of the twelve **Hieroglyphic** gods of the Egyptians, from whom these and other allegories were spread to other nations. Only a very few Egyptian priests understood the true mind and meaning of these things, while everyone else interpreted them as referring to other subjects that did not exist in the nature of things—namely, various gods and goddesses. Hence, after **Vulcan**, Latona had a most sumptuous temple in that same place, covered and adorned with gold, since she was the mother of the *Philosophical* Apollo and Diana. 

But this Latona is dark and blackish, and has many spots on her face, which must be removed by skill—namely, through <term>whitening</term>. <note>original: "dealbationem." This refers to the purification of the "First Matter."</note> Some perform whitenings using white lead, sublimated mercury, talc reduced to oil, and similar things, with which they encrust and cover the external skin and thus "whiten" it. But these whitening encrustations fall off with any breath or liquid, because they do not penetrate to the interior. But the *Philosophers* do not care for such surface colorings, which are used more to deceive the eyes than to tinge the internal substance of the body. For they want the face of Latona to be whitened penetratively and the skin itself to be altered—that is, truly and not deceitfully. 

It might be asked: how does this happen? I respond: first Latona must be sought and recognized. Even if she is extracted from a lowly place, she must nevertheless be <term>sublimated</term> <note>A process of heating a substance until it vapors and then solidifies again, used here to mean "elevated" or "purified."</note> into a worthier one. If, however, she is taken from a worthier place, she must be submerged into a lowlier one, namely into dung. For there she will truly turn white and become <term>white lead</term>. <note>original: "plumbum album." In alchemical terms, this often refers to purified tin or a specific stage of the matter, rather than common lead.</note> Once this is held, there is no reason to doubt success, or the "red lead," which is the beginning and end of the work.

<meta>The catchword "Des" suggests the next page begins with a word like "Desistere" or "Descendere."</meta>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Emblem XII, moving from the poetic epigram to a detailed "discourse" (discursus) that explains the multiple layers of meaning behind the figure of Saturn and the stone he vomited.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XII.

We find that the allegory of Saturn has been interpreted in various ways. For **Astronomers** have referred him to the highest of the planets in order; the **beginners in Chemistry** <note>original: "Chymiæ tyrones." In this context, "tyrones" refers to students or apprentices of the alchemical art.</note> have referred him to the lowest of metals, namely lead. **Heathen Poets** held him to be the father of Jove and the son of Heaven. **Mythologists** have taken him to represent Time. But although all these may seem to have understood correctly in their own respect and to have had a probable reason for their opinion, they nevertheless do not explain the other things said of Saturn: why he devoured and vomited his sons and a stone in place of Jove; why he is the discoverer of truth; why he is conspicuous for a scythe, a serpent, blackness, moroseness, and a club-foot <note>original: "loripes," meaning thong-footed or limping, a traditional attribute of Saturn/Cronus.</note>?

The last group <note>The Mythologists.</note> believe they interpret this best when they say that Time opens up truth and draws it out from darkness; that like a serpent, Time slips away by rolling; that like a scythe, it mows down all things with death; that it devours its sons—that is, all things it has generated; and that it cannot digest or consume hard stones, and therefore vomits them up as it were. But while these things agree in part, they do not fit the reality of the matter and all its circumstances.

However, those **practiced in Philosophy** <note>Meaning experienced Alchemists, often referred to as "The Philosophers."</note> say that Saturn is present first in their work; if he is truly present, they say one cannot err, for truth is found in the darkness. Indeed, nothing exists there except **blackness** <note>original: "nigredo." This refers to the first stage of the alchemical Great Work, symbolizing putrefaction and the reduction of matter to its primal state.</note>. Whence they say: *Whatever color arrives after the blackness is praiseworthy in the assembly of the philosophers.* And: *When you see your matter turn black, rejoice; for it is the beginning of the work.* And: *When it first turns black, we say it is the key to the work,* because it does not happen without blackness, as the *Rosary* <note>The "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous 16th-century alchemical florilegium.</note> holds from **Arnold** <note>Arnold of Villanova, a 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note>.

And from the *Mirror* <note>Likely the "Speculum Alchemiæ" attributed to Roger Bacon.</note>: *When you are in the work, see to it that in the beginning you have the color black, and then you will be certain that you are putrefying and proceeding on the right path.* And soon after: *That blackness is called Earth, which is made by light decoction reiterated so many times until the blackness floats on top.* Hence the same authors say that **Saturn** holds the earth, **Mercury** the water, **Jupiter** the air, and the **Sun** the fire. Therefore, Saturn is the blackness, the indicator of truth, who devours the stone in place of Jove. For blackness—that is, a dark cloud—covers the stone at first so that it cannot be seen. Hence **Morienes** <note>A legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist.</note> says: *Every body, when it lacks a soul, is found to be gloomy and obscure.* And **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy.</note>: *Take its brain, grind it with very sharp vinegar, or the urine of boys, until it is darkened.* This having been performed, it lives in putrefaction, and the dark clouds which were over it...

<sig>H</sig>
<meta>The catchword "&" at the bottom right indicates the first word of the next page.</meta>

<summary>This discourse explores the various interpretations of Saturn—astronomical, mythological, and temporal—before arguing that his true significance lies in alchemy as the "nigredo" or blackening stage, which is the essential beginning of the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Alchemy, Nigredo, Blackness, Putrefaction, Arnold of Villanova, Hermes Trismegistus, Morienes, Time allegory, Lead, Elements</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XII, focusing on the alchemical interpretation of the myth of Saturn. ### DISCOURSE XII. 59

...and they return into his own body before he died. This stone is vomited out again by Saturn when it becomes white, and it is then placed on the peak of Helicon <note>Mount Helicon, in Greek mythology, was the home of the Muses and the site of the spring Hippocrene.</note> as a monument for mortals, as Hesiod writes: for truly whiteness lies hidden under blackness, which is extracted from his belly, that is, from the stomach of Saturn. Hence Democritus says, *Cleanse the tin <note>original: "stannum." In alchemy, this often refers to the base metal or a specific stage of the process rather than just the common metal tin.</note> with a special washing, extract from it its blackness and darkness, and its brilliance [whiteness] will appear.* And in the *Turba* <note>The "Turba Philosophorum" or "Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts.</note> it is said: *Join the dry with the moist, that is, the black earth with its water, and cook it until it is whitened.* Arnold [of Villanova] in *The New Light* <note>original: "in novo lumine," referring to the alchemical treatise "Novum Lumen."</note>, chapter 4, explains this best when he says: *Therefore the moisture which cured the blackness during the boiling shows itself when dried out, as the white color begins to appear:* And a little later: *And my master told me that this brownness <note>original: "bruxitas," a medieval Latin term for a dark or dusky color.</note> was ascending, because the whiteness was being extracted from the belly of its blackness, just as it is said in the Turba.* For when you see the thing itself as black, know that its whiteness is hidden in the belly of that first-appearing blackness.

This same blackness is called Saturn, and also lead: Hence Agadimon says in the *Turba*: *Cook the copper <note>original: "æs," which can mean copper or bronze.</note> until the blackness comes out, which they call the "coin" <note>original: "numum." In this context, it refers to a specific alchemical substance or stage of the matter.</note>, and mix the things of our art well, and you will immediately find the blackness, which is the "lead of the wise," about which the wise have written much in their books:* To this the saying of Emiganus refers, *that the splendor of Saturn, while it climbs into the air, does not appear except as darkened.* And Plato says in the *Rosary* <note>The "Rosarium Philosophorum," a major alchemical text.</note>; *The first rule of Saturn is to putrefy and to place it in the sun:* From these things it is clear that the mind of the Philosophers is far different when they speak of Saturn than what is understood by the common people. This Saturn generates Jove [Jupiter], that is, a dusky whiteness; Jove generates from Latona [Leto] Diana, that is, perfect whiteness, and Apollo, that is, redness <note>original: "rubedo," the final stage of the alchemical Great Work.</note>; and this is the successive transformation of all the perfect colors. This stone, cast out by Saturn, is said to be placed as a monument for mortals on the summit of a mountain, which is most true.

### XIII. English version of the German Epigram.

The Sages' Ore <note>original: "Ertz der Weisen." Often referring to the initial alchemical matter, sometimes called "Philosophical Brass."</note> is entirely overloaded with water in its body,
Therefore it desires water, to bathe itself well therein:
Just as Naaman <note>A biblical figure (2 Kings 5) who was a commander of the Syrian army cured of leprosy by washing seven times in the Jordan River.</note> drove away his leprosy through the Jordan,
It shall be well-scrubbed seven times with water.
Therefore, plunge your bodies into the sweet rivers,
So they will soon enjoy relief from their sickness.

# EMBLEM XIII. On the secrets of Nature.

## The Sages’ Brass is dropsical and wishes to be washed seven times in the river, just as Naaman the leper in the Jordan.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting an alchemical allegory. A naked man with a distended abdomen (representing "dropsy") sits on a rocky riverbank, gazing toward the water. Across the wide river lies a detailed city with Renaissance and Gothic architecture, including a cathedral and a stone bridge with several arches. The scene illustrates the cleansing of the "Philosophers' Copper," likened to the biblical account of Naaman the Syrian being cured of leprosy in the Jordan River.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XIII.

Languishing with an excess of swelling, the Sages’ brass <term>Æs Philosophorum: often translated as "Philosophers' Copper," it refers to the raw, unrefined state of the alchemical matter.</term> distends with dropsy <note>original: "hydrope." In an alchemical context, "dropsy" refers to an unhealthy excess of the "mercurial" or watery element within the metal, which causes it to appear bloated or impure.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Wherefore it seeks the health-bringing waters.
And as Naaman removed the contagion of leprosy in the Jordan,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is washed by its own waters three and four times <note>original: "térque quatérque." Poetically combined to mean seven times, echoing the biblical command given to Naaman.</note>:
Therefore, plunge your bodies into the sweet water,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And soon it will bring the aid of health to your diseases.

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XIII. While the previous page introduced the image and the poetic epigram regarding Naaman the Syrian and the "dropsical" brass, this page provides the philosophical and chemical discourse (Discursus) explaining the allegory of "washing" the metal in "philosophical water" to achieve perfection.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XIII.

That Naaman the Syrian, by the Prophet's command, went to Judea and washed himself seven times in the river Jordan, is to be attributed to his confidence in the Prophet's words; but that he was freed from leprosy by that washing is a miracle of divine omnipotence. For leprosy <note>In alchemical theory, "leprosy" refers to the surface impurities and "sickness" of base metals, particularly lead and copper.</note>, which is rooted in the blood and the foundational members of the human body like a universal cancer, cannot be cured or removed by any external washings, much less by cold water, such as the Jordan was. In the same way, that the Philosophers' Brass <term>Æs Philosophorum: the raw material of the Work, often associated with copper or an alloy</term> laboring under dropsy <note>original: "hydropisi." In alchemy, dropsy signifies an excess of crude, watery moisture that prevents the metal from reaching a solid, "perfect" state.</note> should be freed from it by washings of water—indeed, that the imperfect should be brought to perfection and the sick to health, so that it might bestow the same upon other sick things—is next to a miracle. For no such example exists elsewhere in nature, nor is it nature’s ordinary way to produce the most absolute Philosophical Tincture unless it be governed by the artist, and suitable subjects be administered to it with an external agent. Thus, the resetting of dislocations is not the property of nature, but of art; nonetheless, miraculously in human birth, the sacrum bone <note>original: "os sacrum."</note> opens itself so that the fetus can exit through it, as if through a door, and in this, God, the Best and Greatest, works through nature above nature. 

Thus, that the Stone be perfected may be seen as something supernatural <term>hyperphysicum</term>, although it is in truth physical. Whence a Philosopher in the *Rosarium* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* or "Rose Garden of the Philosophers," a highly influential 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> says: "Know that our Stone is airy and volatile; in its manifest state it is cold and moist, and in its hidden state it is hot and dry." And that coldness and moisture, which is a watery smoke in its manifest state—corrupting, blackening, and destroying itself and all things—flees from the fire. But the heat and dryness, which is in the hidden state, is "hot and dry gold," and is the purest oil, penetrating into bodies; and it is not fugitive, because the heat and dryness of Alchemy performs the tinting <note>original: "tingit." To "tint" or "tinge" is the alchemical process of projecting the elixir onto a base metal to transform its color and nature.</note>, and nothing else. Act therefore so that the watery coldness and moisture, which is in the manifest state, becomes equal to the heat and dryness which is in the hidden state, so that they may come together and be joined, and become together as one thing: penetrating, tinting, and deepening. However, these moistures must be destroyed by fire and the degrees of fire with a gentle temperament and suitable, moderate digestion. 

So it is said there: But if this is true, how can it be freed from waters by waters? The answer is that there are certain waters that are hot and dry in quality, such as many thermal baths, in which the Philosopher must wash. For this is what they say: "Wash with fire and burn with water" <note>original: "lava igne & combure aqua." This famous alchemical paradox suggests that the "water" used is actually a caustic or acidic substance that acts like fire, or that the process requires a specific chemical heat.</note>. For the fire that washes, and the water that burns, differ from each other only in name; they agree in effect and operation. Therefore, with this water, or this fire, that brass 

<meta>catchword: is to be washed [abluen-]</meta>

<summary>This page explains the allegory of Naaman's biblical cleansing as a metaphor for the alchemical purification of "Philosophical Brass." It describes the transition of the "Stone" from a cold, moist, and "sick" state to a perfected, hot, and dry state through a paradoxical process of washing with "fire-water."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Discursus" (Discourse) for Emblem XIII. Following the previous page's introduction of Naaman the Syrian and the "dropsical brass," the text now details the specific medical and alchemical methods required to "dry out" the superfluous moisture that hinders the perfection of the metal.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XIII.

it must be washed of its superfluous humidities, that is, it must be dried out. Regarding those suffering from dropsy <note>original: "hydropicis." In early medicine, dropsy (edema) was seen as an accumulation of watery humors.</note> who were cured by a six-month abstinence from all drink, by burial in hot sand or ox dung, by confinement in a hot oven, and by sweating, along with countless other aids, we know of such experiments; indeed, even through drying hot springs, such as those of Carlsbad <note>original: "Carolinæ." Today Karlovy Vary in the Czech Republic.</note> and Wiesbaden not far from Mainz <note>original: "Moguntia."</note>. In the same ways, this patient <note>Referring to the "Philosophical Brass" mentioned on the previous page.</note> must be cured: sometimes with waters, sometimes with the hot air of ovens, sometimes with dung, and at other times with sands and abstinence from drink. For these remedies are most effective in both cases—to be applied here [to the metal] and there [to the body]. In all of these, however, heat is the operator, because it leads out and consumes superfluous waters through its emunctories <term>Emunctoria: the organs or pores of the body that carry off waste or fluids, such as the kidneys or sweat glands.</term>, or even through the pores of the body. For external heat excites the internal—that is, the vital spirits—so that they may expel the harmful humor from themselves like useless excrement, by which the natural heat was previously suppressed as if by an enemy.

Great diligence and precaution are needed in this cure, lest while one of the internal organs is being helped, another is harmed. In Quartan fever <note>original: "Quartana." A form of malaria where paroxysms occur every fourth day; in alchemical terms, it represents a stubborn, "earthy" imbalance.</note> (as confirmed by the medical authority of Plato), we have experienced that thick and viscous humors—like gum or birdlime from trees—gathered from all the veins and the blood mass, descend through the *vena cava* (the great vein) down to the depths of the back. There, when they obstruct the emulgent veins <note>The renal veins that "milk" or draw fluids from the blood to the kidneys.</note> that draw the watery humor from the blood, or obstruct their openings, those veins are less able to function, and more watery fluid remains in the body. Thus, in a short time, unless provision is made, dropsy can occur even if the other primary internal organs are uninjured. 

Diuretics are of little or no use here, and purgatives even less, unless over time the blockage is diminished and drawn out. Even sudorifics <note>Medicines that induce sweating.</note> clearly cause harm, because they lead out the thinner parts of the fluids while leaving the thicker parts behind, and their continued use prostrates the patient's strength. For nature is accustomed to find a way for itself through the pores to evacuate watery fluids when the way to the bladder is blocked. This is therefore a Scylla, the other a Charybdis <note>In Greek mythology, two sea monsters on opposite sides of a narrow strait; the phrase "between Scylla and Charybdis" means having to choose between two equally dangerous options.</note>, both of which he who wishes to protect himself must avoid. 

But the dropsy which arises from a damaged liver or spleen, if it be confirmed <note>That is, if it has become chronic or "fixed."</note>, is of the most difficult cure. In the case of the Philosophical Brass <note>original: "ære Philoſophico."</note>, however, the cure is not impossible, since the affliction is accidental and secondary rather than essential or primary—provided it is begun cautiously, as we said regarding the abundance of serum in quartan fever. This caution ensures that, by too much drying, the matter does not fall into consumption <note>original: "tabẽ." A wasting away or extreme dryness.</note>, or by too much moistening, it does not fall into a dropsy that is difficult to cure.

<keywords>dropsy, quartan fever, Philosophical Brass, heat, emunctories, Wiesbaden, Carlsbad, Scylla and Charybdis, vital spirits, humors</keywords>

### XIV. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Hunger forces the many-footed fish <note>original: "vielfüßigen Fisch," referring to the polyp or octopus.</note> to consume its own feet,
And humans often nourish themselves with the flesh of humans;
Likewise, it does not grieve the dragon to bite and swallow its own tail,
So that he enjoys a part of his body as his own food.
This dragon must be tamed through the sword, hunger, and imprisonment,
Until he consumes and vomits himself entirely, kills himself, and creates life. <note>This refers to the alchemical cycle of "solve et coagula"—dissolving and reforming—where the matter must be destroyed to be perfected.</note>

## EPIGRAM XIV.

> *And men to nourish themselves on human feasts.*
> *While the Dragon bites its tail with its tooth and swallows it into its belly,*
> *By a great part of itself, it becomes its own food.*
> *It must be tamed by iron, by hunger, and by imprisonment, until*
> *It devours and vomits itself, kills itself and gives birth to itself.*
