<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XIV.</header>

THE ANCIENTS used to say that a serpent that devours another serpent becomes a dragon. For it rages against its own kind like a thief and an assassin against his own. It is well known that such creatures exist in Africa in such size and abundance that they destroyed a large part of Alexander’s army. Among the Asachaeans <note>An ancient Ethiopian tribe mentioned by Pliny the Elder in his *Natural History*.</note>, a people of Ethiopia, the largest are born; these, intertwined like wickerwork, sail with their heads held high toward better pastures. They say the kings of the Indians raised two dragons, one eighty and the other ninety cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters, based on the length of a forearm.</note> in size. From the observations of more recent travelers in our own time, it has become known that such creatures are still found near Angola, so large that they equal the massive masts of ships.

Likewise, in certain mountains of India and Africa, they report there is a great power of gold, but it is guarded by dragons so that no one may approach and take it away. For dragons congregate at the springs or streams that fall from the mountains, and so they are said to stand guard over the gold by chance. From this, the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists or "natural philosophers."</note> ascribe so many dragons to their treasures, such as the Golden Fleece, the Garden of the Hesperides, and other chemical persons or subjects like Cadmus, Saturn, Aesculapius, and Mercury, whose caduceus <note>The herald’s staff, famously topped with wings and entwined by two serpents.</note> is encircled by twin serpents, male and female.

By "Dragons," however, those authors mean nothing other than chemical subjects. Hence they say: *The mountains give dragons to the Rebis <note>term: Rebis (from the Latin "res bina," meaning "double thing"). In alchemy, it represents the end-product of the chemical marriage, often depicted as a hermaphroditic figure.</note>, and the earth gives fountains.* And the fact that the Dragon devours its own tail denotes its extreme hunger. Although others explain this as the year returning upon itself and representing a circle, it was originally applied by the philosophers to their own matters. They wish this dragon to be understood as the serpent that devours another of its own kind, and this is properly called sulfur, as all testify in countless places. 

Lullius <note>referring to Raymond Lull, a famous 13th-century polymath and reputed alchemist.</note> says in his *Codicil*, chapter 31: "This, my son, is the sulfur; it is this snake and dragon devouring its tail, the roaring Lion and the sharp sword, cutting all things, killing and shattering." And the *Rosarius* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a highly influential 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> states: "The dragon does not die unless it is killed with its brother and sister." And shortly after: "The dragon is the quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum," literally "living silver" or mercury.</note> extracted from bodies, having in itself body, soul, and spirit." In the same place, this water is called by another name, "the foul-smelling water," which is obtained after the separation of the Elements. The dragon is said to devour its own tail when it consumes the volatile, poisonous, and hu-

It connects legendary accounts of giant serpents in Africa and India to the chemical transformation of substances within the alchemical "Great Work."</summary>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XIV.</header>

...extremity <note>Original: *pyramidam*. This completes the sentence from the previous page: "the caduceus is surrounded by two serpents, one of which consumes the other’s pointed tail."</note> he consumes, so that afterwards, without a tail, he appears more corpulent and slower, since his motion and agility were derived in great part from the tail. Other animals rely on feet, but dragons, serpents, and worms of that kind use the contraction and extension of their bodies in place of feet; and just as poured water defines itself in certain circuits, bending now here, now there, so it is seen in almost all rivers, how they bend their courses like serpents and twist in oblique spirals. 

Hence, not without cause, the ancients called quicksilver <note>Original: *argentum vivum*.</note> a "serpent," and attributed serpents to Mercury, since the metal both trails a sort of tail and runs forward now here, now there, with a rolling weight. For as a serpent creeps, so too does Mercury <note>Here Maier refers to both the Roman god and the chemical element.</note>, who for that reason has wings on his feet and head. 

In Africa, they say there are flying serpents that would devastate everything if they were not destroyed by the Ibis <note>A wading bird sacred to the ancient Egyptians, often associated with the god Thoth.</note>. Wherefore the Ibis is placed among the sacred Egyptian images, both because of the manifest utility it provides to the whole region, and because of a hidden reason that fewer have understood. 

This dragon, having bitten off its tail and cast off its old skin, is said to acquire a new one along with youth; thus nature has granted a longer life not only to crows, ravens, eagles, and stags, but also to the serpentine race. An aging ant acquires wings; so too do many worms. An aging human is committed to the earth, but being reborn from the earth, he shall be consecrated to eternal life. From any burnt serpent a powder is made, which is taken harmlessly with great effect against all poisons. 

An <term>Alexipharmacum</term> <note>From the Greek *alexo* (to ward off) and *pharmakon* (poison); a traditional term for a medicine that protects against or cures the effects of poison.</note> of this kind ought also to be made from this dragon, which has now devoured its own tail (which is usually cut off in vipers), a most ready remedy against the evils of fortune and the body.

<header>EMBLEM XV. On the secrets of Nature.</header>

### Let the potter’s work, consisting of the dry and the moist, teach you.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving of a potter's workshop. A bearded potter wearing a brown vest, blue breeches, and a yellow cap sits on a wooden stool. He is working at a kick-wheel, shaping a clay vessel with both hands. His left bare foot is positioned on the large wooden flywheel at the base. To his right, a wooden bench holds several finished vessels, a book, and small tools. Light enters through a leaded glass window on the right wall. The interior features brick walls and a tiled floor. In the background, a doorway leads to another room where more pottery rests on the floor. A shallow basin of water sits on the floor near the potter's feet.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XV.

Behold with what a swift axle of the wheel <note>Original: *axe rotae*. This refers to the vertical spindle of the potter's kick-wheel shown in the engraving.</note> the potter shapes his vessels,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;While with his foot he mixes the clay <note>Original: *argillam*. Specifically white potter's clay, often used in alchemy to represent the "earth" of the philosophers.</note> with water:
In these two things rests his confidence: that the moisture
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;May, with skill, temper the thirst of the dry dust.
So you shall also do, having become more learned by this example,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Lest the water overcome the earth, or be overcome by the soil. <note>The "dry" and the "moist" are the two primary qualities the alchemist must balance; too much water results in a "drowning" of the matter, while too much heat or "earth" results in a burnt, useless powder.</note>

<sig>I 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: As</meta>

<summary>Emblem XV uses the metaphor of a potter at his wheel to illustrate the alchemical necessity of balancing "the dry and the moist." It emphasizes that just as a potter must perfectly temper clay with water to make it workable, the alchemist must precisely blend elemental qualities to achieve the Great Work.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose commentary (Discursus) for Emblem XV. While the previous page (69) introduced the emblem through verse and imagery of the potter, this page (78) provides the philosophical and alchemical explanation of why the potter's craft serves as a perfect metaphor for the "Great Work." It focuses on the balancing of the primary qualities: the dry (Earth) and the moist (Water).</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XV.</header>

Just as this globe of the world is rounded into one body by the embrace of earth and water, so too the work of the potter is seen to be composed of these same elements piece by piece—namely, from the dry and the moist—so that one might temper the other. For if the earth were without water, and no ocean, sea, lake, river, or spring existed near the land, the earth would produce nothing from itself, but would remain sterile. Likewise, unless the water were received within the cavities of the earth, but instead stood around it, it would easily cover the entire earth, and thus the land would remain uninhabitable. But when one has entered the other amicably, and the water has moderated the dryness of the earth, and the earth has moderated the moisture of the water by their mutual mixing, the fertility and usefulness of both elements is seen. 

In a similar way, the potter mixes clay with water so that he may create a workable mass from it, which he shapes on his wheel and exposes to warm air so that it may dry out step by step. Afterward, he adds the violence of fire, so that the vessels may be properly hardened and coalesce into a durable stone that can resist both water and fire. The *Philosophers* <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists or natural scientists of the era.</note> themselves testify that nature proceeds in this same way in its natural work, and therefore they assert that instruction ought to be taken from the potters. 

Regarding the dry and the moist—that is, the earth and the water—there is indeed much affinity in both processes. However, we do not doubt that in the manner of the cooking <note>Original: *coctionis*. This refers to "coction" or "digestion," the alchemical process of heating a substance over a long period to mature it.</note> and in the matter and form of the elements to be composed, there exists a great deal of difference. For the potter's vessel has an artificial form, whereas the *Philosophical* tincture <note>Original: *tinctura*. A potent alchemical substance capable of "dyeing" or transforming base metals into gold.</note> has an entirely natural one, and is as much nobler as its material is more excellent than theirs. Both works are indeed earthy, but in the *Philosophical* work it is said that nothing exists which has not sought the "heaven of the air" <note>A metaphor for the process of sublimation or distillation, where the material is vaporized and refined.</note>, whereas in the potter's work, thick and dreg-filled earth predominates. 

The result in both cases is a stone: in the potter's shop it is a common stone, but here it is the *Philosophical* Stone. Under this name, a certain deceived person once shut up a great number of manufactured stones or bricks in one chest, and in another, whiter flint stones. For a certain time, he performed diabolical conjurations over them, by which the former were supposed to be converted into pure gold and the latter into silver. But since these were mistakenly thought to be the "Stones of the Philosophers," and a great sum of money had been poured out to buy various things while the new gold and silver were expected at a set time, when it did not appear, and the stones had not been converted into the hoped-for gold, shame—

<meta>catchword: shame (pudorem)</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical necessity of balancing the "dry" and "moist" elements using the potter's craft as a practical example, while warning against those who try to achieve transmutation through magic or "diabolical conjurations" rather than natural philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>elements, earth, water, potter, alchemy, Philosophical Stone, coction, tincture, transmutation, gold, silver, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of Discourse XV. While the previous provided text ended with a discussion of the elements (earth and water) in the potter's craft, this page shifts toward the limits of alchemy, the difference between biological life and metals, and the concept of the "seed of gold." Note that the page numbers in the source (78 to 71) appear out of sequence, but the thematic continuity regarding the "dry and the moist" remains the focus of the Discourse.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>D I S C U R S U S XV.</header>

...death finished the matter <note>original: "rem mors finiit." This likely refers to a specific case or argument concluded in the preceding text.</note>: For gold or silver should not be sought from that in which it does not naturally exist, since demonic magic finds no place in these divine works, but is as far from them as its author is from a devoted and pious man, or as hell is from heaven. Thus, even if the true <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>A legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life.</note> were possessed, no one should persuade themselves that impossible things can be achieved by it, as Isaac <note>Likely referring to Isaac Hollandus, a famous alchemist often cited for his practical instructions.</note> warns: Since no one is bound to the impossible, whether by the laws of nature or of civil society.

From this, let each person judge for themselves whether the transmutation of gemstones or the creation of <term>malleable glass</term> <note>A mythical material mentioned in ancient Roman accounts that could be hammered without breaking; alchemists debated if the Stone could produce it.</note> through the Stone are possible and suit this nature or not. Geber <note>An 8th-century scholar, often called the "Father of Chemistry," whose Latinized works were foundational to European alchemy.</note> affirms that the Philosophers speak much through allegory; and of himself, he says that where he has spoken clearly, he has said nothing, but where he has spoken through figures, there he has hidden the truth like wheat beneath the chaff.

What a man sows, that shall he also reap—a principle that holds true in vegetables and animals, even though different species sometimes arise from what was sown. One must consider whether this should be applied to metals, which are not propagated through seeds like living things. In metals, the parts are merely <term>homogeneous</term> <note>Uniform in structure throughout, unlike the "heterogeneous" organs of a plant or animal.</note>—namely, sulfur and <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argentum vivum." Mercury, considered one of the two primary principles of all metals.</note>—whereas in living things, parts exist as heterogeneous or organic. In metals, there are no receptacles for seed, whereas in living things, there are. In metals, no nutrition, growth, or extension in all dimensions is found; in living things, these are found most clearly. Indeed, metals are elemental weights that recognize only mixing; living things, besides mixing, possess either a vegetative or sensitive soul.

Nevertheless, it is undoubtedly true that there is something in subterranean places which is not yet gold, but will become gold through nature after a thousand years. Who would deny that this is an **analogous seed of gold**? And since gold and the gold-making nature are of one origin—though the latter is of a more noble form—once the seed of gold is known, this gold-making nature will also be established. The Philosophers affirm that this is the dry and the moist, or sulfur and quicksilver, which must be sought in their purest form from two mountains.

<meta>catchword: Diss (Discursus)</meta>

<summary>This page explores the natural limits of alchemy, arguing that while metals lack the biological seeds and "souls" of plants and animals, they possess a "subterranean seed" that matures into gold over centuries.</summary>

### XVI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The lion, who overcomes the beasts with strength and claws,
Fights without any fear and does not let his flight be seen;
If you place with him a lioness, who is winged, who dares
To lift him up, so that both might live in the skies;
But he remains standing quite firm and keeps himself upon the earth;
This image of Nature shows you the way by which she governs.
<note>In alchemy, the lion and winged lioness represent the union of "fixed" (solid) and "volatile" (evaporating) substances. The goal is to balance the two so the volatile becomes fixed and the fixed becomes spiritualized.</note>

# EMBLEM XVI. On the Secrets of Nature.
## This lion has no feathers, but that one does.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting an alchemical allegory. Two lions are shown in a natural landscape: a large, winged lion on the right is grappling with a wingless lion on the left. ### EPIGRAM XVI.

The LION, victor over the four-footed beasts, strong in chest and claw,
->*Fights without fear, and avoids all flight:*<- <note>original Latin: "Absque metu pugnat, dissimulatque fugam." In alchemy, "flight" refers to volatility—the tendency of a substance to evaporate when heated. The flightless lion represents the "fixed" or solid state.</note>
To him, you must join at the feet a winged Lioness,
->*Who flies and wishes to lift the male up with her:*<- <note>The winged lioness represents the "volatile" principle (often associated with mercury or spirit) that seeks to rise.</note>
But he stands unmoved on the ground, and holds back the one who flies,
->*Let this image of nature show you the way.*<- <note>This "struggle" represents the alchemical process of "fixing the volatile," where the solid material prevents the spirit from escaping, eventually uniting them into a stable stone.</note>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page provides the prose "Discourse" for Emblem XVI of Michael Maier’s *Atalanta Fugiens*. It follows the visual representation and epigram of the two lions—one winged and one wingless—expanding on the natural history of the lion and its symbolic application in the alchemical "Great Work." The text continues from the previous focus on the "fixed" and "volatile" principles.</meta>

### DISCOURSE XVI.

It is known from experience that the Lion prevails over other animals not so much by the size and strength of its body as by its nobility. If seen in a hunt, blushing to turn its back, it withdraws itself gradually if overwhelmed by numbers. Once out of the hunters' sight, it speeds its flight, believing that the shame is purged by concealment. It lacks the leap in flight which it uses while pursuing something. Its bones are solid and not at all hollow, and are said to be so hard that when struck together they give off fire just like steel and flint. Most of all it fears fire. It seems to derive its substance from the nature of the sun. For in force and heat it excels other animals, just as the sun does the stars; furthermore, it is always seen with fiery and open eyes, just as the sun beholds the earth with an open and fiery eye. The lioness, fighting for her cubs, fixes her eyes on the ground so as not to be terrified by the hunting spears. When the lion senses the mating of a leopard <note>original Latin: *Pardi*. In medieval bestiaries, the "pard" or leopard was often seen as a cross-breed or a symbol of adultery.</note>, he takes punishment upon the adulterous lioness, and rises up with all his might for the penalty. Therefore the lioness washes away the guilt and the scent in a river, or, conscious of the crime and in fear of the male, she accompanies the fleeing adulterer.

While the Philosophers <note>In alchemical texts, "Philosophers" refers to the practitioners of the Hermetic art.</note> contemplate the wondrous nature of this beast, they have produced various allegories concerning the Lion, like hieroglyphic writings testifying to their secret work. Noting that the lion is a constant, firm animal, lacking in guile and suspicion, they have likened the more powerful part of their Philosophical composition to it. For just as he himself does not flee, so neither does this <note>The "fixed" part of the alchemical matter which does not evaporate under heat.</note>; he has solid bones, and so this is fixed and knows not how to be conquered. 

But just as the lioness is not always free or immune from adultery, so neither is the Moon or Mercury <note>original: *Luna seu Mercurius*. These represent the "volatile" or silver principle in alchemy.</note> free from every stain; but by the ignorant, it is joined now to this material, now to that, whence results an adulterous conjunction of things disagreeing in nature, rather than a true marriage. For those born of a leopard and a lioness do not have the beautiful manes around the neck and shoulders, with which mark only those conceived from a Lion are adorned. Therefore, let the philosophical lioness be joined to her own mate, and a genuine and noble little lion <term>leunculus</term> <note>literally "little lion," referring to the successful result of the alchemical reaction.</note> shall be born therefrom, who can easily be recognized by his claw. But it is necessary that the lioness be not just any one, but winged, so that she can enter into battle with the lion, relying on the swiftness of her feathers; lest, forsooth, she be suppressed by his excessive wrath, but if at any time 

<meta>catchword: when</meta>

<summary>Maier discusses the natural history of the lion as a symbol for the "fixed" principle in alchemy, contrasting it with the "volatile" lioness. He warns against "adulterous" mixtures of incompatible substances, advocating for a true union of philosophical principles.</summary>

<keywords>lion, lioness, fixed and volatile, alchemy, sun and moon, mercury, philosophical marriage, animal symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Discourse XVI of Michael Maier’s *Atalanta Fugiens*. The previous page described the natural and symbolic characteristics of the lion, including its solar nature and its relationship with the lioness. This page shifts toward the specific alchemical allegory of the "Red Lion" and the "Winged Lioness," representing the union of fixed and volatile principles.</meta>

### DISCOURSE XVI.

when she falls into a rage without cause, she meditates on flight. For while he detains her as she is about to flee, he is incited by a greater love toward her, friendship being restored after the quarrel. But you will say: who has ever seen a winged lioness, or what benefit would feathers be to a lioness? Near Mount Cithaeron <note>A mountain range in central Greece, sacred to Dionysus and famous in mythology.</note> there is a deep valley, in which only winged lionesses are seen; truly, on the very summit of that same mountain, the Red Lion <term>Red Lion: In alchemy, this often represents the "fixed" sulfur or the final stage of the philosopher's stone</term> dwells, of that kind which Hercules slew <note>The Nemean Lion, the first of Hercules' twelve labors, whose skin was impenetrable.</note>. Therefore the lion must be captured and led into the valley, and soon he will be joined with the winged lioness. She also will easily allow herself to be overcome, for like is matched with like. Afterward, both must be sublimated <note>original Latin: *sublimandus*. An alchemical term for turning a solid into vapor, but here also meaning "raised up" to the mountain peak.</note> from the said valley to the peak of the mountain, and from here they will never flee from each other again, but will always remain together in an inviolable pact. 

The capture of Lions, I confess, is not easy, but subject to many dangers; nonetheless, it must be undertaken. The lion does not feed with the lioness, but wanders separately, as they say. Hence the tracking and hunting of each must be done separately. But if lion cubs are sought for both from their "first claws"—when they first begin to walk, which happens two months after birth—and once they have become more mature, as has been said, they are joined together, the matter will be free from all danger. They are born, however, in the springtime—which is to be noted—with open eyes. Since indeed after birth the Lions walk in a crooked path so that their den might not be discovered, they must be searched for with the greatest diligence and care, and their cubs must be carried away.

->K ij<-

<meta>catchword: A</meta>

<summary>Maier explains the alchemical process of uniting the "fixed" Red Lion (terrestrial/solid) and the "volatile" Winged Lioness (celestial/vapor) through the metaphor of a hunt near Mount Cithaeron. He emphasizes that while the adult principles are dangerous to manage, they can be easily unified if caught and trained as "cubs" from the start of the Work in springtime.</summary>

### XVII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

> If you intend to measure well the work of Nature,
> You must not forget to seek four spheres full of fire:
> The first is under Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and blacksmithing, representing the literal heat of the furnace.</note>; the second shows Mercury <note>In this context, Mercury refers to the "philosophical fire" or the volatile spirit within the matter.</note>;

> Which is the fire of Nature; let this chain be strictly entrusted to you,

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Four Spheres of Fire" introduced in the emblem on the preceding page. While page 77 presented the visual and poetic allegory of these fires—associated with Vulcan, Mercury, the Moon, and Apollo—this page provides the "Discourse" (Discursus), a scholarly explanation citing famous alchemical authorities like Raymond Lull and George Ripley to define the specific functions of these four types of heat.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XVII.

Philosophers have mentioned in many places the four kinds of fires necessary for the natural work, namely Lull <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note>, the Author of the *Scala* <note>Referring to the *Scala Philosophorum* (The Ladder of Philosophers), a well-known alchemical treatise.</note>, Ripley <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), an influential English alchemist and author of *The Compound of Alchemy*.</note>, and very many others. And hence, speaking of the fires, Raymond [Lull] says in the *Scala*:

> *It must be noted that here lie contrary operations: Because just as the fire against nature <note>original Latin: "ignis contra naturam." In alchemy, this often refers to a corrosive substance or a sharp, "attacking" heat that breaks down matter.</note> dissolves the spirit of the fixed body into the water of a cloud, and constrains the body of the volatile spirit into frozen earth, so conversely the fire of nature <note>original Latin: "ignis naturæ." This is the internal, creative heat that drives growth and maturation.</note> congeals the dissolved spirit of the fixed body into a globular earth, and the body of the volatile spirit fixed by the fire against nature it resolves not into the water of a cloud but into the water of the Philosophers.*

Ripley speaks more clearly of these fires in his Third Gate, saying thus:

> *There are four kinds of fires which you ought to know: the natural, the unnatural, and the [fire] against nature, and the elemental fire which kindles wood. We use these fires and no others. The fire against nature must torture the bodies; it is the dragon, as I tell you, burning violently like the fire of hell. The fire of nature is the third menstruum <note>original: "menstruum." In alchemy, this term refers to a powerful solvent or catalyst, not to literal biological fluids.</note>; that fire is naturally within every thing. We call the "occasioned" fire <note>original: "ignem occasionatum." This refers to artificial heat provided by external means, like a bath or furnace.</note> unnatural, such as the heat of ashes and baths for putrefying. Without these fires you shall bring nothing to putrefaction, so that your matter may be separated, and simultaneously be proportioned for a new conjunction. Therefore, make the fire inside your glass, which burns the bodies more effectively than elemental fire.*

These are their words. **They are called fires** because they possess a fiery power: the natural fire by coagulating, the unnatural by dissolving, the fire against nature by corrupting, and the elemental fire by administering heat and the first motion. And in these, a chained order is observed, so that the second is incited to action by the first, the third by the second, and the fourth by the third and first together; thus one is the agent and the other the patient, and the same is both agent and patient in different respects. Just as it is observed in iron rings or rods continued by a magnet and connected by mutual touch, the same occurs in these fires. For the elemental fire, like a magnet, sends its force through the second and third even to the fourth, and joins one to the other through mutual operations and makes them cohere, until the internal action between the higher ones is completed. The first is the elemental fire in fact and in name; the second is air or aerial; the third is watery or of a lunar nature; the fourth is earthy. Concerning the first, it is superfluous to say anything, because it is known to everyone who sees and touches. The remaining three are dragons, solvents, waters, sulfurs, and Mercuries. They are called "dragons" because, partaking of venomousness, they devour their own kind...

<summary>This page explains the four types of alchemical "fire" (Elemental, Unnatural, Against Nature, and Natural) required to transform matter, citing authorities like Lull and Ripley. It describes a "chained" relationship where these heats work together like magnetic rings to dissolve and then recompose the philosophical matter.</summary>

<keywords>four fires, Raymond Lull, George Ripley, fire against nature, fire of nature, menstruum, alchemical solvent, magnetism in alchemy, putrefaction, dragons, elemental fire</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Discourse XVII," providing further explanation of the four fires or spheres mentioned previously. It transitions from the definitions provided by George Ripley to a deeper discussion of how these fires function as "menstrua" (solvents/nourishers) and "sulphurs" (active principles) in the creation of the alchemical Stone.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XVII.

...serpents of [that] kind, they wear down and alter the bodies mixed with them; that is, they dissolve and coagulate them. They are called **Menstrua** <note>In alchemy, a *menstruum* is a solvent or a substance that provides nourishment for growth, named by analogy to the biological fluid thought to nourish a fetus in the womb.</note> because from them the Philosophical fetus is produced and nourished until its birth. Raymond Lull, in his *Book on the Fifth Essence*, distinction 3, holds that there is a double menstruum: vegetable and mineral. Ripley, in the preface to his *Twelve Gates*, says there is a triple one, though in truth they are one and agree with each other. For from all of them together the generation of the fetus occurs, and the **white water** <note>original Latin: "aqua alba"</note> precedes its birth; this water is not of the substance of the fetus but from its superfluity, and therefore it must be separated. 

They are called **Waters** because they show an aqueous nature in the fire—that is, the flow and liquidity that belongs to water. It is well known that there are diverse and wonderful properties of waters, some of which are stone-forming, as they are coagulated into very hard tufa-stones suited for human buildings. To these, the mineral waters of the Philosophers are very similar, for they harden and pass into a stony resistance. 

They are also called **Sulphurs** because of the power of sulphur which they possess. For the sulphur of nature is mixed with another sulphur and becomes one, and two sulphurs are dissolved by one, and one is separated from two, and sulphurs are contained by sulphurs, as Yximidius says in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers) is one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts, written as a record of a debate between pre-Socratic philosophers.</note>. What these sulphurs are is declared in the same place by Dardaris in these words: 

> *The sulphurs are hidden souls in the four elements, which, having been extracted by art, naturally contain and join themselves together. If, however, you rule and well cleanse with water that which is hidden in the belly of the sulphur, that hidden thing, meeting its own nature, rejoices, just as the water does with its equal.*

Mosius also says: 

> *I will now tell you what it is: One is indeed the **fiery quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum igneum: literally "living silver" or mercury, described here as having a fiery or active quality.</term>; two is the body composed in it; and the third is the water of sulphur, by which the one is washed, ground, and governed until the work is finished.*

What is said of the sulphurs should be understood in the same way regarding the same number of Mercuries. For so Mosius himself says in the following: 

> *The quicksilver of **Cambar** <note>A technical term in older alchemy, often referring to a specific state of mercury or a processed mineral.</note> is **Magnesia** <note>In this context, magnesia does not mean magnesium, but a "primitive" or "all-containing" mineral substance.</note>; but the quicksilver or **orpiment** <note>original Latin: "auripigmentum," a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral.</note> is the sulphur which ascends from the mixed compound.*

But since these testimonies are infinite and available to anyone, I will cease to bring more of them here. These four fires are enclosed as if in spheres and orbs; that is, each has its own individual center from which or toward which its motion tends. Nevertheless, they are observed to be so bound together—partly by nature, partly by art—that one operates little or not at all without the other; indeed, the action of one is the passion <note>In the philosophical sense, "passion" refers to the state of being acted upon by an external force.</note> of the other, and vice versa.

<meta>catchword: Das (The)</meta>

<summary>This page explains the "four fires" using the analogies of the menstruum (nourishment) and sulphur (the active soul), citing authorities like Lull, Ripley, and the Turba Philosophorum. It describes how these substances act as solvents and coagulants to "birth" the alchemical fetus.</summary>

## Fire makes things fiery, not golden; but Gold makes things golden.

### XVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Every active thing of Nature lets its power go round about itself,
Through which a multiplication may occur;
Just as fire is accustomed to make fiery everything that nears it,
And nothing brings forth a work so noble without a cause;
Gold cannot give the power of fire, nor fire the power of gold,
For in every thing its own natural seed adheres. <note>This expresses the alchemical axiom *actiones sunt suppositorum*—that agents produce results according to their own specific nature. To make gold, one must start with the "seed" of gold.</note>

<meta>catchword: EMBLE- (referring to the next page, EMBLEMA XVIII)</meta>

<summary>This page contains the musical score and epigram for Fugue XVIII, establishing the alchemical principle that every natural substance acts within its own sphere to reproduce its own likeness—specifically, that only gold can produce gold.</summary>

# EMBLEM XVIII. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->Fire loves to make things fiery, not to make them golden; but Gold makes things golden.<-

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving showing an alchemist or artisan in a workshop. The man, wearing a purple tunic, yellow breeches, and a red cap, is using a long blue tool to tend a large furnace filled with bright orange flames and billowing smoke. To his left, a large golden bowl overflowing with gold coins or medallions sits upon a wooden pedestal. A dark dog sits on the tiled floor in the foreground. Through an open doorway in the background, a lone figure walks across a field toward a stone building with a steeple. A small red circular stamp with the letters "B.R." appears in the lower left of the illustration.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XVIII.

> If there be any <term>active agent</term> <note>original Latin: "agens." In alchemy, this is the substance or force that initiates change in a "passive" matter.</note> of nature, it sends its forces
>    Out into a circle, and desires to multiply its own kind.
> Thus fire makes all things it encounters fiery; nor is there any thing
>    Which produces a noble work without its own proper cause.
> Gold makes nothing fiery of itself, and fire gilds nothing:
>    For each thing recognizes the seed that is within itself.

<meta>catchword: NATURE</meta>

### XVIII. English version of the German Epigram.

Every active thing of Nature lets its power go round about itself,
Through which a multiplication may occur;
Just as fire is accustomed to make everything it meets fiery,
And no noble work comes about without its own specific cause.
Gold cannot make things burn, and fire cannot turn things to gold,
For each thing brings forth only what is in its own seed.

<summary>This page illustrates a fundamental alchemical principle: agents of nature can only reproduce their own likeness, meaning fire creates heat while only "philosophical gold" can produce gold. It uses the metaphor of a "sphere of activity" to explain how natural forces radiate and multiply.</summary>
<keywords>multiplication, active agent, seed of gold, alchemical furnace, like produces like, sphere of influence</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XVIII, following the emblem and epigram which established the principle that agents (like fire or gold) multiply their own nature. Here, the text provides scientific and philosophical evidence for this "simple process" of nature across various fields: anatomy, chemistry, and physics.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XVIII.

Nature’s method of operation in all the individual things of the universe is such that she employs a simple process to complete one simple motion. This is apparent in the **Anatomy** of the human body, in which one muscle serves only one motion, namely the **attractive** <note>original: "attractivo." In modern anatomy, we would call this contraction.</note>, while another, opposite to the first, serves the **expansive**; thus, if a limb is to be moved in a circle, this is accomplished by various muscles positioned in a circle. In the same way, the operation of **fire** is one and simple: namely, to heat and ignite; indeed, it assimilates and burns all things to which it is applied, provided they are combustible.

Hence **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and alchemy were foundational in Europe.</note> says in his book *On the Congelation of Stones*: "Whatever falls into salt-flats becomes salt, and whatever falls into fire becomes fire," though some things change more quickly and others more slowly, according to the **active power** of the agents and the **resistance** of the passive subjects. There is also a place in **Arabia** which colors all bodies existing within it with its own color. Thus, individual natural things possess a power infused by nature, by which they act upon things mixed with or moved near them by **assimilating** and altering their nature and form. What occurs in plants and animals as generation through the propagation of seeds, occurs in simple or simply-mixed bodies as the **infusion** and **assimilation** of power.

The **Sun**, the light of the heavens, casts its rays from itself upon the earth; when these are collected in concave or burning **mirrors**, they demonstrate that they were produced by such a cause, and appear, as it were, like the projectib<insert>i</insert>le <note>The Latin "projectibiles" refers to forms or rays "thrown forward" from a source.</note> forms of the Sun. From this it is clear that **solar rays** are nothing other than a fiery flame extended and dispersed over a wide breadth; when these are collected and condensed back into themselves by concave, transparent, and circular instruments, or by reflective, concave, and steel mirrors, they burst forth as a flame and burn whatever is in their way.

In the same manner, a certain power, like a vapor, is dispersed within a certain <term>elementated body</term> <note>A body composed of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note>; if this power is collected and drawn into one, it becomes water, and from that water, earth. Hence Avicenna, in the place cited before, says: "From water comes earth, when the qualities of earth overcome it, and vice versa." 

Furthermore, there is a certain thing used by some ingenious men when they wish to coagulate a dry thing, which is composed of two waters and is called **virgin's milk** <note>original: "lac virginis." An alchemical term for a white, milky liquid, often a solution of mercury or a precipitate formed by mixing two clear liquids.</note>, which is its most certain effect. Thus says Avicenna. There are also those who promise that they can double or multiply the forces in a **magnet** stone; indeed, we have seen such a stone enclosed in silver, weighing scarcely one pound, which attracted and supported an iron anchor of 28 pounds—which would have been impossible unless its force had been rendered increased and stronger

in

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical principle of assimilation, arguing that all natural forces—from human muscles and fire to solar rays and magnets—act by projecting and multiplying their own specific nature into other bodies.</summary>

<keywords>Avicenna, anatomy, fire, solar rays, burning mirrors, virgin's milk, magnetism, assimilation, alchemy, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Discourse XVIII regarding the "multiplication" of natural properties. The previous page discussed how fire creates fire and how the Sun's light would be concentrated; this page moves into the alchemical application of these principles to metals and the debate over whether minerals can reproduce like living things.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XVIII.</header>

...ens, would have existed in it; which without doubt was accomplished through the calling back of dispersed virtues into a single point or pole, as it were, or through the attraction of those same virtues from a large body into a smaller one. Others are found who say that a **lead-making stone** can be made from the "sulfurous aura" of **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturni." In alchemy, Saturn is the traditional name for the metal lead.</note> into common **Mercury**, provided it is infused and retained there until it coagulates; they claim this stone continuously converts common Mercury into lead. Some boast that they can produce **copper** from **antimony**, or from its **starred Regulus** <note>original: "stellato Regulo." The 'Regulus' is the metallic heart of a mineral. 'Starred' refers to the star-like crystalline pattern that forms on the surface of antimony when it is purified.</note>, using the "odor" of copper in the time it takes for one to eat an egg—indeed, they claim to have made all metals. But let them be granted their due credit, even if this does not seem likely to me. Bolder—though I know not if more fortunate—are those who try to derive **gold** from gold, according to the saying of the "Golden Poet": *He whose heart is set on barley, sows barley; in gold are the seeds of gold.* <note>A reference to the idea that "like produces like." To make gold, one must start with the "seed" or essence of gold.</note>

Every natural thing indeed possesses a certain power of **multiplying** itself, but this is only brought into action in **vegetables** and **animals**, and by no means in metallic minerals, fossils of the earth, or "meteors" <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric or geological phenomena, including minerals formed in the earth.</note>. Some **plants**, arising from a tiny grain of seed, often produce a thousand or more grains of seed, and thus they multiply and propagate themselves, and do so annually. **Animals** also have their offspring, in greater or lesser numbers according to the nature of each. But **gold, silver, lead, tin, iron, copper,** or **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum," literally "living silver."</note> have never been found to multiply themselves or their own species in that way, even though one is very often found to have been changed into another and "ennobled" <note>Transmutation of a "base" metal like lead into a "noble" metal like gold.</note>. 

Nevertheless, the **Philosophers** <note>Usually referring to the "Philosophers by Fire," or alchemists.</note> affirm that just as the principle of "igniting" exists in fire, so the principle of "gold-making" exists in gold. But a **tincture** <note term="tincture">A potent alchemical substance capable of "staining" or transforming the internal nature of a metal.</note> is sought, by means of which gold may be made. This must be investigated in its own proper principles and generations, not in foreign ones. For if fire produces fire, a pear tree a pear, and a horse a horse, then lead will generate lead (and not silver), and gold will generate gold (and not a tincture). To this, it is a tenet of the Philosophers—which they do not deny—that gold must be added as a **ferment** <note term="ferment">In alchemy, a 'ferment' acts like yeast in bread; it is a small amount of the desired result (gold) added to the alchemical 'Stone' to give it the final direction to produce more gold.</note> to the **gold-making Stone** at the end of the work; they assert it is necessarily required. For the ferment leads the thing being fermented into its own nature, and without it, the entire composition would never return to perfection.

It concludes that while metals do not reproduce naturally like living things, gold can act as a "ferment" to transform other substances into its own nature through alchemy.</summary>

### XIX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Four brothers stand in a long row, of whom one clearly
Holds Earth in his hand, the other Water most clear;
The others have Air and Fire. If you wish that all
Die quickly, let death fall upon just one of them;
And soon they will all die and end their lives,
Because they are joined together by natural bonds. <note>This refers to the four elements—Earth, Water, Air, and Fire—which alchemists believed were the building blocks of all matter. In this allegory, the "death" of one element represents the breakdown of the entire chemical compound.</note>

<header>EMBLEM XIX. On the secrets of Nature.</header>

## **If you kill one of the four, suddenly all will be dead.**

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting five men in an outdoor landscape. On the left, a group of four nude men stand together, representing the four elements. One man on the far left holds a torch with bright orange flames (Fire); another holds a grey, textured mass resembling a bundle of sticks or earth (Earth); the others signify Water and Air through their proximity and poses. ### EPIGRAM XIX.

> Twice two brothers stand in a long row, <note>original: "Bis duo stant fratres longo ordine." The "twice two" refers to the four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>
> One of whom sustains the weight of earth in his right hand, the other of water:
> The portion of air and fire belongs to the rest; if you wish
> That they perish, you need only strike one with death:
> And all will be carried off in a <term>shared funeral</term> <note>original: "consanguineo... funere." Literally a "blood-related" funeral, implying that because they are of the same family or substance, the death of one is the death of all.</note>,
> Because the mutual bonds of Nature bind them together.

<sig>L 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Poets</meta>

<summary>This emblem uses the allegory of four brothers to represent the four classical elements—Earth, Water, Air, and Fire—illustrating their absolute interdependence. In the alchemical tradition, it teaches that the physical world is a unified whole where no single part can be altered or destroyed without affecting the balance of the entire system.</summary>

<keywords>four elements, alchemy, Michael Maier, interdependence, natural bonds, Earth, Water, Air, Fire</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse associated with Emblem XIX, which uses the myth of the four elements and the "shared death" of the brothers as an allegory for the chemical and physical unity of matter in the Great Work.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>

<header>DISCOURSE XIX.</header>

Poets imagine that Geryon, the king of Spain, existed with three bodies, and that he possessed cattle of a purple color, over which a two-headed dog and a seven-headed dragon were appointed as guards. Geryon himself is said to be the son of Chrysaor, born from the blood of Medusa, and the dragon is said to be the offspring of Typhon and Echidna. Since these tales do not agree with any historical truth or factual reality as they sound, yet they are widely celebrated in all <term>alchemical allegories</term> <note>original: "Chymicis allegoriis." Alchemy was often referred to as the "Chymic Art" in this period.</note>, they have been added by us to these emblems with good reason. 

By the three bodies of Geryon, we understand three faces seen in one father, according to the mind of **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundations of alchemy are attributed.</note>; or, as others wish, four bodies, looking toward the same number of elements. For from a **quadrangle** <note>a square</note> a **triangle** must be made, so that as the former was made from a **circle**, so this one shall return into a **circle**. 

There is, moreover, such a great consanguinity and natural conjunction of the bodies of Geryon, or of the elements, that if one is overcome or killed, the rest also collapse and putrefy of their own accord, without any manual force being applied. Concerning those with two bodies, it is well known that when one dies, the other also wastes away; just as we saw a **two-bodied boy** of four years of age in Italy, who hid the head of the brother attached to him inside his own body, while the rest of the parts hung down as far as the navel, where they were bound together or born-joined. If that one who hung down—and who, being much smaller, was carried by the other—were touched too roughly around the hands or feet, the larger boy felt the pain; indeed, he even felt hunger if the smaller one was emptied of food. 

And this is the combination and **sympathy** <note>A philosophical and medical term meaning "suffering together," where one part of a system reacts to what happens to another.</note> of nature, by which the members and parts of one body, or even one born joined to another, are affected and moved mutually within themselves. From these, if one part remains healthy and unharmed, the rest do not necessarily remain healthy or unharmed; but if one is gravely injured, the others also suffer and perish by the same evil. 

So it is that if a neighbor makes a great profit, no benefit reaches his neighbor from it; but if he suffers a fire, much evil follows. For *your own property is at stake when the neighbor's wall is on fire* <note>original: "Nam tua res agitur paries cùm proximus ardet." A famous Roman proverb from Horace (Epistles 1.18.84), warning that shared danger requires collective action.</note>. Therefore, it is not inconsistent with the truth that from the death of one of these four brothers, the other three also die, since this can happen in various ways: either because being produced from one birth and one father and mother, they experienced the same limits of life as they did beginnings (as we read has happened to some, perhaps from the inclination of the stars), or because they cohere not only in their minds but also in the limbs of the body, or of the mind—

<meta>catchword: conster-</meta>

<summary>Maier interprets the myth of the three-bodied giant Geryon as an alchemical allegory for the four elements, arguing that they are so interconnected that the destruction of one inevitably leads to the death of the others. He supports this with a contemporary account of conjoined twins in Italy and a classical proverb about a neighbor's house on fire.</summary>

<keywords>Geryon, four elements, alchemical allegory, Hermes Trismegistus, conjoined twins, physical sympathy, squaring the circle, medical observation</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "four brothers" (the elements) and their bound fate. It expands on the idea that in the Great Work, the destruction of one component necessitates the transformation of the others, drawing parallels from historical customs and classical mythology.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>

<header>DISCOURSE XIX.</header>

...by consternation, or by a powerful imagination during a pestilential plague, or by the vow of a covenant. In India, under the empire of that **Great Mughal** <note>Refers to the ruler of the Mughal Empire in India; at the time of writing, this likely refers to Jahangir, the descendant of Tamerlane.</note> (who now reigns there as the ninth descendant of **Tamerlane**), there lives a certain ethnic group called the **Pythagoreans** <note>The author likely identifies Hindus as "Pythagoreans" due to their shared belief in the transmigration of souls.</note>, among whom this custom has been observed from ancient times until now: if a husband dies, his wife is burned in the fire, or, as happens now, she lives forever in the deepest infamy, abandoned by all and regarded as if dead. This was done so that wives would not cut short the lives of their husbands with poisons, unless they themselves wished to die. 

So it is in the **Philosophical Work** <term>The Great Work</term> <note>original: "opere Philosophico," the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>: when one of the brothers has died, the others also perish by fire—not by force, but voluntarily—lest they live as survivors in sorrow and infamy. Or if one is attacked with a club, iron, or a stone, he will begin an internal war with his brothers, as is seen in the **Earth-born men** <note>The warriors who sprang from the dragon's teeth sown by Jason and Cadmus.</note> born from the dragon's teeth against **Jason**, and elsewhere also rising up against **Cadmus**, and thus they all fall by mutual slaughter. 

For if you touch the **Air-bearer** <note>original: "aërigerum," representing the element of Air.</note>, he will rise up against the two closest to him—namely the **Water-bearer** and the **Fire-bearer**—but these two on either side will oppose the **Earth-bearer** and the one who first incited the fight, until they have mutually given and received wounds by which they are extinguished. For it is so arranged with brothers that the more intensely and sharply they love one another, if once they begin to hate, they are irreconcilable and pursue each other to the death; just as from the sweetest honey, in a too-hot stomach or a corrupted liver, the most bitter bile is generated. 

Therefore, kill the living, but in such a way that you resuscitate the dead; otherwise, the death of the slain will have profited you nothing. For death will reveal him, namely when he rises again, and death, darkness, and the sea shall flee from him, as **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> testifies; and the **Dragon**, who used to watch the crevices, will flee the rays of the sun, and our dead son lives, and the King comes from the fire. 

<margin>Chapter 3.</margin>**Belinus** <note>Usually identified as Apollonius of Tyana in alchemical literature.</note> in his metaphor found in the **Rosarium** <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* or "Rose Garden of the Philosophers," a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> implies the same thing when he says: *“And let this be done when you have extracted me partly from my nature and my wife partly from her nature, and afterward you kill the natures; then we are raised in a new and incorporeal resurrection, for the reason that afterward we cannot die.”*

<summary>This page uses ethnographic accounts of India and Greek myths of dragon-teeth warriors to illustrate the alchemical necessity of "mutual destruction" among the elements. It concludes that this death is a precursor to a "new and incorporeal resurrection" where the matter becomes immortal and perfected.</summary>

### XX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The flame, which devours everything just like a dragon, did desire
To consume a maidenly image with the violence of its fire;
Therefore, she was seen wet with tears by a certain man,
Who showed her help with both counsel and deed as best he can;
As soon as he covered her beneath his shield, he awakened the enemy,
And taught her to thereafter despise his threats entirely.

<header>EMBLEM XX. On the secrets of Nature.</header>

## Nature teaches nature, so that she may overcome the fire.
<note>original: "Naturam natura docet, debellet ut ignem."</note>

### EPIGRAM XX.

**T**he flame, which devours all things like a **Dragon**, diligently pressed on
*To overcome the exceptional beauty of the **Maiden** by force;* <note>In alchemical symbolism, the "Maiden" often represents the pure, volatile substance of the Work, while the "Dragon" represents the corrosive acid or the destructive "Sulfur" of common fire.</note>
*Hence, while she happened to be seen by a man, drenched in tears,*
*He was ready to bring help to the wretched woman.*
*Immediately, covering her with his shield, he struggled against the enemy,*
*And taught her to despise such great threats in her mind.*

# DISCOURSE XX.

<meta>catchword: tuntur</meta>

It is a common motto of the Philosophers, by which they recognize one another, that **nature is led, taught, ruled, and overcome by nature**; <note>original: "naturam à natura duci, doceri, regi, ſuperari"</note> namely, the disciple by the teacher, the handmaid by the mistress, the subject by the queen, and indeed the daughter by the mother and a relative by a relative. How true this is appears from daily experience in the raising of offspring among humans and in other actions, such as literary instruction, governance, and the like. Pliny writes of **nightingales** that one trains another in singing, attending, observing, imitating, and either conquering or grieving in defeat; and that in a contest, the one who is surpassed occasionally dies from a ruptured throat and falls lifeless in the middle of her song. We see also how all kinds of birds begin to accustom and instruct their chicks, still featherless and tender, toward flight, so that in them not only nature but also art or habit might acquire the practice of flying—although nature alone gave the power and the organs to exercise that action, without which neither instruction nor art would find a foundation or place. Thus foals are instructed for the race by the mare, puppies for barking by the dog, and little foxes for tricks; nor is any nature or animated and sensitive species of nature found which does not lead, teach, and rule another nature—namely its offspring—or allow itself to be overcome by another—namely its parent.

In vegetables we do not notice instruction of this kind, yet practice and the hands of men are observed to be very effective in them; for while the harvest is still in the blade, it can be purged of useless thistles and darnel; while a tree is still a sapling, it can be bent and forced to grow according to one's pleasure. So in metals and the subjects of **Philosophy** <note>In this context, "Philosophy" refers specifically to Alchemy, the "Spagyric Art."</note>, one nature holds, preserves, and protects another in the fire; as is known to smelters and <term>assayers</term> <note>original: "docimaſtis"; specialists who test the purity of ores and metals.</note>, and especially to the masters of natural things. **Iron** greatly assists **silver** and **gold** when they are still tender and "spiritual," as they say, mixed in their ores with <term>Cadmia</term> <note>A term used historically for various zinc-bearing ores or cobalt crusts.</note>, arsenic, or predatory antimony, and it offers the "hand of a midwife" if it is cast into the furnaces for the roasting of ores. In the same way, iron itself, if it must be changed into **steel**, is saved from being burned by white stones found at the seashore. Some, when melting metallic powders, throw over them powders of crystalline glass or **glass-gall** <note>original: "fel vitri"; the scum or dross that forms on the surface of molten glass.</note> so they do not perish by excessive fire. In place of this, the Philosopher uses his **Eudica** <note>A coded alchemical term, likely referring to a specific alkaline salt or "the good way" of tempering the heat.</note> u...

<summary>Maier explains the alchemical axiom that "nature teaches nature" by drawing parallels with animals raising their young and the use of iron or salts to protect precious metals during smelting.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Pliny, nightingales, metallurgy, iron, gold, silver, Eudica, alchemy, education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the prose commentary for Discourse XX. The previous page discussed how nature instructs nature, using birds and animals as examples of parents teaching their young. This page transitions those biological observations into the realm of alchemy and the "Philosophical Work," using mythological and physiological analogies.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XX.

<sig>M ij</sig>

...observed, as Morienus says it is also the "gall of glass" <note>original: *fel vitri*; an alchemical term for the dross or scum that forms on molten glass, used here as a metaphor for a protective substance.</note> and must be sought in glass vessels. For the heat of fire consumes the body itself by rapid combustion. But if **Eudica** <term>Eudica: A term from the alchemist Morienus, often interpreted as a salt or "feces of glass" that acts as a flux or protective agent.</term> is added to it, she will care for those same bodies—once they have been changed into earth—against all combustion. For after bodies no longer retain their souls, they are quickly burned. Eudica (the dregs of glass) indeed suits all bodies well; for she vivifies and prepares them, and defends them from all burning. Thus says Morienus the Roman. <note>Morienus was a legendary 7th-century hermit-alchemist said to have taught the art to the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>

This, therefore, is the nature which teaches another nature to fight against fire and to become accustomed to it; she is the teacher instructing the disciple, and if you look closely, she is the queen ruling the subject, and the daughter ennobling the mother. This is that **red servant** <note>The *servus rubeus* is a common alchemical symbol for sulfur or the active, masculine principle in the "Great Work."</note> who is joined in marriage to his fragrant mother, and from her begets an offspring far nobler than his parents. This is **Pyrrhus**, the son of Achilles, a youth of red hair, golden clothing, black eyes, and white feet. This is the **knighted horseman** armed with sword and shield against the dragon, so that he may rescue from its jaws the inviolate virgin, **Albifica**, also known as **Beya** or **Blanca**. <note>Maier links the image of the knight on page 89 to these figures. "Beya" and "Gabritius" are famous allegorical siblings/lovers in alchemical texts representing white and red, or mercury and sulfur.</note> This is **Hercules**, the monster-slayer, who freed **Hesione**, daughter of Laomedon, from the beast after she was exposed to a monstrous sea-creature. This is that **Perseus**, who defended **Andromeda** (daughter of Cassiopeia and Cepheus, King of the Ethiopians) from a sea monster by showing the head of Medusa, and having snatched her from her chains, took her as his wife. This is he who can be compared to those ancient Roman liberators and purifiers: **Marcus Curtius, Lucius Scaevola, Horatius Cocles, Manlius Capitolinus**, and those like them, who rescued the city, as if it were their mother, from dangers.

For this is the reason and the way of a nature tending toward the perfection of some work, so that from one thing it may lead out another—the more perfect from the imperfect—and turn potentiality into action, completing everything not in a moment, but one after the other. Nor is this all, but she first appoints a **Vicar** for herself, to whom she leaves the power of life and death—that is, the power of forming other things. For example, in the generation of a human being, she uses a long process of ten months, where first, according to the mind of **Aristotle**, she fashions the **heart** as her deputy and principal organ; the heart then outlines, shapes, and perfects the remaining limbs necessary for nutrition, life, sensation, and generative power, imparting to them life and vivifying spirits through **systole** and **diastole** <note>The rhythmic contraction and expansion of the heart and arteries.</note>, or the dilation and compression of the arteries, as long as it is not hindered by disease or violence. And thus one nature teaches another, which you shall note and follow as a most clear example for the Philosophical Work.

<meta>catchword: Mache (Macherie/Materia)</meta>

<summary>Maier interprets the "nature teaching nature" principle through alchemical metaphors (Eudica and the Red Servant) and classical hero myths (Perseus, Hercules). He concludes with a physiological analogy, arguing that nature works through a "Vicar"—the heart—to develop the rest of the body over time.</summary>
<keywords>Morienus, Eudica, Red Servant, Pyrrhus, Albifica, Perseus and Andromeda, Hercules, Roman heroes, Aristotle, Heart, Systole and Diastole, Alchemical Allegory</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page provides the prose discourse for Emblem XXI, following the musical score and the visual depiction of the "Squaring of the Circle." It moves from the symbolic image of the man, woman, and geometric shapes into a philosophical debate regarding innate knowledge and the origins of science, specifically addressing the Platonic and Aristotelian views of education.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

<header>DISCOURSE XXI.</header>

Plato, that most celebrated philosopher, taught that the foundations of all arts and sciences—the concepts of human knowledge—are, as it were, engraved and imprinted upon the mind in actuality. He believed that through remembrance <note>original: "rememoratione"; referring to Plato's doctrine of *anamnesis*, or the idea that the soul remembers knowledge it had before birth.</note> and repetition, anyone could grasp and understand all doctrines. To prove this, he introduces a boy still of a tender age, unrefined and uneducated in letters, with whom he conducts a geometric interrogation. He does this in such a way that the boy is observed to respond correctly to all questions, and—whether willingly or unwillingly, or even unknowingly—to have reached the innermost chambers of such a difficult science. 

From this, Plato concluded that all doctrine and learning are not learned or drawn out from the beginning, but are at least recalled to the mind and revolved by children through recollection. He played this idea against his theory of the <term>Great Year</term> <note>A cosmic cycle after which the stars and planets return to their original positions; here cited as 48,000 years.</note>, in which he said that forty-eight thousand solar years ago, the same persons, things, and actions existed before the revolution of the heavens in the same way they do now. But everyone understands that these ideas are very much like dreams, without any foundation in truth.

We do not deny that certain <term>sparks of knowledge</term> <note>original: "scintillas noticiarum"</note>—mere potentials—are implanted in us, to be brought into action through practice and instruction. However, we refuse to believe that these sparks are so great or of such a nature that they serve as the seeds of arts and sciences without any previous cultivation. Others may ask: where did the sciences and arts come from if men did not invent them? Were they first handed down from heaven or by the gods of the nations? 

I respond: it is one thing to say that burning coals are covered by ashes in such abundance that if the ashes are merely removed, they suffice for cooking food or warming our cold limbs. It is quite another to affirm that only tiny sparks lie hidden there, which—before they are useful for cooking or warming—must be fostered, cultivated, and increased with their own fuel by human industry, art, and care; otherwise, they can easily be extinguished and reduced entirely to cold ashes. 

The <term>Aristotelians</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle, who believed the mind is a *tabula rasa* or blank slate at birth.</note> assert the latter; the <term>Platonists</term> <note>Followers of Plato, who believed in innate ideas.</note> assert the former. But reason and experience support the latter view, while only fantasy or imagination supports the former. Hence it could be asked: why did Plato inscribe on the door of his school that one unskilled in <term>Geometry</term> <note>original: "Geometriae"</note> is not admitted there, when he also claimed that even little boys actually know it? Are adult men perhaps more ignorant than children? Or have those of older age committed to oblivion the things that children know?

<summary>In this discourse, the author critiques Plato’s theory of innate knowledge (anamnesis), arguing instead that while humans have a "spark" of potential, arts and sciences like geometry must be actively cultivated through effort and instruction.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, Aristotle, geometry, innate knowledge, Great Year, education, anamnesis, philosophy of science</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument against Plato's theory of innate knowledge (recollection). The text transitions from a critique of abstract geometry to an explanation of "Physical Squaring"—the alchemical process of transforming matter through various geometric stages and colors to reach the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>

### DISCOURSE XXI.

...learned? <note>This completes the word "sciverunt" from the previous page: "did they know?"</note> That should not be believed; for we see that a brute animal, taught by nature, abhors and avoids dangers from fire, water, steep falls, and the like, even when recently born. Yet a human infant does not know or avoid such things unless they have been hurt, or like a moth <note>original: "pyraustæ"; a mythical insect supposed to live in fire, often used as a metaphor for a creature drawn to its own destruction.</note> that burns its wings in a candle flame and falls, they have slightly scorched a finger. Why do the little bee, the fly, and the gnat not throw themselves into the fire with their swift flight, even though they do not know from experience that danger will arise from it? Because nature taught them, but not the newborn human.

If *Geometry* is so natural and easy for boys, how does it happen that the squaring of the circle was not known to *Plato* himself? Indeed, *Aristotle*, *Plato’s* disciple, affirmed that it was "knowable," but not yet "known." However, it appears that this was not unknown to the *Natural Philosophers* <note>In this context, Maier is referring to alchemists, who called themselves natural philosophers.</note>, because they command that a circle be converted into a square, and this, through a triangle, back into a circle. 

By the circle, they understand the simplest body without angles; by the square, they understand the four *Elements*. It is as if they were saying that a corporeal figure of the simplest form that can be found should be taken, and it should be divided into the four Elemental colors, which will become an equilateral square. Anyone can understand that this *Squaring* is physical and agreeable to nature. From this, far more utility comes to the State, and more enlightenment to the human mind, than from that mathematical version which is merely theoretical or abstracted from matter. 

To learn the latter, the *Geometer* dealing with solid bodies must investigate how the depth of solid figures—for example, the *Sphere* and the *Cube*—may be recognized and transferred to manual use or practice. For instance: if the capacity or circumference of a *sphere* is 32 feet, how long will one of the *cube’s* sides be so that it equals the capacity of this *sphere*? Conversely, if a *sphere* holds 32 measures in such a circumference, how long will one side of the *cube* be so that it holds the same amount, whether looking at the measures the *sphere* or *cube* can hold, or at the feet of each circumference? 

Similarly, the *Philosophers* want the square to be led into a triangle; that is, into body, spirit, and soul. These three appear in the three colors that precede the final redness: namely, the body or earth in *Saturn's blackness* <note>original: "Saturni nigredine"; the alchemical stage of Nigredo.</note>, the spirit in the *lunar whiteness* <note>original: "lunari albedine"; the stage of Albedo, associated with water.</note>, and the soul or air in the *solar yellowness* <note>original: "solari citrinitate"; the stage of Citrinitas.</note>. Then the *Triangle* will be perfect, but this in turn must be changed into a circle—that is, into invariable redness <note>The Rubedo, or the final red stage of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. By this operation, the female is converted into the male and made one with him. Thus, the number six, the first of the perfect numbers, is completed through one and two, and returns again to the *monad* <note>The "One" or the point of unity.</note>, in which there is eternal rest and peace.

<summary>Maier argues that while mathematical squaring of the circle is theoretical, the alchemical ("physical") squaring is a practical process of transforming the elements through specific color stages—black, white, and yellow—to reach the red "monad" of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<keywords>squaring the circle, alchemy, Plato, Aristotle, four elements, Nigredo, Albedo, Citrinitas, Rubedo, geometry, monad</keywords>

### XXII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

<margin>(Against</margin> <note>The marginal fragment "gen" likely refers to "gegen" or "against," possibly indicating a counter-point or a specific instruction.</note>
You who desire to accomplish much with little effort,
Should sprinkle a portion of snow into Saturn's face (which is so black)
And there will be for you a lead of very white clarity,
After which nothing remains but only woman's work; <note>term: "Woman’s work" (Latin: *opus mulierum*) refers to the alchemical process of decoction or gentle boiling, which was considered simple and requiring only patience, much like tending a kitchen hearth.</note>
Then cook, as a woman does when she lets her pots stand on the fire,
But let you the "beloved" <note>original: "Trawt"; likely referring to the "trawte" or the dear substance/mercury being worked upon.</note> melt away in her own water.

<meta>EMBLEM...</meta>

<summary>Emblem XXII introduces the "Work of Women," an alchemical metaphor for the gentle heating (decoction) required to transform the blackness of lead (Saturn) into a white, purified state. The text and music together emphasize that once the initial "snow" (whitening agent) is applied, the rest of the process requires simple patience rather than strenuous labor.</summary>

<header>EMBLEM XXII. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>
