### Having obtained the white lead, perform the work of women, that is, COOK:

<image-desc>A hand-colored woodcut illustration of a domestic or alchemical interior. On the left, a large open hearth contains a roaring fire with white smoke billowing into a chimney hood; one black pot hangs over the fire from a chain, while another sits among the logs. In the center, a woman dressed in a long red gown and a white apron stands looking down at a wooden workspace where a pair of bellows lies. Beneath this bench and on the floor in the foreground are several rectangular grey-blue slabs, representing lead. To the right, a window shows a view of a rural landscape. In the corner of the room, there is a bucket, a low bench, and a wooden tub filled with two fish. A cat sleeps curled on the floor near the tub. A red circular library stamp is visible on the hearth and another is stamped over the right side of the epigram text below.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAMMA XXII.

*Whoever you are who loves to achieve much with easy labor,*
*Scatter snow upon the face of <term>Saturn</term><note>In alchemy, Saturn represents both the planet and the metal lead, as well as the initial, dark stage of the "Great Work" called the nigredo.</note> (which is black):*
*And you will be given a material of the whitest lead,*
*After which, nothing remains except the <term>work of women</term><note>original: "fœmineum... opus". This refers to the second stage of alchemy, the Albedo (whitening), which was said to be "child's play and women's work" because it required only gentle, steady heat—like cooking or washing—rather than the intense fires of the earlier stages.</note>.*
*Then **COOK**, just like a woman who places her pots upon the fires,*
*But see to it that the <term>trout</term><note>original: "TRVTA". The fish is a common symbol for the "mercury of the philosophers" or the evolving substance of the Stone that must "melt" or dissolve in its own internal moisture.</note> melts in its own waters.*

<summary>This emblem describes the alchemical transition from the "black" stage of lead (Saturn) to the "white" stage (Albedo). It uses the domestic metaphor of a woman cooking to explain that once the material is purified, the remaining process requires only gentle, patient heat.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, lead, work of women, cooking, Albedo, trout, purification, domestic alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues from the illustration and epigram of Emblem XXII, which introduced the concept of the "work of women" (cooking) and the whitening of lead. This "Discourse" provides the philosophical and mythological explanation for those symbols.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXII.</header>

JUST AS statues of <term>Mercury</term><note>Statues of the god Mercury, or "herms," were often used as boundary markers or signposts at crossroads in the ancient world.</note> are usually placed at crossroads together with a pointer and an inscription to lead uncertain travelers onto the right path, so too certain sharp opinions are scattered here and there by Philosophers in their allegorical and obscure books and volumes. These are placed so that the seeker of truth may be warned by them and led, as if by the hand, onto the true path. Among these is this emblematic inscription, the meaning of which is held to be this: that from "philosophical bronze" one must make <term>lead</term><note>Representing the heavy, dark, initial state of matter in the alchemical process.</note>, and from lead, <term>tin</term><note>Associated with the planet Jupiter and the "whitening" of the work.</note>. This tin is called "white lead" by <term>Geber</term><note>A reference to Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a foundational figure in alchemy; his Latinized works were central to European alchemy.</note>, who also teaches the method by which <term>Saturn</term> (lead) migrates into <term>Jupiter</term> (tin) through a washing with <term>Mercury</term>. Therefore, one must trust this guide—as one would trust <term>Battus</term><note>original: "Battologo." In mythology, Battus was a shepherd who witnessed Mercury stealing Apollo's cattle; he was turned to stone for his betrayal. Here, the author suggests we must listen to the "pointer" that reveals the secret location of the materials.</note>—whenever he betrays the location of the "Philosophical Oxen." "While they were on the mountains," he says, "they were also under those mountains." For many, as <term>Arnaldus</term><note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist. The work cited is "The New Light" (Novum Lumen).</note> testifies in *The New Light, chapter 1*, wander in the mountains and yet do not recognize these animals, even though they are sold openly at the lowest price.

On the highest mountains, even in summer, snow is sometimes observed, and clouds very frequently; by these, as if by vapor and water, the "black lead" is washed and turned into whiteness. In the deepest valleys and mines, however, their crystals are found, frozen and hardened from ice, together with <term>lapis specularis</term><note>Transparent stone or mica, often used as a window material in antiquity.</note> and <term>talc</term>, which is commended for the whiteness and beauty of the face if an oil is made from it. But above all, there is the clear and running <term>Mercury</term>, which, when well prepared, corrects the blemishes of Saturn and elevates it into the "leaf of Jupiter." 

However, <term>Saturn</term> and <term>Jupiter</term> should not be understood as the common metals, because common metals do not enter into the <term>physical work</term><note>The "physical work" or "opus physicum" refers to the actual alchemical process of transformation, as opposed to mere chemistry.</note>; rather, they refer to metals purified by long preparation and made "physical." Saturn is the father of all the "Golden Ones"—or rather, the "Aureoles"—and the first gate of the secrets. "With this," says <term>Rhazes</term><note>Rhazes (865–925) was a Persian physician and alchemist; the "Epistle" refers to his influential alchemical writings.</note> in his *Epistle*, "the gates of the sciences are opened." He is succeeded by his son, <term>Jupiter</term>, who drove his father from the kingdom and castrated him, specifically so that he would not generate more sons. From the virile member cast into the sea, <term>Venus</term>, the most beautiful of women, was born. From Jupiter—who is the prepared "white lead"—the remaining planets were born: <term>Mars</term> from <term>Juno</term>, <term>Mercury</term> from <term>Maia</term> (the daughter of Atlas of the mountain of Mauretania), and the <term>Moon</term> and <term>Sun</term> from <term>Latona</term>. These four are brought into the light solely by "coction," or the "work of women."

<meta>catchword: pro-</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical transition from the "black" stage (Saturn/lead) to the "white" stage (Jupiter/tin) using meteorological metaphors and the mythological narrative of Jupiter overthrowing Saturn. It emphasizes that the metals used are "philosophical" rather than common commercial metals.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Jupiter, Geber, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Rhazes, white lead, work of women, alchemical transition, Mercury, philosophical bronze</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXII, moving from the mythological "theft of the oxen" and the chemical transmutation of metals into the specific method of heating. It explains the "Work of Women" metaphor—cooking—as a sophisticated alchemical process of maturation using vapor rather than direct contact with liquid.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXII.</header>

...come forth. Indeed, by "cooking," we understand maturation and the dispersal of the cruder parts, which is achieved through <term>Vulcan</term><note>The Roman god of fire; here used to represent heat or the alchemical furnace.</note> in the vessels of <term>Philosophy</term><note>A common term for the alchemical art itself.</note>. For it must not be thought that this is common cooking as far as the method of operation is concerned, but it agrees with it in its purpose. For just as a woman matures fish in water—that is, she softens, boils, and cooks them by resolving all their superfluous moisture into the water and the air—so too does the Philosopher treat his subject matter. He treats it in its own "water"—which is stronger than the sharpest vinegar—by soaking, melting, dissolving, coagulating, and fixing it in the <term>vessel of Hermes</term><note>The hermetically sealed flask or "philosopher's egg" used in alchemical experiments, named after Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, the joints of which should be most tightly closed, as is proper, so that the water does not evaporate and that which <gloss>that which</gloss> is in the vessel is not burnt.

This is the "vessel upon a vessel" and the "Philosopher's pot," a <term>Laconic bath</term><note>A dry, hot sweat bath or sauna, named after Laconia (Sparta). In alchemy, it refers to a specific heating method using steam or indirect heat, like a sand bath or vapor bath.</note> in which the old man sweats. There are those who boil fish, crayfish, crabs, or fresh peas in a double pot, such that the items mentioned are in the upper pot and only water in the lower, with the pots arranged one above the other with rings so that no vapor escapes. By this method, only the rising water vapor penetrates the contents and matures them, making them tender and soft much more perfectly than if they had boiled directly in the water. 

This is the most praised method of the Philosophers, by which they soften what is hard, dissolve what is compact, and rarefy what is dense. For it is air, or an imperceptible vapor, that matures, cooks, and perfects fruit on trees, not raw and cold water as such. It is also air that tints and colors the <term>golden apples</term><note>A reference to the Garden of the Hesperides in Greek mythology; in alchemy, these symbolize the final, perfected stage of the Work (the Gold).</note> in the gardens of the <term>Hesperides</term>. For, if well considered, the boiling of water—by which raw meats are cooked until they are fit to eat—is nothing other than the rarefaction of water and its transmutation into aerial vapor; since the bubbles are air contained within the water, which easily vanish as the air moves from the waters into its own sphere and the water settles back into its center.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical concept of "cooking" (coctio) as a process of maturation through indirect heat and vapor. It draws a parallel between domestic cooking—specifically steaming fish or vegetables—and the "Work of Women" in the laboratory to soften and refine the Matter.</summary>
<keywords>cooking, maturation, Vulcan, Hermes' vessel, vapor, Laconic bath, Hesperides, transmutation, alchemy</keywords>

### XXIII. English translation of the German version of the Latin Epigram.

It is a wondrous thing that the Greeks teach us as truth,
Which was famous in Rhodes in ancient times;
They testify that a fruitful rain of gold fell,
When the <term>Sun</term><note>In alchemy, the Sun (Sol) represents Gold or the active, masculine principle.</note> lay with <term>Venus</term><note>Representing Copper or the passive, feminine principle; their union symbolizes the "Chymical Wedding."</note> in the heat of love;
Just as <term>Pallas</term><note>Another name for Athena, the goddess of wisdom, who was born fully armed from the head of Zeus.</note> ascended out of <term>Jove's</term><note>Jupiter/Zeus.</note> brain,
So must gold also appear like rain within your vessel.

## EPIGRAM XXIII.

The matter is wondrous, but Greece has given us faith in it <note>original: "Res est mira, fidem fecit sed Græcia nobis"</note>,
That which was celebrated among the inhabitants of <term>Rhodes</term><note>An island in the Aegean, legendary home of the Colossus and sacred to the Sun god, Helios.</note>.
They report that a golden rain fell from the clouds,
When the <term>Sun</term> was joined in love with the <term>Cyprian Goddess</term><note>Venus, the goddess of love, often named for her association with the island of Cyprus.</note>:
Also then, when <term>Pallas</term><note>Athena, the goddess of wisdom and strategic war, who represents the "fixed" or perfected stone in alchemy.</note> emerged from the brain of <term>Jupiter</term>,
So let gold fall from its vessel like rain water.

<sig>N 3</sig>
<meta>Catchword: GOLD</meta>

<summary>This page describes a mythological allegory where a "rain of gold" occurs during the birth of Pallas Athena and the union of Sol and Venus. In alchemical terms, these events symbolize the purification of matter and the emergence of the "Golden" state of wisdom from the "brain" or highest part of the work.</summary>

<keywords>Pallas Athena, Rhodes, Jupiter, Sun and Venus, golden rain, alchemical marriage, birth of wisdom, Sol, Venus, Vulcan</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page contains the "Discourse" (prose commentary) for Emblem XXIII. It expands upon the mythological imagery of the previous page—the birth of Pallas and the union of Sol and Venus—by providing a natural-philosophical and alchemical interpretation of the "golden rain" at Rhodes.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXIII.</header>

To assert that gold once rained upon the lands, unless it is understood allegorically <note>original: "allegoricè"</note>, would be madness. For there are no gold-bearing rivers or mines of golden minerals in the clouds, such that it could be said to be generated there; nor is gold of such a slight weight that it might be thought to be drawn up with the vapors. But a trope <note>a figure of speech or metaphor</note> admits and excuses all these things. For just as truly as <term>Pallas</term> was born from the brain of <term>Jupiter</term> and the <term>Sun</term> was joined in adultery with <term>Venus</term>, just as truly did a golden rain fall; not that we should doubt that either occurred, but rather that we should remove the common and literal sense from these allegorical sayings. For if we follow the naked words of this Emblem, nothing is more absurd; but if we follow the mind <note>the underlying meaning or intent</note>, nothing exists more true.

Now, <term>Rhodes</term> is an island first called *Ophiusa* because of its multitude of serpents; later it was called *Rhodes* from the rosebushes blooming there, and finally *Colossicola* from the <term>Colossus of the Sun</term>, which stood there and was numbered among the seven wonders of the world. From this, the ancient Philosophers took certain similarities to the island of Rhodes: for their <term>mercurial matter</term>, when crude, behaves like a serpent <note>representing the volatile, "unfixed" state of the matter</note>, but afterward, when prepared and cooked, it assumes the purple color of a Rose. This is also the same reason why they ascribed a golden shower to it when the Sun (or Apollo) lay with Venus. This, originally spoken metaphorically, provided the occasion for the Rhodians—becoming more arrogant because such great gods had labored to produce children among them—to erect an idol of the Sun of astounding size and expense.

For that Colossus was, as the histories relate, seventy cubits in height and so positioned that ships with sails spread could pass between its legs. Its fingers equaled common statues in size, and few could wrap their arms around its thumb. The craftsman was **Chares of Lindos**, a disciple of **Lysippus**, by whom it was made over twelve years. After fifty-six years, it was thrown down by an earthquake, yet even lying on the ground it was a marvel. When the **Sultan of Egypt** occupied Rhodes, he is said to have loaded nine00 camels with the bronze from this statue.

What the Sun is among the planets, says the Philosopher, so is gold among the metals; and this is especially appropriated to the sun by its heat, color, virtue, and essence. Hence, a golden rain is owed to the generation of the sun, when "little suns" are conceived by Venus. For Venus has a rosy color in her face, which, if it be infused into the seed of the Sun, the off-

<meta>catchword: spring (Latin: sobolem)</meta>

<summary>Michael Maier explains that the "golden rain" at Rhodes is not a literal weather event but an alchemical allegory for the perfection of matter, transitioning from the "serpent" state to the "rose" state through the union of solar and venereal principles.</summary>
<keywords>Rhodes, golden rain, alchemy, Sol and Venus, Pallas Athena, Colossus of Rhodes, Chares of Lindos, mercurial matter, allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the birth of Pallas (Athena) and the golden rain at Rhodes, shifting from the general allegory to a specific alchemical interpretation of the "Son of the Philosophers." It links various myths—Hercules, Jason, and Perseus—to the transformative power of the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
### DISCOURSE XXIII.

Now truly, Rhodes must be born from this source: Here is the beautiful one, like a rose, the **Son of the Philosophers** <term>filius philosophorum; a traditional name for the Philosopher's Stone in its perfected state</term>, who draws and attracts the eyes and minds of all to himself, and deserves love. When he is born, it is no wonder if miracles happen: For he himself will be miraculous in all his deeds and is destined to call forth a golden rain. This is the brother of **Augeas**, son of the **Sun**, who possessed cattle as his inheritance, whose dung-filled stables **Hercules** purged in a single day <note>This refers to the Fifth Labor of Hercules; in alchemy, this "cleansing" symbolizes the rapid purification of the base matter</note>. He is also the brother of **Aeëtes**, who possessed the **Golden Fleece** seized by **Jason**. Regarding **Pallas** coming forth from the brain of **Jupiter** without a mother, they report that she was produced near the river **Triton**, and was therefore called **Tritonia**. She is imagined as the Goddess presiding over Wisdom, who, being born from the brain—the seat of wisdom—is not without merit so called. A golden rain also decorated her birth at Rhodes, so that it might remain in the memory of men when she came into this light. In times of public joy or festivals—such as the coronation of a King or the birth of a royal heir—golden coins are sometimes thrown among the common people like a golden rain. So it happened at the emergence of Pallas. For Pallas is wisdom or *Sophia* <note>original: "sophia"</note>, who carries health in her right hand and riches in her left, and looks out for the salvation and benefit of men. To her, **Perseus** brought the stone-making <note>original: "saxificum," meaning petrifying or turning things to stone</note> head of **Medusa**, horrific with its hair of serpents and vipers; she used this on her shield against her enemies—uncultivated and rude peoples—by changing them into stone. And truly wisdom, or natural Philosophy, renders those incredulous haters and envious ones stupid <note>In the sense of being "stunned" or "paralyzed" by wonder</note> and void of sense and understanding through that very thing from which **Chrysaor** was born (who was the father of the previously mentioned three-bodied **Geryon**); that is, the petrifying **Gorgon's blood**, which is nothing other than the **Tincture of the philosopher's stone** <term>Tinctura lapidis philoſophici; the substance capable of transmuting base metals and "fixing" the volatile</term>.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This discourse identifies the "Son of the Philosophers" with the island of Rhodes and the goddess Pallas, interpreting the myth of the golden rain as the manifestation of the Philosopher's Stone. It links Medusa’s petrifying head to the alchemical process of "fixation," where the Stone renders its observers—and its materials—solid and permanent.</summary>
<keywords>Son of the Philosophers, Pallas Athena, Golden Rain, Rhodes, Medusa, Perseus, Tincture, Fixation, Alchemical Allegory</keywords>

## The Wolf has devoured the King, and how, having been burned, he was restored.

### XXIV. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Take heed to catch the greedy wolf, and let this be your command,
And throw the King’s body before him, so that he may swallow it down;
Lay him upon the wood, and let **Vulcan** <note>The Roman god of fire; in alchemical texts, "Vulcan" refers to the heat of the furnace or the transformative power of fire</note> kindle the flames,
So that this same monstrous beast may be consumed by the fire;
Do this repeatedly, and the King shall rise again from death,
And step forth proud and bold, with the heart of a **Lion** <term>Leo; in this context, the "Lion" symbolizes gold that has been purified and "fixed," meaning it can no longer be destroyed by fire</term>.

Through the death and "burning" of the wolf, the King (gold) is reborn in a more perfected, "lion-hearted" state.</summary>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>EMBLEM XXIV. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>

### The wolf devoured the King, and once burned, restored him to life.

## EPIGRAM XXIV.

*Let it be your concern to capture the voracious wolf* <note>original: "Multivorum captare lupum"</note>,
->*Throwing the body of the King before him, so that he might satisfy*<-
*His gluttony; then place him upon a funeral pyre, where Vulcan* <note>The Roman god of fire; here representing the alchemical furnace.</note> *kindles*
->*The flame, by which the beast may be reduced to ashes.*<-
*Do this again and again; thus from death shall rise*
->*The King, and he will be proud with the heart of a Lion.*<- <term>Leonino corde; the "Lion" represents the fixed, solar nature of purified gold, which remains after the volatile "wolf" is burned away.</term>

<meta>catchword: HOW GREAT [original: QUANTA]</meta>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page provides the prose discourse for Emblem XXIV. Following the visual and poetic representation of the King being devoured by the Wolf (the purification of gold via antimony), this text elaborates on the natural history of the wolf, its mythological associations, and how alchemical "death" differs from the funerary rites of ancient cultures by offering the hope of rebirth.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXIV.</header>

**H**ow great the hunger and voracity of the wolf is, is commonly known: for where food is lacking, in extreme hunger it feeds upon the earth; it is also said to fill its belly with earth when it is about to attack large herds, so that, having become heavier by that "ballast" <note>original: "farcinâ"; literally a stuffing or packing</note>, it is not so easily shaken off, but resists more strongly. When it enters the sheepfolds, it does not only kill what is enough for its stomach, but also slaughters the entire flock everywhere out of greed. It is sacred to *Apollo* and *Latona*, for it was present at her labor: for *Latona* could not have given birth without the presence of a wolf. For this reason, it is believed the wolf is quite rightly accepted by *Apollo*, whose birth it celebrated; furthermore, because its eyes shine at night and dart out light. Therefore, the lifeless Royal body <term>Regium corpus; in alchemy, this represents gold in its impure or "dead" state before being processed.</term> is cast before this beast, beset by immense hunger—not so that the wolf might consume it entirely and reduce it to nothing, but so that it might restore life and strength to the King by its own death. For there is a certain amatory power in a wolf's tail which is infused into the half-dead king, whereby he becomes most pleasing to all men as he recovers his health and former beauty. 

The *Hyrcanians* <note>An ancient people living near the Caspian Sea.</note> raised dogs for no other use than to cast those who had finished their lives to them to be devoured, as *Cicero* records <note>Referring to Cicero’s *Tusculan Disputations* 1.102.</note>: thus also the *Massagetae* give those who have died of disease to dogs as prey. But the Philosophers give their King to the wolf. Nor, indeed, did the custom of the *Sabaeans* please them, who treated the bodies of the dead in the same way as dung, and who even threw their kings among the dunghills; nor that of the *Troglodytes*, who bound the neck of the deceased to the feet and carried them out hastily with jokes and laughter, and, having no regard for the place, committed them to the earth. Instead, they preferred to follow the custom of the *Magi*, who did not bury the bodies of their own people unless they were first torn by wild beasts; or that of the *Indians*, who ordered themselves to be burned alive while crowned and singing the praises of the gods, lest old age should overtake them. 

Yet, for all those peoples, a final fate imposed this custom without any hope of resurrection or renewal of life. It is arranged far differently among the *Philosophers*: for they knew with the greatest certainty that from the dead king devoured by the wolf, a living, strong, and young king would appear, and the wolf would be burned in his place by fire. For the wolf is easily killed if it has so burdened its belly; the King, however, although dead, nevertheless possesses a Martial or Cygnean strength <note>original: "Martiali vel Cygnea vi"; "Martial" refers to Iron (Mars) used in the process, and "Cygnean" (Swan-like) refers to the white stage of the alchemical work.</note>, so that he cannot be wounded nor consumed. But where must this wolf be hunted, and from whence must the King be received? The *Philosophers* answer:

<meta>catchword: Philosophers [original: Philof.]</meta>

<summary>Maier compares the alchemical process of purifying gold with antimony to various ancient funerary rites, emphasizing that the 'death' of the King in the wolf's belly is a unique path to rejuvenation and indestructible strength.</summary>
<keywords>antimony, gold purification, wolf, Apollo, Latona, funerary customs, resurrection, alchemical rebirth, Cicero, Magi</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXIV, detailing the alchemical allegory of the King (Gold) being devoured by the Wolf (Antimony) to achieve a state of higher purity and power.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXIV.</header>

The Philosopher says that everywhere in mountains and valleys the wolf wanders to seize prey, who must be dragged from his caves and kept for use. But the King, coming from the east, fatigued by the weariness of a long journey, finally falls down; grief also hastens his death, because he sees that among foreigners he is held in no honor and valued little, so that he is sold into slavery for a few gold pieces. It is necessary, however, for the wolf to be taken from a colder region: for those born in a cold zone are fiercer than those in Africa or Egypt, because of the greater hunger arising from the external cold. The King, having been devoured here, revives with a lion’s heart; he who can then tame all wild beasts. And although he is the vilest in appearance among his six brothers <note>The "six brothers" are the other metals known to alchemy: Silver, Mercury, Copper, Iron, Tin, and Lead. Gold is the "youngest" because it is the final perfection of the work.</note>, being the youngest of all, yet after many tribulations and miseries he will finally arrive at the most powerful kingdom. Hence Gratian says in the *Rosarium* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (Rosary of the Philosophers), a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>: *“In Alchemy,” he says, “there is a certain noble body which is moved from lord to lord, in whose beginning there will be misery with vinegar, but in the end joy with gladness.”* And Alan <note>Alanus ab Insulis, a 12th-century theologian and alchemist.</note> in the same place: *“One thing is to be chosen from all, which is of a leaden color, having a clear and liquid metallic appearance; it is a hot and moist thing, watery and burning, and it is living oil and living tincture, a mineral stone and water of life of wondrous efficacy.”* For kings to travel outside the borders of their kingdoms is not always safe; for if they are recognized while wishing to remain hidden, they are held as traitors by their adversaries and imprisoned; if they proceed recognized without an army, they are endangered in the same way. So it happened to this Indian King, or would have happened had death not intervened. This is the first <term>sublimation; the process of vaporizing and re-solidifying a substance to purify it</term>, washing, and <term>ennobling; the process of increasing the purity or "perfection" of a metal</term> which the Philosophers use so that the second and third stages may occur more happily. For those are of no importance without this one, while the king is still faint-hearted, sleepy, and sick. For he must first demand tax and tribute from his subjects, from which he may prepare clothing and other necessities for himself, and afterward he will be rich enough to be able to bestow new garments on all his subjects as often as he wishes. For great things, having mostly risen from small beginnings, can then support small things, or even support great ones if they wish. Let cities be an example, which were small at the beginning, but later ruled over great kings and made spacious and populous towns out of villages.

<sig>D ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This text explains the alchemical process of purifying gold (the King) using antimony (the Wolf), framing it as a journey of suffering and rebirth. It emphasizes that the initial "death" or degradation of the metal is a necessary step toward achieving the "lion's heart" and supreme power of the completed Stone.</summary>

<keywords>antimony, gold, sublimation, purification, Rosarium Philosophorum, Alanus ab Insulis, transformation, Indian King</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the discourse on the King and the Wolf in Emblem XXIV to the musical and poetic presentation of Emblem XXV. It focuses on the symbolic death of the Dragon, a common alchemical trope for the stabilization of volatile substances.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>FUGUE XXV. at the 5th or 12th below:</header>

### The dragon does not die, unless he be killed by his brother and sister, who are the Sun and Moon.

### XXV. German version of the Latin Epigram.

It is no matter of small skill to overcome the dragon,
So that he does not crawl again and return to life;
His brother and sister together smash his head
With clubs; no one else dares to try to kill him.
Apollo is the brother, and Diana is the sister; Python <note>In Greek mythology, the serpent or dragon Python was the monstrous guardian of the Delphic oracle, eventually slain by the god Apollo.</note>
Was killed by him, but Orion <note>The giant hunter Orion was killed by the goddess Diana (Artemis) in several versions of the myth, often involving a scorpion or a trick by her brother Apollo.</note> was killed by her.

->The dragon does not die, unless he be killed by his brother and sister, who are the Sun and Moon.<-

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting an alchemical or mythological scene. In the foreground, a man and a woman stand on either side of a green, winged dragon that lies on its back. Both are striking the dragon with heavy wooden clubs. The man on the left (Sol) has a radiant sun emblem behind his head; the woman on the right (Luna) has a crescent moon atop her head. ## EPIGRAM XXV.

> It is a task of no small art to have laid low the Dragon, <note>In alchemy, the Dragon often represents "crude mercury" or the "volatile principle" that must be "tamed" or "fixed" to progress in the Great Work.</note>
> By death, lest he should soon crawl again, revived, upon the ground.
> Both the brother and the sister, joined together,
> Strike his mouth with a club, nor does any outside thing bring about his death.
> Phoebus <note>A name for the Sun or Apollo.</note> is his brother, and Cynthia <note>A name for the Moon or Diana/Artemis.</note> is his sister;
> Python fell by her hand, while Orion fell by his. <note>original Latin: "Python illâ, ast Orion hac cecidêre manu." The poet references classical myths where the Sun and Moon deities slew monsters to parallel the alchemical process of "killing" the raw matter to refine it.</note>

<sig>O 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: IN; red library stamp "B.R." (Bibliothèque Royale) below image</meta>

<summary>This page presents the visual and poetic components of Emblem XXV, illustrating the symbolic slaying of the Dragon by the Sun (Sol) and Moon (Luna). The text emphasizes that the transformation of the alchemical matter must be performed by its own internal "kin" rather than external forces.</summary>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXV, following the epigram which introduced the central metaphor: the death of the Dragon at the hands of its solar brother and lunar sister. This page provides the "Discourse," the prose commentary where Maier explains the chemical and philosophical meaning behind the myth of Jason and the Golden Fleece.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>DISCOURSE XXV.</header>

In the acquisition of the Golden Fleece, the Dragon had to be killed before all else. Because this labor had been attempted by many in vain, they were overcome by the Dragon and slain by its fatal poison. The cause was that they were not sufficiently protected against the dragon’s venom, nor instructed by what trickery it ought to be killed. Jason, however—acting as a physician <note>original: *medicus*. Maier plays on the name Jason, which in Greek (*Iasōn*) is related to the word for healing.</note>—neglected no kind of remedy, of which he received various types from Medea—representing the counsel of the mind—and among these, he received images of the Sun and Moon. By using these, he successfully achieved victory along with the prize <note>original: *brabejo*, from the Greek *brabeion*, meaning a prize or trophy in a contest.</note>, that is, the **GOLDEN FLEECE**. Therefore, the Dragon was removed from the scene by the sun and the moon, or their images, as the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the long lineage of alchemical authors.</note> emphasize everywhere. For thus the author of the *Rosarium* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a seminal 16th-century alchemical text.</note> quotes from others: *Hermes says; The Dragon does not die, unless he be killed with his brother and sister; not by one alone, but by both together, namely by the sun and the moon.* The Philosophers say: *Mercury never dies unless he be killed with his sister; that is, he must be congealed with the moon or the sun.* 

Note that the Dragon is the quicksilver <note>original: *argentum vivum*, literally "living silver."</note> extracted from bodies, having within itself body, soul, and spirit. Regarding this, it is said: *The Dragon does not die, unless with his brother and his sister,* that is, the sun and moon—which is to say, the extracted sulfur, having in itself the nature of moisture and coldness by reason of the moon. With these, the dragon dies; that is, the quicksilver extracted from those same bodies from the beginning, which is the **permanent water** <note>original: *aqua permanens*. In alchemy, this is a "solvent" that can dissolve metals into their primary matter.</note> of the Philosophers, which is created after putrefaction and after the separation of the elements; and that water is called by another name, **stinking water** <note>original: *aqua foetida*.</note>. Thus says he, and all others agree with him, so that there is no need to cite the authority of each individual. 

The Epirotes <note>Inhabitants of Epirus, in Northwest Greece.</note> worshipped the Dragon in the temple of Apollo because of the slaying of Python. The Dragon has a natural war and hatred with the Elephant; it seeks the elephant's eyes and throat until the Elephant, falling to the ground, kills the dragon simultaneously with the mass of its body. From this, dragon's blood <note>Maier refers to the resin or pigment known as "Dragon's Blood," which medieval lore claimed was the mixed blood of these two clashing animals.</note> is brought to our shores. The dragon has eyes valued as gems; it has a very sharp and clear sight, which is why it is often employed to guard treasures, such as the apples of the Hesperides, and, as mentioned, the Golden Fleece among the Colchians. The ancients also added it to Aesculapius <note>The god of medicine and healing.</note> as a hieroglyphic symbol. But the Chemists <note>original: *Chymici*, meaning alchemists.</note> appropriate dragons for their works, not indeed in reality, but through allegory. For the dragon always denotes Mercury, whether it be fixed or volatile. Hence, Mercury is seen with two serpents entwined on his caduceus <note>The messenger's staff of Hermes/Mercury.</note> (since the dragon is a huge serpent), and Saturn with one that devours its tail, just as Janus

<meta>catchword: nus (completing "Janus" on the following page)</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical symbolism of the Dragon as Mercury, which must be "killed" or fixed by the Sun and Moon (Sulfur) to produce the "permanent water." Maier blends mythological examples like Jason and Aesculapius with natural history to illustrate the Dragon's role as a guardian of hidden treasure.</summary>
<keywords>Jason, Medea, Golden Fleece, Dragon, Sun and Moon, Mercury, Quicksilver, Rosarium Philosophorum, Permanent Water, Elephant, Aesculapius, Caduceus</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the Discourse on Emblem XXV. The previous page ended by explaining that Mercury (the Dragon) must be "congealed" or solidified with its "sister" (the Moon) and "brother" (the Sun). The word "nus" at the start of this page completes the name "Saturnus" (Saturn) from the preceding sentence.</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXV.</header>

...-urnus <note>This completes the name "Saturnus" from the previous page, referring to the planet or the alchemical lead.</note>: to Aesculapius <note>The god of medicine and healing in Greek mythology.</note>, the son of Apollo and the inventor of medicine <margin>Philosophy of Medicine</margin>, a serpent is dedicated. It is believed that he was brought from Epidaurus to Rome in the form of a serpent, and was always worshipped there because the cessation of a pestilential plague was thought to have been caused by him. 

Moreover, the Dragon of the Philosophers <term>Philosophers: a common term for alchemists throughout the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</term> is always most vigilant and alive, and it is not easily wounded, both because of the thickness of its skin and because of the sharpness of its teeth and the venom with which it is armed. For although common dragons are said to be devoid of poison, this one nonetheless is not without it; unless it is handled cautiously, it emits it against anyone it meets. Therefore, it can rarely be overcome by force, unless the cunning of its kinsmen is also added. For truly the poet <note>The Roman poet Ovid, in *The Art of Love*.</note> said: 

> A safe and frequent way it is to deceive through the name of a friend,

which in other places and in other affairs—though not here—*Although it may be a safe and frequent way, it holds a crime.* <note>original: *Tuta frequénsq; via est p amici fallere nomen... Tuta frequénsq; licet sit via, crimen habet.*</note>

Mountebanks and quacks are said to expel worms from children by using the powder of other similar worms that have been killed—that is, brothers with brothers and sisters. In the same way, the Dragon here must be killed with its brother and sister together, that is, with the Sun and Moon. From this it appears that the Dragon is also among the number of the planets; namely, as has already been shown from the *Rosarium* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical text.</note>, the Dragon is Mercury extracted from the bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refers to the base metals like lead or copper.</note>. 

There are some among the Greeks who hand down the story that, while Herod was reigning among the Jews, a dragon loved a marriageable and beautiful girl and lay with her in bed; and another was kept by the Emperor Tiberius among his delights, which he was accustomed to feed from his own hand according to habit. In the same way, the Dragon of the Philosophers, if handled rightly, lays aside its savagery and becomes a friend to man, although it is hostile if treated otherwise. 

Xanthus, the author of histories cited by Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of *Natural History*.</note>, bears witness that a slain dragon cub was restored to life by its parent with a herb which he calls *Balin*. However, I refer this rather to the allegory of the Philosophers than to true history, since in chemistry alone is life restored to the dead Dragon and death to the living by alternating turns. 

But someone may ask, from where and how is the Dragon to be captured? The Philosophers respond most briefly through a verse: *Mountains, dragons, and the earth give the springs of the Rebis.* <note>original: *Dant Rebis montes, dracones, terráq;, fontes.* The "Rebis" is the alchemical "Two-fold Thing," representing the union of opposites.</note> Concerning its capture, indeed, let Tacitus <note>A famous Roman historian.</note> be consulted to see with what great care and industry many people kept watch so that they might capture a huge dragon observed in Africa and lead it to the Emperor Tiberius. Specifically, they hedged in the dragon's usual path between stones, gradually narrowing it into a tight space; there, enclosed by nets and chains, it was finally tamed with clubs and blows, and loaded as a burden for many wagons onto the ship by which it was carried to Rome.

<summary>Maier explains that the alchemical Dragon (Mercury) must be tamed or "killed" using its solar and lunar counterparts, using historical and mythological anecdotes of serpents to illustrate the process of chemical stabilization.</summary>
<keywords>Aesculapius, Mercury, Sun and Moon, Rebis, alchemy, medicinal serpent, Tiberius, Pliny, Rosarium Philosophorum</keywords>

### XXVI. German version of the Latin Epigram. <note>Though the fugue is numbered XXII, the text here labels the epigram as XXVI.</note>

No greater wisdom in human affairs is made known to us,
Than that through which wealth arrives, and a long, healthy life.
In her right hand, she shows how to attain great age without hardship, <note>This imagery is drawn from Proverbs 3:16, describing Lady Wisdom: "Length of days is in her right hand; and in her left hand riches and honor."</note>
In her left, as many goods as anyone could ever desire.
If someone approaches this with reason and a steady hand,
He perceives the fruit of the Tree of Life in all his deeds.

### EMBLEM XXVI. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.
## The fruit of human wisdom is the Tree of Life.

<image-desc>A hand-colored etching depicting a central allegorical figure of a woman representing Wisdom (Sapientia). The scroll on the left (her right hand) reads "Length of days and Health" <note>original: "Longitudo dierum et Sanitas"</note>, and the scroll on the right (her left hand) reads "Glory and infinite riches" <note>original: "Gloria ac divitiæ infinitæ"</note>. ### EPIGRAM XXVI.

No wisdom in human affairs is greater,
*Than that by which riches and a healthy life come.* <note>original: "Major in humanis non est sapientia rebus, / Quam quâ divitiæ vitæque sana venit."</note>
Her right hand holds the healthy span of an expansive time,
*While her left hand stores up heaps of wealth.*
If anyone approaches her with both reason and the labor of the hand,
*The fruit of life will be found for them in her, just like a tree.*

<note>This emblem identifies the "Philosophers' Stone" or the result of the Great Work not merely as a physical substance, but as the personification of Wisdom herself, who provides both physical longevity and spiritual or material wealth.</note>

<meta>This page follows Emblem XXVI, which depicted Wisdom (Sapientia) as the source of health and wealth. The previous page established the visual and poetic theme of Wisdom as the "Tree of Life." This discourse expands on that theme by defining man's unique nature through reason and identifying the highest form of human wisdom as the practice of alchemy.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXVI.

Most excellently has *Cicero* <note>original: "Tullius"</note> described the essential difference of man, by which he differs from the rest of the animal kind, in this manner: *Just as a bird is born for flight and a horse for running, so man is born for reasoning: For just as lions, bears, and tigers exercise and show themselves in ferocity; elephants and bulls in the strength of their bodies; and eagles, falcons, and hawks in the preying upon birds and the swiftness of their wings; so man excels them and all others in reason, the discourse of the mind, and the intellect.* For this reason, there is no ferocity, physical power, speed, or anything of that kind in wild beasts that cannot be mitigated, tamed, and overcome by reason alone. For reason is not something merely human, or sprung from the earth, but as the *Poet* <note>Maier is quoting the Roman poet Horace, *Satires* 2.2.79.</note> says, it is a "particle of divine breath" <note>original: "divinæ particula auræ"</note> sent from heaven into the human body. However, it is sometimes called memory, and sometimes intellectual force; and if use or experience is added to it, from these is born wisdom, which is the most precious thing belonging to man. Indeed, Experience is said to be the father, and Reason or Memory the mother, of such a noble offspring. Hence that common saying: *Experience sired me, but Mother Memory bore me.* <note>original: "Usus me genuit, mater verò peperit memoria"</note>

But it is asked: what is true wisdom and the thing most worthy of human inquiry, since there are infinite opinions about it, with everyone turning it toward their own imaginations? The answer is that wisdom—always excepting divine matters looking toward the salvation of the soul—in human affairs consists not in sophistical cleverness, nor in oratorical and ornate speeches <note>original: "phaleratos sermones," literally "speeches decorated like horse-trappings."</note>, nor in the poetic sounds of verses, nor in the critical subtleties of grammarians, nor in the cunning of piling up coins and wealth through right or wrong, through deceits and perjuries, through lies, or through ruthlessness and the sweat of the poor. Rather, it is nothing else but the true knowledge of <term>Alchemy<note>original: "Chymiæ." In Maier's time, alchemy was viewed as the highest natural science, combining chemical practice with philosophical insight.</note></term>, joined with its practice, which is most useful to the human race. This is the wisdom that stands above all others, which penetrates with its right hand to the East and its left to the West, and embraces the whole earth.

<margin>
Sirach 6:4, 13.
Wisdom 8:16.
Sirach 4:20.
</margin>
Indeed, *Solomon* discusses wisdom with great discernment in the Book of Wisdom. For thus he says in chapter 8, verses 9 and 18: *Those who are her kin have eternal permanence, and those who are her friends find sincere pleasure; and he who inquires after her diligently shall receive great joy. For there is no weariness in being occupied with wisdom, nor any distaste in being near her, but only cheerfulness and joy. And although wine and music also gladden the heart of man, yet wisdom is more pleasant than these.* <note>original: "jucundior"</note>

<meta>The catchword "jucundior" (more pleasant) links to the start of the next page.</meta>

<summary>Maier argues that man's defining characteristic is reason, a divine gift that, when combined with experience, produces Wisdom. He identifies this true wisdom specifically as the practice of alchemy, citing the biblical Solomon as an authority on its joys.</summary>

<keywords>reason, intellect, wisdom, experience, memory, Cicero, Solomon, alchemy, Chymia, divine breath, Tree of Life</keywords>

# DISCOURSE XXVI.

<margin>Ecclesiastes 7:10</margin>

*Regarding this,* Morienus the Philosopher <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century alchemist and hermit who supposedly taught the secrets of the Philosopher's Stone to King Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> *says: This science is that which draws its*
*master away from the misery of this world and leads him back to the knowledge of future blessings.*
*That it is indeed a gift of* God, *the same author testifies in these words: For* God *bestows*
*this divine and pure science upon His faithful and servants—specifically*
*those to whom He disposed to grant it from the very first nature of things by His wonderful*
*strength. And presently: For this thing can be nothing other than a gift of* God *Most High, who according to*
*His will, also commits and shows it to whichever of His servants and faithful He chooses:*
*It is fitting, therefore, to be humble and entirely subject to the almighty* God *in all things.*
*And later: It also concerns you to know, O* King, *that this mastery is nothing other*
*than the mystery and secret of secrets* <note>original: "secretum secretorum"</note> *of* God *Most High and Great: For He*
*entrusted this secret to His Prophets, whose souls He has placed in His paradise. It is truly called the tree of life, not because it possesses eternal salvation*
*within itself, but because it shows the way to it, as it were, and bears fruits useful to this life,*
*which this life cannot do without, such as health, the goods of fortune,*
*and the goods of the soul; for without these, a man is a living dead man, and not*
*unlike a beast, even if he outwardly represents the person he ought to be but is not*
*in his better part.*

<header>FUGUE XXVII. at the octave above.</header>

# He who wishes to enter the Rose Garden of the Philosophers without the key is like a man who wishes to walk without feet.

<image-desc>The page displays three musical staves containing the vocal parts for a three-voice fugue. The notation is in a Renaissance style. Each part is labeled with its mythological role in the alchemical process: Atalanta, Hippomenes, and the Golden Apple.</image-desc>

<margin>Atalanta fleeing.</margin>
The Rose Garden of Wisdom abounds with diverse flowers, but its door is always closed with firm bolts. 
<note>original: "Luxuriat Sophiæ diverso flore Rosetum semper at est firmis janua clausa seris."</note>

<margin>Hippomenes following.</margin>
The Rose Garden of Wisdom abounds with diverse flowers, but its door is always closed with firm bolts.

<margin>The delaying apple.</margin>
The Rose Garden of Wisdom abounds with diverse flowers, but its door is always closed with firm bolts.
<note>In Maier’s musical-alchemical system, these three voices represent the "fleeing" mercury (Atalanta), the "following" sulfur (Hippomenes), and the "stabilizing" lead or salt (The Apple) that slows the reaction.</note>

### XXVII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The Garden of Wisdom is adorned with roses of many kinds,
->But its door stands fast, provided with locks at all times;<-
A lowly thing, which opens it, may be found in the world,
->Without which you walk just like one without feet on the earth;<-
In vain you strive to ascend the high mountain of Parnassus,
<note>Parnassus is a mountain in Greece which, in classical mythology, was sacred to Apollo and the Muses. To "climb Parnassus" means to seek high intellectual or poetic achievement.</note>
->You who can hardly stand on one leg on a level path.<-

<sig>EMBLEM XXVII.</sig>

<summary>This page presents the 27th emblem and fugue from Michael Maier's alchemical work, using the metaphor of a locked Rose Garden to represent the secrets of the "Philosophers' Stone." It warns that intellectual pursuit is futile without the specific "key"—a physical substance or secret process—necessary to begin the work.</summary>

### EPIGRAM XXVII.

The Rose Garden of <term>Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Sophia"</note> flourishes with various flowers,
*But the door is always closed with firm bolts:*
The only key for it is considered a worthless thing in the world,
*Without this, you will set out on your journey without legs.*
In vain you strive for the heights of <term>Parnassus</term> <note>A mountain in Greece sacred to Apollo and the Muses; in this context, it represents the difficult ascent toward true knowledge.</note>, you who
*Can hardly steady yourself on level ground.*

<image-desc>The page displays three musical staves containing the vocal parts for a three-voice fugue. The notation is in a Renaissance style. Each part is labeled with its mythological role in the alchemical process: Atalanta, Hippomenes, and the Golden Apple.</image-desc>

### XXVII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The Garden of Wisdom is adorned with roses of many kinds,
->But its door stands fast, provided with locks at all times;<-
A lowly thing, which opens it, may be found in the world,
->Without which you walk just like one without feet on the earth;<-
In vain you strive to ascend the high mountain of Parnassus,
<note>Parnassus is a mountain in Greece which, in classical mythology, was sacred to Apollo and the Muses.</note>

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of a locked rose garden and a man without feet to emphasize that alchemical or spiritual progress is impossible without the "key"—a substance or secret often overlooked by the ignorant.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophical Rose Garden, Wisdom, Parnassus, alchemical key, first matter, Atalanta, Hippomenes</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page provides the prose "Discourse" (Discursus) that expands upon the imagery and epigram of Emblem XXVII. It moves from the allegorical image of the locked garden to a philosophical and mythological explanation of the "two legs" of alchemy: Experience and Reason.</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE XXVII.</header>

It is written of **Erichthonius** <note>A legendary early ruler of Athens, often depicted as having the lower body of a serpent.</note> that he was born from the seed of **Vulcan** <note>The god of fire and metalworking.</note> spilled upon the earth during his struggle with **Pallas**, the goddess of wisdom; because of this, he was born with serpent-like feet instead of human ones. Those who rely solely on the labor of Vulcan <note>Meaning manual labor or the heat of the furnace.</note> without the wisdom of Pallas, give birth to very similar offspring: namely, monstrous, "footless" <note>original: "Apodas"</note>, and premature births that can neither support themselves nor benefit others. It is a miserable thing for a man to walk as a quadruped—that is, on hands and feet together—but it is far more miserable to be entirely deprived of feet and to use arms in their place. Indeed, those who move in the manner of worms or serpents seem to have degenerated into the nature of creeping things. In a human being, the two legs are the organic members without which true walking cannot happen, just as there is no vision without eyes, or grasping of tangible things without hands.

In the same way, it is taught that **Medicine**, and every operative art, has two legs upon which it stands: namely, **Experience** and **Reason**. Without either one of these, that art would be mutilated and crippled; it would neither be perfect in its traditions and precepts, nor would it reach its proposed goal. In particular, **Alchemy** <note>original: "Chymia"</note> enjoys a double subject (acting as its legs), one of which is the **key**, and the other the **bolts**. By these, the <term>Philosophical Rose Garden</term> <note>original: "Rosetum Philosophicum"</note>, which is locked on every side, is opened, and entrance is granted to those who enter lawfully. But if one of these is missing for the person attempting to enter, it will be as if a "lame-foot" <note>original: "loripes," meaning one with twisted or bound feet.</note> attempted to outrun a hare in a race. 

He who enters a garden that is closed or hedged in on every side without a key imitates a thief; coming in the dark of night, he discerns nothing of what grows in the rose garden, nor can he enjoy what he steals. Furthermore, the **key** is a most "worthless thing" <note>original: "res vilissima," a common alchemical trope describing the First Matter as something ignored or despised by the ignorant.</note>, which is properly called the "stone known in the chapters," and it is the root of the **Rhodes** <note>A play on the Greek word *rhodon*, meaning rose.</note> without which the bud is not pushed forth, the gem does not swell, the Rose does not bloom, nor do the thousand-fold leaves unfold.

But it might be asked: where is this key to be sought? I respond with the Oracle: it must be investigated in the same place where the bones of **Orestes** <note>Referring to a story from Herodotus where the Spartans could only defeat the Tegeans after finding the bones of Orestes by solving a riddle.</note> were said to be found; namely, **WHERE THE WINDS, THE SMITER, THE REBOUNDER, AND THE DESTRUCTION OF MEN** are found together. This is, as **Lychas** interpreted it, in a blacksmith’s forge <note>original: "officina fabri ærarii"</note>. For by the "winds," the oracle meant the bellows; by the "smiter," the hammer; by the "rebounder," the anvil; and by the "destruction of men," the iron. This key in the [northern] Zodiacal hemisphere...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "Zodiacise-" with the catchword "pten," likely completing the word as "Zodiaciseptentrionali," referring to the Northern Zodiac.</meta>

<summary>This discourse argues that alchemy requires both practical experience and intellectual reason ("two legs") to succeed. It introduces the "Key" to the Philosophical Rose Garden, identifying it through a classical riddle as being found within the realm of the blacksmith (fire, iron, and striking).</summary>
<keywords>Erichthonius, Vulcan, Pallas Athena, Experience and Reason, Philosophical Rose Garden, The Key, Orestes' Bones, Blacksmith's Forge, Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XXVII, concluding the explanation of the "two legs" of alchemy (Reason and Experience) and transitioning into the specific allegory of the Rose Garden, where the seeker must find the hidden entrance and navigate the dangers within.</meta>

<header>### DISCOURSE XXVII.</header>

...one will truly find [the key] in the northern region, if he knows how to count and discern the signs well, and the thong of the bolt in the southern region. Once these are secured, it will be easy to open the door and enter. In the very entrance, he will see **Venus** with her beloved **Adonis**; for she stained the white roses purple with her own blood. <note>According to myth, as Venus ran to the side of the dying Adonis, she stepped on a rose thorn, and her blood turned the flowers from white to red.</note> In the same place, a **dragon** is observed—just as in the gardens of the **Hesperides**—who keeps watch to guard the roses. 

The scent of roses is said to be intensified by other plants being planted near them, and this is because of the excellent degree of heat present in the others, through which the rose resists cold poisons. For roses require the heat of the sun and the earth before they acquire a color and scent most pleasing to the eyes and nostrils. Furthermore, the smoke of common **sulfur** makes red roses white wherever it touches them; on the contrary, **spirit of vitriol** <note>Sulfuric acid</note> or **aqua fortis** <note>Nitric acid</note> restores them with a saturated red color that lasts. For common sulfur is opposed to **philosophical sulfur**, although it cannot destroy it, whereas the dissolving water <note>acid</note> is friendly to it and preserves its color. 

The **Rose** is dedicated to **Venus** because of its beauty, in which it excels all other small flowers. Indeed, the Rose is a virgin whom Nature has armed so that she might not be violated with impunity and without vengeance. Violets are unarmed and are trampled underfoot; but Roses, hidden among thorns, have yellow hair <note>the stamens</note> within and a green garment <note>the sepals</note> without. No one shall pluck these except the wise man who knows how to separate them from the thorns; if he does otherwise, he will feel the sting in his fingers. Thus, none but the most cautious of Philosophers will gather the flowers, lest he experience the stings of bees and needles in the hives, and find gall in the honey. 

Many have entered the **Rose Garden** <note>original: "Rosarium"</note> secretly with predatory hands, but they have carried away nothing but pain from it—that is, they have lost both their oil and their labor. Hence, **Bacuffen** <note>A speaker in the *Turba Philosophorum*, an ancient alchemical assembly.</note> says in the ***Turba***: *For our books, he says, seem to do a great injury to those who read our writings only once, twice, or thrice; for when their understanding and all their study are frustrated, they also (which is worst) lose the wealth, labor, and time they have spent on this art.* And a little later: *For while someone thinks he has made it, and possesses the world, he will find nothing in his hands.*

<meta>The catchword "Der" indicates the transition to the German translation of the same discourse, which typically followed the Latin in Maier's original edition.</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the discourse by explaining the allegorical Rose Garden, representing the Alchemical Work. It warns that success requires both chemical knowledge—such as how acids and sulfurs interact—and a cautious, scholarly approach to avoid the "thorns" of financial and intellectual ruin.</summary>

<keywords>Venus, Adonis, Rose Garden, Rosarium, Dragon, Hesperides, Sulfur, Vitriol, Aqua Fortis, Turba Philosophorum, Bacuffen</keywords>

### XXVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

> King Duenech <note>Duenech is an alchemical name often associated with antimony or a specific state of "sick" metal</note> (who carries a green lion <note>The "Green Lion" typically refers to vitriol or the raw, acidic material used to dissolve metals</note> upon his shield)
> Seemed entirely wild in his manners through the overflow of bile; <note>Black bile, or melancholy, was thought to cause a cold, dry, and dark disposition</note>
> Therefore he calls to him the physician Pharut, who promises
> To help him, and soon prepares a vaporous <note>original: "lüfftig," literally "airy" or "atmospheric"</note> water-bath.
> Inside this he bathes very often, surrounded by a glass vault,
> Until, moistened by the dew, he lived free from the bile.

# EMBLEM XXVIII. On the Secrets of Nature.

->The King sits bathing in a vaporous bath,<-
->and is freed from black bile by Pharut.<-

A rectangular window in the side of the box reveals the king inside. To the left, a small furnace with a red flame produces heat and vapor. ### EPIGRAM XXVIII.

King Duenech <note>original: "Rex Duenech". An alchemical name, likely derived from Arabic, often signifying a "sick" metal or antimony requiring purification</note> (whose shield shines with the arms of the Green Lion <note>The Green Lion is a famous alchemical symbol representing raw mineral acid or vitriol used to dissolve metals</note>)
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;was swollen with bile and accustomed to harsh manners.
Hence he calls the physician Pharut to himself; he promises health,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and provides airy waters from a fountain:
With these he washes and re-washes him, under a glass vault, until
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;by the dripping dew, all the bile was driven away.

### XXVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

> King Duenech <note>Duenech is an alchemical name often associated with antimony or a specific state of "sick" metal</note> (who carries a green lion <note>The "Green Lion" typically refers to vitriol or the raw, acidic material used to dissolve metals</note> upon his shield)
> Seemed entirely wild in his manners through the overflow of bile; <note>Black bile, or melancholy, was thought to cause a cold, dry, and dark disposition</note>
> Therefore he calls the physician Pharut to himself, who promises him health,
> And prepares for him airy waters from the fountain:
> In these he washes and re-washes him, under a glass dome <note>original: "vitreo sub fornice". This refers to the chemical vessel or alembic used in distillation</note>
> Until all the black bile is washed away by the moistening dew.

<summary>This page illustrates the alchemical allegory of King Duenech being healed of "black bile" (impurities) by the physician Pharut using a vapor bath. This process represents the purification of metallic substances, specifically associated with the "Green Lion" or vitriol, through distillation and washing.</summary>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXVIII, moving from the allegorical epigram and musical fugue of King Duenech into the prose commentary. It establishes a physiological parallel between the human body’s three stages of digestion and the chemical purification of metals.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
# DISCOURSE XXVIII.

JUST AS there are three **digestions** <term>concoctions: from the Latin 'concoctio', referring to the 'cooking' or refining of substances within the body through heat</term> in man—the first in the stomach, the second in the liver, and the third in the veins—so too are there an equal number of universal evacuations of waste products which correspond to them, carrying away their excesses daily. Namely, the first is through the bowels, which is specific to the first digestion; the second through urine, which belongs to the second; and the third through expiration from the pores of the whole body, or through sweat, which is suited to the third. In the first stage, **chyle** <note>original: "chylus", the milky fluid formed in the stomach</note> is produced; in the following, **chyme** <note>original: "chymus", the partially digested mass passing into the liver</note>; and in the last, **dew** <note>original: "ros", a term used here for the most refined nutritive substance</note> or a dewy substance is elaborated and applied to each individual part of the body.

The waste products or feces of the first stage are thick, bilious, and fatty, which are carried out through the intestines to the rear; and if they should ever be delayed, they are ejected by purgatives or stool-inducers, whether mild, moderate, or strong. The excrements of the second stage are liquid, thinner, bilious, and salty, which are led out from the veins through the kidneys and bladder as if through water-conduits; the **sediment** <note>original: "hypostasis", the solid matter that settles in a urine sample</note> of these serves as an indicator of the urine’s health. The excesses of the third stage are even thinner, and therefore mostly expire of their own accord through the finest pores, or are carried out together with the watery part of the humors as sweat. These are assisted by **sweat-inducing medicines** <note>original: "sudorificis"</note>, just as the previous ones are helped by **diuretics** <note>original: "diureticis"</note>.

The ancient Greeks and Romans were exceedingly concerned with evacuating these final wastes. For this reason, many kinds of games and exercises were employed by them, such as the morning rubbing of all the limbs, anointing with oil, and wrestling, the art of boxing, running races, and games of the **hand-ball, net-ball, and large-ball** <note>original: "pilae palmaris, reticularis & magnae"</note>, as well as daily washing and bathing in rivers or artificial baths. For the convenience of these, such magnificent buildings were constructed in Rome that it is more possible for us to admire them than to imitate them. An example is the **Baths of Diocletian** <note>The Thermae of Diocletian, the largest of the imperial baths in Rome</note>, a great part of which still remains and is dedicated, if I am not mistaken, to the **Archangels** <note>A reference to the Basilica of Santa Maria degli Angeli e dei Martiri, built by Michelangelo within the ruined ruins of the baths</note>; how high, proud, and splendid those works are.

Almost the same species of digestions as those mentioned before exist in metallurgy. For the first occurs in its own way in the **Great Year** <note>original: "magno anno", a cosmic cycle of time</note>, that is, by the revolution of the highest sphere; the second in the revolution of the lowest sphere; and the third in the middle sphere. But the Philosophers, so that they might more conveniently draw out these piles of excremental superfluities by the service of their art, also find various ways—such as washings, purifications, bathings, and **vapor-baths** <note>original: "laconica", a dry sweating room or sauna</note>—by which they perform that in the Philosophical work which physicians do in the human body. Duenech, therefore...

<meta>The catchword "itaque" (therefore) indicates the sentence continues on the next page, likely further explaining the chemical treatment of King Duenech.</meta>

<summary>This page draws a direct analogy between human physiology (the three stages of digestion and waste removal) and alchemical metallurgy. It emphasizes the importance of purification—likening chemical refinement to Roman bathing rituals and the biological process of sweating.</summary>
<keywords>digestion, concoction, chyle, chyme, sweat, Roman baths, Diocletian, metallurgy, spheres, purification, Duenech, hygiene</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXVIII, moving from the internal physiology of the first and second digestions to the final purification process symbolized by the "Laconic bath." It focuses on the transition from a melancholic, "base" state to a refined, royal one.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXVIII.</header>

Therefore, he is introduced by **Pharut** <note>likely an allegorical name for a guide or a specific chemical intermediary</note> into the **Laconic bath** <term>Laconicum: a dry, sweating room or sauna-like bath, named after the Spartans of Laconia</term>, so that he may sweat there and excrete the dregs of the **third digestion** <note>the final refining stage of the body's nutrition, occurring in the tissues</note> through his pores. Now, this king’s condition is **melancholic** or **atrabilious** <note>original: "atrabilarius," referring to an excess of "black bile," believed to cause gloom and heaviness</note>. Because of this, he is held in lower regard and value by all other princes, as he is afflicted by the moroseness of **Saturn** and the **choleric** <note>related to yellow bile or anger</note> wrath of **Mars**.

The King himself, therefore, wished either to die or be cured, if such a thing were possible. One physician was eventually found who, swayed by pleas and rewards, undertook this assigned task. This allegory is very common in the writings of the **Philosophers** <note>Alchemists often referred to themselves as philosophers</note>, such as those of **Bernard of Trevisan**, **Alanus**, the little treatise on **Duenech**, and countless others. For this reason, we will not add further details that can be found in those works.

It is sufficient here to observe which digestion's waste is drawn out through bathing, for the hinge of the whole matter turns upon this point. In hot baths or thermal springs, the heat trapped within the body is usually drawn back to the surface of the skin along with the blood. This bestows a healthy, beautiful color upon the face and the entire body. If this color appears, it is a sign that the **melancholic blackness** infecting the skin can be gradually evacuated, and all the **humors** <note>the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that were thought to determine health</note> corrected, so that the finest rosy blood is generated afterward.

It is necessary that the entire temperament of his body be reformed. Currently, his temperament rebels against good blood; it is **cold and dry** <note>the elemental qualities of the melancholic humor</note>, whereas healthy blood is, by contrast, **hot and moist**. Whether such a transformation is possible must be foreseen and predicted by the physician through his prognostics.

There have occasionally been those who mistook a **common laborer** <term>Cerdo: a Greek term for a low-born craftsman or cobbler</term> for a great prince or the son of a king, only to realize his true origin and upbringing through certain signs. The practitioner must take care to avoid this, ensuring he chooses a true royal offspring from the start. Even if this royal seed does not shine with golden ornaments, but appears in a humble and mean garment with a pale, melancholic color, the artist should not reject him or seek another in his place. For if he is thoroughly washed, his royal nature will soon shine through—just as it did for **Cyrus**, **Paris**, and **Romulus**, who were all raised among simple country folk.

One must take care, however, that the bath be **Laconic**—that is, vaporous and sweat-inducing—so that the water does not scald his tender flesh or block the pores, which would cause more harm than benefit and fail to cure him. No one need worry about the royal garments he will wear after his bath. Just as the daughter of **King Alcinous** <note>Nausicaa, who famously aided the hero in Homer's Odyssey</note> once provided clothing to the shipwrecked and naked **Ulysses**, so too will there be someone to send him the most precious robes, so that he may be rightly recognized as the **Son of the Sun** <note>original: "filius Solis," an alchemical term for gold or the perfected substance</note>.

<sig>Q ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: How</meta>

<summary>This page describes the alchemical "sweating bath" of King Duenech, an allegory for purifying a base metal (symbolized by a melancholic king) into gold (the Son of the Sun). It emphasizes the need for the practitioner to recognize true "royal" matter even when it appears degraded or "common."</summary>

<keywords>King Duenech, Laconic bath, third digestion, melancholy, black bile, humors, Saturn, Mars, Pharut, Alcinous, Son of the Sun, transmutation</keywords>

<page-num>124</page-num>

## FUGUE XXIX. at the 9th interval, or below. <note>original: "in 9. seu infra"</note>

### As the salamander lives in the fire, so also does the Stone.

<image-desc>Musical score for a three-voice fugue in mensural notation. The voices are labeled in the left margin as "Atalanta Fleeing", "Hippomenes Following", and "The Apple Delaying". Latin lyrics are printed beneath each staff of music.</image-desc>

The Salamander <note>original: "Salamandra"</note>, more powerful, dwells in burning fire,
Nor, O Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and smithing</note>, does she heed your threats.

<margin>Hippomenes Following.</margin>
The Salamander, more powerful, dwells in burning fire,
Nor, O Vulcan, does she heed your threats.

<margin>The Apple Delaying.</margin>
The Salamander, more powerful, dwells in burning fire,
Nor, O Vulcan, does she heed your threats.

### XXIX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The Salamander lives powerfully and unharmed in a fierce fire,
And, O Vulcan, she does not turn at all toward your threats;
So also does it care little for the cruel ignition of the flames—
That which is born in constant fire: the Philosophers' Stone <term>Philosopher's Stone: the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life</term>;
The former <note>the salamander</note> is cold and extinguishes the fire, escaping unhurt,
But the latter <note>the Stone</note> is hot, and therefore stands within equal heat.

<vocab>Salamander, Vulcan, Philosophers' Stone, Fugue, Atalanta Fleeing, Hippomenes, The Apple Delaying</vocab>

<summary>This page presents Emblem XXIX, using the mythological salamander as an allegory for the Philosopher's Stone's ability to withstand and thrive within the alchemical fire. It distinguishes between the salamander's "cold" nature which resists fire and the Stone's "hot" nature which matches the fire's intensity.</summary>
