<header>EMBLEM XXIX. On the secrets of Nature.</header>
<sig>Q 3</sig>

## As the Salamander lives by fire, so does the Stone:

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting the alchemical symbol of the salamander in the fire. A yellowish-green salamander with dark spots on its back lies horizontally across a bed of vibrant orange and red flames. Thick, billowing clouds of grey and blue-toned smoke rise above the fire, filling the upper portion of the frame. The base of the fire sits upon a small patch of green and dark brown earth. A red circular library stamp is impressed over the lower part of the fire.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XXIX.

*The more powerful Salamander <note>A legendary lizard-like creature believed in medieval and Renaissance times to be capable of living in and even extinguishing fire</note> dwells in burning fire,*
*Nor, O Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and the forge</note>, does she value your threats:*
*So also does the Stone <term>Philosopher's Stone: the ultimate goal of alchemy, a substance that has undergone "fixation" and can no longer be destroyed by heat</term> not refuse the cruel conflagrations of the flames,*
*Which was born in unceasing fire.*
*The one <note>the salamander</note>, being cold, extinguishes the heat and emerges free,*
*But the other <note>the Stone</note> is hot, and a similar heat therefore benefits it.*

<meta>Catchword: Duo; Red circular library stamp "B.R" centered at the bottom of the illustration frame; Page from Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens" (1617/1618).</meta>

<summary>This page features an iconic alchemical emblem comparing the Philosopher's Stone to a salamander, emphasizing that the Stone is perfected and sustained by the same intense fire that would consume lesser substances. It draws a distinction between the salamander's external resistance to heat and the Stone's internal, sympathetic affinity for it.</summary>

<keywords>Salamander, Vulcan, Philosopher's Stone, alchemical fire, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, elemental heat, fixation</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXIX, moving from the poetic epigram to a naturalistic and philosophical explanation of the elements and the legendary salamander.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

### DISCOURSE XXIX.

<meta>catchword: its own</meta>

There are two elements in which animals live—water and air—and just as many in which no living thing can survive: earth and fire. For while the former are of a middle and temperate composition in their primary and secondary qualities, the latter are extremes; their bodies are either too thick or too subtle. Thickness does not admit other bodies, while subtlety does admit them, but only to penetrate and burn them. As for the fact that men live in cells and underground mines, this happens by reason of the air descending that far and filling those places so they are not empty. But here we speak of each element by itself. In the water, fish live in incredible number, variety, and fertility—indeed, the largest of all animals. In the air live humans, quadrupeds, birds, worms, and insects. Whatever is said about wandering spirits hidden in the earth is another matter, for they are not animals.

In fire, however, they say only the Salamander lives. The Salamander is a creeping worm not unlike a lizard, but with a slower gait, a larger head, and a different color. I remember seeing such a creature myself in the Alps, specifically on the Splügen Pass <note>A high mountain pass in the Alps connecting Switzerland and Italy</note>, having emerged from the caverns of the rocks after thunder and rain. It was lingering in the road, and a certain local peasant advised me that it was called **a newt** <note>original: ein Molch</note>. It had around it a slow and viscous moisture, by which it passes freely through fires without harm. 

But the **Philosopher's Salamander** <term>A symbolic representation of the "Fixed" substance in alchemy that remains stable in high heat</term> is far different, although it is compared to this one. For the philosophical one is born in fire, which this natural one is not. Rather, if the natural one falls into fire, it is not immediately consumed because of its abundant and extreme coldness and moisture; indeed, it can pass through the flame freely. The philosophical one is hot and dry, while the natural one is cold and moist. For everything reflects the nature of its mother’s womb, or imitates its place and homeland. Fire produces nothing except what is hot and dry, as something like itself; and conversely, the moist and cold caverns of the rocks, filled by rains, send forth this cold and moist worm. The philosophical salamander rejoices in fire due to its similarity of nature; the natural one extinguishes the fire by its contrary nature, or repels the fire's force from itself for a short time.

They say that from the copper furnaces of Cyprus, the **fire-insect** <note>original: pyrausta; a legendary insect supposed to live in fire</note> flies out, having been born in the fire. But one should not believe this to be true except by way of allegory. For fire destroys and corrupts the bodies of any animals if it is continued, since it even burns earth into glass and solid wood into ashes, and all other compound things, with a few exceptions—such as **mercurial things** <term>Substances containing mercury, which in alchemy were known for their unique relationship with fire—either remaining completely stable or evaporating entirely without breaking down</term>, which either remain whole or fly out of the fire whole, with no separation of their parts occurring within them. **Vulcan** <note>The Roman god of fire and smithing, used here as a metaphor for the transformative and destructive power of heat</note> is a most rigid executioner, who brings all things mixed from the elements to the test...

<summary>Maier distinguishes between the common Alpine newt and the alchemical Salamander, explaining that while the animal merely survives fire through cold moisture, the alchemical Stone is actually born from and perfected by heat.</summary>

<keywords>four elements, salamander, Splügen Pass, newt, fire-insect, pyrausta, Vulcan, mercury, alchemical fire, fixed substances</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical discussion of the Salamander as an alchemist's metaphor for "fixity"—the ability of a substance to remain stable and unchanging even in the most intense heat of a furnace.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

### DISCOURSE XXIX.

Vulcan calls it to his own judgment <note>Vulcan is the Roman god of fire and metalworking, here personifying the alchemical furnace</note>, although some things are exempt from his court as if by the privileges and pardons of Nature the Empress. Over these things he holds no legal right by himself, unless he joins other judges <note>original: Areopagitas, referring to the high jurors of Athens</note> to himself; they are as Salamanders in the face of his violence, which they do not fear. Avicenna <note>A Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians and philosophers of the Islamic Golden Age</note> in his "gate" <note>a chapter or section of a book</note> enumerates various temperaments of bodies, all of which are unbalanced and thus corruptible by fire and other injuries. 

But he describes one temperament that is perfectly balanced, which has as much heat as cold, and as much dryness as moisture—not according to weight, but according to "justice" <note>An alchemical and medical term for perfect proportion or "just" balance</note>, as doctors call it. And this is that which is more passive than active. In this body, if the fire strives to dissolve the water—its enemy—into air—its friend—the earth does not allow this dissolution because it is incorporated into the water. And the internal fire of the compound approves this decision of the earth by its vote, because fire is the closest friend to earth. 

Thus, the judgment of Vulcan ceases, and he tries another trick: he attempts to burn the earth to ashes, as is his custom; but the water adhering to it brings forward objections, showing that she is united to the earth and has air on her side, while the earth has fire on the other side. Therefore, whoever wishes to incinerate the earth must also incinerate the remaining elements. And so Vulcan, having been eluded, suspends his judgment so that he is not a laughingstock to everyone. 

This body is like the truest Salamander, in which the elements are balanced on the scales of their powers. Regarding this, the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> says, quoting Geber <note>An 8th-century chemist often called the "Father of Chemistry"</note>: "Likewise," he says, "this philosopher wants that substance of Mercury to be killed <note>original: mortificatam; in alchemy, this refers to a change of state or 'death' before the substance can be reborn</note>; but naturally, his Mercury is within that venerable Stone, as is manifest to all: Therefore, etc." 

Likewise, this philosopher wants that substance of Mercury to be fixed, as is clear, because he teaches the methods of fixing with many cautions and skills; but who would doubt that the substance of this precious stone is most highly fixed? Certainly no one who knows it. From this, it is clear that the Stone must be reduced to the nature of the Salamander through fixation; that is, to the highest degree of fixity, which does not flee from or refuse the fire. For it cannot be a Salamander before it has learned to be most patient in enduring fire, which must happen through a long delay of time. 

Concerning Achilles and Triptolemus <note>Figures from Greek mythology who were placed in fire by gods—Thetis and Demeter respectively—to burn away their mortal parts and make them immortal</note>, who were placed under the glowing coals of fire by night until they could bear the most vehement heat, more will be said later in the 35th emblematic discourse; they too obtained the property of the Salamander through habit and practice. For habit is a second nature, which nevertheless can achieve little or nothing unless it communicates power and begins an alteration as if it were a master. 

Hence it is that it is impossible to fix ice by fire, but it is possible for crystal, because nature began this process. So it must be thought of the watery and volatile Mercury, that it cannot be fixed by its own nature, except through the embrace of its married sulphur, which is the **Tincture of the Philosophers** <term>The final elixir or "Stone" capable of transforming and stabilizing base metals</term> and fixes all flying spirits.

<summary>This page explains the "Philosopher's Salamander" as a metaphor for a perfectly balanced substance where the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) protect one another from destruction by heat. It details the process of "fixation," where volatile Mercury is made stable in fire by being joined with Sulphur.</summary>

<keywords>Salamander, Vulcan, Avicenna, Geber, Rosarium Philosophorum, Mercury, Sulphur, fixation, elements, alchemy, Achilles, Triptolemus, Tincture of the Philosophers</keywords>

# The Sun needs the Moon, just as the rooster needs the hens.

<image-desc>Six musical staves of printed notation representing a three-part canon. The voices are labeled in the left margin. Latin lyrics are printed below the notes on the staves.</image-desc>

### XXX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

O Sun, you accomplish nothing alone, if I do not act with my strength,
Just as the rooster is held to be in vain without the hens;
And likewise, I, the Moon <note>original: "Luna," the feminine alchemical principle often associated with silver or the "volatile" substance</note>, desire your present help,
Just as the rooster is greatly desired by the hens;
Those whom Nature has willed to be joined together—
They are not wise who are not ashamed to separate them.

<meta>catchword: EMBLE-</meta>

<summary>This page presents Emblem XXX of Michael Maier’s alchemical work, using a musical fugue and poetry to illustrate the interdependence of the Sun (gold/sulfur) and the Moon (silver/mercury). It argues that neither principle can achieve anything alone, comparing their union to the natural pairing of a rooster and its hens.</summary>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>EMBLEM XXX. On the secrets of Nature.</header>

## The Sun needs the Moon, just as the rooster needs the hen.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting two nude figures standing in a landscape. The male figure on the left has a head replaced by a radiant sun with a face (Sol). The female figure on the right has a head replaced by a moon face surrounded by golden rays (Luna). At their feet stands a rooster and a hen. The background features a body of water with small sailing vessels, surrounded by hills and a small settlement on the left bank. A red circular library stamp is present at the bottom center of the illustration.</image-desc>

### E P I G R A M  X X X.

> O SUN, you achieve nothing alone if I am not present with my powers,
> Just as the rooster is useless without the help of the hen.
> And I, the Moon <note>original: "Luna"</note>, being present, in turn demand your assistance,
> Just as the rooster is sought after by the hen.
> He who would wish to bind with chains <note>original: "vinclis religare," implying a forced or restrictive connection</note> those things
> Which Nature demands be joined together, is truly lacking in mind <note>original: "mentis inops," meaning foolish or senseless</note>.

<margin>R Avi <note>A signature mark for the book's printing assembly.</note></margin>

<gloss>Sun, Moon, rooster, hen, nature, to be joined, emblem, epigram</gloss>

<summary>This page illustrates the fundamental alchemical principle of "conjunction," arguing through the metaphor of poultry that the masculine (Sun) and feminine (Moon) forces are mutually dependent. It suggests that their union is a natural necessity that should not be forced or misunderstood by the unlearned.</summary>

<keywords>conjunction, alchemical marriage, Sol and Luna, duality, natural law, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse XXX, following the visual and poetic introduction of the Sun and Moon’s union as symbolized by the rooster and hen. It moves from the emblem’s imagery into a detailed natural and mythological commentary on the necessity of masculine and feminine principles in the alchemical "Art."</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>D I S C O U R S E  XXX.</header>

Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and philosophy were foundational to Western alchemy.</note> in his book *On the Soul* <note>original: "lib. de anima"</note> several times advises that eggs are not to be taken for the Art unless they come from those hens to which a rooster has been present: that is, that without the masculine virtue, the feminine subject is worth nothing, and vice-versa, nor will the rooster be useful without the hen. Hence, the twin sexes must be joined in the court of the Philosophers <note>original: "corte Philof."</note> so that multiplication may occur. Indeed, the Philosophers use a comparison taken from the rooster because he, above all, corresponds more to the power of <term>sulfur</term> <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and hot principle, as opposed to the passive, feminine, and cold mercury.</note> than any other male among birds, since one may preside over many hens, nor does he easily endure a rival in the same dungheap, considering himself an equal and sufficient match for each one.

The bird of Mars <note>The rooster.</note> was transformed, as the Poets tell in their fables, from the boy <term>Gallus</term> <note>Refers to Alectryon, the youth Mars stationed to watch for the rising Sun during his secret affair with Venus. Alectryon fell asleep, and when the Sun discovered the lovers, Mars turned him into a rooster to forever announce the Sun's arrival.</note>, who should have observed the Sun so that he did not spy upon the adultery of Mars with Venus; he is quite warlike in battle, where he fights with his adversary even unto death. He represents the Sun <note>original: "Solem"</note> in the Philosophical work, just as the hen represents the Moon <note>original: "luna"</note>. Therefore, there is the same necessity of joining the Sun with the Moon as there is of joining the rooster with the hen. The rooster is also sacred to the Sun; he rises when the Sun arrives and goes to sleep with it. He frequently gazes at the sky and raises his sickle-shaped tails on high; he fights against serpents on behalf of the hens. He is the messenger of light and dear to Latona <note>Also known as Leto, the mother of the twins Apollo (the Sun) and Artemis (the Moon).</note> because he stood by her as she was giving birth. For Latona bore the Sun and the Moon, which is why the bird is assigned to both the mother and the offspring.

But the Sun, Moon, and Latona correspond to chemical subjects; so too do the rooster and the hen. Yet these also emerged from an egg, and in turn they leave behind eggs to be hatched into poultry chicks; so too the Philosophers have their own "eggs" that pass into birds of the same kind, provided they are nurtured by a temperate heat, just like the heat of a brooding hen, which lasts continuously. For while in other birds the male also sits upon the eggs, the rooster alone shows himself immune from this office or burden. Thus, the entire care and labor of hatching the eggs and raising the chicks falls upon the hen. 

In this, one may observe her industriousness and diligence—how quickly she eats and drinks and relieves her bowels, and soon runs back to the eggs before they grow cold. Then, with what force and impetus she defends her chicks, with what kindness she receives and covers the naked ones under her wings, with what a voice—like a bell—she calls and gathers them, and with what zeal she breaks the harder crumbs or grains for them and serves them with her beak as if with a knife; it is a work of nature worthy of admiration. And all this so that eggs or young chicks are not lacking for the nourishment of man. In the same manner, the Philosopher or craftsman [must be] most diligent and...

<meta>The catchword "provide" suggests the next page begins with a word like "providentissimus" (most foreseeing/provident), continuing the comparison between the craftsman and the diligent hen.</meta>

<summary>This discourse uses the biology and mythology of the rooster and hen to explain the alchemical necessity of the "Conjunction"—the union of masculine (Sulfur/Sun) and feminine (Mercury/Moon) principles—and emphasizes the diligent, constant heat required to "hatch" the Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, Sulfur, Mars, Venus, Sun, Moon, Latona, brooding, philosophical egg, diligence, conjunction</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical commentary on Discourse XXX. The previous page introduced the rooster as a symbol of the active, masculine sulfurous principle and recounted the myth of Gallus. This page transitions from the symbolic nature of the bird to the specific laboratory operations required to achieve the "Philosophical Marriage" of the Sun and Moon.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXX.</header>

He [the alchemist, following the nature of the rooster] proceeds providently in all his operations. For he gathers the eggs from their places where the rooster was present, having examined them to ensure they are not "wind-eggs" <note>original: "subventanea"; unfertilized eggs, which ancient naturalists believed were produced by the wind alone and could never hatch.</note>. Afterwards, he purges, prepares, and arranges them in his vessels, as if in nests, and provides a suitable heat, by which from day to day, being mixed together, the subjects act and suffer upon one another, until after a long time, passing through various colors, they arrive at a single color and essence. In this work, <term>solution</term> <note>The breaking down of a solid into liquid.</note>, <term>coagulation</term> <note>The solidifying of a liquid.</note>, <term>sublimation</term>, <term>ascension</term>, <term>descension</term>, <term>distillation</term>, <term>calcination</term>, and <term>fixation</term> are completed as intermediate steps. For that which is hard and compact cannot be altered; therefore, solution is performed first, so that it may be liquefied and softened. But where something has been dissolved, it is likewise fitting that it be coagulated—not to its former hardness, but to a honey-like malleability. <term>Sublimation</term> <note>The process of vaporizing a substance to purify it.</note> truly separates the pure from the impure, and renders the base more worthy, and the lower higher. Hence, this process should not be absent, but is as the mistress and teacher of all. While sublimation occurs, some parts rise higher, which is <term>ascension</term>, and others sink back down, which is <term>descension</term>. From this, <term>distillation</term> clarifies the whole, being frequently repeated, and that which remains at the bottom is <term>calcined</term> <note>Reduced to a dry powder by heat.</note>. Both are then fixed, and thus the work is perfected. 

If anyone should gather all these special operations under the general one, which is **COOKING** <note>original: "COCTIO"; in alchemy, this refers to the slow, steady maturation of the substance over heat, often called "digestion."</note>, he will not stray far from his purpose. For just as many chicks as there are, running here and there, gather under one mother or nursing hen, so do these various ways and modes of operating converge in the "work of the woman" <note>original: "opus mulieris"; a common alchemical phrase suggesting the Work is as patient and routine as domestic cooking or washing.</note>, that is, a single cooking. It is <term>Luna</term> <note>The Moon, representing the feminine principle or silver.</note> who comes to be exalted to the sublimity of the <term>Sun</term> <note>Representing the masculine principle or gold.</note>; for her sake all these things are done—that is, a lasting marriage between the Sun and Moon is intended. If this is achieved, all embassies, betrothals, couplings, and doubts will cease, and there will be one bed and the flesh of both will be a mutual and constant love, an eternal peace, and an indissoluble covenant. 

The Sun without the Moon is of no great esteem, and the Moon without the Sun is of a dejected condition and base origin. But she receives from the Sun, her husband, splendor, dignity, and strength, or the firmness of soul and body; the Sun, in turn, receives from the Moon the multiplication of offspring and the propagation of his kind. Hence the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosarius"; likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a seminal 16th-century alchemical text.</note> says: 
> *If in our Stone there were only one of these two, the medicine would never easily flow, nor would it give a tincture; and if it did give one, it would not tint, except insofar as the other was present; and the Mercury would fly away in smoke, because there would be no receptacle for the tincture within it.* 
And Geber <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in early chemistry and alchemy.</note> proves in his *Book of Examinations* <note>original: "libro examinun"</note> that if the Sun and Moon are incorporated together through the Art, they are not easily separated.

R ij
<meta>catchword: De (Concerning)</meta>

<summary>This page details the specific alchemical laboratory processes, such as solution and sublimation, required to achieve the "Chemical Marriage" between the Sun and Moon. It emphasizes that all these operations are ultimately part of a single "cooking" process that unites the masculine and feminine principles into an inseparable, perfected Stone.</summary>
<keywords>alchemical operations, solution, coagulation, sublimation, distillation, calcination, Sun and Moon, alchemical marriage, Rosarium Philosophorum, Geber, Coctio</keywords>

### *German version of the Latin Epigram XXXI.*

The King, whose head is made quite heavy by a golden crown,
Calls out thus very loudly, swimming in the deep sea:
"Why do you not help me? Why does everyone not come running?
Since, if I am rescued from the distress of the water, I can bestow gifts;
Bring me, if you are wise, into my kingdom, and at no time shall
Poverty oppress you, or wearisome sickness of the body."

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXI.</header>

## The King swimming in the sea, calling out with a loud voice: He who rescues me shall have a great reward.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting a bearded man wearing a golden crown swimming in a choppy sea. He has his right arm extended as if reaching out or calling for help. To his right, another person's legs are visible emerging vertically from the water, suggesting they are drowning. In the background, there is a coastal landscape with a fortified city featuring towers and a harbor with several ships. A library stamp with the letters "B.R" is visible at the bottom center of the illustration.</image-desc>

<meta>stamp: B.R</meta>

### EPIGRAM XXXI.

*The King, whose head a crown <note>original Latin: "Diadema". In alchemical symbolism, the crown represents the perfection and fixed nature of Gold or the "Sun".</note> weighs down, swims in the vast sea,*
*And using a loud voice, he says:*
*“Why do you not bring help? Why do you not all run to me,*
*Whom, once rescued from the waters, I am perhaps able to bless?*
*If you are wise, lead me back into my kingdom, and neither*
*Shall poverty oppress you, nor any sickness <note>original: "lues", implying a plague, infection, or physical decay.</note> of the body.”*

<sig>R 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: PRIMA</meta>

<summary>This page presents the 31st emblem of Maier's work, depicting the "King" (symbolizing Gold or the Fixed principle) submerged in the "Sea" (the Mercurial solvent). The King's plea for rescue represents the alchemical process of dissolution and the subsequent promise of the Philosopher's Stone to provide both wealth and health.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Michael Maier, King, dissolution, gold, Mercurial sea, healing, wealth, Epigram XXXI</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Emblem XXXI, expanding on the themes introduced in the epigram on page 133. While the previous page focused on the King's poetic plea for help from the "sea" of mercury, this page provides the philosophical and historical justification for why "swimming" (the ability of the spirit to survive dissolution) and "literacy" (the understanding of alchemical secrets) are essential for the survival of the Royal Work.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXI.</header>

**FIRST**, in ancient times, the basic elements of all education were the skill of swimming and instruction in letters. Because of this, it was common to say of an unrefined and unpolished man: "He knows neither how to swim nor his letters." For the ancients considered that swimming would frequently be useful for saving the body and snatching it from the dangers of the water, while the knowledge of letters would save the soul from the waves of fortune. 

Furthermore, swimming served military needs especially in times of war, while the knowledge of letters served at home in times of peace. Just as we observe that beasts have their own weapons ready and provided by nature, in their place, man has been granted hands and intellect against external force, so that the latter might devise and find weapons, and the former might make and use them. In the same way, the ability to swim is naturally implanted in those same beasts, but not at all in man. They often escape from the waters by swimming even when young, while in those same waters a man, whether old or even very strong, is suffocated. 

Therefore, it was necessary to command the exercise of swimming for boys, as it is always most useful for every life, so that what was lacking through nature might be supplied by acquired skill. This same exercise was used by the great, by princes, and by kings, for the protection of their own bodies. For those born in a high position are not at all free from the accidents of fortune, but are exposed to them just as other men. 

If Dionysius <note>Dionysius II, the tyrant of Syracuse (4th century BCE), who was deposed and famously became a schoolteacher in Corinth.</note> had known neither how to swim nor his letters, when he was driven from the kingdom of Sicily as a tyrant, he would have perished in the waves of the sea as a shipwrecked man in the Corinthian Gulf. But having been snatched from danger by swimming, he betook himself to Corinth, where he opened a school for teaching boys and professed the liberal arts <note>original: "literas humaniores"</note>. From a king he was made a schoolmaster <note>original: "ludimoderator"</note>, holding a rod <note>original: "virgâ"</note> instead of a scepter <note>original: "sceptro"</note>, from which comes the saying, "Dionysius at Corinth." 

In a similar way, unless the royal son of the Philosophers <note>In alchemy, the "King" or "Son" refers to the perfected gold or the "Stone" in its intermediate, vulnerable state during the process of dissolution.</note> could swim, no one would hear his cry, nor would anyone bring help to him who had long ago been submerged in the waters. Therefore, swimming is necessary and most useful for a man of every condition: for even if it does not immediately free him from the waves of the immense sea, it nevertheless grants a span of life during which he may be freed by others. 

Indeed, the said King sustains himself for a very long time and cries out even until now, although he is heard or seen by very few because of the vastness of the sea and its remoteness. For perhaps in swimming he reached a rock or a very large stone, by which he might be held if the waves should prevail. But it is asked, what kind of sea is it? I answer, the Erythraean <note>The ancient name for the Red Sea, often associated in alchemy with the "Redness" or Rubedo phase, the final stage of the Great Work.</note> or Red Sea, situated under the Tropic of Cancer; in which, since magnets are most abundant

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>Maier explains the ancient necessity of swimming and literacy, using the historical example of the exiled King Dionysius to illustrate how the Alchemical King must survive the "sea" of dissolution through these metaphorical skills. He identifies the sea as the "Red Sea," a location rich in magnetic force and symbolic of the final alchemical transformation.</summary>

<keywords>swimming, literacy, education, Dionysius of Corinth, Alchemical King, Red Sea, Tropic of Cancer, dissolution, preservation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Emblem XXXI, detailing the survival of the King after his shipwreck. It transitions from the general necessity of swimming to a specific alchemical allegory: the King (the solar principle or the "Stone" in its early stage) escaping the "magnetic" dangers of the sea (the mercurial solvent) to eventually be restored and wed, producing a more powerful successor.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXI.</header>

at the bottom, where it is not safe for ships constructed with iron or heavily burdened to pass: For they could easily be drawn to the bottom by the force of a magnet. Since the aforementioned king was ignorant of this, and the ship was submerged while the rest perished, he alone escaped by swimming. His crown remained with him, shining with remarkable rubies, by which he could easily be recognized and restored to his kingdom. 

But what good things are there that this royal son could or would wish to bestow upon the one by whom he is led back to his own kingdom? Certainly not the kind that King Ptolemy, the last king of Egypt, gave to Pompey—by whom his father had been restored to the Egyptian throne—namely, betrayal and death <note>Ptolemy XIII famously ordered the execution of Pompey the Great when he fled to Egypt, despite Pompey’s past support of Ptolemy’s father.</note>; but rather health, the removal of diseases, the preservation of life from them, the use of necessary things, a <term>cornucopia</term> <note>original: "cornu copiae," the horn of plenty, representing endless abundance.</note>, honor, and love. Since these are not common or mediocre things, but the highest provisions and ornaments of this life, who but a <term>leaden man</term> <note>original: "plumbeus"; alchemical shorthand for someone dull, heavy-spirited, or lacking the "gold" of intellect.</note> would not seek them? Who would not swim toward him? Who would not offer a hand and pull him into a boat? 

But one must see to it that, while helping him, the diadem does not fall into the sea: For then he would scarcely be recognized as a king, nor would he be received by his subjects. For the <term>pyropus</term> <note>A "fiery" bronze or a deep red gemstone, likely a ruby or garnet.</note>, venerable to all, would have perished, and the <term>Bezoar stone</term> <note>A stone-like mass found in the stomachs of animals, historically prized as a universal antidote to poison.</note>, promising health to all, would have vanished. Hence the *Rosarius* <note>The "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> cites Aristotle in these words: *Choose for yourself as a stone that through which kings are honored in their crowns, and through which physicians have the means to cure their sick; because that stone is close to the fire.* For without its medicinal virtue, the crown would be of no value. 

But what must be done for the king now that he is freed? He must be liberated from the swallowed waters through <term>sudorifics</term> <note>Medicines that induce sweating.</note>, from the cold through the heat of fire, from the numbness of his limbs through moderately warm baths, from hunger and starvation through the administration of a suitable diet, and from other external evils through their contrary and health-giving remedies. Then, a Royal marriage must be provided for him, and in due time from it will be born a most desired offspring—most pleasing to all, most beautiful and most fertile—who will surpass all his ancestors in power, kingdoms, dominions, peoples, riches, and wealth, and who will subject his enemies to himself, not by war, but by humanity; not by tyranny, but by clemency, which is his genuine and proper nature.

->XXXII. German version of the Latin Epigram.<-

A moist plant, growing in the sea not far from Sicily,
Has finely spread its little branches beneath the warm water;
Its name is Coral, which soon changes into hardness,
As soon as a dry wind blows from cold lands;
It becomes stone, and keeps its red color and many branches;
This is a fitting example of the natural <term>Stone</term> <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the goal of the alchemical Great Work, which like coral is said to transition from a liquid/soft state to a permanent, hard, and precious form.</note>.

->XXXII. German version of the Latin Epigram.<-

Its name is Coral, which soon changes into hardness,
As soon as a dry wind blows from cold lands;
It becomes stone, and keeps its red color and many branches;

<summary>This emblem compares the alchemical Philosopher's Stone to the growth of coral, emphasizing its transition from a soft, plant-like state underwater to a hard, red stone when exposed to the air and cold wind. This process represents the "fixation" of the alchemical matter into its final, stable form.</summary>
<keywords>coral, Boreas, Philosopher's Stone, Sicily, fixation, coagulation, red color, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XXXII, following the image and epigram on page 137 which compared the Philosopher’s Stone to coral—a substance that is soft under water but hardens into stone when exposed to air.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXII.</header>

**PHILOSOPHERS** call their stone "vegetable" <note>original: "vegetabilem"; in alchemy, this refers to the "vegetative" power of growth, life, and multiplication, rather than the stone being a plant.</note>, because like a plant it thrives, grows, increases, and multiplies. To the ignorant, this indeed seems marvelous and far from the truth, since it is well established that stones do not vegetate, nor do they grow in this manner, nor do they have any relation to liquefiable metals. But those people are deceived by their own judgment. For they assume that what is unknown to them does not exist in the nature of things, measuring the immensity of the universe by their own limited understanding. Who would have ever believed that a stone grows under the waters, or that a plant generated there turns to stone, unless **Experience** and the steady reliability of writers proved it? Where does that stone-making or color-giving power exist—the power that hardens and tints the coral? Does it exist in the waters, in the air, or in the earth? It is likely that the plant is soft and pliable while it is under the water, as many affirm; yet it is of a very earthy nature. When it is cut away and exposed to the cold winds, it hardens and acquires a stony brittleness, as the abundant watery parts are dried out by the cold and dry air (for these northern winds induce dryness with their breath), and the remaining earthy body is congealed by the qualities of the earth: coldness and dryness. For only the earth possesses the power of constriction, not water or air, in regards to their own genuine and natural virtues.

Furthermore, in other places, the sea provides three medicinal stones, partly from the vegetable genus, partly from the animal, or rather, partly drawn from the secrets of nature: namely, **pearls**, **amber**, and **ambergris**. The origin and capture of pearls are known to us, but not so much for the others. **Amber** <note>original: "succinum"</note> is collected on the maritime shores of Sudavia <note>Sudavia refers to Samland, a region in Prussia famous for its amber deposits.</note>, on the beaches, after a more violent blowing of the Northwest winds <note>original: "Circii sive Cori"; Circius and Corus are classical names for specific northwest winds.</note>. It undoubtedly bubbles out from the veins of the earth into the sea, or is washed out by the waters and driven to the shores by the waves. Indeed, we have seen certain minerals of iron and silver attached to amber, which could not have happened except within the earth. As for the fact that flies, gnats, spiders, butterflies, frogs, and serpents are seen in certain pieces (just as we once possessed 120 little spheres turned from amber, each of which contained flies, gnats, spiders, or several butterflies—one piece even held nine—not without a singular miracle of nature), this happens from the "out-flowing" and the **imagination of the heavens**, as we have demonstrated elsewhere <note>The "imagination of the heavens" refers to a Renaissance belief that celestial influences could project forms into matter, explaining how complex creatures appeared to be trapped in solid stone or resin.</note>.

<meta>catchword: Ambergris</meta>

<summary>Maier defends the alchemical term "vegetable stone" by comparing it to coral and amber, arguing that nature frequently transforms soft, organic-like substances into hard minerals through environmental cooling and celestial influence.</summary>
<keywords>vegetable stone, coral, amber, pearls, ambergris, Samland, Northwest winds, celestial imagination, mineral growth</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 138, where the author argued that although the Philosopher’s Stone is called "vegetable," it shares properties with materials found in the sea like coral, which transitions from a soft plant-like state to a hard, stony state.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXII. 139</header>

It cannot be denied that **Grey Amber** <note>original: "Ambra Grysea"; this refers to ambergris, a waxy substance produced by sperm whales, highly prized in perfumery and medicine</note> is found in the same way on the shores of both the East and West Indies. Although some people attribute it to the sap or gum of trees (as was previously said of yellow amber), those who believe it emerges from the veins of the earth judge more probably. For trees fertile in ambergris and yellow amber <note>original: "succinum"</note> have never been seen; yet it is quite certain that such trees, if they existed, would grow in the open air outside of the water. Therefore, we classify both types of amber as subterranean veins or stones, just as we classify pearls among the **Zoophytes** <note>original: "Zoophyta"; organisms like sponges or corals that appear to be between animals and plants</note> and coral among the vegetables.

Indeed, the **Philosopher’s Stone** is compared to these stones, and especially to coral. For just as coral grows in the waters and takes its nourishment from the earth, so too the Alchemical Stone <note>original: "lapis Philosophicus"</note> congeals from **mercurial water** <note>the fundamental "liquid" matter of the alchemists</note> and has assumed from it whatever is earthy for its own increase, while the superfluous moisture evaporates. A red color is also intensified in it through coagulation, which more recent writers call the **tincture of corals**; this is no different from the Medicinal Stone, which turns red in its final thickening and appears like the reddest coral, which is its tincture.

However, while coral hardens through cold and dryness, the Stone hardens through heat and dryness. When this heat is increased, the Stone likewise melts—contrary to the nature of other stones, which indeed melt, but turn into glass, a thing that does not suit our Stone at all. And just as coral is prepared into various medicines of great power, so too the **Philosopher’s Coral** has transferred into itself the powers of all herbs; this single Stone can do as much as all the medicines of all the vegetables combined. For the celestial Sun, which pours medicinal power and efficacy into vegetables, has granted more to this, its son and earthly representative, than to all others. This is the **philosophical coral**: vegetable, animal, and mineral, which hides in the vast sea and is not recognized unless it is placed before the eyes of the ignorant and put into their hands.

Yet it must be cut away under the waters with the greatest caution, lest it lose its juice and blood, and nothing remain but an earthy chaos without its true form. For in this lies the whole difficulty of capturing the coral, and beyond this, hardly any other occurs: I mean the **superfluous moisture**, which kills the stone unless it is separated, and which does not allow the coral-redness to appear, nor admit coagulation while it is present.

### XXXIII. English version of the German translation of the Latin Epigram.

The two-headed Hermaphrodite resembles a man who is dead,
When he suffers great distress because his moisture has been stolen.
Since he shrouds himself in the dark night, you must give him more fire,
Which, as soon as you do, brings him back to life again.
All the power of the **Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone; the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting eternal life</term> resides in the fire; all red **sulfur** <note>original: "Schwebel"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle</note> exists in the gold,
And the white **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"; the passive, feminine, and fluid principle</note> alone comes forth from the silver.

It emphasizes the roles of sulfur, mercury, and fire in transitioning the matter from a death-like state to a perfected form.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Discourse XXXIII, providing the philosophical commentary on the emblem of the Hermaphrodite. It explains the necessity of heat (fire) for the transformation of the "dead" matter into the living Stone, using biological and mythological analogies.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXIII.</header>

**It is** among the secrets of nature that, as winter sets in, frogs and swallows, submerged in the waters, lie as if dead, and in the new spring, through the action of the Sun's new heat, they recover their senses and movement or carry out the actions of sensitive life. Indeed, if they are found during the very time of the winter solstice and brought into the air or a warm room <note>original: "hypocaustum"; a heated chamber or room</note>, they soon begin to move just as in summer. From this it appears that they lack nothing but external heat, which might excite the enclosed internal natural heat and bring it into action. In the same way, the Philosophers speak of their **Hermaphrodite** <term>an alchemical symbol of the union of opposites, often representing the Prime Matter or the 'Rebis'</term>, who, if he appears dead while lying in darkness, requires the heat of fire. He is said to lie in darkness while he is left through the dark and coldest night of winter; that is, he remains in the **blackness** <note>original: "nigredine"; referring to the *nigredo* or putrefaction stage</note>, which is a sign of coldness, from which he must be led to **whiteness** <note>original: "albedinem"; the *albedo* stage</note> by a greater intensity of fire, and further yet, by a greater heat, to **redness** <note>original: "rubedinem"; the *rubedo* or final stage of the Great Work</note>. For without heat, as **Bodillus** <note>A philosopher cited in the *Turba Philosophorum*, an influential early alchemical assembly text</note> says in the *Assembly of Philosophers*, nothing is generated; a bath of intense heat causes destruction, but if it is cold, it drives [the life] away, whereas if it is temperate, it becomes suitable and pleasant for the body. In the same place, **Bonellus** <note>Another figure from the *Turba Philosophorum*</note> says, *“All things that live, by God’s will, also die: therefore that nature which has lost its moisture, when it is left through the nights, seems like a corpse; and then that nature requires fire, until the body and its spirit are turned into earth, and then it becomes dust like a dead man in his tomb: these things being finished, God restores to it its spirit and soul, and with all weakness removed, our nature is strengthened and improved.”* Therefore, it is necessary to burn that thing without fear, and so on. Thus fire, which destroys all other things, builds up this one; that which brings death to others brings life to this. There is but one **Phoenix**, which is restored by fire, which is renewed by flames and emerges reborn from the ash; and this, known to the Philosophers alone, is cremated and recalled to life, whatever others may dream about some winged bird that exists nowhere and has never been seen except in fables. Moreover, the **Hermaphrodite** of whom the Philosophers speak is of a mixed nature, male and female, one of which passes into the other through the operation of heat. For from the female, the male is made, which ought not to seem so wonderful in the work of the Philosophers, since many are found to have changed their sex, if history is to be believed. The Poets commemorate **Caeneus, Iphis, and Tiresias** <note>Figures from Greek mythology who underwent gender transformations</note>, as **Pontanus** <note>Giovanni Pontano (1426–1503), an Italian humanist poet</note> does in his work *On the Stars*:

<summary>Maier explains that the alchemical "Hermaphrodite" requires external heat to awaken its internal life, comparing this process to the hibernation of animals and the mythical rebirth of the Phoenix. He links the stages of heat to the transition from the "blackness" of death to the "redness" of the perfected Stone.</summary>

<keywords>Hermaphrodite, Fire, Heat, Nigredo, Phoenix, Transmutation, Turba Philosophorum, Rebirth, Nature</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Hermaphrodite from page 142. While the previous page focused on the "death" and "revival" of the matter through heat using the analogy of hibernating animals, this page moves into historical and medical accounts of biological sex changes to justify the alchemical transformation of the "female" (cold/moist) matter into the "male" (hot/dry) perfection of the Stone.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XXXIII.</header>

> Let them repent of their kind, and prefer to be *Caeneus* <note>original: "Cenea"; referring to the mythological figure Caeneus, who was born a woman (Caenis) and transformed into an invulnerable man by Poseidon</note>, 
> Or to pass entirely into a female form: And *Ausonius* <note>A 4th-century Roman poet</note>:
> *Caeneus mourns, called back into her former shape.*

Thus, during the consulship of *Licinius Crassus* and *C. Cassius Longinus*, a boy was created from a girl at *Cassino*: And *Licinius Mutianus*, as reported in *Pliny* <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist</note>, relates that he saw at Argos one Ariston, whose name had previously been Aristusa; she had even been married, but soon a beard and masculinity emerged, and he took a wife. *Pliny* himself says he saw in Africa a citizen of *Thysdrus* <note>original: "Tisdritanum"; a Roman city in modern-day Tunisia</note> named *L. Cossicius*, who was changed into a male on his very wedding day. That these things are true could be proven by many other examples if there were a need. Indeed, it is certain that when heat is increased, the genital or male members are thrust out from the body. For since a female is far colder than a male, and possesses hidden within her those parts which a male has on the outside—as the physicians say—nature, being at first uncertain whether she was generating a male or a female, expressed a female outwardly, even though she intended a male inwardly. Hence, as heat increases with age and movement is added, the hidden members break forth and show themselves to be seen by all. 

In the same way, among the Philosophers, the female becomes male through increased heat; that is, the **Hermaphrodite** <term>in alchemy, the 'Rebis' or dual-natured matter representing the union of opposites</term> loses its female sex and becomes a strong and stern male, possessing nothing of womanly softness or fickleness. Thus, we have sometimes seen, through the industrious surgical art of that most celebrated physician *Gasparo Tagliacozzi* of Bologna <note>original: "Cass. Tagliacotio Bononiensi"; Tagliacozzi (1597) was a pioneer of plastic and reconstructive surgery</note>, a noble **Hermaphrodite** boy changed—or rather promoted—into a perfect male, fit (as was hoped) for procreating offspring, by making a new opening through the penis (which was lacking there) and closing the lower, woman-like opening. 

The Philosophers do not require all these manual operations. For when the coldness and moisture of the **Moon** <note>associated with Silver and the 'female' principle</note> are present, they call it female; when the heat and dryness of the **Sun** <note>associated with Gold and the 'male' principle</note> are present, they call it male; when all four of those qualities are present together, they call it **Rebis** <term>from the Latin 'res bina', meaning a 'double thing'</term> or **Hermaphrodite**. And so, the conversion of the female—that is, of coldness and moisture—into the male can easily be achieved, which is done by the heat of fire alone, as has been said. For heat sequesters and separates the superfluities of moisture and establishes its own **Idea** <note>the essential form or blueprint</note> in the **Philosophical Subject**, which is the **Tincture** <term>the spiritual essence or 'medicine' capable of transmuting base metals into gold</term>.

<meta>catchword: Im</meta>

<summary>This page explores historical and medical accounts of biological sex changes (specifically female-to-male) as a natural justification for alchemical transmutation. It argues that the "female" state of matter is merely an "incomplete" or "cold" male state, which can be perfected into the "male" Tincture through the application of heat.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical explanation of Emblem XXXIV. While the previous page provided the visual allegory and epigram of the "Philosophical Child" conceived in water and born in air, this discourse interprets those symbols through both historical mythology and laboratory practice, specifically focusing on the stage of putrefaction.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>

# DISCOURSE XXXIV

Human opinion or flattery has attributed marvelous, and certainly fabulous, origins to certain individuals above all others. For instance, it was said that **Alexander the Great** was sired not by Philip, King of the Macedonians, but by **Jupiter Ammon** <note>A Greco-Egyptian deity often depicted with ram's horns; Alexander claimed him as his true father to assert divine right.</note>; that **Romulus and Remus** were born of **Mars**; and that **Plato** was born of the virgin Perictione, who had been visited by a phantom of **Apollo**. In this way, the Pagans wished to demonstrate that they were descended from the gods, just as **Thessalus**, the son of the physician **Hippocrates**, attempted to persuade the Athenians, among other things, that he traced his lineage back to Apollo. 

But we are not so gullible. We know that those whom they claim as their ancestors were neither men nor gods in that sense. Even if these heroes existed as quasi-divine figures among mortals, we realize—through the flattery of subjects, disciples, or others hawking their greatness to the world—that these tales were fabricated and planted in the minds of the common folk through forged writings. 

It is a different matter entirely when the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to the Alchemists, the "Lovers of Wisdom."</note> attribute an unusual conception and birth to their own "son." For this son has a quality unique among all things born into this world: his **conception** takes place in **baths**, and his **birth** in the **air**. 

We know that sterile women, suffering from excessive coldness and dryness, can be greatly assisted by warm baths so that they may be rendered fit and capable for conception. Yet, that a conception should actually *occur* inside the baths, or be completed there, is unheard of. This feat seems to belong to this "son" alone, due to the power of a marvelous nature that is vastly different from others. 

Elsewhere, the Alchemists say that his **conception** happens in the **bottom of the vessel**, and his **birth** in the <term>alembic</term> <note>The top part of a still where vapors condense.</note>. This explanation is more clear: for the waters of the baths, if any are present, will not be at the top or middle of the vessel, but at the bottom; whereas in the alembic, the vapors are "aerial." Therefore, once the conception has taken place, the substance ascends into the alembic, and his birth occurs in the **white color** <note>The stage of *Albedo*, or Whiteness, following the initial darkness.</note>. 

In the bottom of the vessel, **blackness** <note>The stage of *Nigredo*, symbolizing death and decomposition.</note> dominates. Concerning this, the *Rosarium* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a seminal 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> says: *"Conception is when the earth is dissolved into black powder and begins to retain a little of the mercury; for then the male acts upon the female—that is, the <term>Azoth</term> <note>A term for the universal solvent or the essential "mercury" of the philosophers.</note> acts upon the earth."* 

And a little later: *"Conception and betrothal take place in <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The process of breaking down matter so it can be reborn.</note> at the bottom of the vessel, and the generation of those generated will occur in the air—namely, in the head of the vessel, which is the alembic."* 

Furthermore, "conception in the baths" is nothing other than **putrefaction in dung** <note>original: "putrefactio in fimo" — Alchemists often used the steady heat of fermenting horse manure as a low-temperature incubator for their vessels.</note>. For the same *Rosarium* continues: *"The body does nothing unless it be putrefied, and it cannot be putrefied except with mercury."* And soon pu- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the word "putrefactio" (putrefaction) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This discourse contrasts the mythological divine births of figures like Alexander the Great and Plato with the symbolic "birth" of the Alchemical Stone. It explains that the "conception in baths" represents the stage of putrefaction at the bottom of the vessel, while the "birth in air" refers to the sublimation and whitening of the substance in the alembic.</summary>

<keywords>putrefaction, alembic, Alexander the Great, Plato, Rosarium Philosophorum, Azoth, Mercury, Nigredo, Albedo, Alchemical Vessel</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXXIV. The previous page ended with the beginning of the word "putrefaction," which is completed here. This section transitions from the philosophical origins of the "son" to the practical laboratory conditions required for his development, moving through the stages of blackness (putrefaction), whiteness (distillation), and redness (perfection).</meta>

->DISCOURSE XXXIV. 147<-

...putrefaction <note>original: "putrefaction"; the process of breaking down a substance through gentle heat to allow for its transformation.</note> be performed by the most gentle heat of warm and moist dung, and by no other means, so that nothing ascends. For if anything were to ascend, a separation of the parts would occur, which ought not to happen until the male and the female are perfectly joined and one has received the other. The sign of this is a blackness upon the surface, which indicates a perfect solution. Its "birth" is white, occurring at the summit of the mountains—that is, in the air or the alembic <note>A cap or still-head used in the process of distillation.</note>. This is explained by Rosinus <note>The Latinized name of Zosimos of Panopolis, a 3rd-century alchemist.</note> to Euthicia in this way: The wise man said: "Take things from their mines and exalt them to higher places, and cast them down from the peak of their mountains and lead them back to their roots." And later: By "mountains" he signified the cucurbits <term>cucurbit: a glass vessel used in distillation, named for its gourd-like shape</term>, and by the "peaks of the mountains," the alembics. To "cast down" according to this likeness is to receive their water through the alembic into the receiver; to "lead back to the roots" is to return it upon that from which it came. He named the cucurbits "mountains" because the Sun and Moon <note>In alchemy, the Sun and Moon represent Gold and Silver, or the active and passive principles.</note> are found in mountains; likewise, in these mountains which are cucurbits, their own Sun and Moon are generated. Thus he spoke.

Afterward, it becomes red and begins to "walk upon the waters"—that is, upon the metals liquefied in the fire, which stand like mercurial water. For this is the lord of the waters, over which he exercises power like Neptune; he is the king of the sea and the possessor of the mountains. They tell of **Xerxes**, the King of the Persians, that when he was about to move his expedition into Greece, he sent an embassy to the sea and to Mount Athos, ordering that they should do him no injury—the one by its waves, the other by its surges of flame—otherwise he would exact punishment from both. But the story was told to deaf ears: the sea submerged some of his ships, and Athos destroyed many by fire. At this the King grew angry and, as if he were the master of the sea and mountains, he ordered a certain number of lashes to be inflicted upon the sea, and a great part of the mountain to be thrown into the water. But these things demonstrate the audacity rather than the prudence of such a great king. 

The subject of whom we speak, however, purifies all waters from obstacles and impurities, not by edict, but by deed. He walks freely through them and (what is more) congeals them, so that they support chariots by their hardness where they previously carried ships. He himself levels mountains with valleys and does not fear the flames of fire; therefore, wherever he wishes, he hastens freely from the **Pillars of Hercules** <note>The Strait of Gibraltar, the western edge of the known world.</note> to the furthest shore of India at the **Pillars of Dionysus** <note>The eastern boundary of the world in classical geography.</note>.

->T ij<-

### XXXV. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Look upon Triptolemus and Achilles, courageous in the fray,  
As they were prepared by their mothers to endure the heat;  
Ceres hardened the former, and Thetis the latter, in the fire  
By night, and gave them milk by day of a good kind;  
Not otherwise must the Medicine of the Sages <term>Medicine of the Sages: Another name for the Philosopher's Stone, believed to heal both metals and the human body.</term> be governed,  
As a child at the mother's breast, nourished through the fire.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Emblem XXXV, moving from the poetic epigram to a philosophical and alchemical discourse on the power of "habituation." It connects the social education of humans and animals to the physical tempering of minerals in the alchemist's furnace.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXV.

->**DISCOURSE XXXV.**<-

Lycurgus, the famous Spartan lawgiver, demonstrated to the people in a public assembly how much influence habit and use—whether good or bad—possess, using a visible example. He brought out two puppies born of the same litter. In their midst, he placed a pot full of porridge and a live hare. One of the puppies, seeing the food and the hare, left the food and chased the hare because he had been trained to do so; the other, however, ignored the hare and emptied the pot, for he had been accustomed to that. When this was done, Lycurgus said: "Behold, here you see how much education and habituation from a young age matters, even in those whom nature produced as equals and near-identical siblings." In this same way,

<margin>5</margin>

it is proper to improve nature or direct it toward better things; for nature is like wax, such that it can easily be molded toward vice or virtue. What Lycurgus proved true in political matters, the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to natural scientists and alchemists.</note> also agree holds true in Physics <note>Original: *Physicis*, referring to the study of the natural world.</note>. Daily examples are seen throughout the whole world of how much habituation matters in men and beasts. In vegetables, too, many examples occur. But that the same should occur in minerals and metals is a matter of rarer experience. Nevertheless, the practitioners "fix" <note>To "fix" a substance in alchemy is to make a volatile material stable so that it does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat.</note> their Stone through habituation in a fire suitable to it, just as they suggest in countless places. For it must be nourished with fire, just as a child is nourished by milk upon its mother's breasts. Hence Emiganus says: "Look upon the nursing child, and do not hinder him." And Bodillus says: "The offspring, once extracted, is nourished only by milk and by fire, through its own nature and gradually while it is small; and the more it is scorched, its bones being strengthened, it is led into youth, and reaching that stage, it is self-sufficient." And Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova, a famous medieval alchemist.</note> in the *Rosary of the Philosophers*, book 2, chapter 7, says: "It is necessary, however, that the medicine itself be roasted over the fire for a long time and nourished like a child at the breast." The most ancient Philosophers wished to demonstrate these same things in the allegories of Triptolemus and Achilles, who were accustomed to lie beneath the fire and were hardened by it. Both characters denote nothing other than the chemical subject; otherwise, the fable would be tasteless and unworthy of being adapted to moral things or drummed into the ears of learned men. Ceres, acting as a nurse, nourished Triptolemus with milk by day and placed him beneath the fires by night. Because the boy was being nourished so excellently, his father Eleusius eventually observed this. For this reason, Ceres slew Eleusius and gave the boy Triptolemus a chariot drawn by serpents, with which 

<summary>This discourse uses the Spartan lawgiver Lycurgus's story of two puppies to explain that just as living things are shaped by habit, alchemical metals must be "habituated" to fire to achieve stability. It links this chemical tempering to the myths of Ceres and Thetis, who placed their children in fire to make them immortal.</summary>

<keywords>habituation, Lycurgus, education, fixation, Philosopher's Stone, Ceres, Triptolemus, Achilles, Arnold of Villanova, alchemy, tempering</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXXV, focusing on the mythological figure of Triptolemus as an allegory for the alchemical process. It transitions from the general concept of habituation to the specific "nurturing" of the Philosopher’s Stone in the fire.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXV.

->**DISCOURSE XXXV.**<-

by which he hastened through the air into all parts of the world and taught mortals the sowing of grain. But this Triptolemus is the **Tincture of the Philosophers** <note>original: *tinctura Philosophorum*</note>, nourished under the fire in such a way that, drawn by serpents—that is, by **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurio*</note>—he taught men how the **Seeds of the Philosophers** <note>original: *semina Philosophorum*</note> must be cast into their own earth. These same deeds are ascribed to **Osiris**, who traveled across the orb of the earth for this reason (as we have demonstrated elsewhere), and to **Dionysus** <note>Also known as Bacchus, the god of wine.</note>, who journeyed through the world teaching men the use of wine. In truth, these three—Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysus—share one purpose and office; indeed, they are one and the same thing.

This is also like **Achilles**, who was to be sent as the strongest warrior to the Trojan War. His father was **Peleus** (which signifies "earth" or "Mount Peleus") and his mother was **Thetis**, a goddess of the sea or of the waters; from these, Achilles is born. At their wedding, the golden apple of **Eris** <note>The goddess of Discord; her "Apple of Discord" began the chain of events leading to the Trojan War.</note> was brought forth as the first cause of the Trojan War; thus, being born from that marriage, he emerged not without reason as the war's executor. Achilles is said to have been "hardened" by his mother in the same way as the aforementioned Triptolemus was by **Ceres** <note>The goddess of agriculture; in myth, she placed Triptolemus in fire to make him immortal.</note>. We have spoken sufficiently about this in Book 6 of the *Hieroglyphics*, so we deem it unnecessary to repeat it here.

The nourishment of the **Stone** <note>term: *Lapis* — The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical Great Work.</note> is therefore fire. However, one should not wrongly think that it expands in length, breadth, or depth because of this, nor does it increase in weight; rather, it acquires its power, maturity, and color from the fire, bringing all other things with it like provisions and travel supplies. For when its parts have been gathered from various places, purified, and joined together, it possesses within itself everything it needs. Whence the Philosopher says in the *Rosary* <note>original: *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical series of images and texts.</note>:

> *This water brings with it everything that the offspring requires.*

Indeed, nothing foreign is added to it from the beginning to the end unless it is of the same kind <note>original: *homogeneum*</note>, nor is anything separated unless it is of a different kind <note>original: *heterogeneum*</note>. Anyone, however, must take care to properly recognize the dragons that must be yoked to the chariot of Triptolemus before beginning anything; these are winged and volatile. If you wish to know them, you will find them in the **philosophical dung** <note>term: *fimus Philosophicus* — A metaphor for the gentle, moist heat of fermentation or a common, base substance from which the work begins.</note>. For they are dung, and they are generated from dung, and they are that **vessel** which **Mary** <note>referring to Mary the Jewess, a legendary foundational alchemist often credited with inventing the *bain-marie* or water bath.</note> says is not "necromantic" <note>meaning it does not involve dark magic or the summoning of spirits.</note>, but is the regulation of your fire, without which you will achieve nothing. I have opened the truth to you, which I have drawn with incredible labor—and not without the expense of many years—from the monuments of the ancients.

### XXXVI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

They say the Stone is a rejected and base thing,
Lying in the road, so that both rich and poor may rightly have it.
Others say that it is to be seen in high mountains,
Or in the air; others, that it is nourished in the meadows <note>original: *Awen*, referring to watery floodplains or meadows near rivers</note>.
All is true, according to its proper understanding; I wish for you to perceive
Such a gift on the mountains, where it is most easily found.

### EPIGRAM XXXVI.

*The* **STONE** <note>original: *Lapis*</note> *is said to be a vile dross and to lie*
->*by chance in the roads, so that from here both the rich and the poor may obtain it for themselves.*<-

->*within the air, while others say it is nourished by the rivers.*<-

->*to seek such great gifts in mountain places.*<-

<summary>This page presents Emblem XXXVI of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens," focusing on the paradoxical nature of the Philosopher's Stone (Mercury), which is described as being simultaneously common as dirt and hidden in the highest reaches of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Stone, Mercury, Secrets of Nature, Alchemy, Michael Maier, Epigram, Four Elements, Prima Materia</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem XXXVI, expanding upon the paradoxical nature of the Philosopher's Stone described in the previous page's epigram and illustration. The text explores why such a precious object is described as being "cast upon the earth" and "vile."</meta>

<header># DISCOURSE XXXVI.</header>

All those who have heard of the name and power of the **stone** <note>original: *lapis*; the Philosopher's Stone</note>, unless they are among the completely incredulous, are wont to ask where it is to be found, so that they may run towards it as if by a straight path. The Philosophers answer in two ways: First, that Adam brought it with him from Paradise, that it is in you, in me, and in every person, and that the birds <note>original: *volantes*; literally "the flying ones"</note> carry it with them from distant places. Second, that it is found in the earth, mountains, air, and river. Which path should be followed, then? This one or that? Both, in my opinion, but each in its own respect; although this second path pleases us more and seems safer. 

It is said to be "cast upon the earth" because the element of earth first appears in a dark and black body. Then, it is so called because it is a vile thing of little price, which is trodden underfoot in the paths of travelers and in the dung itself. Whence the *Rosary* <note>original: *Rosarius*; referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> says: 
> "And if I called it by its true name, the foolish would not believe it to be so." 

And Morienus <note>a legendary hermit-alchemist</note>, replying to Khalid <note>original: *Calidi*; Prince Khalid ibn Yazid, an early patron of alchemy</note> when he asked if much of it could be found, answers: 
> "It is only as the wise man says: available to the rich and the poor, the generous and the greedy, to the traveler and the sitter. For it is cast in the paths, and trodden underfoot in their dung-heaps, and many have already dug in dung-heaps to extract this from them, and in this they were deceived." 

Mundus <note>a speaker in the *Turba Philosophorum* or "Assembly of the Philosophers"</note> also says in the *Turba*: 
> "If the sellers knew it, they would not sell it for such a low price." 

And Arnold <note>original: *Arnoldus*; Arnold of Villanova, a medieval physician and alchemist</note> asserts that the stone can be had for free, in as great a quantity as anyone wants, nor should anyone be questioned for that reason. All these things are true. For who, unless they were inhumane, would deny water and earth to someone asking for them? The ancient Cimbri <note>a Germanic or Celtic tribe that fought Rome</note>, when they had asked for these same gifts from the Romans and could not obtain them, invaded Italy with vast forces and slaughtered many thousands of Romans along with their Consuls, as the histories testify. 

For the earth, as the mother of all, is most precious; yet as the final matter of putrefied things, it is most vile. Nothing is viler than mud or filth, which nevertheless is nothing else than earth mixed with water. What is more common than a clod of earth? Yet Eurypylus, son of Neptune, offered it as a guest-gift to the Heroic Argonauts; because they did not refuse it but accepted it with a grateful mind, Medea prophesied many things after it was dissolved in the water. For it is necessary that the earth be dissolved in water; otherwise, neither the one nor the other is worth anything. In this way, the stone is cast upon the earth, in which, however, it does not remain cast away,

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<summary>Maier explains the alchemical paradox that the Philosopher's Stone is both a divine substance from Paradise and a common, "vile" material found in dung-heaps and dirt. He uses classical and alchemical authorities to argue that the "earth" of the stone must be dissolved in "water" to become valuable.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Adam, Rosarium Philosophorum, Morienus, Khalid, Arnold of Villanova, Cimbri, Eurypylus, Argonauts, Medea, earth and water, putrefaction</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XXXVI from page 154. The previous page discussed how the Philosopher’s Stone is "cast upon the earth" and found in "vile" places like dung-heaps. This page shifts focus to its more "exalted" locations in mountains, air, and water, identifying the Stone’s material with the god Mercury.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXVI.

...but it is lifted up to the mountains, such as Athos, Vesuvius, Etna, and similar flame-vomiting peaks, many of which are seen in various parts of the world. For in these places a perpetual fire burns, which sublimates <note>to convert a solid directly into vapor by heat, often used in alchemy to "purify" a substance</note> the **stone**, and raises it to the highest dignity. Just as it grows in the mountains in a raw form from sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: *argento vivo*</note>, so it is matured and perfected at the peaks of the mountains. There, a certain herb also grows, without which the fire cannot be tempered; for when this cold and moist herb is cast into the fire, its violence is blunted by its opposite. From the mountains, it passes into the air, where it finds a home. For the air becomes a house for it, surrounding it; this is nothing other than what is meant by it being "carried in the belly of the wind" and "born in the air," terms which have been discussed previously. 

Finally, **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius*; the Roman messenger god, here representing the alchemical element of the same name</note> is nourished in the rivers—that is, in the waters. For this reason, the Athenians used to perform the *hydrophoria* <note>a Greek festival involving the carrying of water, which the author links to alchemical processes</note> in his honor, because the material of the **Philosophers’ Stone** is water, as the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*</note> says, and this is understood as the "water of those three." 

For this reason, Mercury was also called "three-headed," as he was of the sea, the heavens, and the earth, since he was present in water, earth, and air. He is said to have been raised by **Vulcan** <note>the god of fire and smithing</note>, and is noted for his thievery, because Mercury is taught to become accustomed to the fire, yet he is volatile and carries away with him whatever he is mixed with. In ancient times, he dictated laws and discipline to the Egyptians, and indeed religion to the Theban priests and a great part of the world, because the Egyptians derived their governance and sacred rites from <term>chemical principles</term> <note>original: *chymicis institutis*; the author argues that ancient religion and law were actually based on the secrets of alchemy</note>; from them the Greeks, from the Greeks the Romans, and from those many other nations, as has been demonstrated more extensively elsewhere. 

He killed Argus with a rock (or stone) and turned Battus into a touchstone <note>original: *lapidem indicem*; a stone used to test the purity of gold and silver</note>. Why say more? All the volumes of the <term>alchemists</term> <note>original: *Chymicorum*</note> emphasize nothing but Mercury, and they confirm his power sufficiently with this single verse: 

> *In Mercury is whatever the wise seek.*

Therefore, he must be sought until he is found, whether he lingers in the air, fire, waters, or earth. For he is a wanderer, running now here, now there, in the service of the "chemical gods," as if at their feet—a duty indicated by the fact that some ascribe to him a daughter named Angelia <note>from the Greek word for "message" or "news," emphasizing Mercury's role as a messenger</note>.

V ij
<meta>catchword: Three (original: Drey)</meta>

<summary>This page describes the omnipresence of Mercury (the Philosopher's Stone) across all elements—fire, air, earth, and water—and identifies it as the source of ancient Egyptian law and religion. The text characterizes Mercury as a volatile, transformative substance that must be "tempered" and "perfected" through alchemical processes.</summary>
