### XXXVII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Three things belong to the Mastery: water that is stinking,
And the snow-white smoke, together with the green lion for certain;
Water is the mother of all elements; for the appearance,
So that it might be made, water must be the first and the last;
The green lion is called the copper of <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy; his "copper" or "ore" often refers to a specific stage of purified matter</note> and the Stone,
Recognized in the chapters of the books; the white smoke is known as water.

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page provides the prose commentary (Discourse 37) for the previous page's emblem. It elaborates on the three essential components of the alchemical "Mastery"—the foundation, walls, and roof—paralleling them with the three substances introduced: white smoke, the green lion, and stinking water.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXVII.

Just as three essentials are required for the construction of any building—without any one of which no perfection can exist—namely, the foundation, the walls, and the roof, so too are three things needed to complete the compound of the Philosophers, which are named here by their proper names. The Author of the *Aurora* <note>original: "Author Auroræ"; likely referring to the *Aurora Consurgens*, a famous medieval alchemical treatise</note>, speaking in Chapter 20 on the separation of elements, says: "Earth is left behind in that place, so that the other three elements may take root in it; if Earth were not present, the elements would have no foundation upon which to build the new House of the Treasury." This foundation is called here "stinking water," which is the mother of all elements, according to the *Rosarium* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a key 16th-century alchemical text</note>, from which, through which, and with which the Philosophers prepare the <term>Elixir</term> <note>A substance believed to be capable of transmuting metals or prolonging life</note> at the beginning and at the end.

It is called "stinking" because it emits a sulfurous stench and the odor of graves. This is that water which Pegasus drew out from Parnassus by striking it with his hoof <note>Referring to the fountain of Hippocrene, created by the winged horse's hoof-strike</note>, which the mountain of Nonacris in Arcadia <note>A reference to the icy and toxic waters of the River Styx, which ancient legend claimed could only be contained in a vessel made of a horse's hoof</note> sends forth from its ridges, breaking out from the rock, and which can only be kept in a horse’s hoof because of its extreme strength. This is the "dragon’s water," as the *Rosarium* names it, which must be produced through an <term>alembic</term> <note>A type of distilling apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note> without any other substance added; in the making of it, a great stench is produced. 

When certain people heard these words, they turned to distilling human or animal dung; in this operation, they certainly perceived a great stench, but they found only dung within dung. But do not think the Philosophers are dung-beetles <note>original: "scarabæos"</note> who work in filth; know instead that the stench, if any is present, is soon changed into a great fragrance, just as Lullius <note>Ramon Lull, a prominent 13th-century philosopher often associated with alchemical works</note> testifies concerning his <term>Quinta Essentia</term> <note>The "Fifth Essence" or quintessence, the purest and most concentrated part of a substance</note>. To this, he assigns such a sweetness of scent—if it is made correctly—that if placed in an upper room of a house, it draws flying birds to itself and makes them stop. However, he places his quintessence in dung <note>Meaning he uses a "dung-bath" (balneum fimi) to provide a gentle, steady heat for fermentation or digestion</note>, by whose most temperate heat that fragrance follows. Certain people tried this with strong wine, but in vain, and therefore they accused Lullius of vanity, when they themselves were rather to be convicted of folly, having never tasted the wine of Lullius. But that golden poet understood Lullius better, who sings thus in the *Chrysopoeia*, Book 1 <note>A didactic poem on the making of gold by Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli</note>:

> *But he indeed did not perceive those things which at first*
> *glance he seemed to say, nor was he then mixing*
> *dripping wines, etc.*
<note>original: "At non ille quidem sensit, quæ dicere primâ / Est facie visus, neque tum stillantia vina / Miscebat, &c."</note>

<meta>Catchword: After (original: Post)</meta>

<summary>This discourse identifies the "stinking water" as the vital foundation of the alchemical work, comparing it to the foundation of a building. It warns readers against literal interpretations of "stench" (such as working with actual filth), explaining that this substance is a refined philosophical water that ultimately produces a heavenly fragrance.</summary>

<keywords>stinking water, foundation, Elixir, Pegasus, Rosarium Philosophorum, Quinta Essentia, Lullius, distillation, fragrance vs stench</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse 37, which describes the three essential components of the alchemical Work. Having discussed the "stinking water" (the foundation) on the previous page, the author now proceeds to the "Green Lion" and the "White Smoke."</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
# DISCOURSE XXXVII.

After the stinking water, the <term>Green Lion</term> <note>A common alchemical symbol often representing raw mineral ore, vitriol, or a powerful acidic solvent capable of "devouring" metals.</note> occurs. Concerning this, the <term>Rosarium</term> <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical text.</note> says: "You have asked about greenness, thinking that copper was a leprous body because of that greenness it has. For I tell you that all that is perfect in copper is that greenness alone which is in it: because that greenness, through our mastery, is quickly turned into our truest gold, and we have experienced this. However, you will not be able to prepare the stone without green and liquid <term>duenech</term> <note>original: "duenech"; an archaic alchemical term often referring to antimony or a specific mineral spirit.</note>, which is seen to be born in our mines. O blessed greenness, which generates all things! Whence you should know that no vegetable nor any fruit appears by sprouting without a green color being there; likewise, know that the generation of this thing is green, which is why the Philosophers called it the 'germ' [seed] itself." Thus says the *Rosarium*. This is the gold and copper of the Philosophers and the stone known in the chapters: smoke, vapor, and water, the <term>spittle of the moon</term> <note>original: "sputum lunæ"; likely referring to white mercury or a refined distillate.</note>, which is joined to the light of the Sun. This **green lion** fights with the dragon; but it is overcome by it and, in the course of time, is devoured. Once the lion has putrefied, a sweetness is hoped to come forth from its mouth (just as from the lion killed by Samson) <note>A biblical reference to Judges 14:8, where Samson finds honey in the carcass of a lion he had slain.</note>. The <term>Dragon</term> <note>In alchemy, the dragon often symbolizes volatile or acidic substances.</note>, having become superior, fills itself so much with the lion’s flesh that after a little while it bursts and dies. From this—since the fat of a lion by itself heals daily fevers, and grace and favor are won for those anointed with it among kings and peoples—a most excellent **Medicine** can be made, which is most useful for many ailments.

Thirdly follows the <term>white smoke</term> <note>The vapors produced during the sublimation of alchemical substances.</note>, which if it be coagulated, becomes water; and this water performs the service of washing, dissolving, and wiping away stains like soap. This is the <term>fire against nature</term>, which see that you find; so called because it is contrary to nature by unweaving and destroying that which nature composed with diligent care. This fire is not kindled from spirit of wine or oil, but from incombustible matter, of equal duration and heat; it is a fire without light and combustion, of great virtue and efficacy. To find it in the darkness, since it does not shine, is of no small difficulty—but to apply it to the work in the proper manner is far greater. We have sufficiently described its circumstances and properties in various places.

<meta>catchword: Das</meta>

<summary>This page describes the second and third components of the alchemical mastery: the "Green Lion" (a vital mineral force) and the "White Smoke" (a cleansing "fire against nature"). The text uses the metaphor of a battle between a lion and a dragon to illustrate the chemical transformations required to create a universal medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Green Lion, Rosarium Philosophorum, duenech, spittle of the moon, Dragon, Samson's lion, white smoke, fire against nature, alchemical medicine</keywords>

### XXXVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

The ancients called two things by the name of <term>Rebis</term>,
Since in one body, man and woman are joined together.
For <term>Hermaphroditus</term> <note>In Greek mythology, the son of Hermes (Mercury) and Aphrodite (Venus) who became physically united with a nymph, possessing both male and female qualities.</note> was chosen upon two mountains,
Whom <term>Venus</term> bore to <term>Mercury</term>.
Do not despise this double sex, which is both woman and man,
For it can afterwards give birth to the <term>King</term> <note>The "King" is a common alchemical symbol for the completed Philosopher’s Stone or perfected Gold.</note> for you.

<header>EMBLEM XXXVIII. On the secrets of Nature.</header>

## The <term>Rebis</term>, like a <term>Hermaphrodite</term>, is born from two mountains: those of <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Venus</term>.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving from an emblem book. In the foreground, the Roman gods Mercury and Venus are seated on a grassy bank. Mercury, on the left, wears a winged Petasos and sandals, with his caduceus lying on the ground behind him. He embraces Venus, who is semi-nude with a red cloth draped over her lower body. To their right, a small winged Cupid holds a golden torch aloft. In the background, a two-headed nude figure representing the Rebis or Hermaphroditus stands atop a landscape featuring two distinct, rocky hills. The scene is set in a rustic landscape with trees and hills under a clear sky. A red oval library stamp with the letters "B.R." and a crown is visible at the bottom center of the image.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XXXVIII.

*The ancients called the **REBIS** <note>original: "REBIS"; derived from the Latin "res bina," meaning a double thing or matter.</note> a double thing, because in one*
*Body this is both male and female, an **Androgynous** being.*
*For **HERMAPHRODITUS** is said to be born on two mountains,*
*Whom nourishing **Venus** bore to **Hermes** <note>Hermes is the Greek name for the god Mercury.</note>.*
*Do not despise this dual sex, for the same*
*Male and one and the same female will give you the **King** <note>The "King" refers to the Philosophers' Stone or the ultimate goal of the alchemical Great Work.</note>.*

<sig>X</sig>
<meta>catchword: So-</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the 38th emblem of Michael Maier's Atalanta Fugiens, depicting the Rebis—a mythological and alchemical hermaphrodite—resulting from the union of Mercury and Venus. It symbolizes the reconciliation of opposites and the dual nature of the primary material used in the alchemical process.</summary>
<keywords>Rebis, Hermaphroditus, Mercury, Venus, Androgyny, Alchemical King, duality, union of opposites</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Discourse 38, following the visual emblem of Mercury and Venus and the epigram regarding their offspring, the Hermaphrodite. While the previous page introduced the "double thing" (Rebis) poetically, this discourse provides a philosophical and geographical context for its origin.</meta>

<page-num>163</page-num>
<header>D I S C O U R S E XXXVIII.</header>

SOCRATES, when asked from where he came, replied that he was a *Cosmopolitan*, or a citizen of the world. By this, he wished to imply that, although he was born in *Athens* in the body, he nevertheless freely traversed the whole world in his mind as if it were his homeland and all that is contained within it; for to a wise man, every land where he may live well is his country. In the same way, if anyone should ask the Philosophers from where their *Hermaphrodite* <note>original: "Hermaphroditus"; a symbolic figure representing the union of opposites, specifically Mercury and Venus.</note> comes, they reply that it is "of the world," or that it is available in every corner of the world where the elements may be found. It is, namely, the son of the wise, who shares a common homeland with them. Yet, since it does not happen that one is born twice or several times, but enters this light first in one specific place—just as *Socrates* is recognized as an *Athenian*—so the <term>Rebis</term> <note>original: "Rebis"; a term derived from the Latin "res bina," meaning a "double thing" or "two-fold matter."</note> is considered an inhabitant of two mountains: specifically those of *Mercury & Venus*. From these two parents, the name *Hermaphrodite* was bestowed upon it. These mountains are its household gods and its exalted fatherland; therefore, it exists as one born from a high place. Indeed, a noble and grand fatherland is of no small importance for performing distinguished deeds, for in such a place citizens are preferred over foreigners and promoted to public offices so that they do not remain hidden in obscurity, as happens in lowly places. And even if some light reaches them from their fatherland, it is a greater thing to emerge through one's own virtues, even if one's domestic circumstances are narrow, and to provide light to one's own country from oneself. In this manner, these mountains—unknown to many—acquire fame from the *Hermaphrodite* because of its illustrious deeds and its name, which is most celebrated throughout the whole world. For who, having been even slightly versed in the books of the Philosophers, has not recognized the *Rebis*? Who has not seen or noticed the two-headed *Androgynous* being? This figure is indeed known even as far as the *Indians*, and its fame is spread wider than even that of King *Alexander*. Very many people set out from distant shores to see and speak with some learned man, or one otherwise very well known for singular military industry, art, or science; however, many more would betake themselves to the said Mountains of the *Rebis*, provided they knew where they were to be found. With what zeal and care <term>Morienus</term> <note>Morienus was a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist who reportedly taught the secrets of the art to King Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> the Alexandrian, having set out from *Rome*, sought and finally found it, he himself testifies in his book. Therefore, he is to be judged more fortunate and pleasing to God, because he learned of this thing—that is, the birthplace of the *Rebis*—and saw it in person from a living teacher and not from "mute masters" <note>original: "mutis magistris"; a common philosophical idiom referring to books, which provide knowledge but cannot answer questions or demonstrate actions like a living tutor.</note>.

<meta>catchword: Not</meta>

<summary>This discourse uses the figure of Socrates as a 'citizen of the world' to explain the dual nature and universal presence of the Rebis, while specifying its symbolic birthplace on the mountains of Mercury and Venus. It emphasizes that while the matter is everywhere, the actual discovery of its 'homeland' or true nature requires diligent seeking, as exemplified by the alchemist Morienus.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Cosmopolitan, Hermaphrodite, Rebis, Mercury, Venus, Alchemy, Morienus the Alexandrian, dual nature, philosophical mountains</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse XXXVIII, expanding on the allegorical geography of the Hermaphrodite’s homeland. The previous page established that the "Rebis" or "double-thing" is born of two parents—Mercury and Venus—and resides on their mountains. This page uses the metaphor of a difficult sea voyage and a mountain ascent to describe the arduous search for this alchemical mystery.</meta>

<page-num>163</page-num>

### DISCOURSE XXXVIII.

No less diligence and persistence must be employed by those who seek the homeland of the King from within themselves, guided by reason and the evidence of books. Yet, although a certain clarity may sometimes seem to exist in books, a great obscurity surrounds and veils it on every side, so that the light can scarcely be recognized or distinguished from the darkness. For this reason, one must proceed cautiously with these texts, lest those things prepared as a remedy be consumed as poison. 

Certainly, they are an immense ocean, in which wandering sailors—having tested their astronomical instruments—can know their latitude (the elevation of the equator above the horizon) as the magnet points to the North Pole. But they can by no means know their longitude, or by how many degrees of distance they are separated from the Prime Meridian <note>The "first meridian" was historically set at the Fortunate Isles (the Canaries) by Ptolemy.</note> near the Fortunate Isles. Thus, they remain uncertain of where they are positioned between the sunset and the sunrise. 

What, then, is to be done here? The same as those sailors usually do: consult experience through reason, and learn from her how to determine the long journey through particular signs—promontories, islands, and other landmarks—lest the unwary fall upon sandbanks and reefs. Yet here there is less danger if the matter does not succeed; but if it does, there is greater profit than in a sea voyage, where goods and life may be lost in a single hour. 

Furthermore, the mountain of **Philosophical Mercury** <term>Mercurius Philosophicus</term> <note>The transformative "living" mercury of the alchemists, not the common metal.</note> is not Mount Nonacris, nor Atlas, where he is elsewhere believed to have been born, but the double-peaked **Parnassus**. Upon one peak dwells **Hermes**, and upon the other, **Venus**. Here also is **Apollo** with the **Muses**, and the Pegasus-spring, **Hippocrene** <note>original: "Hippocrene"; the fountain on Mount Helicon said to have been struck open by the hoof of the winged horse Pegasus.</note>, with the evergreen laurel. It is one mountain in name but twofold in reality, just as the **Hermaphrodite** is seen as two-headed and two-limbed in a single body. 

But who among a thousand perseveres in striving for the summit of this mountain? Who does not remain stuck at the roots, hindered by I know not what delays? How few ever reach its middle navel?

> *For the ascent is not easy if one seeks the heights,*
> *Much sweat carries him there; he dies to the*
> *Insomnia of the nocturnal oil lamp <note>original: "nocturnae olivae"; referring to the olive oil used in lamps for late-night study.</note>, and destroys what he*
> *Just now praised in himself—he who desires to be*
> *Gifted with the honor of the eternal leaf.*

Therefore, it is no wonder if at least one in ten thousand exhausts himself in these **Herculean labors** so that he may plant his foot upon the summit of the Mountain and enjoy the immortal prize of the laurel. It is to be uniquely hoped that all those of good mind—the teachable, and those devoted to virtue and literature—may rejoice in this, while the *Grilli* <note>Referring to Gryllus, a companion of Odysseus whom Circe turned into a pig and who refused to be turned back; here signifying the ignorant or base-minded.</note> and the *vertagi* <note>A type of swift hunting dog or greyhound; here perhaps meaning those who chase the goal with thoughtless speed.</note> are frustrated.

<sig>X 2</sig>

<summary>This page compares the alchemical search to a difficult nautical voyage and the ascent of the double-peaked Mount Parnassus, where Mercury and Venus reside. It emphasizes that while many begin the journey, very few possess the persistence and wisdom to reach the summit and obtain the "immortal laurel" of the Stone.</summary>

### XXXIX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Oedipus, through his art, cunningly brought to death the Sphinx, who was very terrifying to the citizens of Thebes with her riddles.
The question was asked: What has four feet in the morning, only two at noon, and three feet again in the evening? <note>This refers to the famous Riddle of the Sphinx. The answer is Man: as an infant he crawls on four limbs, as an adult he walks on two, and in old age he uses a staff as a third leg.</note>
The conqueror of this riddle kills his father, who would not give way, and rightfully <note>In alchemy, this "incestuous" union often symbolizes the returning of a substance to its own "mother" or primary matter to be purified and reborn.</note> takes his own mother as his wife.

<header>EMBLEM XXXIX. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>

# Oedipus, having overcome the Sphinx and slain his father Laius, takes his mother as his wife.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving illustrating the myth of Oedipus. In the foreground, a young man in a red tunic (Oedipus) stands beside an elderly man with a walking stick (Laius). To their left, a figure crawls on all fours, representing the first stage of the Sphinx's riddle. In the mid-ground, a creature with a woman's torso and a serpentine tail (the Sphinx) is shown twice: once perched on a cliff and once being addressed by a man in Roman-style armor. In the background is a fortified city with hills behind it. To the right, a royal couple (Oedipus and Jocasta) is depicted. A red circular library stamp ("B.R") is visible at the bottom of the illustration.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XXXIX.

*Oedipus, by his skill, had turned the Sphinx—who was to be feared by the Thebans for her enigmatic speech—toward her own death. <note>original: "Sphyngem ænigmatico Thebis sermone timendam Oedypus ad propriam torserat arte necem."</note>*
*The question was asked: who has four feet in the morning, but two at midday, and three when evening arrives? <note>This is the famous Riddle of the Sphinx. The answer is "Man," who crawls on all fours as a baby, walks on two legs as an adult, and uses a staff (a third leg) in old age. In alchemy, this often represents the transformation of the "Stone" through different geometric or elemental states.</note>*
*The victor then slew Laius, who refused to give way, and took as his wife her who was his mother. <note>original: "Victor abhinc Lajum nolentem cedere cædit, Ducit & uxorem quæ sibi mater erat." In alchemical terms, the "slaying of the father" and "marriage to the mother" symbolize the dissolution of the old form and the conjunction of the chemical substances to create a new, unified whole.</note>*

<sig>X 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: BA-</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates and recounts the myth of Oedipus solving the Sphinx's riddle and his subsequent tragic fate, serving as a metaphor for the alchemist's intellectual mastery over the complexities of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Oedipus, Sphinx, Riddle, Laius, Thebes, Alchemical Marriage, Transformation, Geometry</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XXXIX. While the previous page provided the poetic epigram and the visual representation of Oedipus and the Sphinx, this page begins "Discourse 39," providing a philosophical and alchemical explanation of the Sphinx’s significance as a guardian of secret knowledge.</meta>

-># DISCOURSE XXXIX.<-

**Bacasser** <note>A philosopher frequently cited in the *Turba Philosophorum*, an early Latin alchemical text.</note> the Philosopher said in the assembly: "That which you seek is by no means of small price, for you seek the greatest treasure and the most excellent gift of God." And know, O investigators, that which the Philosophers long ago intimated, saying: **THAT WHICH IS RIGHT** <note>original: "QUOD RECTUM"</note> is not discerned except through error, and nothing generates more pain in the heart than error in this art and work. For while one thinks that he has succeeded and holds the world in his hands, he will find nothing.

The ancient Philosophers wished to hint at these same things by putting forward the **Sphinx**, as if she herself denoted the obscurity and entanglements of the art. For this reason, the Egyptians—in the sacred rites of Isis, which were performed in honor of Osiris by priests wearing miters, with shaved heads and clothed in white, floor-length linen garments—erected a statue of Silence, which was called **Sigalion** <note>The Roman name for Harpocrates, the god of silence and secrets.</note>, at the beginning of the altar. This was so that those mysteries would remain covered and unknown to the common people. Those standing by were commanded to "favor with their tongues" <note>original: "favere linguis" – a ritual command to maintain a holy silence.</note> and turn their eyes toward that image. For the same reason, they added statues of **SPHINXES** at the corners of the altar to denote the secret knowledge of sacred things, as **Boissard** <note>Jean-Jacques Boissard (1528–1602), an antiquary who wrote extensively on Egyptian and Roman monuments.</note> demonstrates from the ancients.

For the Sphinx is a kind of most obscure monster proposing riddles to the Thebans—and not only to them, but as she did first to the Egyptians, so she does to all others aspiring to the art. As the Philosopher says in his book, she lies in wait as if before the gates of Thebes; if anyone simply passes the monster by, they suffer no harm from her. But whoever, relying on the audacity of their mind or talent, attempts to solve her riddles, unless they succeed, they prepare destruction for themselves—that is, pain in the heart and the loss of their property through error in this work.

He who refers such allegorical things only to history has a "mushroom for a brain and a pumpkin for a heart," as the Comic poet <note>Likely a reference to a phrase from Plautus or Terence used to describe a fool.</note> says, and possesses no more wisdom than one who raves while on the right path. These stories are far too childish and worthy of old women if they are taken literally; otherwise, they are the proofs and signs of profound doctrine. It is said, indeed, that there are monstrous wild beasts in Africa by the name of Sphinxes, but those are not the subject of our inquiry here, even if the origin and name of this fictional Sphinx seems to be taken from them. 

The **Sphinx of the Philosophers** understood and used human speech, specifically the Greek tongue, and proposed subtle riddles <term>gryphos</term> <note>original: "gryphos" – from the Greek *griphos*, meaning an intricate or knotted riddle.</note> and enigmatic questions in which a singular sharpness of intelligence and learning appears, which is not so obvious to other men (and from which brute beasts are furthest removed). Anyone who has at least some experience in the dogmas of the Philosophers will easily understand that such things exist. For where one thing is said, and another is understood, there...

<meta>catchword: ...is equity (æquivocatio).</meta>

<summary>This discourse explains that the Sphinx represents the "obscurity and entanglements" of the alchemical art, serving as a guardian of secrets that can only be solved through true understanding rather than literal interpretation. It links the Sphinx to the Egyptian god of silence, Sigalion, emphasizing that the "treasure" of the philosophers is hidden from the unworthy.</summary>

<keywords>Sphinx, Bacasser, Sigalion, silence, Egyptian mysteries, Isis and Osiris, Boissard, riddles, allegory vs. literalism, alchemical error</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse XXXIX. The catchword "Auß" (German for "Out") suggests this manuscript may be related to a bilingual edition where German translations followed the Latin text. This page shifts from the historical symbolism of the Sphinx to the specific myth of Oedipus and its alchemical interpretation.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XXXIX.

Equivocation gives birth to error, which is not only permitted but even commanded by the **Philosophers of the Sun** <note>Alchemical masters who align their work with the solar/gold principle.</note>. Thus, after the city of **Thebes** had been long troubled by the riddles of the **Sphinx**, a certain **Oedipus** finally arrived, who answered the questions posed in such a way that the Sphinx herself was forced to hurl herself down from the rock. But who was this Oedipus? He was the son of the King of the Thebans. When he was born, since it had been predicted to the father by an oracle that he would be killed by his son, the father ordered Oedipus to be killed. Having been suspended from a tree and abandoned with a rope passed through his feet, he was rescued and raised by another countryman. When he grew up, he indeed had **swollen feet** <note>The name "Oedipus" literally means "swollen foot" in Greek.</note>, but he demonstrated a vigorous intellect above all others in unraveling this riddle proposed by the Sphinx.

It is said there were very many riddles of the Sphinx, but this was the primary one presented to Oedipus: **"What is it that in the morning is four-footed, at noon two-footed, and in the evening three-footed?"** What was answered by Oedipus is unknown; but others have interpreted it as referring to the ages of man <note>Infancy (crawling), adulthood (walking), and old age (using a cane).</note>, and they are mistaken. For the **quadrangle** <note>A square representing the four elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note>, or the four elements, must be considered first of all; from there one arrives at the **hemisphere** (having two lines, straight and curved), that is, the **white Moon** <note>A stage in the alchemical process associated with silver and purity.</note>; and from there to the **Triangle**, which consists of body, spirit, and soul, or **Sun, Moon, and Mercury**. Hence **Rhazes** <note>Abū Bakr al-Rāzī (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and foundational figure in alchemy.</note> says in his *Epistle*: "*The Stone is triangular in its Being, and quadrangular in its quality.*" The twenty-first emblem and its exposition also relate to this point.

Moreover, Oedipus is notorious for **patricide** and **incest**, which are two of the foulest vices that can ever be devised; nonetheless, they advanced him to the kingdom (which was otherwise due to him). Because he killed his father, who was unwilling to give way to him on the road, and he took the queen, the wife of Laius and his own mother, as his wife. But this was not written for anyone as history, nor as an example to be imitated, since it was invented and introduced **allegorically** by the Philosophers solely to reveal the secrets of their doctrine. For in this work, both things occur: the first **agent** <note>The "efficient cause" or starting material.</note>, that is, the father, is removed and overcome by his own **effect**, that is, the son; and afterward, the same effect joins itself to the second agent until it becomes one with her. Thus the son is joined to his mother in marriage, and he gains possession of the father's kingdom by right of arms, marriage, and succession, as if by a triple law. He has swollen feet because he cannot run, and he is like a **bear**, as it holds the greatest secret, or a **toad**, walking with a slow pace; because that which is **fixed** <note>A substance that does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat.</note> fixes another and does not flee or shrink from the fire, which is the very thing the Philosophers need most, though it be a humble means.

<column-break/>

X iij
<meta>catchword: Auß</meta>

Two wells well up with a strong flow, clear and sweet:

## EPIGRAM XL.

There are two fountains leaping from a liquid source,
From here, one supplies the warm water of the **Boy**; <note>The "Boy" (Puer) and "Virgin" (Virgo) are alchemical metaphors for the active/masculine and passive/feminine principles, often associated with Sulfur and Mercury.</note>
The other holds cold water, which is called the **Wave of the Virgin**.
Join the latter to the former so that the two waters become one;
Then this stream will possess the combined powers of both,
Just as the fountain of **Jupiter Ammon** <note>original: "Jovis Hammonii." This refers to the famous "Fountain of the Sun" at the Temple of Jupiter Ammon in the Siwa Oasis, which ancient writers like Herodotus claimed changed temperature based on the time of day—cold at noon and boiling at midnight.</note> is both hot and cold.

->German translation of the 40th Latin Epigram.<-

Two wells well up with a strong flow, clear and sweet:
One of the boy, who gives warm water in abundance;
The other is cold, called the **little spring of the Virgin** <note>In alchemy, these two springs often represent the "Sulfur" and "Mercury" or the masculine and feminine principles that must be unified.</note>.

<summary>Emblem 40 uses the metaphor of two distinct springs—one hot and masculine, the other cold and feminine—to describe the alchemical union of opposites into a single, potent "water of health." The text references the classical marvel of the Fountain of Jupiter Ammon to illustrate how a single source can contain seemingly contradictory properties.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, union of opposites, Jupiter Ammon, Puer and Virgo, Sulfur and Mercury, medicinal water, sacred spring</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page begins the prose discourse for Emblem 40, following the image and epigram. It expands on the concept of "holy water" by cataloging legendary springs and rivers from classical antiquity before connecting them to the alchemical "Quinta Essentia" (Fifth Essence).</meta>

# DISCOURSE XL.

<meta>catchword: healing-</meta>

So many and so great are the **miracles of waters**, that they can scarcely be contained in a large volume, about which various authors have written here and there. But above all others, two **Philosophical waters** are celebrated by that name, because they not only rival but also surpass the powers and properties of almost all others. *Sybaris*, *Axus of Macedonia*, and *Melas of Boeotia* are rivers that turn flocks black if they drink from them; but the *Crathis*, the *Clitunus of Mevania*, and the *Cephisu* rivers turn them from black to white. The *Sinuessanae* waters in the region of *Campania* <note>The Sinuessa baths were famous in Roman times for treating infertility.</note> remove sterility in both sexes. The *Afrodisius* river makes women sterile. The *Cabura* spring of *Mesopotamia* has a pleasantly smelling water; the water of *Anygri* in the *Peloponnese* stinks significantly. The spring of *Jupiter Hammon* <note>The Oracle of Amun in the Libyan desert, famous for its "Fountain of the Sun."</note> is cold by day, hot by night, and lukewarm in the morning and evening by turns. Not to linger on the rest, all things—even those contrary to one another—are provided by the waters of the Philosophers. Concerning these, **Lully** <note>Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315), a major figure in medieval alchemy and logic.</note> says in his *Book on the Fifth Essence, Distinction 3, on Inceration*: <term>Inceration</term> <note>The alchemical process of adding liquid to a dry substance to bring it to a waxy, fluid state.</note>

> And so, he says, there is a double consideration in the art: namely, to compose from one nature of one metal two liquors contrary in composition; one which has a fixing, congealing, and hardening power, and another which is volatile, unfixed, and soft. This second liquor is indeed hardened, fixed, and congealed by the first. From both of these liquors results one congealed, fixed, and hardened stone, which has the power of congealing what is not congealed, hardening the soft, and softening the hard.

From these things, it is clear what those two waters are, and why they must be reduced into one water: for the **Stone** is called water because it flows, and vice versa, the water is called stone because it is ground. Truly, these waters are brought from different places, sometimes across a long distance, just as can be seen in **Rome** regarding the *Aqua Virginis* <note>The "Virgin Water," one of the great aqueducts of ancient Rome, still feeding the Trevi Fountain today.</note> and other artificial springs, and then they are to be mixed by flowing together so that one is made from two. For if one is of a hot quality and the other cold, when mixed they will obtain combined powers and will temper each other in a marvelous way. From this, the most excellent medicated waters and thermal baths will arise, which will drive away all kinds of diseases and ailments, and restore a person to firm health. Nature indeed, by her secret artistry of composition, mixes and blends many waters in the bosom of the earth with various powers of minerals, which are for many sick people

<summary>This discourse catalogs legendary natural springs to explain the alchemical "Philosophical waters," which combine opposing qualities—the volatile and the fixed—to create a healing "Stone." It emphasizes that the union of these two contrary liquids results in a balanced, medicinal substance capable of restoring health and transforming matter.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Philosophical waters, Raymond Lully, Fifth Essence, inceration, medicinal springs, Aqua Virginis, union of opposites, transmutation, healing</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Philosophical waters" and their medicinal superiority. It picks up from a previous argument (referencing Lully) about how the alchemical art can combine metallic natures into a single, more potent substance.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XL.

...healing waters, but if art is added with the proper governance, following universal evacuations and other necessary steps, and if it mixes together those things that ought to be mixed, that composition will be far more effective. Although it may seem artificial, it is nevertheless purely natural, because the resulting thing is one and simple—a homogeneous whole made from diverse parts—something that can never be achieved by art alone. 

A <term>crama</term> <note>original: "Crama" — a Greek-derived term for a mixture, often used in medicine to denote a physical blend rather than a deep chemical union.</note> or a mere confusion of ingredients is caused by art without the aid of nature; it is not a true and natural union, which is produced by nature alone. In **Theriac** <note>A famous ancient "universal" medicine or antivenom composed of dozens of ingredients, often fermented for years to allow the parts to "mellow."</note>, there is an artificial mixture of various simple ingredients produced through grinding and fermentation, but no one—unless they were acting rashly—would claim it is a natural composition, much less a **homogeneous medicine**.

It is well established regarding the artificial mixture of substances that they do not penetrate one another through their <term>minima</term> <note>The "smallest parts" or atoms of matter in medieval physics. The author argues that in human-made mixtures, the tiny particles just sit next to each other rather than merging.</note>, even though human industry cannot distinguish or separate them again. But there is a debate regarding the mixture of all qualities: do the primary qualities of all the simple ingredients in Theriac transform into a single **Fifth Essence**, or do they still reside in their own powders or substances as they did before, like accidents in a subject or color on a wall? And if so, what should be said of the secondary, tertiary, and quaternary qualities? 

It is probable that all qualities still cling to their own specific subjects and are not combined by a true, natural mixture. Otherwise, if qualities were to leave their bodies, there would be four "fifth essences" in every artificial compound—corresponding to the order of qualities (primary, secondary, and so on)—existing without their bodies and capable of being separated, which is simply not the case. 

Concerning **hare’s rennet** <note>original: "coagulo leporino" — a substance from a hare's stomach used in traditional medicine to regulate blood flow.</note>, authors write that in a flux caused by thinness of the blood, it stops and, as it were, coagulates it; yet in cases of coagulation and clots, it cuts into and dissolves them. Likewise, vinegar, lead, and many other substances possess contrary operations depending on their use, because nature has mixed them so marvelously. In the same way, **Philosophical water** possesses diverse and contrary virtues, because nature—with the assistance of art—has mixed it from contraries into a single, indivisible substance. This substance is nothing other than the **Fifth Essence** in relation to the other things to be mixed with it.

->Y 2.<-
<meta>catchword: Adonis</meta>

<summary>This page explores the distinction between artificial mixtures (like the medicine Theriac) and the true, natural union found in the alchemical Fifth Essence. The author argues that only nature can create a truly homogeneous substance capable of performing contrary medicinal actions, such as both clotting and thinning blood.</summary>
<keywords>Fifth Essence, Theriac, natural mixture, Philosophical water, hare's rennet, primary qualities, alchemy, minima, Aristotle</keywords>

### XLI. English translation of the German version of the Epigram.

> From her own father, Myrrha brought forth Adonis,
> Whom Venus loved dearly; but a wild boar slew him in fury.
> Venus rushed to his side, and in doing so, wounded her leg on a rose bush;
> Thus, by her blood, the white roses were turned to red.
> The goddess weeps (the tears of the gods move every man to sorrow),
> And she soon laid him to rest beneath the soft lettuce <note>original: "Lattich" — In mythology, Venus hides the infant Adonis in a chest, but in later traditions and alchemical symbolism, the "lettuce bed" is associated with the cooling of sexual passion or the burial of the vegetative god.</note>.

# EMBLEM XLI. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

### Adonis is slain by a wild boar; as Venus rushes to him, she stained the roses with her blood.

## EPIGRAM XLI.

From her own father, Myrrha conceived the beautiful Adonis,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The darling of the Cyprian <note>original: "Cypriae" — An epithet for Venus, who was said to have risen from the sea foam near the island of Cyprus.</note>, whom a boar laid low in death.
Venus rushes to him, and her foot being wounded, with her blood
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;She bestowed a red blush upon the rose, which before was white.
The Goddess weeps (the Syrians weep, there is a common mourning in the world)
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And she placed him upon soft lettuces <note>original: "lactucis" — In ancient myth, Venus hid the dead or dying Adonis in a bed of lettuce, a plant associated with cooling and death.</note>.

<sig>Y 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: ADO-</meta>

### XLI. English translation of the German version of the verses:

<note>This section translates the German rhyming couplets that Maier provided alongside the Latin text in his original 1617 edition.</note>

Myrrha bore a son from her own father’s bed,
Adonis, whom the Cyprian Goddess loved instead.
A wild boar struck him down and left him for dead;
As Venus ran to help, her wounded foot bled.
Thus the white rose turned to a blooming red;
She placed him in lettuce as the world’s tears were shed.

<summary>This page presents the myth of Adonis’s death as a symbolic narrative for the alchemical transition from the white stage (albedo) to the red stage (rubedo), where the "blood" of the goddess transforms the work.</summary>
<keywords>Adonis, Venus, Myrrha, wild boar, Red Rose, Alchemical Rubedo, Mythology, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XLI, moving from the poetic epigram into a detailed prose discourse. Maier here contrasts common mythological interpretations of Adonis with his own alchemical reading, identifying Adonis with the "Philosophical Sun."</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLI.

How improperly some mythologists explain the allegory of Adonis, referring him now to the Sun, the boar by which he was killed to the shaggy Winter <note>original: "Brumam hirsutam" — referring to the winter solstice, often depicted as "hairy" or "shaggy" due to frost or the appearance of dormant, scrubby vegetation.</note>, and now to the seeds of grain which remain for six months with Proserpina beneath the earth and just as many above the earth with Venus, has been sufficiently explained and refuted by us elsewhere. Here we declare, with the agreement of all, that the **Philosophical Sun** is the true meaning understood under the name of Adonis. Whence the little verse:

->All are the same: Dionysus, the Sun, and Adonis.<-

And Orpheus says: You who rejoice in the varied name of Adonis,

->Author of the sprouts, and likewise both boy and girl:<- <note>original: "puer atque puella" — This refers to the hermaphroditic or androgynous nature of the alchemical Rebis, containing both masculine/active and feminine/passive principles.</note>

All of which are in no way to be understood concerning the Sun of the heavens, but concerning the **Philosophical Sun**; for this one expresses both sexes, while the celestial Sun does not. Thus, they attribute the very same thing to Dionysus and the Sun as they do to Adonis, and vice versa, just as they do to Osiris. 

In truth, Adonis is killed by the Boar—that is, by the sharpest vinegar or dissolving water <term>aqua solutiva</term> <note>A corrosive solvent or acid used in the early stages of the Great Work to break down the material.</note>, which has boar-like and lightning-fast teeth with which it strikes Adonis; because the **Philosophical Sun** is mortally wounded by it, dissolved in its members, and torn apart. But Venus tries to bring help to her lover, and when he was dead, she placed and guarded him among the lettuces. 

In the same way, Osiris is killed by Typhon <note>Typhon, or Set, represents the chaotic or "corrupting" force in Egyptian myth, similar to the alchemical "boar."</note> and is dissected into various parts, which Isis, the wife of Osiris, collected and, having joined them, handed over for burial. The same mourning that followed the death of Osiris in Egypt every year also followed that of Adonis in Syria and neighboring kingdoms; where for several days wailing and lamentation were heard, and afterwards signs of joy and dances were given, as if he who had been dead were now living anew, having been brought into heaven. From this arose the vanity of their religion or Heathen superstition, which grew immensely, with the Devil providing the opportunity and procuring false miracles. 

Adonis was born from Cinyras (as the poets imagine), the king of Cyprus, and his daughter Myrrha; he is said to be born from incest—if the history is viewed, it is a nefarious crime, but if the allegory is viewed, it is not illicit, but especially necessary. For unless the conjunction is made between mother and son, or between father and daughter, and the child is born from there, nothing is perfected in this art. For here, the more the spouses are related to each other by blood, in the first or second degree of consanguinity, the more they are... <note>original: "consanguinitas" — Alchemists used the metaphor of incest to describe the chemical affinity between substances that share the same "root" or metallic origin.</note>

<meta>The catchword "fœcun-" (fecund) indicates the next page will discuss the fruitfulness of these "incestuous" chemical unions.</meta>

<summary>Maier interprets the myth of Adonis not as a seasonal metaphor, but as an alchemical allegory where Adonis represents the "Philosophical Sun" being dissolved by a corrosive solvent (the boar) before being reunited and reborn.</summary>
<keywords>Adonis, Philosophical Sun, Alchemy, Venus, Osiris, Isis, Dissolution, Consanguinity, Myrrha, Cinyras</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose discourse for Emblem XLI. The previous page began explaining why the myth of Adonis represents the "Philosophical Sun" and how his death by the boar represents the action of a corrosive solvent. This page continues that chemical-mythological parallel, specifically addressing why alchemical "marriages" between close relations (like mothers and sons) are necessary for the Work.</meta>

...more fruitful and, conversely, the more remote the relations, the more unfruitful they become—a condition that is not tolerable in human marriage. Hence, Oedipus marries his own mother, Jupiter his sister; so too do Osiris, Saturn, the Sun, the **Red Slave** <note>original: "servus rubeus" — an alchemical personification of crude or unrefined sulfur.</note>, and Gabritius <note>Gabritius and his sister Beya are famous alchemical figures representing the masculine and feminine principles whose union results in the philosopher's stone.</note>. Concerning Adonis, the Sun—speaking of himself in the metaphor of Belinus <note>Belinus is a legendary figure or sage often cited in medieval alchemical texts like the *Rosarium Philosophorum*.</note> within the *Rosary of the Philosophers*—speaks in this way:

> Know that my father, the Sun, gave me power over all power, and clothed me in a garment of glory. And shortly after: For I am unique and am made like my father, etc. I draw my servants out of their power and nature, and I clothe them in my splendor and beautiful light which my father gave to me, in all their works. For I am excellent, I who exalt and cast down all things, and none of my servants can prevail over me, except one, to whom it is granted because he is my opposite. And he destroys me, yet he does not destroy my nature. And he is Saturn, who separates all my members. Afterward, I go to my mother, who gathers together all my divided and separated members. I am the one illuminating all that is mine, and I make light appear clearly on the journey from my father Saturn, and also from my mother, who is hostile to me.

The things said thus far are so clear that, even for one only moderately practiced in the reading of authors, they should dispel the darkness from the mind’s eyes and reveal the solar light, which is abundantly perceived in the produced harmonies of things and persons. For those things which are true, even if hidden under the veil of **allegory**, agree among themselves with a marvelous consensus; those which are false fight with themselves and with others, and go off in different directions.

<meta>Catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Maier explains that alchemical processes require the union of "related" substances, using the incestuous myths of gods like Oedipus and Osiris as allegories. He quotes the Rosarium Philosophorum to illustrate the cycle of the Sun being destroyed by Saturn (lead/putrefaction) and restored by its mother (the transformative matrix).</summary>

<keywords>Adonis, Philosophical Sun, alchemy, allegory, Saturn, Rosarium Philosophorum, Gabritius, Red Slave, solar light, putrefaction</keywords>

This new emblem shifts focus toward the methodological requirements for the alchemist, outlining the intellectual and sensory tools needed to navigate the "Great Work."</meta>

FUGUE XLII. In the fourth above. Counterpoint.
<meta>catchword: EMBLE-</meta>

# Let Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading be the guide, staff, spectacles, and lamp for him who is occupied with chemical affairs <note>original: "in Chymicis" — refers specifically to alchemical pursuits.</note>.

---

<image-desc>Three sets of musical staves representing a three-part fugue. The voices are labeled in the left margin. Latin lyrics are printed beneath the notes.</image-desc>

> Let Nature be your guide, and you, by art,

### XLII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Let Nature be your guide, whom you must follow from afar 
Willingly; otherwise you stray, if she does not lead you. 
Let Reason be your staff, and Experience must strengthen 
Your vision <note>original: "Gesicht" — meaning both the sense of sight and the faculty of perception.</note>, so that you can see what is laid out far and wide. 
Let Reading be like a lamp, shining bright and clear in the darkness, 
By which you may avoid the dangers of the matter and of words <note>original: "der Sach vnd Wörter Gefahr" — a warning that alchemical texts are full of linguistic traps and material risks.</note>.

<summary>Emblem XLII presents a fundamental instruction for the alchemist: to succeed, one must follow Nature as a guide, use Reason for support, rely on Experience for clarity, and use Reading to illuminate the way.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Reason, Experience, Reading, Alchemical Method, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, Fugue, Epigram</keywords>

# EMBLEM XLII. On the Secrets of Nature.
<sig>Z</sig>
<meta>image quality: excellent; catchword: Cas-; library stamp: B.R.</meta>

->For one who is occupied in chemical affairs <note>original: "In Chymicis versanti" — refers to the practice of alchemy.</note>, let *Nature*, *Reason*, *Experience*, and *Reading*<-
->be the *Guide*, *staff*, *spectacles*, and *lamp*.<-

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving illustrating the motto. A female figure (Nature) walks toward the left in a rural landscape, carrying flowers and a cornucopia of fruits. She wears a flowing reddish-brown gown and a yellow sash, with a dark veil trailing behind her. Following her path and stepping into her footprints is an elderly man with a long grey beard. He wears spectacles and a violet robe with a hood. In his right hand, he leans on a wooden staff, and in his left, he carries a lighted lantern. The background consists of green hills, trees, and a small house under a pale sky where a crescent moon is visible.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XLII.

> Let Nature be your Guide, and you, by your art, be her willing
> *follower <note>original: "pedissequus" — a servant or footman who follows a master; here it implies the alchemist must follow Nature's lead, not force her.</note>; you go astray unless she herself is the companion of the journey.*
> Let Reason provide the help of a staff, let Experience strengthen
> *the eyes <note>original: "Lumina" — literally "lights," used poetically for eyes.</note>, so that they may be able to see from afar.*
> Let Reading be a lamp shining in the darkness, so that you,
> *being forewarned, may beware the heaps of things and words <note>A warning against the confusing and voluminous nature of alchemical literature.</note>.*

---

### XLII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Let Nature be your guide, whom you must follow from afar 
Willingly; otherwise you stray, if she does not lead you. 
Let Reason be your staff, and Experience must strengthen 
Your vision <note>original: "Gesicht" — meaning both the sense of sight and the faculty of perception.</note>, so that you can see what is laid out far and wide.
Let Reading be your light in the darkness, so that you
Are wary of the many words and things that may confuse you.

<summary>This emblem outlines the four essential tools for the alchemist: following Nature's lead, using Reason as support, relying on Experience for clarity, and using Reading to illuminate the darkness of the unknown.</summary>
<keywords>methodology, Nature, Reason, Experience, Reading, alchemy, guide, staff, spectacles, lamp</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Emblem XLII. The previous page concluded the poetic epigram, which introduced the four essential tools for the alchemist: Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading. This page begins the prose "Discourse," using a metaphor of a difficult nighttime journey to explain why these four elements are indispensable for the "Philosophical Medicine."</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLII.

The mishaps that can happen to travelers are countless, especially if they set out to travel on foot at night through slippery and dangerous places. For such a journey, four things are required as being highly necessary—not to mention traveling funds and a robust body. First: a companion or guide who is not ignorant of the places through which one must go. For if the ignorant leads the ignorant, the same thing happens to them as happens to the blind: both are plunged into errors and wanderings, if not into a pit. Second: a small staff or **walking stick** <note>original: "scipio" — a staff often associated with authority or support</note>, by which the slipperiness of the road is guarded against, lest it prove harmful to anyone. Third: healthy eyes; for to the blind or the bleary-eyed, journeys of this kind are most dangerous. Fourth: a lamp or a lit torch, so that the hazards of the roads may be distinguished.

In the same way, if anyone commits themselves to that most difficult journey of seeking out **Philosophical Medicine** <note>original: "Medicina Philosophica" — a term for the Philosopher's Stone or the ultimate alchemical goal</note>, they will require four things parallel and equally corresponding to those mentioned before: namely **Nature**, **Reason**, **Experience**, and **Reading**. If one or another of these is missing, the rest will help little or not at all. For on these, as if on four wheels, the **Philosophical chariot** proceeds; if one of the wheels is missing, it cannot stay upright, and is of no use.

**Nature** presupposes natural bodies and spirits as the subjects first provided by Nature, upon which Art later acts by preparing, purifying, and making them fit, so that from them may be made what Art promises as the end goal. Thus the potter takes water and earth; the glassmaker, ashes and sand; the smith, iron, copper, tin, lead, silver, or gold; the tanner, raw hides; and so others take other things.

So also the practitioner of **chemistry** <note>original: "chymiæ artifex" — the artist or craftsman of alchemy</note> looks to his materials. To the former craftsmen, their materials are well known from the very first day; to the latter, when he begins, they usually remain unknown for many years—not to say for his whole life. Nature indeed points her finger at the materials, but there are many things that obscure the impression of Nature so that she cannot be recognized. Therefore, the first intention is to intimately contemplate Nature—how she proceeds in her operations—to the end that the natural subjects of Chemistry may be obtained without defect or superfluity. Hence Nature becomes the guide and companion of so great a journey, whose footsteps must be followed. Secondly, **Reason** becomes like a **staff** <note>original: "scipio"</note> to steady the steps and stabilize the feet so that they do not waver. Without reason-

<summary>Maier uses the metaphor of a traveler in the dark to argue that the alchemist requires four specific tools: Nature (as a guide), Reason (as a staff), Experience (as sight), and Reading (as a lamp).</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Reason, Experience, Reading, Philosophical Medicine, Alchemy, allegory of travel, materials of art</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XLII, which compares the alchemist’s journey to a perilous nighttime trek requiring four tools: Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading. The previous page introduced these four "wheels" of the philosophical chariot and began describing their functions. This page continues with the role of Reason (the walking stick), Experience (the spectacles), and Reading (the lamp).</meta>

-># DISCOURSE XLII.<-

...lest anyone be prone to falling into error. Hence the **Philosophers** <note>The "Philosophers" usually refers to the collective authority of alchemical writers through the ages.</note> say: Whatever you hear, reason whether it can be so or not. For no one is driven to believe or perform the impossible, unless they are of weak memory, dull wit, and foolish imagination, so that they deceive themselves by accepting false things as true and rejecting true things as false. They also say that they do not care about words, whatever is said, but rather about the things themselves, what is understood; and that words exist for the sake of things, and not things for the sake of words. For example, if someone says that glass becomes malleable through the **Philosophical tincture** <note>original: "tinctura Philosophica" — a legendary substance, often the Philosopher’s Stone, capable of "dyeing" or transforming the properties of a material.</note>. Why should I not believe this, provided that reason dictates it?

Thirdly, **Experience** will provide **spectacles** <note>original: "perspicilia" — early optical lenses or telescopes. The author suggests that practical experience acts as a tool to enhance the natural limitations of human perception.</note> through which distant things can be seen; these are optical instruments helping and correcting the weakness of human eyes, discovered and made by art. Very similar to these are the experiments attempted, seen, or truly heard regarding **mineral matter** <note>original: "mineralis materia" — the raw substances used in the alchemical work.</note> of every kind; the more of these there are in the memory, the more reasoning will draw from them and compare among and with others, so that it may notice what is true and what is not. 

Fourthly, **Reading** kindles as it were a clear lamp in the intellect, without which there will be darkness and thick clouds everywhere. However, the reading of good authors must be repeated often; otherwise it will be of no use. Hence **Bacasser** <note>A figure cited in the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, which takes the form of a debate among assembly of philosophers.</note> in the **Crowd** <note>original: "Turba" — referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*.</note> says:

> "Therefore," he says, "he who will be long-suffering, and who freely enjoys patience, will walk in the right path of this art; but he who thinks he can gather fruit from our books more quickly is mistaken, and it would have been better for him not even to have looked at them, than to have ever happened upon them."

And what follows in the same place.

<summary>This page elaborates on the four requirements for alchemical success, emphasizing that Reason must judge the possible, Experience must sharpen observation like spectacles, and Reading must serve as a lamp through the "Crowd of Philosophers."</summary>
<keywords>Reason, Experience, Reading, Philosophical Tincture, malleable glass, spectacles, mineral matter, Bacasser, Turba Philosophorum, patience</keywords>

**Upon** the high peak of the mountain the Vulture sits crying out loudly
Without ceasing: "I alone am the white and the black,
The citron-colored and the red. I do not lie; the same is also the Raven
Who flies—even though his wings have been cut off—
In the dark of night, and even when the sun stands high at noon;
For from this one, or that one, the whole Art <note>The "Art" (German: "Kunst") refers to Alchemy, or the Great Work of transforming matter.</note> proceeds."

<header>EMBLEM XLIII. Concerning the secrets of Nature.</header>

->Listen to the talkative vulture, who in no way deceives you.<-

I AM BLACK, WHITE, YELLOW, AND RED <note>original: "EGO SUM NIGER ALBUS CITRINUS ET RUBREUS" — these colors represent the four traditional stages of the alchemical process: nigredo (blackening), albedo (whitening), citrinitas (yellowing), and rubedo (reddening).</note>

### EPIGRAM XLIII.

On the high summit of a mountain stands a vulture
Incessantly crying out: I am white and black,
I am called yellow and red; I tell no lie: the same
Is the Crow, who is accustomed to fly without wings
In the dark of night, and in the middle of the light of day,
For either he or that other one is the head <note>The "head of the art" (*artis caput*) refers to the primary material or the starting point of the alchemical Great Work.</note> of your art.

<sig>Z 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: TALKATIVE (original: LOQUACES)</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the symbolism of the Vulture and the Crow, which represent the transformation of matter through the four stages of alchemy—black, white, yellow, and red. The vulture, perched on a mountain, acts as a paradoxical guide who "speaks" the truth of the chemical process.</summary>

<keywords>vulture, crow, four colors, nigredo, albedo, citrinitas, rubedo, mountain summit, alchemical stages, Great Work</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem XLIII. The previous page introduced the "Talkative Vulture" and its relationship to the four alchemical colors and the "wingless crow." This discourse expands on the nature of these birds and the sequence of colors in the Great Work.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLIII.

We everywhere and every day hear talkative birds, or those that rival the human voice, namely **parrots, crows, jackdaws, and magpies**. Thus **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author and natural philosopher famous for his *Natural History*.</note> writes that in his own time, when he published his history, **Agrippina** (the wife of **Claudius Caesar**) possessed a thrush that could imitate human speech. The young **Caesars** also had a starling and nightingales trained in both the **Greek and Latin** languages, which constantly spoke new things in long strings of words. Since birds of this kind are not uncommon, they are now considered less worthy of wonder, provided that practice and training can make any bird endowed with a wider tongue vocal and talkative in this way.

But that **Vulture** of whom the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists, the "philosophers by fire."</note> make mention does not learn its speech from practice, if it utters any at all; rather, its own nature expresses them silently. Indeed, the Philosophers say that it constantly cries out and pronounces with a high voice who and what it is. In this, it imitates great **Princes**, who always wish their titles and inheritances to be known at the beginning of their proclamations—not out of any mark of pride, but for the sake of others, so that they may know which princedoms they rule or what hereditary rights they claim. Thus, it is of great importance to recognize which colors, like the insignia of arms and titles, the **Philosophical bird** enjoys and by which it excels all others.

> I am, it says (as the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (Rosary of the Philosophers), a 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> quotes from **Hermes**), the black of the white, and the yellow of the red; and certainly I am truthful and not lying. <note>original: "Ego... sum nigrum albi, & Citrinus rubei, & certè veridicus sum & non mentiens."</note>

It affirms itself to be **black, white, yellow, and red**; and such it truly is. For even if it does not yet possess the last three colors in actuality, it nevertheless awaits their inheritance. Hence **Rosinus** <note>Likely Zosimos of Panopolis, an early alchemist whose name was often Latinized as Rosinus.</note> in his *Book of Divine Interpretations* says:

> Take the stone, which is black, white, red, and yellow; it is a wonderful bird that flies without wings in the blackness of night and the clarity of day. <note>original: "Recipe... lapidem, qui est niger, albus, rubeus, citrinus, avis mirabilis, qui nigredine noctis, & diei claritate sine alis volat."</note>

For from the bitterness existing in its throat, coloration is received; but from its blood, pure water is received, just as **Alexander** <note>Reference to a pseudo-Alexandrian alchemical text, possibly the *Secretum Secretorum*.</note> said:

> Take the stone of the thread of four colors. <note>original: "Recipe lapidem quatuor colorum fili." This refers to the "thread" or sequence of the four stages.</note>

The books of the Philosophers dictate to the point of nausea that all these colors, which are the principal ones, are present in the stone in a successive order. Truly, it is not without reason that the Philosophical subject is called a **Vulture**. Among vultures, the black ones prevail; they are predatory, but they fly slowly because of the mass of their bodies. They say this bird conceives without the seed of males and without union

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence, continuing with the catchword "junctione" (union/conjunction) on the next page, likely discussing the myth of the vulture's parthenogenesis as an allegory for the self-contained nature of the alchemical vessel.</meta>

<summary>This page explains why the "Philosophical Vulture" announces its four colors (black, white, yellow, and red), comparing its titles to those of a prince. It cites authorities like Hermes, Rosinus, and Alexander to link these colors to the stages of the alchemical "Stone."</summary>
<keywords>Vulture, Pliny, Agrippina, alchemical colors, nigredo, albedo, citrinitas, rubedo, Rosarium Philosophorum, Hermes Trismegistus, Rosinus, Alexander</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and symbolic description of the Philosophical Vulture, which was introduced on the previous page as a bird that proclaims its own identity and colors.</meta>

[...by mating] produce offspring, and their young live to a great age, even up to a hundred years. They nest on high cliffs, and no one has reached their nests. Generally, two chicks are seen; they are helpful against serpents. They become pregnant by the East Wind <note>A common ancient belief, found in Pliny and Virgil, that certain animals could be impregnated by the wind, symbolizing a spiritual or "volatile" conception.</note>. When they begin to lay eggs, they bring something from the Indian regions which is like a nut, having something inside that moves and makes a sound; when they place this near themselves, they produce many offspring, but only one remains, which is called the **I M M U S U L U S** <note>A term from Roman augury referring to a specific type of vulture, often interpreted as the "sanctified" or "chosen" one.</note>. Hermodorus Fonticus, as testified by Caelius, says vultures are the most innocent of all animals because they touch nothing that men sow, plant, or nourish. Moreover, they kill no living thing. They even abstain from birds, even dead ones, through some sense of kinship. Hence they were held in the highest esteem in auguries, as the origins of the City of Rome indicate <note>Referring to the legend of Romulus and Remus, who watched for vultures to determine where to build Rome.</note>. 

Since the Philosophical bird expresses almost all these properties of the vulture, the vulture is not undeservedly called by Hermes and others that which is both slow in flight and black in color. It conceives from itself. For so says the **Rosary** <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> near the end: *And it is the dragon that marries itself, impregnates itself, and gives birth in its own day, etc.* And **Rosinus** <note>Likely a corruption of Zosimos of Panopolis, a 3rd-century alchemist.</note> to Sarratant: *And it is the serpent sporting with itself, impregnating itself, and giving birth in a single day, etc.* It lives for a very long time and survives, multiplying itself. For what Virgil writes of the Phoenix bird fits this one equally (since they are the same):

->The crow outlives the swift-footed stag three times over,<-
->But the Phoenix, the bird that can be reborn, outlives the crow nine times:<-

Touching its nest is very difficult. It fights with the Mercurial serpent and overcomes it—that is, the Sun with the Moon <note>This represents the union of the fixed (Sun/Gold) and the volatile (Moon/Mercury/Serpent).</note>. It is conceived from the wind, carried in its belly, and born in the air. The Eagle-stone <note>original: *Ætites lapis*. A hollow geode with a loose stone inside that rattles; it was historically believed to assist in childbirth.</note>, having a small stone sounding within it, is called the "whole" by many. At least one "immusalus" is found in the Philosophical nest. The Philosophical bird is also the most innocent, for it harms no one, benefits all who know it, and is most excellent in auguries. 

But why does it nest on a mountain and cry out while sitting thus? **Rosinus** answers from **Rhazes** <note>An influential Persian physician and alchemist (c. 865–925 AD).</note>, who says: *Contemplate the highest mountains, which are on the right and the left, and ascend there: there our stone is found. And on another mountain which bears every kind of pigment, and spirits or species, it is likewise there.* Dying, he says: *Climb the high mountains planted with trees, for there our stone is found and hidden.* And **Hermes** says: *Receive the blessed stone, crush it and wash the red stone from which it is extracted, which is found in mountains and especially sometimes in old sewers.* <note>The mention of "sewers" or "dung" is a common alchemical trope: the "Great Work" begins with humble, discarded, or "vile" materials.</note>

<summary>This page explores the natural and mythological history of the vulture to explain its alchemical symbolism as a self-begetting, innocent, and long-lived creature. It equates the vulture with the Phoenix and the dragon, emphasizing that the "Philosophical Stone" is found in high, difficult places or hidden within common filth.</summary>
<keywords>vulture, Phoenix, Immusulus, Rosarium Philosophorum, Rhazes, Eagle-stone, self-conception, alchemical marriage, mountains</keywords>

# FUGUE XLIV. In the 5th or 12th above. With the base existing only in proportion. <note>This refers to the musical structure of the piece, a three-part canon where the voices enter at specific intervals (a fifth or an octave plus a fifth above the previous voice), while the "base" or foundational melody remains in a fixed mathematical ratio.</note>

## Through deceit Typhon kills Osiris and scatters his limbs, which the far-famed Isis brings together again.

<note>In this alchemical allegory, Osiris represents the "Sol Sophiae" or the gold of the philosophers (the perfected matter). His brother Typhon represents the destructive, corrosive force that dissolves the matter. Isis, the feminine principle or "mercury of the philosophers," acts as the agent that gathers and reunites the scattered pieces to achieve the final "resurrection" or completion of the Stone.</note>

Syria has Adonis, Greece has Dionysus,

### *German version of the Latin Epigram XLIV.*

Syria has Adonis, and Greece likewise has Dionysus;
Egypt has Osiris; thus they are the Sun given to the Sages.
Isis is the sister, as well as the mother and the wife of Osiris,
Whose limbs Typhon divided; she joins them again into one body.
But for him, the male member was lacking, lost in the water,
For the sulfur <note>original: *Schwefel*</note> perishes, which brought forth the sulfur <note>original: *Schwebel* (an archaic variant of sulfur). This refers to the alchemical concept where the outward, combustible sulfur is destroyed to reveal the inward, fixed "Sulfur of the Sages."</note>.

It identifies various ancient deities as symbols for the Sun of Wisdom, the central goal of the Great Work.</summary>

# EMBLEM XLIV. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->far and wide, but the renowned Isis gathered these together.<-

<note>In this alchemical allegory, Osiris represents the "Sol Sophiae" or the gold of the philosophers (the perfected matter). His brother Typhon represents the destructive, corrosive force that dissolves the matter. Isis, the feminine principle or "mercury of the philosophers," acts as the agent that gathers and reunites the scattered pieces to achieve the final "resurrection" or completion of the Stone.</note>

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting a scene from the myth of Osiris. In the foreground, three men in classical and orientalist attire are gathered around an open wooden coffin. One man, wearing a red cloak over a blue tunic, reaches into the coffin towards the body of a bearded, crowned man (Osiris). A man in a gold-patterned robe and a turban stands on the right, gesturing towards the scene. In the background on the left, a woman in a red dress (Isis) is seen in an outdoor courtyard, bending down to collect severed limbs from the ground. To the right, another room is visible through an archway, where people are seated at a dinner table. A faint red circular library stamp with the initials "B.R" is visible just below the center of the image.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XLIV.

<margin>Atalanta fleeing.</margin>
Syria has Adonis, Greece has Dionysus,
Egypt has Osiris, who are nothing but the **SUN** of Wisdom <note>original: *Sol Sophiæ*</note>:
Isis is there, sister, and wife, and mother of Osiris,
Whose limbs Typhon cuts apart, and she binds them together.
But the modest part <note>original: *pudibunda pars*. This refers to the phallus of Osiris, which in the myth was lost in the river and eaten by fish; alchemically, this represents a volatile component lost during the "dissolution" stage.</note> flows down into the sea, scattered through the waves,
For the Sulfur, that **SULFUR** which generated it, is missing.

### *German version of the Latin Epigram XLIV.*

Syria has Adonis, and Greece likewise has Dionysus;
Egypt has Osiris; thus they are nothing but the Sun of Wisdom.
Isis is present, the sister, wife, and mother of Osiris,
Whose limbs Typhon cuts into pieces, and she binds them up.
But the modest part flows into the sea, scattered through the waves,
For the Sulfur, the Sulfur that produced it, is absent.

<summary>This page presents Emblem 44, using the Egyptian myth of Osiris's dismemberment by Typhon and his reassembly by Isis as an allegory for the alchemical process of dissolution and coagulation. It identifies Osiris with the "Sun of Wisdom" or philosophical sulfur, which must be broken down before it can be perfected.</summary>
