--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Emblem XLIV, moving from the poetic epigram and image into the "Discourse." Maier transitions from the myth of Osiris being dismembered by Typhon to a broader argument: that all classical mythology is actually a veiled language for the "Ancient Chemistry" or alchemy.</meta>

# DISCOURSE XLIV.

I have elsewhere—specifically in the first book of my *Hieroglyphics* <note>Referring to Maier's 1614 work, *Arcana arcanissima*, where he argues that Egyptian and Greek myths are alchemical allegories.</note>—reduced the allegory of Osiris to its true origin, which is chemical, and explained it fully. Therefore, I consider it superfluous to repeat the same things here, although the same truths must be spoken of them. Nevertheless, I will here establish a parallel discourse that will always move and remain within the confines of Ancient Chemistry, which has been entirely sung and figured by the poets.

Will you persuade me that Osiris was a God or an Egyptian king? I will not believe it, even if you should persuade me to believe it. For, as the proverb goes, "dogs smell quite differently, and sows smell quite differently" <note>original: "Longè aliter enim canes, longè aliter sues olent." A proverb meaning that things of a different nature are easily distinguished by those with discernment; the "scent" of historical fact is different from the "scent" of chemical truth.</note>. I plainly deny he is a God, and you will agree with me unless you are a Pagan or of an opinion distorted from right reason. That he was never a king either is demonstrated by all the circumstances considered elsewhere. 

He is the Sun—but the <term>Philosophical Sun</term> <note>The alchemical "Sol" usually refers to the perfected "Gold of the Sages" or the active, masculine principle of the Work.</note>. Since the name of the Sun is read everywhere as being attributed to him, the common folk, who know no other light but that mundane one in the sky, have interpreted him as such. The Sun of the Philosophers takes its name from the sun of the world because it contains the properties of nature descending from that celestial sun, or agreeing with it. 

Therefore, Osiris is the Sun, as are Dionysus, Bacchus, Jupiter, Mars, Adonis, Oedipus, Perseus, Achilles, Triptolemus, Pelops, Hippomenes, and Pollux. Conversely, the Moon is Isis, Juno, Venus, the mother of Oedipus, Danaë, Deidamia, Atalanta, and Helen; likewise Latona, Semele, Europa, Leda, Antiope, and Thalia. These are the parts of the "composite" <note>The combined substance in the alchemical vessel.</note>. Before the operation, it is called the "Stone," and by the name of every metal, <term>Magnesia</term> <note>In alchemy, Magnesia is not the modern magnesium, but a term for the "primordial matter" or the crude mineral substance containing the secret spirit.</note>. After the operation, it is called Orcus, Pyrrhus, Apollo, or Aesculapius. 

To these are added the destructive or challenging forces: Typhon, Python, and the Boar. The "Artificer" or chemist is represented by Hercules, Ulysses, Jason, Theseus, and Pirithous, along with the innumerable labors and dangers which were endured by these heroes. One should look at the labors of Hercules, the wanderings of Ulysses, the dangers of Jason, the attempts of Theseus, and the delays of Pirithous. This is a great volume of matter and doctrine, through which Vulcan, Mercury, and Saturn run and return on every page: Saturn as the father of all and the <note>original: "causa sine qua non"</note> indispensable cause; Vulcan as the efficient cause <note>The fire or heat that drives the reaction.</note>; and Mercury as both the matter and the form. 

The Sun takes his sister, the Moon, as his wife. Jupiter takes Juno, as Saturn takes Rhea, and Osiris takes Isis. Dionysus is snatched from his mother Semele—who was burned by the lightning of Jupiter—to be matured in the thigh of his father Jupiter; likewise, Aesculapius is snatched from his mother Coronis...

<meta>catchword: Diony-</meta>

<summary>Maier argues that Osiris and other mythological figures are not gods or historical kings, but symbols for alchemical principles, specifically identifying Osiris as the 'Philosophical Sun' and Isis as the 'Moon' or feminine principle.</summary>
<keywords>Osiris, Isis, Philosophical Sun, Magnesia, Alchemy as Myth, Alchemical Principles, Vulcan, Saturn, Mercury, Heroic Labors</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the Discourse for Emblem XLIV. The previous page argued that Osiris is not a historical king or a literal god, but rather a representation of the "Philosophical Sun"—the golden seed or active principle in the alchemical Great Work. Here, Maier expands this allegorical interpretation to include nearly all of Greek and Egyptian mythology.</meta>

### DISCOURSE XLIV.

Dionysus, once grown, showed men the new drink of wine, undertaking an expedition as far as India; Osiris and Triptolemus showed the sowing and use of grain; and Aesculapius showed the administration of medicine. Dionysus, so named by the Greeks, is Bacchus to the Romans, Osiris to the Egyptians, and Adonis to the Syrians. Oedipus killed his father and married his mother; Perseus slew his grandfather; Typhon killed his brother Osiris; the boar killed Adonis; and Ceres, the nurse of Triptolemus, killed his father Eleusius. <note>Maier is listing these violent mythological acts to suggest they represent chemical "destructions" or transformations, such as the "killing" of a base metal to release its spirit.</note> Hippomenes defeated Atalanta with a golden apple; Tantalus, the father of Pelops, won Hippodamia in a chariot race <note>In traditional myth, it was Pelops himself who won Hippodamia; Maier may be emphasizing the ancestral line or the transmission of the "tincture."</note>. Osiris was cut into pieces and joined together again by Isis, who was his mother, sister, and wife. The boy Pelops was cooked and boiled, and then restored to life by Ceres with an ivory shoulder added, after she had accidentally eaten his original one. Achilles and Triptolemus were placed under hot coals at night and nourished with milk by day—the latter by his nurse Ceres, the former by his mother Thetis. Achilles and Helen were the causes of the Trojan War: she as the impulsive cause, and he as the efficient cause. Helen was born from an egg; and at the wedding of Peleus and Thetis (from whom Achilles was born), the Apple of Discord—the primary cause of Helen's abduction—was cast. Pollux was present with the Argonauts, who are presupposed to have lived nearly fifty years before the start of the Trojan War (if they lived at all). Therefore, Helen must have been an old woman when she was abducted by Paris. <note>Maier points out these chronological absurdities to prove that these stories cannot be literal history, but must be alchemical ciphers where time and age represent stages of a process.</note> Medea married Achilles in the Elysian Fields, though she was then a toothless old woman—unless perhaps she restored her own youth, as she did for Aeson, the father of Jason, and as Ceres did for Pelops, which is why he is called <term>twice-pubescent</term> <note>original: "bis pubescens." An alchemical term for a substance that has undergone a second "ripening" or purification.</note>. Perseus received a winged horse from Pallas and brought her the head of Medusa in return, while Mercury provided him a curved sword and other Gods provided other weapons. Triptolemus received from Ceres a chariot drawn by winged dragons. When Pallas was born from the brain of Jupiter, it rained gold at Rhodes, just as it did when the Sun lay with Venus. And Jupiter embraced Danaë as a shower of gold; Leda as a swan; his sister Juno as a cuckoo; Europa as a bull; and Antiope as a satyr—and thus there is a concordance in all these things. <note>Maier concludes that all these diverse myths are "concordant"—they all describe the same chemical secrets through different symbols.</note>

Aa ij
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Maier argues that various myths—including those of Dionysus, Achilles, and Jupiter’s many forms—are not history, but consistent alchemical allegories for the "Philosophical Gold." He points to chronological impossibilities in the stories of Helen and Medea to prove they must be interpreted symbolically rather than literally.</summary>

<keywords>Dionysus, Osiris, alchemical allegory, Triptolemus, Pelops, Helen of Troy, Pallas Athena, concordance of myths, twice-pubescent, Philosophical Gold</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse XLV, following the musical fugue and epigram of the same number. It moves into the philosophical and scientific explanation of how the Sun and its shadow—metaphors for alchemical processes—operate in both the physical cosmos and the "Great Work" of alchemy.</meta>

<header>DISCOURSE XLV.</header>

Just as in a **round Palace** or one of spherical form, fire kindled in one place illuminates all the upper and lower walls round about, except those where a table or board standing in the middle obstructs it by its presence and admits a dark shadow; so also the **Sun**, positioned in that great palace of heaven or vaulted Theater, illuminates with its rays the entire concavity of heaven and the transparent bodies capable of receiving light contained within it—that is, all the stars, both wandering <note>The "wandering stars" refer to the planets.</note> and fixed—unless where the density of the intervening Earth prevents it. There, a black and dark shadow, which is called **night**, remains for as long as it is put to flight by the presence of the sun, and its light is poured out and seen in its place. Therefore, shadow and night are the deprivation or absence of solar light; day, on the contrary, is its irradiation and surrounding flow.

Shadow is that which cannot endure the sight of the sun, and therefore it flees and hides itself now on this part of the earth, now on that, just as the sun is on the opposite side. The Sun and shadow have never seen each other, though in any given moment, if nature allowed it, they could; but the sun, hearing that the shadow is his enemy, always pursues it as it flees, nor can he ever capture the exhausted thing, as **Buchanan** <note>George Buchanan (1506–1582) was a Scottish scholar whose poem *Sphaera* described the cosmos in verse.</note> beautifully sang in his book on the sphere.

Following the imitation and example of that great sun and its shadow, the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists.</note> observed that their own sun also possesses a black, misty, and fleeting shadow. Hence **Hermes** says, "My son, extract from the ray its shadow" <note>original Latin: *extrahe à radio suam umbram*</note>; that is, see that you lead your sun around through the <term>primum mobile</term> <note>The "first movable" sphere which, in pre-Copernican astronomy, was believed to carry all other celestial spheres around the Earth once every 24 hours.</note>, over which **Vulcan** <note>The Roman god of fire, often used as a metaphor for the alchemist's furnace.</note> presides, so that even that part of your Earth which is now covered by shadowy night may enjoy clear solar light. 

For unless the entire Firmament of heaven, with all it contains, were led around once every natural day—that is, twenty-four hours—by the "first motion," but instead the Sun moved only by its own proper "second motion" (called the annual motion), it would happen that the <term>Antipodes</term> <note>People living on the opposite side of the globe.</note> who are below us would have night for nearly a six-month space of time while we had one day, and afterward vice versa, they would have day and we night. Thus, the entire year would consist of only one night and one day, just as these turns are now proven by experience and very true reasoning to occur under both poles. But it seemed far otherwise to **Divine Providence**, which therefore ordained the double motions of the planets, the first and the second, and thus distributed the year into many days.

<summary>This discourse compares the astronomical phenomenon of day and night to alchemical processes, using the Sun's pursuit of its shadow as a metaphor for the refinement of matter. It explains how the daily rotation of the heavens prevents perpetual darkness, just as the alchemist must "rotate" their work through the fire of Vulcan.</summary>

<keywords>Sun and Shadow, alchemy, astronomy, primum mobile, Hermes Trismegistus, Vulcan, George Buchanan, night and day, Antipodes, Divine Providence</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse XLV from page 190. The word "buit" is the conclusion of the Latin word "contribuit" (contributes) from the previous page, where the author was discussing how the sun and shadow interact to create the cycle of time.</meta>

<header># DISCOURSE XLV.</header>

...contributes: But that shadow and the sun together make day and night, which the sun alone could not do by itself; for its nature is to illuminate all opposing places and bodies, not to create shadow, except accidentally through its absence. In the same way, the **Philosophical Sun** <note>original: *Sol Philosophicus*; a metaphor for the active, masculine principle in alchemy, often identified with gold or sulfur.</note> with its shadow creates day—that is, light—and night or darkness, namely **Latona** <note>In alchemy, Latona (the mother of Apollo and Diana) often represents the "black" stage of matter or the base substance that must be purified.</note> or **magnesia** <note>A term for the "primitive matter" of the alchemist’s work.</note>. Democritus <note>A Greek philosopher often cited by alchemists as an authority on the transformation of matter.</note> teaches at the beginning of Book 3 of the *Golden Table* <note>original: *Aurea Mensa*; a historical work by Michael Maier documenting alchemical traditions.</note> that this shadow must be destroyed and burned away with a "fiery medicine" <note>original: *ignito pharmaco*</note>.

The usefulness of shadows in **Astronomy** <note>original: *Astronomicis*</note> is so great that without them, that science could hardly be completed; **Chemists** <note>original: *Chymici*; here meaning alchemists.</note> likewise credit their own shadows for the fact that their art reaches perfection. For what is the **Sun** here without its shadow? It is like a clapper without a bell: the former indeed provides the initial movement so that a sound may be produced, but the latter gives the sound itself. The former is the pick <note>original: *plectrum*</note>, the latter the instrument; the former is the tongue, the latter the great mouth.

Shadow is a most lowly thing, close to non-being; so too the **Philosophers' shadow** is something "blacker than black" <note>original: *nigrum nigrius nigro*; a standard alchemical description of the *nigredo* or blackening stage.</note>, as they call it, or "viler than seaweed"—not because of its own nature, but because of human opinion and its abundance. What is more useful than fire? What more precious than water? What more lovely than the earth, which gives us flowers and all delightful things? What more pleasant than the air, for if it is cut off, all pleasant things cease to be? And yet, because they are available for human use across their vast spheres, they are considered most vile by a perverse imagination.

Thus both the common and the **Philosophical shadow** are undervalued. Those who live for a long time in subterranean shadows, if suddenly brought out into the bright light of the sun, lose their sight and the sharpness of their eyes; likewise, those who linger and work only in the **Philosophical shadow**, and do not join the sun to it, are deprived of judgment and the eyes of the mind, and are frustrated in their results.

At noon, when the heavenly sun is high, the heat is greater and the shadow is smaller; so too here, as the heat is increased, the shadow is diminished, and vice versa. One must begin, therefore, when the sun turns again from the southern side toward our zenith, in **Capricorn** <note>The zodiac sign associated with the winter solstice, marking the rebirth of the sun's strength.</note>; and the first operation will be completed by **Aries** <note>The spring equinox.</note>. Then begins the "work of women" <note>original: *opus mulierum*; an alchemical term for a gentle, steady heating process, often compared to domestic tasks like washing or cooking.</note>, lasting until **Leo** <note>The height of summer.</note>; and afterward, labor follows labor, until the year catches its tail with its head, like a snake <note>This refers to the Ouroboros, the ancient symbol of a snake eating its own tail, representing the completion of a cycle and the unity of the work.</note>—that is, until it is completed.

<summary>This page explains the vital alchemical relationship between light (the Sun) and shadow (Latona/Magnesia), arguing that the "blackness" or shadow is a necessary stage for perfection. It concludes by mapping the timeline of the alchemical process onto the solar year, from Capricorn to the completion of the cycle.</summary>

<keywords>Philosophical Sun, Latona, Magnesia, Democritus, Nigredo, Shadow, Alchemical Year, Ouroboros, Opus Mulierum, Zodiacal timing</keywords>

This emblem continues that theme of duality seeking unity.</meta>

<header>FUGUE XLVI. Reciprocal.</header>

# Two Eagles, one from the East, the other from the West, come together.

->The first voice begins from the start:<-

<margin>Atalanta Fleeing</margin> <note>original: *Atalanta Fugiens*. In Maier’s musical-alchemical work, the three voices are named after the characters in the myth of the race between Atalanta and Hippomenes. This first voice represents the fleeing, volatile spirit.</note>

> Jupiter is said to have sent twin eagles from Delphi

<note>original: *Jupiter è Delphis aquilas misisse gemellas. Plagas ad Eoas occiduasque fertur.* The text is broken into syllables to fit the musical notes.</note>

->The second [voice] begins from the end, and let it sing toward the beginning.<-

<margin>Hippomenes Following</margin> <note>original: *Hippom. Sequens*. This voice represents the "fixed" principle that pursues the volatile. In this "Reciprocal Fugue," it sings the exact same melody as the first voice, but in reverse (retrograde), symbolizing the meeting of opposites.</note>

> toward the Eastern and Western regions is said.
> eagles twin sent have to Delphi from Jupiter

<image-desc>Musical notation for the third voice. The notes are longer durations (cantus firmus style). Two lines of Latin text are printed below the staff.</image-desc>
> Jupiter is said to have sent twin eagles from Delphi
> toward the Eastern and Western regions.
<margin>Go backward.</margin> <note>original: *Retrogredere*. This instruction suggests the music or the process must be reflected or reversed to achieve the center.</note>

### XLVI. German version of the Latin Epigram.

Jupiter of Delphi sent out two eagles,
Toward the rising and the setting sun, as far as the earth ends,
Because he wished to know and understand the center of the earth,
Which (as they say) were seen together again at Delphi;
But there are two appearances <note>original: *Schein*; can mean "shines," "lights," or "manifestations." In alchemy, this refers to the two opposing forces or "spirits" of the matter.</note>, this one from the East and that from the West,
Which come together and fix themselves into one.

The musical structure of a retrograde fugue reinforces this theme, with voices meeting from opposite directions.</summary>

<header>EMBLEM XLVI. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>
<sig>Bb</sig>

->Two eagles, one from the East, the other from the West, meet together.<-

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting a bearded, muscular man (Jupiter) wearing a golden crown and a red robe draped over his shoulder. He stands with his arms outstretched on a small, rocky island in a body of water. On each of his hands perches a large eagle with its wings spread. In the background, there is a serene seascape with a distant city nestled at the foot of mountains and a single sailboat on the water.</image-desc>

### EPIGRAM XLVI.

Jupiter is said to have sent twin eagles from **Delphi** <note>original: *Delphis*. Delphi was an ancient Greek sanctuary believed to be the center, or "navel" (omphalos), of the world.</note>
Toward the Eastern and Western regions:
*While he desires to seek out the middle place of the World,*
*(As the story goes) these returned to Delphi at the same time.*
*But those are two stones, one from the east,*
*The other from the west, which join together well.* <note>original: *lapides bini... qui bene conveniunt*. This refers to the alchemical process of uniting the "fixed" and "volatile" principles to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

<header>FUGUE XLVI. Reciprocal.</header> <note>In music, a "Reciprocal" or "Crab" fugue (canon cancrizans) involves one voice playing the melody forward while another plays the same melody backward. This perfectly mirrors the emblem's theme of two eagles meeting in the center from opposite directions.</note>

# Two Eagles, one from the East, the other from the West, come together.

->The first voice begins from the start:<-

<margin>Atalanta Fleeing</margin> <note>original: *Atalanta Fugiens*. In Maier’s musical-alchemical work, the three voices are named after the characters in the myth of the race between Atalanta and Hippomenes. This first voice represents the fleeing, volatile spirit.</note>
<image-desc>Musical notation in common time on a five-line staff with diamond-shaped notes (mensural notation). Two lines of Latin text are printed below the staff, aligned with the notes.</image-desc>

> toward the Eastern and Western regions.
<note>original: *Jupiter è Delphis aquilas misisse gemellas. Plagas ad Eoas occiduasque fertur.* The text is broken into syllables to fit the musical notes.</note>

->The second voice begins from the end, and let it sing toward the beginning.<-

<margin>Hippomenes Following</margin> <note>original: *Hippom. Sequens*. This voice represents the "fixed" principle that pursues the volatile. In this "Reciprocal Fugue," it sings the exact same melody as the first voice, but in reverse (retrograde), symbolizing the meeting of opposites.</note>
<image-desc>Musical notation for the second voice, which is the retrograde (backwards) version of the first voice. The lyrics are also printed in reverse order below the staff.</image-desc>

<page-num>194</page-num>

### DISCOURSE XLVI.

Cicero, in his book *On the Nature of the Gods*, relates that the most ancient Apollo was born of **Vulcan** <note>original: *Vulcano*. In alchemy, Vulcan represents the terrestrial fire used to heat the furnace.</note> and was the guardian of Athens. If this opinion is transferred to allegory, as it ought to be, it is most true: because **Vulcan** produces the **Philosophical Sun**, which is Apollo. But the common view prevailed, that he was born of **Jupiter**. Yet when **Latona** was carrying the twins Apollo and Diana in her womb, whom she had conceived by Jupiter, the jealous **Juno** sent **Python**, a serpent of horrendous vastness, to pursue and harass the pregnant woman. The miserable woman, after long wanderings, was at last carried by ship to the island of **Ortygia**, to her sister **Asteria**, who reigned there. And since that island was almost entirely inundated by the sea, it gave a place to Latona in labor; from this it was called **Delos** <note>Meaning "The Visible" or "The Manifest."</note>, which previously was *Adelos* <note>original: "*Αδηλο*" (Adēlos), meaning 'hidden' or 'obscure'.</note>. 

There she brought forth her children. **Diana** emerged first from the womb, and she acted as a midwife to her mother while she was laboring in the birth of her brother Apollo. From this it happened that pregnant women invoke her Divinity in childbirth, and they called her either **Lucina** or **Ilithyia**, because she showed the light to newborn infants once their eyes were opened. 

Therefore Apollo, having been born and reached adulthood, slew **Python**, his mother's tormentor, with his arrows, and he killed the **Cyclopes** because they had forged the lightning bolt for Jupiter to kill his son **Aesculapius**. Jupiter had thrust Aesculapius down to the underworld with a lightning bolt because he had restored **Hippolytus**, who had been torn apart by horses, back to life. 

We have demonstrated in many places that these things are purely <term>Chymical</term> <note>The author uses 'Chymica' to mean Alchemy, the art of transformation. He argues that myths are not literal history but chemical recipes disguised as stories.</note>. For Latona, Cynthia <note>Another name for Diana.</note>, Apollo, and Python are the requirements of the art, which relate to one another in the manner that has been narrated. However, when these things were made public in the writings of the most ancient poets—such as **Orpheus**, **Linus**, **Musaeus**, and **Homer**—they provided an occasion for religion and the worship of Apollo among the ignorant. Hence, Apollo was worshipped in many places in Europe and Asia, and innumerable temples were erected to him. 

He held a temple especially worthy of veneration by ancient religion at **Delphi**, in which many statues of solid gold and silver of great weight and craftsmanship were placed by kings and princes, along with other most precious gifts which were hidden in the sanctuaries by men of every status for the sake of religion. **Pausanias** <note>A 2nd-century Greek traveler and geographer.</note> relates that a bronze skeleton of admirable workmanship was hung from the dome of the temple by **Hippocrates**. To Apollo 

<meta>The catchword "quoque" (also) indicates the next page will continue listing things dedicated to Apollo or his attributes.</meta>

<summary>This discourse reinterprets the birth of Apollo and Diana as an alchemical allegory, where Vulcan (fire) produces the Philosophical Sun. It explains that ancient myths were intended as chemical instructions that the "ignorant" mistook for literal religious history.</summary>

<keywords>Apollo, Vulcan, Latona, Python, Delos, alchemy, Chymica, Diana, Aesculapius, Orpheus, Delphi, Pausanias, allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical interpretation of the Apollo myth, specifically focusing on the sacred objects and locations associated with his oracle at Delphi. ### DISCOURSE XLVI. 195

...was also consecrated that celebrated **tripod** <note>original: *tripes*; a three-legged stool or altar associated with prophecy.</note>, dedicated by **Pelops** when he married Hippodamia, daughter of King Oenomaus of Elis; this tripod **Mulciber** <note>An epithet for Vulcan, the god of fire and metalworking.</note> had fashioned and given to Pelops. It was erected in the middle of the temple, and sitting upon it, the **Pythia** <note>The high priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi.</note> would receive the breath of the Spirit <note>original: *Dæmonis*; in a classical context, this refers to a guiding spirit or divine inspiration rather than an evil entity.</note> breaking forth from the deep chasm. Seized by this spirit, she would prophesy and give answers to those inquiring about the outcome of future events. **Delphi** was situated in **Boeotia** at the foot of **Parnassus**. Near the temple was a prophetic fountain, **Cassiotis** <note>Likely referring to the Cassotis spring at Delphi.</note>, which possessed a strange quality: if burning torches were moved close to it, it extinguished them; yet if they were moved far away, they would suddenly ignite and catch fire. Furthermore, drinking the water of this fountain granted the power of prophecy, though the drinkers of its waters were rendered shorter-lived. Since there was a great gathering from all parts of **Europe** and **Asia** to the Delphic oracle, the poets fabled that this place—namely **Parnassus**—was in the center of the earth. They proved this by the example of **Jupiter**, who had tested it by releasing two eagles. Truly, since this matter does not rely on historical certainty, it is not far from the truth to ascribe it to the **alchemists** <note>original: *chymicis*.</note>, especially since the whole of **Apollo**, as has been said, is alchemical in origin—even if a spirit later confirmed human superstition under that name and gave prophecies.

The "two eagles" are two stones, one of which comes from the East and the other from the West, as the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" usually refers to the collective authority of alchemical authors (the *adepti*).</note> have demonstrated in many ways. **Jupiter** sent them forth as his own armor-bearers. Now, the eagle is seen to be a friend to **Apollo** (or the Sun), because it tests its chicks against the Sun; if they cannot endure its gaze, it rejects them as degenerate. Its feathers, when mixed with other things, are said not to decay, and to consume the feathers of other birds; they also easily accept gilding. The eagle does not die of old age or sickness, but of hunger. For as it grows, the upper curve of its beak becomes so hooked that it cannot take in food. Once this is cast off, the eagle plunges itself three times into a fountain, and in this way is restored to youth. Hence the **Psalmist** <note>original: *Psalmographus*; referring to the author of the Biblical Psalms.</note> says: *Your youth shall be renewed like the eagle's* <note>original: *Renovabitur, ut aquilæ, juventus tua*; a reference to Psalm 103:5.</note>. Among winged creatures, it is never struck by lightning. It is in a constant struggle with the dragon, which for that reason hunts the eagle's eggs. All these gifts of its nature provided the reason why the Philosophers praise the eagle in their art and liken the Stone to it. Since countless examples of this matter are found in their books, we shall not bring forward more here.

Bb ij

### XLVII. German translation of the Latin Epigram.

The Wolf comes running from the rising of the Sun, but the Dog 
From the setting; these both have a great heap of gall <note>original: "Galln"; refers to the bitter digestive fluid, symbolizing caustic chemical reactivity.</note>,
This one has bitten that one, and that one this one in great anger,
And they have torn one another apart with fury;
They are two stones, which are given to everyone for free
In all places and times; keep them very well.

## The Wolf coming from the East and the Dog coming from the West have bitten each other.

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting a wolf and a dog in a violent confrontation. The two animals are reared up on their hind legs, gripping each other and biting at their respective necks. The wolf is positioned on the left, and the dog on the right. They stand in a grassy landscape. In the background to the left, a sun with a human face shines over a body of water with ships. ### EPIGRAM XLVII.

From here, where the Sun rises, the Wolf approaches, but where in the Sea
The Sun is merged, from there comes the dog; both are swollen with bile:
<note>original: "bile tument." In alchemical theory, "bile" or "choleric humor" represents the corrosive, sharp, or acidic nature of the chemicals as they begin to react and dissolve one another.</note>
This one bites that one, and that one bites this one, driven by a stimulating fury,
And each appeared with a rabid, gaping mouth.
These are the twin stones, which are given freely everywhere
To all people and at all times, if only you can hold them.
<note>The "twin stones" refer to the two primary materials of the Work. Alchemists often claimed their "stone" was common and "thrown in the streets," meaning the raw material is ubiquitous but its value is unrecognized by the uninitiated.</note>

<vocab>Wolf (Lupus), Dog (Canis), East (Oriens), West (Occidens), The Sun rises (Sol oritur), stones (lapides), secrets of Nature (secreta Naturae), epigram (epigramma)</vocab>

<summary>This page illustrates the violent "matrimony" or chemical reaction between two opposing substances—symbolized by the Wolf of the East and the Dog of the West—which must consume each other to produce the Philosopher's Stone. It emphasizes that the materials for this divine art are common and accessible to all, though their true nature is hidden.</summary>
<keywords>volatile and fixed principles, duality, canine symbolism, alchemical conflict, primary matter, universal availability</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse XLVII, providing the philosophical commentary on the emblem of the Wolf and the Dog. It builds on the previous page's imagery of "twin stones" and the violent interaction between the "Western" and "Eastern" forces.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XLVII.</header>

Philosophers in many places have made mention of two stones given to us for free, as Isaac, Arnold, and others do; among them, Avicenna says they lie cast away in dung, neglected by the common people, which, if joined together, complete the mastery.
<note>Isaac Hollandus, Arnold of Villanova, and Avicenna (Ibn Sina) are foundational authorities in medieval and Renaissance alchemy. The idea of the "Stone" being found in "dung" or "vile places" is a common alchemical trope—meaning the raw material of the Great Work is ubiquitous and undervalued by those who lack wisdom.</note>
Some extol the "Western Mercury," which preferred itself over gold and overcame it.
<note>original: "Mercurium occidentalem." This often refers to the volatile principle or the "quick-silver" derived from mineral ores.</note>
But best of all, the author of the *Counsel on the Marriage of the Sun and Moon*, drawing from the *Epistle of Aristotle*, describes the two stones where he says:
*That there are two principal stones of this art, the White and the Red, of a marvelous nature. The White begins to appear upon the face of the waters at the setting of the sun, hiding itself until midnight, and afterwards it sinks into the depths. But the Red works in the opposite way, for it begins to ascend above the waters at the rising of the sun until midday, and afterwards it descends into the depths.*
<note>original: "Albus in occasu solis... Rubeus vero ex opposito." This describes the cyclic nature of the alchemical process, where the "White" (Lunar/Silver) and "Red" (Solar/Gold) stages of the work follow the movement of the heavens.</note>

These stones, therefore, are the aforementioned eagles sent forth by Jupiter from Delphi.
<note>In Greek mythology, Jupiter (Zeus) released two eagles from the opposite ends of the world; they met at Delphi, marking it as the center or "Omphalos." In alchemy, this represents the meeting of two opposing forces at a central point of equilibrium.</note>
These are also the wolf and the dog, coming from different or opposite regions of the earth, one of which bit the other, and both were made rabid, as Rhazes testifies in his *Epistle*.
<note>Rhazes refers to the 9th-century Persian physician and alchemist Al-Razi.</note>
These stones are the truest <term>Bezoar</term>, of which the East Indies sends the most excellent kind, taken from the bellies of wild beasts, while the West Indies (Peru) gives another of less efficacy, obtained from tame animals.
<term>Bezoar: A "stone" or mass found in the digestive systems of animals, highly prized in the Renaissance as a universal antidote to poison.</term>
Thus the East gives the most ferocious wolf, and the West gives the dog familiar to men; that is, <term>Sulphur</term> comes from the Eastern region and <term>Mercury</term> from the Western.
<note>original: "Sulphur ab Eoo, Mercurius ab Hesperio." These represent the "Fixed" (Sulphur/Wolf) and "Volatile" (Mercury/Dog) principles.</note>
Of these, the latter is soft and manageable, while the former is choleric and irritable. As soon as these two strike one another, they fall into mutual biting. The dog, distinguished by its size, wins the first victory by prostrating the wolf and rendering it half-dead. But the wolf, gathering its strength, throws down the dog afterward and presses upon the fallen one even unto death. Meanwhile, the wolf receives no fewer nor less lethal wounds from the dog than those it had inflicted, until they consume and kill each other with their mutual bites.

Regarding the wolf, Rosinus says to Euthicia:
*That he is the victorious soldier of the two, strengthened, of great price and most intense vigor, piercing bodies when he meets them; and he is white in appearance, but red by experiment; and he is the male who took the Moon to wife, whom some think to be gold of the most precious connection, whose frozen state is never dissolved, nor are its footprints erased, which God has bestowed upon the holy Philosophers and the elect. Know, that nature began*—
<note>original: "De lupo Rosinus ad Euthiciam." Rosinus is the Latinized name for Zosimos of Panopolis, an early alchemist. This passage describes the transformative power of the "Wolf" (the acidic or corrosive agent) which, though it appears "white" (volatile), reveals a "red" (fixed) nature through the fire of experiment.</note>

<meta>The catchword "com-" at the bottom of the page indicates the next word is likely "completum" (completed) or "commisceri" (to be mixed), continuing the description of the chemical reaction.</meta>

<summary>This page explains the symbolism of the Wolf and the Dog as the two primary alchemical stones or principles, Sulphur and Mercury. Their violent conflict represents a chemical reaction where two substances dissolve and "kill" each other to produce a new, transformed matter.</summary>
<keywords>Sulphur, Mercury, White Stone, Red Stone, Bezoar, Rosinus, Zosimos, chemical marriage, duality, Delphi eagles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the "two stones" from page 198, focusing on the symbolic combat between the "Oriental Wolf" and the "Western Dog," which represent the interaction of sulfur and mercury in the alchemical process.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XLVII.</header>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

...I compare [her] as an enemy. *And soon after,* <gloss>that</gloss> *sulfur,* he says, *is most strong and fights against the fire because it contains and is contained:* 
<note>The Latin text uses the word "sulphur" to describe the active, fiery principle of alchemy. To "contain and be contained" refers to the paradoxical state where the substance is both the vessel and the matter being transformed.</note>
For from these joined together a most precious color emerges, and the sulfur, which naturally flees, can never flee afterward because the soul has likewise pierced it, and the tincture of the soul is mixed with the body; thus the body has contained the soul and prohibited its natural flight. 
<note>original: "naturaliter fugit." In alchemical terms, a substance that "flees" is volatile—it evaporates or escapes when heated. "Fixing" the volatile substance so it remains stable in fire is a primary goal of the Work.</note>
*And then to the one asking which of the stones is stronger, he responds:* The stone which is not a stone is stronger than the other enemy; but the red is stronger than that which has strengthened its companions with its own fortitude. 
<note>The "Stone which is not a stone" (original: "Lapis qui non est lapis") is a classic alchemical riddle suggesting that the transformative agent is not a common mineral, but a spiritual or philosophical substance.</note>
Thus the Oriental Wolf is stronger than the Western Dog, although he does not enjoy the effect of victory, but dies together with his enemy: but from both of them is made a tinting poison. 
<term>Tinting poison: (Latin: "venenum tingens") In this context, "poison" does not mean a lethal toxin but a potent, penetrating substance capable of "infecting" or transforming the color and nature of base metals.</term>
The difference between the wolf and the dog is small, since a mastiff or a very large dog shows the form and appearance of a wolf, so that it seems to be a wolf in origin, but made tame by long breeding: In the same way, sulfur and mercury differ little between themselves; because that takes its origin from this, or this from that: Mercury indeed brought forth sulfur, but sulfur purified Mercury and rendered it such. 
<note>This passage explores the "Sulfur-Mercury theory," the medieval belief that all metals are composed of varying proportions and purities of these two principles.</note>
Regarding these, the same Rosinus, *when [the soul] asks From whence comes its color? responds: from its most intense bitterness. And she: From whence comes its bitterness and intensity? He responds: from the impurity of its metal. And she: Does its red color never stand out above? He responds: Yes. And she: Is it never even hotter than fire? He responds: fire in respect to it is as water in respect to fire. And she: Is it stronger than fire? He responds* no. *And she:* Why then do you assert it to be stronger than fire? He responds: because when fires meet each other, one eats the other. 
<note>Rosinus is the Latinized name for Zosimos of Panopolis, a 3rd-century alchemist. This dialogue describes the "Red" stage (Rubedo), where the substance attains a heat and intensity that transcends ordinary physical fire.</note>
It is clear, therefore, that one becomes the nourishment and food of the other, and as much as decreases in one, that much increases in the other, until the increasing one prevails and the dragon has devoured the serpent. 
<note>The dragon devouring the serpent is a symbol of the "Fixed" principle overcoming the "Volatile," or the completion of a cycle of purification.</note>
In great battles, it often happens that those who have suffered the greater slaughter obtain the field and the victory; so also the dog, although prostrate, has not fallen entirely conquered, since he holds the enemy so tightly that the latter cannot live without him, nor can the former die without the latter.

<vocab>sulfur, mercury, Rosinus, Oriental wolf, Western dog, dragon, stone, tincture</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a fight between a wolf and a dog to explain the chemical union of sulfur and mercury. The text emphasizes that while these elements seem like enemies, they must "die" together to create the "tinting poison" or philosopher's stone.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur-mercury theory, Rosinus (Zosimos), Oriental Wolf, Western Dog, Rubedo, alchemical combat, fixation of the volatile</keywords>

### XLVIII. German version of the Latin Epigram.

A King, mighty in goods and people, favored a spring-water above all others,
Which he commanded his servants to bring to him;
He drank this often, until he found his veins burdened and oppressed by it,
Becoming entirely misshapen; then he was taken in by famous physicians,
By whom he was purged through sweat, stool, and also through the mouth,
So that his cheeks henceforth appeared as red as roses at that hour.
<note>This "purging" (original: "purgirt") through three ways—sweat, stool, and mouth—refers to the alchemical processes of evaporation, precipitation, and sublimation used to cleanse the "King" (the primary matter or gold).</note>

The allegory illustrates the alchemical need to purify the "King" (the matter of the Work) through various chemical "purges" to restore its health and vibrant color.</summary>

## The King, having drunk water, falls ill; cured by physicians, he
->attains health.<-

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving illustrating an alchemical allegory. In the foreground, a king stands on the right wearing a gold crown, a blue breastplate over a yellow tunic, and a flowing red cloak. He is approached by a young man in a blue tunic and purple mantle who offers him a golden chalice. Between them stands a bearded man in a yellow robe. In the background, visible through a large archway supported by a central column, is a bedroom where a sick figure lies in a bed with red curtains, attended by a physician in a blue robe. On the far right, soldiers with spears are partially visible. A large, ornate golden ewer sits on the floor in the lower left corner.</image-desc>
<insert>Red circular library stamp: B.R</insert>

---

->EPIGRAM XLVIII.<-

> **A** King, powerful in riches and peoples, loved a fountain,
> And the waters which he demanded his servants bring to him:
> These he drinks and drinks again, until his veins are soon filled;
> <note>In alchemical symbolism, the King's over-consumption of water represents the "dropsy" of the matter—a state where there is an excess of moisture (mercury) that prevents the "fixed" gold from manifesting.</note>
> Discolored, he is taken up by illustrious physicians;
> By whom, once he was purged by sweat, by the bowels,
> <note>original: "sudoribus, alvo." These refer to alchemical processes of purification: evaporation (sweat) and precipitation or filtration (the bowels).</note>
> And by the mouth, soon both his cheeks were tinged with roses.
> <note>The "roses" on the cheeks signify the achievement of the "Rubedo" or Red Work, the final stage of the alchemical process where the substance reaches perfection.</note>

***

<summary>This page illustrates the alchemical process of purification through the metaphor of a King who falls ill from drinking too much water and must be "cured" by physicians to regain his health and color. It represents the transition from the "dissolution" of matter to its final, perfected "red" state.</summary>
<keywords>King, physicians, fountain, dropsy, purification, Rubedo, Michael Maier, alchemy, healing, transformation</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse for Emblem XLVIII. While the previous page provided the image of the King being offered a drink and the poetic summary of his illness, this page provides a prose commentary (Discursus) that blends historical anecdotes about the King Xerxes and famous physicians with alchemical theory.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
<header>### DISCOURSE XLVIII.</header>

**Xerxes**, that most powerful King of the Persians, when he was leading his army through dry and uncultivated places under the heat, being parched with thirst, did not reject a draught of turbid <note>Original: *turbidae*; meaning muddy or foul water.</note> water offered by a certain soldier. Instead, he drank it most gratefully and decorated the one who brought it with a most ample gift. And indeed, if anyone at this very time (as some very recent histories testify) should travel through the borders of Persia, it is said that they would very rarely find springs of sweet water, since the stagnant waters are salty, and the very soil offers a most abundant saltiness on its surface.

In that same way, the King of whom the Philosophers remember, being vexed by thirst, commanded that an abundance of sweet water be brought to him. Having had it brought, he drank to satiety, just as is clear to anyone from <term>Merlin’s allegory</term>. <note>In alchemical literature, "Merlin" is a pseudonym for an author of allegories (such as those in the *Rosarium Philosophorum*) describing the chemical "death" and "rebirth" of matter, rather than the Arthurian wizard.</note>

The cure of the sick and discolored King is undertaken by various physicians. The **Egyptians**, having administered their medicines, moved the <term>humors</term> <note>In ancient medicine, the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which must be balanced for health.</note> while they were still crude; <note>Original: *crudos*. In alchemy, "crude" matter is unrefined or raw material that has not yet been "cooked" or purified by fire.</note> yet **Hippocrates** asserts that humors ought to be purged only after they have been "concocted" <note>i.e., ripened or processed by the body’s heat.</note>, unless they are fluid and wandering. For then they must be drawn out immediately, lest they perhaps make an attack and rush into the more noble parts or viscera. Because of this, dangerous symptoms befell the king, namely <term>lipothymia</term> <note>fainting or a sinking feeling.</note> and <term>syncope</term> <note>a sudden loss of consciousness.</note>.

The **Alexandrian physicians**, however, arriving last during this chronic illness, were held to be more successful, and they restored the King to his former health. It is worth the effort to heal such a great King, who, having been made healthy, offers a benevolent hand and a serene face to his physician. We read that the cures of many were excellently rewarded by various kings: such as **Democides** by **Polycrates**, the tyrant of the Samians, with two talents; or **Erasistratus** (whom **Pliny** writes was the disciple of **Chrysippus**, born from the daughter of **Aristotle**) for healing **King Antiochus**, who was sick out of love for his stepmother **Stratonice**. Erasistratus was rewarded with a hundred talents by Antiochus's son, **Ptolemy**. We might also mention **Jacques Coictier** <note>Original: *Jacobi Cocterii*. He was the infamous and highly-paid physician to King Louis XI of France (though the text here identifies him as Louis II).</note>, the physician of the French King **Louis II**, from whom he received four thousand gold crowns every month as a salary—not to mention other more recent figures.

Yet the healing of *this* King <note>Referring to the Alchemical King, or the "Stone" in its perfected state.</note> is rewarded by a far greater payment or prize. For as **Hermes** and **Geber** say in the *Rosary*:

> "He who shall once have perfected this art, if he were to live a million years, and every day had to feed four thousand men, he would not lack for anything."

**Senior** confirms this, saying:

> "He who possesses the stone from which the Elixir is made is so rich, just as he who possesses fire, *can*..." <meta>The sentence continues on the next page: "can give light to others."</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical allegory of a King who falls ill after drinking impure water, using medical theory and historical anecdotes to show how "crude" matter must be properly "concocted" and purified. It emphasizes that the successful alchemist, like a great physician, receives a reward of inexhaustible wealth and health.</summary>
<keywords>Xerxes, Merlin's Allegory, Hippocrates, Alexandrian physicians, humoral medicine, Erasistratus, Jacques Coictier, Rosarium Philosophorum, Hermes Trismegistus, Geber, Senior Zadith, Philosopher's Stone</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose commentary for Discourse XLVIII. The previous page ended by describing how the "King" (the alchemical material) was treated by Egyptian physicians who attempted to balance his humors while they were still in a raw or "crude" state. This page compares the King's immense wealth to historical figures like Xerxes and Pythius, and explains the alchemical process of transmutation using symbolic language.</meta>

<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>### DISCOURSE XLVIII.</header>

He can give fire to whomever he wishes, whenever he wishes, and in whatever amount he wishes, without any danger or loss to himself. The father of **Democritus** <note>A Greek philosopher often associated in later tradition with alchemical secrets.</note> was so wealthy that he provided a banquet for the entire army of **Xerxes**. Likewise, a certain very opulent man named **Pythius** offered that same King the pay for his entire army and provisions for five months, provided only that he be allowed to keep the youngest of his five sons at home—who was the comfort of his old age—rather than being forced to go to the royal camp. However, the barbarian King, taking Pythius’s request most indignantly, ordered the youngest son to be cut into two parts and fixed upon poles on either side of the royal road through which the entire army was to pass, as **Sabellicus** <note>Marcantonio Sabellico (1436–1506), a Venetian historian.</note> notes in Book 2, Ennead 3. Yet the riches of these men are nothing compared to the wealth of this King, which is without measure or number. Once this King had been healed and freed from the waters, all the kings and powerful men of other regions honored and feared him. And when they wished to see evidence of his wonders, they would place one ounce of well-washed **Mercury** <term>Quicksilver</term> into a crucible and cast upon it a tiny amount—about the size of a grain of millet—of his nails, his hair, or his blood. Then, blowing lightly upon the coals, they would allow it to cool with them, and they would find the Stone, such as I know. <note>Original: *lapidem, qualem scio*. This is a common alchemical phrase where the author hints at personal experience with the "Philosopher’s Stone."</note>

This is the one whom **Count Bernhard** <note>Bernhard Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist whose allegories often feature a King bathing to regain his youth.</note> mentions, saying that he gives as much of a kingdom to six of his courtiers as he himself possesses, provided they wait until he has recovered his youth in the bath and is adorned with various garments: namely, a **black breastplate**, a **white shirt**, and **purple blood**. <note>These colors represent the three primary stages of the alchemical "Great Work": *Nigredo* (blackness/decay), *Albedo* (whiteness/purification), and *Rubedo* (redness/perfection).</note> For then he promises that he will give to each of them some of his blood, making them sharers of his own riches.

->Cc ij<-

<summary>This page concludes Discourse XLVIII by contrasting the cruel and limited wealth of historical figures like Xerxes with the infinite, healing wealth of the Alchemical King. It describes the "projection" process where a tiny amount of the Stone transforms mercury into gold and explains the symbolic stages of the work through the King's clothing.</summary>

### XLIX. German version of the Latin Epigram.

When Phoebus, Mercury, and the God of Fire, as fables show,
->Had placed their powerful seed into a cow’s hide,<-
And became three fathers at once of the great Orion,
->So it is said that there are three fathers of the Son of Wisdom;<- <note>The "Son of Wisdom" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the completed alchemical Work.</note>

->**EMBLEM XLIX. On the secrets of Nature.**<-
->**The Philosophical Child acknowledges three fathers, just like Orion.**<-

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting five male figures in an outdoor landscape gathered around a large bull's hide spread on the ground. ## EPIGRAM XLIX.

> *A fable is told, that Phoebus, Vulcan, and Hermes* <note>original: "Phœbus, Vulcanus & Hermes". In alchemy, Phoebus represents the Sun or gold; Vulcan represents the fire of the furnace; and Hermes represents Mercury or the transformative spirit.</note>
> *placed their seeds into the hide of a bull;* <note>original: "in pellem bubulam". This refers to the mythological origin of the giant Orion, born from the shared essence of three gods.</note>
> *And together these three were the fathers of the great* ORION:
> *Indeed, they say the Offspring of Wisdom* <note>original: "Sobolem Sophiæ", referring to the Philosopher's Stone or the perfected alchemical result.</note> *is thus triple-fathered:*
> *For the* SUN <note>original: "SOL"</note> *is the first, while Vulcan is said to be the second,*
> *And the third father excels in this art.* <note>The "third father" is Hermes, representing the "Art" of alchemy itself and the volatile spirit of Mercury.</note>

<sig>Cc 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: WOMAN-</meta> <note>original catchword: "MULIE-", likely referring to "Mulier" (woman) on the following page.</note>
<meta>institutional stamp: B.R.</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the mythological birth of Orion as an allegory for the creation of the Philosopher's Stone, which is said to require three "fathers": the Sun (gold), Vulcan (fire), and Hermes (mercurial art).</summary>
<keywords>Orion, Phoebus, Vulcan, Hermes, Sun, Sophia, Philosophical Child, triple fatherhood, alchemy, fire, mercury</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse associated with Emblem XLIX, expanding on the "Epigram" from page 205. It transitions from the physical laws of human reproduction to the allegorical "supernatural" generation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>
->### DISCOURSE XLIX.###<-

**WOMEN** who prostitute themselves to various men rarely conceive viable offspring due to the confusion of different seeds. For Nature, in the generation of humans and most animals, does not admit **superfetation** <note>The conception of a second fetus while one is already present in the womb; here, the author refers to the idea of multiple men contributing to a single pregnancy.</note>, except perhaps very rarely. Hence, every offspring is born from a single father and mother, whether it be one child or many. This remains true despite the histories and outcomes cited by those who judge otherwise—most notably the case of **Margaret**, the wife of Herman, Count of Henneberg. It is said that in the year 1276, she gave birth to three hundred and sixty-five children. All of them were baptized; the males were named John and the females Elizabeth. After they died, they were buried in the church of Loosduinen <note>original: "Lauſdunenſi"</note>, situated one mile from The Hague <note>original: "Haga comitum"</note> toward the sea in Holland. There, one can still see the bronze basin in which they were baptized, along with an inscription of the history. 

The cause of this is said to be that the Countess, seeing a poor woman carrying twins in her arms, called her an adulteress, as if it were impossible for more than one child to be produced from one conception by one man, but necessarily from different men. Whereupon a curse was made by the poor woman, who knew herself to be pure of that crime: that the Countess might conceive as many in her womb at one time and from one man as there are days in the year. This was indeed a miracle, yet it was a natural work that occurred through divine vengeance. 

In the **Philosophical Work**, however, which is in other respects contrary to nature, such a thing is easily admitted under the veil of allegory. For here, one fetus is said to have three fathers, or even two, as well as mothers. Hence **Raymond [Lull]** <note>A 13th-century polymath and major authority in alchemical literature.</note>, as the *Rosarium* cites him, says: *"Our infant has two fathers and two mothers."* And because he is dearly nourished by the whole substance in the fire, he never dies. Likewise, **Dionysus** or **Bacchus** is called **"twice-mothered"** <note>original: "bimater"</note>; when his mother [Semele] was consumed by fire, he was snatched premature from her womb and sewn into his father's thigh, so that the father was made a mother. 

But this is more clearly declared in the conception of **Orion**, who is said to have been born from the mixed seeds of **Apollo**, **Vulcan**, and **Mercury** enclosed in a bull’s hide for ten months. These things would be entirely monstrous, and not merely fabulous, were it not that a secret of nature lay hidden beneath these coverings, which is not obvious to everyone. **Lull**, in his *Theory of the Testament*, attributes many of these same fathers to the Philosophical fetus—namely the **Sun**, who is **Apollo**...

<meta>catchword: or</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the biological rarity of "superfetation" (multiple paternity), contrasting the legendary 365 children of Margaret of Henneberg with the alchemical "Philosophical Child" who allegorically requires three fathers: Apollo (the Sun), Vulcan (Fire), and Mercury.</summary>

<keywords>superfetation, Margaret of Henneberg, Loosduinen, alchemical allegory, Orion, Bacchus, Raymond Lull, Apollo, Vulcan, Mercury, Philosophical Stone</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse XLIX from page 206. While the previous page discussed the natural and miraculous generation of humans (using the example of Margaret of Henneberg), this page moves into the allegorical generation of the Philosopher’s Stone, comparing its "three fathers" to mythological figures.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE XLIX.</header>

...or the celestial Sun is the primary author of this generation, who by his inexpressible and hidden—or astral—power works upon a certain matter known to the Philosophers, as if into a woman’s womb. Within it, he produces a son or offspring similar to himself, to whom he will later hand over and bequeath his own weapons and the insignia of his virtues by paternal right. This is the power to ripen the immature and to <term>tinge<gloss>In alchemy, to 'tinge' means to transform or dye a metal into a more noble state, like gold</gloss></term> and purify that which is neither colored nor cleansed. 

For whatever the Sun completes in a thousand years, this son of his will achieve in half an hour. Therefore, so that his power may become a thousandfold stronger than that of the Sun, his father entrusts him to **Vulcan** <note>The Roman god of fire, representing the alchemist’s furnace and controlled heat</note> for instruction, and to the **Artificer** <note>The alchemist or chemist performing the work</note> simultaneously, so that his noble character may be cultivated by them and his strength multiplied. Indeed, it is well known that much depends on the habits formed in youth <note>original: "à teneris assuescere multum esse"</note>. 

Thus **Achilles**, **Jason**, and **Hercules** were all handed over to **Chiron** <note>The wise centaur and tutor of heroes, representing the specialized knowledge required to refine raw power</note> to be educated for this same purpose. For **Milo of Croton**, who carried a calf when he was a boy, was able to lift a full-grown ox as a man simply because of this habit. 

It is not without reason, then, that besides the Sun, **Vulcan** and the **Artificer** are called the fathers of this child. The first father caused him to exist, but the others caused him to be such a being of such great stature. Indeed, a tuition fee <term>didactron<gloss>A Greek term for a teacher's fee or reward for instruction</gloss></term> equal to the value of the instruction cannot be paid to these masters, just as no reward can truly compensate parents for the act of generation. The parents prepare the body; the teachers prepare the mind. If the mind is superior to the body, then no less gratitude is owed to the teachers than to the parents. 

Furthermore, in the birth of **Orion**, **Mercury** provided the matter, **Apollo** gave the form, and **Vulcan** provided the heat or the external <term>efficient cause<gloss>In Aristotelian philosophy, the agent or force that brings something into being</gloss></term>. So too in the **Philosophical Work** <note>The Great Work or *Magnum Opus* of alchemy</note>, it is fitting that three fathers should seem to have conspired together to produce a single offspring, in which the delights of the Philosophers are found.

<meta>catchword: The (translated from 'Der')</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical concept of 'three fathers' (the Sun, Vulcan, and the Alchemist) who collaborate to produce the Philosopher's Stone, which is described as being a thousand times more powerful than the natural Sun. It uses the education of mythological heroes like Achilles and the birth of Orion as allegories for the refinement of alchemical matter.</summary>

<keywords>Sol, Sun, Vulcan, Artificer, Chiron, Milo of Croton, Orion, three fathers, Philosophical Work, Magnum Opus, maturation, tinging</keywords>

Let a grave be dug deep for the poisonous Dragon, to whom the Woman
->Shall be bound firmly by her body,<-

<header>EMBLEM 50. On the Secrets of Nature.</header>

->The dragon kills the woman, and she kills him, and together<-

<image-desc>A hand-colored engraving depicting a dramatic alchemical allegory. In a shallow pit or grave in the foreground, a human figure (representing a woman, dressed in a red garment) lies on her back. A large, winged dragon with yellow and green scales is coiled around her body and neck, biting her face, while she appears to be struggling with it. ## EPIGRAM 50.

> *Let a deep tomb be dug for the venomous Dragon,* <note>The "Dragon" in alchemy often symbolizes "volatile" sulfur or crude mercury that must be "fixed" or tamed.</note>
> *To whom let the woman be firmly joined in her own bond:* <note>The "Woman" represents the "fixed" or earthly principle; her "bond" is the chemical attraction that holds the two together.</note>
> *While he plucks the joys of the marriage bed,*
> *She dies, and with her the Dragon is covered by the earth.* <note>This "death" refers to <term>putrefaction<gloss>the stage of the alchemical process where the matter decomposes and turns black, a necessary step before purification.</gloss></term></note>
> *From this his body is surrendered to death, and with gore*
> *It is stained: This is the true path of your work.* <note>The "work" is the <term>Magnum Opus<gloss>The Great Work, the traditional term for the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</gloss></term></note>

<summary>As the final emblem of the collection, this page depicts the "chemical marriage" as a mutual sacrifice where the dragon (volatile) and woman (fixed) perish together to be transformed. Their shared "blood" and burial signify the crucial stage of putrefaction required to achieve the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Dragon, Woman, Tomb, Marriage Bed, Blood, Putrefaction, Magnum Opus, Conjunction of Opposites</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on Emblem 50, providing the theoretical and allegorical explanation for the image of the dragon and the woman killing one another in a tomb. It transitions from the poetic epigram to a prose commentary on the chemical elements involved.</meta>

<page-num>210</page-num>
# DISCOURSE 50.

<meta>catchword: post (original Latin: "after")</meta>

The dwelling of dragons is in the caverns of the earth, but that of humans is above the earth in the nearest air; these two are contrary elements and are commanded by the Philosophers to be joined, so that the action of one upon the other may occur. <note>In alchemy, "conjunction" is the union of opposites, such as a solid "earthy" substance and a volatile "airy" one.</note> Regarding the woman, others understand her as **Basil** <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemical author.</note> describes in his second "key." *It is not useful,* he says, *for the eagle to place its nest in the Alps: for its chicks would die from the cold of the snow at the summit of the mountains. But if you add the cold dragon—who has had his habitation in the rocks for a long time and creeps out of the earth's caves—to the eagle, and place them both in the infernal seat,* <note>original: "infernalem sellam." This refers to the bottom of the alchemical furnace or a specific heating chamber designed to mimic subterranean heat.</note> *then Pluto* <note>The Roman god of the underworld, here representing the secret, internal fire of the earth.</note> *will blow the wind and draw out a fiery, volatile spirit from the cold dragon. This spirit, with its great heat, will burn the feathers of the eagle and stir up a sweat-inducing bath so that the snow on the highest mountains melts and becomes water; by this, a wretched bath is well prepared, granting fortune and health to the king.* It is indeed wonderful that the cold dragon should emit a fiery spirit from himself; yet experience declares this to be true in the case of burnt serpents, which emit a poisonous flame that infects those standing by. Nor is it in vain that dragons are called the fire-breathing guardians of chemical treasures, such as the **Golden Fleece**, the gardens of the **Hesperides**, <note>A mythological garden with golden apples guarded by the dragon Ladon.</note> those of **Cadmus**, and similar tales. 

Now, this dragon lives in the narrow places of subterranean rocks, where it must be captured and joined to the eagle or the woman—to the woman in a tomb, or to the eagle in a nest, if you prefer. For the nature of the dragon is otherwise to lie in wait for the eagle's eggs, and it carries on a deadly war with the eagle. There are some among the Greek writers who report that a dragon once loved a girl and slept with her. What wonder is it, then, if the Philosophers wish their dragon to be shut up in one cave with a woman? **Greverus** <note>Jodocus Greverus, a 16th-century alchemist.</note> joins red and black dragons in the deepest pit of a mountain and burns them with fire; and when the black ones perish, he says the guardian of the mountain searches for them everywhere and brings them into the mountain. **Merlin**, <note>A reference to the *Allegory of Merlin*, a popular alchemical text where the prophet Merlin undergoes a chemical transformation.</note> in his vision (unless it be a forgery), makes mention of a white and a red dragon; these dragons, whoever they may be—whether one is a woman or a female dragon—act upon each other until both die and emit blood from their wounds, with which they are drenched. Here, the <term>Dragon</term> <note>Representing the dry, "fixed" principles of Earth and Fire.</note> is understood to represent the element of earth and fire, and the <term>Woman</term> <note>Representing the moist, "volatile" principles of Air and Water.</note> represents air and water. Whence the *Sound of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangor buccinæ," a well-known alchemical treatise.</note> says that the dragon is the matter remaining at the bottom...

<summary>This page provides the philosophical and allegorical commentary on the union of the dragon (earth/fire) and the woman (air/water). It cites several alchemical authorities to explain how these contrary elements must be "killed" or transformed to create the medicinal "bath" for the King.</summary>

<keywords>Basil Valentine, Pluto, alchemy, conjunction, elements, Greverus, Merlin, dragon, chemical marriage, Clangor Buccinae</keywords>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page concludes the commentary on Discourse 50, identifying the symbolic "dragon" and "eagle" as specific alchemical principles (internal heat and aerial water). It draws on authorities like Hermes Trismegistus and Bernard of Trevisan to warn against using common materials like store-bought mercury, arguing instead for a "mercury of the philosophers" derived from the metals themselves.</meta>

<page-num>211</page-num>
# DISCOURSE 50.

...after the distillation of water from it. And from Hermes: <note>original: "Hermete." Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> *The water of the air, existing between heaven and earth, is the life of every thing. For this water itself dissolves the body into spirit, and from the dead makes the living, and creates the marriage between man and woman. Indeed, it performs the entire benefit of the art. Of the earth he also says the following:* And understand further, that this very earth which we tread upon is not the true element; rather, it is "elemented" <note>original: "elementata." In alchemical theory, the physical world is a mixture ("elemented") of pure, philosophical elements.</note> by its true **fifth element**. Nor does the elemental **quintessence** <note>The "fifth essence" or purest form of a substance, beyond the four physical elements.</note> depart from its elemented body from which the earth is formed. And soon: *But in the center of the earth there is a virgin and true element, which fire will not be able to burn: This is the **dragon** of whom we speak, working his way into the very center of the earth, where, since the heat is great, he conceives a flaming heat within himself, with which he burns the **woman** or **eagle**.*

The woman or the eagle is the **aerial water**; which some call the white or celestial eagle, and they labor to prepare it from common Mercury or sublimated salts, following in this the guidance of certain blind men in this art who pretend to be sharp-sighted; <note>original: "lynceos." Literally "Lyncean," referring to the mythical Lynceus who could see through stone.</note> But I say to you, says **Count Bernard** <note>Bernhardus Comes, or Bernard of Trevisan, a famous 15th-century alchemist.</note> in his Epistle, that *no water dissolves a metallic species by a natural reduction, except that which remains with it in matter and form, and which the metals themselves can re-congeal.* And soon: *Water does not belong to bodies in solutions if it does not remain with them in congealings.* And a little after: *Truly I say to you, that the **oil** which naturally waxes <note>original: "incerans." To "incerate" is to soften a substance to a wax-like consistency, making it ready for fusion or transformation.</note> and joins natures, and naturally introduces the medicine into other bodies to be tinged, is not composed of anything foreign, but only from the vitals of the body to be dissolved.*

Thus, having perceived this, the eagle and the woman, as well as the dragon and almost all the secrets of the art, are understood; which we have perhaps exposed and declared to the sons of doctrine with the womb of nature opened too wide, so that from this point **TO GOD BE THE GLORY.**

<sig>Dd 2</sig>

It warns that the true alchemical solvent must come from within the metals themselves rather than external chemicals.</summary>
